--- Session 1, page 5 (combined page 5) ---
Motivation





 ■We will revisit this question in detail in another session...

  ■Financial statements...
         • Reveal the answer. (Messi was an expense at Barcelona; Ronaldo, an asset at Real Madrid)

         • But human judgment might affect the numbers:
         • For example, a player’s “value" in the financial statement can be affected by the coach if the
           player’s an asset (e.g. via impairment) but not an expense.
               Impairment testing: If the coach decides a player is no longer going to be used (e.g., out of favour,
              dropped from squad), the accounting team might judge that the player’s “recoverable value” is
                lower than what’s still on the books.

         • What does this mean for the future performance of the team/club?

--- Session 1, page 6 (combined page 6) ---
Introduction




  ■Financial statements are a major source of information on a company.
         • Financial health,

         • Future developments,

         • Time-series and cross-sectional performance.

 ■In this ------   , you will learn the key take-aways from financial statements that you need
     in order to do financial analysis.
         • Reading those numbers. (what are the key numbers?)

         • Assessing the quality of those numbers. (what are the reporting choices that affect those
         numbers?)

         • Interpreting these numbers within the plethora of other financial information. (how does one
           firm compare to others & to itself in the past?)

    UK/EU-based companies), but we will go through the main differences with US GAAP
     for each topic.
         • Importantly, many of the same principles also apply under US GAAP.

                                                                                                                                                    26 / 106

--- Session 1, page 29 (combined page 29) ---
Business performance in a given period...





 ■When analyzing the financial health of a business in a given period, there are three
     particular questions we need to ask:
           1  What cash movements took place?→Cash Flow Statement→liquidity
           2 How much wealth was generated?→Income Statement→profitability

           3  What is the accumulated wealth of the business at the end of the period and what form does
                  it take?→Balance Sheet→wealth

         • He began the venture with £400 of his own money, in cash.

         • On Monday, Paul bought wrapping paper for £400 and sold 3/4 of it for £450 cash.

A simple example





  ■Paul’s business is really simple, but the same principles apply to large and complex
    businesses.

 ■The key take-away is that to understand the financial health of a business we need to
     look at all the three major statements.

 ■Each of them, on its own, gives an incomplete picture of reality.





                                                                                                                                                    37 / 106

--- Session 1, page 39 (combined page 39) ---
Today’s session





 ■Income Statement: measures performance over a period by matching revenues with
     the expenses incurred to earn them. (profit)

 ■Cash Flow Statement: shows where cash came from and where it went during that
   same period. (liquidity)

 ■Balance Sheet : reports the company’s financial position at at a point in time – what it
    owns (assets), what it owes (liabilities), and the owners’ residual (equity).

How the three financial statements connect





                                                Accrual view (timing)


        Balance Sheet                  Income Statement                       Balance Sheet
            Start (t)                        For t →t+1                      End (t+1)
   Assets(t) = Liab.(t)+Equity(t)            Rev −Exp = Net income             Assets(t+1) = Liab.(t+1)+Equity(t+1)





                                  Cash Flow Statement
                                         For t →t+1
                              CFO + CFI + CFF = ∆Cash


                                        Cash view (liquidity)





                                                                                                                                                    39 / 106

--- Session 1, page 41 (combined page 41) ---
The Income Statement



 ■The income statement provides a structured summary of how a business generates
    wealth during a given period.

  ■Its structure is as follows...
           1 We begin with the revenues (or sales) for a period.

           2  Then we subtract the cost of goods sold, obtaining gross profits.

           3  Next, we deduct operating expenses (such as selling, general, and administrative costs,
          R&D...), which leads to the operating profit or EBIT (“earnings before interest and taxes”).

           4  Finally, we incorporate financing costs (interest expense and income) and taxes, which
          leads to the final outcome: the net income.




         Note: Purchases of long-term assets (e.g. machines, buildings, patents) do not appear as expenses on the income statement. They are capitalized on the balance sheet, and

           their cost is allocated over time through depreciation or amortization. Financial investments (e.g. shares, bonds) are also capitalized; the income statement only shows the

         returns (dividends, interest, gains/losses) or impairments.





                                                                                                                                                    40 / 106

--- Session 1, page 42 (combined page 42) ---
The Income Statement





 ■We will discuss the income statement in depth next session.

 ■But the key organizing concept of the income statement is that of accrual accounting:

 ■Revenues (expenses) are recognized when they are earned (incurred), regardless of
    when cash is exchanged.

                                                                                                                                                    45 / 106

--- Session 1, page 46 (combined page 46) ---
Back to Unilever... Thoughts on this income statement?



 ■One interesting feature of this income statement is that Unilever does not report many
     details of its operating profit on this condensed income statement (e.g. COGS).

  ■If you want to see the details of this operating profit, you need to go to the notes of the
     financial statements.

 ■Why do companies do this? Presentation is a choice: what sits in the notes draws less
     attention than what sits on the face of the statement, so a condensed statement steers
    the reader towards the subtotal management wants to be judged on.

 ■But this is about to change! Starting in 2027, IFRS 18 replaces IAS 1, and companies
      will be required to report:
         • The operating profit subtotal (this is already included in Unilever’s income statement)

         • The operating expense analysis has to be presented in the income statement itself, rather
         than in the notes.

--- Session 1, page 51 (combined page 51) ---
Back to Unilever...




 ■The decrease in net profit does not come from a worsening of the operating
     performance...

  ■Rather, it comes from a reshaping of the business and taxation:
         • Unilever sold several businesses during the year (Russia, Elida Beauty, Pureit and Qinyuan),
         which turned a e489m disposal gain in 2023 into a e406m loss in 2024, and were the main
         reason for the effective tax rate jumping from 24.1% to 29.0%.

  ■... and from higher net financing costs and hyperinflation in Argentina and Turkey.

 ■To see this, Unilever offers a non-GAAP measure called underlying profit, which strips
    out the post-tax effect of disposals, restructuring and impairments...

                                                                                                                                                    53 / 106

--- Session 1, page 53 (combined page 53) ---
The Statement of Other Comprehensive Income



 ■The income statement stops at net profit. Some gains and losses never pass through
        it...

 ■Other comprehensive income (OCI) collects them: changes in the owners’ wealth that
    are deliberately kept out of profit.
         • They are typically unrealised, and driven by things largely outside management’s control —
        exchange rates, discount rates, market prices.

         • The idea is to protect the profit number as a measure of performance, without pretending
           that these gains and losses did not happen.

         • At the same time, the balance sheet is updated to reflect these changes in wealth (e.g.,
        remeasurement of assets).

 ■So the statement is a very short one:
                Net profit +   OCI  = Total comprehensive income
                   income|   {zstatement}      everything|{z} else



                                                                                                                                                    54 / 106

--- Session 1, page 54 (combined page 54) ---
Back to Unilever... OCI (Other Comprehensive Income)





                                                                                                                                                    55 / 106

--- Session 1, page 55 (combined page 55) ---
The Statement of Other Comprehensive Income




  ■Notice that the statement splits OCI into two groups...
           1  Items that will never be reclassified to profit or loss (e.g. remeasurements of pension plans,
          gains and losses on equity investments).

           2  Items that may be reclassified to profit or loss later on (e.g. currency retranslation of foreign
           subsidiaries, cash flow hedges).

  ■“Reclassified” means the amount is sitting in equity now, but may be moved into profit
     later — when the gain or loss is finally realised.

  ■Unilever gives us an example: the notes recorded an e545m currency translation
     write-off on the disposals of its Russian business, Elida Beauty, Pureit and Qinyuan.
         •  It accumulated in OCI over many years →and then landed in 2024’s profit, on the day those
         businesses were sold.

                                                                                                                                                    57 / 106

--- Session 1, page 57 (combined page 57) ---
Back to Unilever... what does OCI add?




 ■Net profit was e6,369m, but OCI added another e1,923m →total comprehensive
    income of e8,292m.

 ■That is close to a quarter of the gain recognised in 2024 that never appeared in the
    income statement.

 ■Almost all of it is currency retranslation (e1,389m): Unilever has subsidiaries in
    dozens of currencies, but reports in euros.

 ■Two things to keep in mind:
         • OCI never touches earnings per share — EPS is based on profit, not on comprehensive
         income.
         • These amounts do not disappear: they land directly in equity →we will meet them again in
          the shareholders’ equity statement.





                                                                                                                                                    58 / 106

--- Session 1, page 58 (combined page 58) ---
The Cash Flow Statement



 ■The cash flow statement shows how cash actually moves in and out of the business
     during a given period.
         • Does the firm’s core business generate enough cash to sustain itself and finance
          investments, or is it dependent on external financing?

  ■Its structure is as follows...
           1 We begin with cash flows from operating activities (that is, cash generated or used by the
         company’s core business →this includes expenditures on current assets).

           2  Then we move to cash flows from investing activities (that is, expenditures on non-current
         assets and proceeds from asset sales).

           3  Next, we record cash flows from financing activities (that is, flows from raising and
          repaying capital).

           4  By combining 1-3, the statement yields the change in cash — which reconciles the cash
         balance at the beginning with that at the end of the period.





                                                                                                                                                    59 / 106

--- Session 1, page 59 (combined page 59) ---
The Cash Flow Statement





 ■We will discuss the cash flow statement in depth later in the ------    .

 ■For now, it is important to note that it is prepared under cash accounting principles:

  ■Transactions are recorded only when money changes hands.
         •  If a firm makes a sale on credit, no cash flow is recorded until the customer pays.

         • We don’t consider accounting estimates, credit sales, and non-cash expenses such as
          depreciation.

finance investments, or is it dependent on external financing?

--- Session 1, page 61 (combined page 61) ---
Back to Unilever... Does it generate enough cash to sustain itself and
finance investments, or is it dependent on external financing?



 ■Yes! Look at the net cash flow from operating activities, e9,519m...

 ■That covered CAPEX (e1,738m of PP&E plus e233m of intangibles) almost 5 times
     over!

  ■After CAPEX and net interest, free cash flow was e6,932m:

    NetCFO+PP&E+Intangibles+DisposalofPP&E+InterestReceived+InterestPaid = ...

                        9, 519 −1, 738 −233 + 37 + 432 −1085 = 6, 932

 ■This is a free cash flow margin of 11.4% of turnover.

  ■It was also enough to distribute dividends to shareholders & repurchase shares.





                                                                                                                                                    62 / 106

--- Session 1, page 62 (combined page 62) ---
The Balance Sheet





 ■The balance sheet reports the company’s financial position at a point in time — what it
    owns, what is owes, and the owner’s residual.

 ■This statement is prepared based on some important accounting conventions.

  ■Let’s take a look...





                                                                                                                                                    63 / 106

--- Session 1, page 63 (combined page 63) ---
Accounting Conventions





   1  Business entity convention: for accounting purposes, the business and its owner(s) are
     treated as being distinct.
         • Owners are treated as claimants against their own business.

         • That is why “Equity” appears on the right hand side of the Accounting Equation:

                                     Assets = Liabilities + Equity

         • In practice, changes in profits or losses across periods are “absorbed” into the equity term.

         • Remember Paul’s story: his money was treated as an injection of cash in the cash flow
          statement, and as a claim in the balance sheet.





                                                                                                                                                    64 / 106

--- Session 1, page 64 (combined page 64) ---
Accounting Conventions





   2  Historic cost convention: the value of assets shown on the balance sheet should be
    based on their historic cost (i.e. the acquisition cost).
         • This is meant to minimize problems of measurement reliability.

         • This can be misleading because the historical cost often becomes quickly outdated.





                                                                                                                                                    65 / 106

--- Session 1, page 65 (combined page 65) ---
Accounting Conventions - Example





   2  Historic cost convention: the value of assets shown on the balance sheet should be
    based on their historic cost (i.e. the acquisition cost).
         • Suppose Company A buys Company B and recognizes £500m of goodwill.

         •  If B’s brand and customer base become more valuable over time, the carrying value stays at
        £500m.

         • The reported number quickly becomes outdated, because it never reflects growth in value —
          only potential impairment.





                                                                                                                                                    66 / 106

--- Session 1, page 66 (combined page 66) ---
Accounting Conventions - Example





   2  Historic cost convention: the value of assets shown on the balance sheet should be
    based on their historic cost (i.e. the acquisition cost).
         • Meta (Facebook) acquired Instagram in 2012 with $435 mi of recorded goodwill.

         • Since then, it is likely that its current value has gone up...

         • Instagram scaled from around 30 mi users at acquisition to more than 1 bi within a few years,
         launched Stories/Reels, and became a major ads surface.





                                                                                                                                                    67 / 106

--- Session 1, page 67 (combined page 67) ---
Accounting Conventions





   2  Historic cost convention: the value of assets shown on the balance sheet should be
    based on their historic cost (i.e. the acquisition cost).
         • Importantly, fair value measurement has been increasingly adopted in accounting standards
            in recent years... But historic cost still applies to many assets.

         • Why is this convention important?
                This means that the assets are based on verifiable numbers, rather than on subjective estimates
                  of current market value.
                            It also limits how management can manipulate the balance sheet by inflating asset values.
                But this also means that the balance sheet is not a valuation of the business — it is a snapshot
                  of the accounting records at a point in time.





                                                                                                                                                    68 / 106

--- Session 1, page 68 (combined page 68) ---
Accounting Conventions





   3  Prudence convention: caution should be exercised when preparing financial statements.
         • Reporting “good news” (e.g. profits or increases in asset values) demands a higher
         standard of evidence than “bad news”.

         • The effect is that “bad news” is often reported much earlier than “good news”.


                            Assets  =   Liabilities  + Equity
                                no overstatement| {z }       no |understatement{z   }


         • An example of “no overstatement” of assets is that goodwill can only be reevaluated
         downwards, through impairment.





                                                                                                                                                    69 / 106

--- Session 1, page 69 (combined page 69) ---
Accounting Conventions - Example





   3  Prudence convention: caution should be exercised when preparing financial statements.
         • An example is the recording of credit losses on banks’ loan books...

         • Banks must record expected credit losses up front, even if customers are still paying.

         • But they cannot book expected interest gains before they are actually earned.





                                                                                                                                                    70 / 106

--- Session 1, page 70 (combined page 70) ---
Accounting Conventions - Example





   3  Prudence convention: caution should be exercised when preparing financial statements.
         • Another example is pharmaceutical companies facing lawsuits...

         •  If it’s probable they’ll lose and a reliable estimate exists, they must recognize a provision.

         • But probable wins from lawsuits are never booked until the cash is actually received.





                                                                                                                                                    71 / 106

--- Session 1, page 71 (combined page 71) ---
Accounting Conventions





   4  Dual aspect convention: every transaction has two aspects...
         • A credit into one account, and a debit to another account.

         • This guarantees that the Accounting Equation always holds:

                              Assets = Liabilities + Equity





                                                                                                                                                    72 / 106

--- Session 1, page 72 (combined page 72) ---
Accounting Conventions





   5  Going concern: the business will continue its operations for the foreseeable future
     (unless there is evidence of the contrary).
         • There is no intention, or need, to sell non-current assets.

         •  If a business’s going concern status is called into question, losses must be anticipated and
             fully reported.





                                                                                                                                                    73 / 106

--- Session 1, page 73 (combined page 73) ---
Accounting Conventions - Example





   5  Going concern: the business will continue its operations for the foreseeable future
     (unless there is evidence of the contrary).
         • With COVID-19, airlines were severely hit. Still, IAG (owner of British Airways) stated in its
        2020 annual report:


      “Having reviewed the Base Case, Downside Case and additional sensitivities, the
        Directors have a reasonable expectation that the Group has sufficient liquidity to
     continue in operational existence for the foreseeable future and hence continue to adopt
        the going concern basis in preparing the financial statements. (...) The financial
    statements do not include the adjustments that would result if the Group was unable to
                               continue as a going concern.”





                                                                                                                                                    74 / 106

--- Session 1, page 74 (combined page 74) ---
Accounting Conventions - Example





   5  Going concern: the business will continue its operations for the foreseeable future
     (unless there is evidence of the contrary).
         • In February 24 2023, Silicon Valley Bank filled its 2022 annual report, which received a
          clean audit and did not flag risks related to the ability of the business to continue as a
        “going concern”.

         • In March 8 2023, SVB announced a $1.8bn loss on securities sales and a planned equity
           raise. By March 10 2023, the bank collapsed.

         • Auditors are required to issue a going concern warning if there is substantial doubt about the
          business survival and management’s disclosures are adequate. If disclosures are missing or
          misleading, the auditor must disclaim their opinion.

         • Should KPGM have issued a going concern warning?





                                                                                                                                                    75 / 106

--- Session 1, page 75 (combined page 75) ---
Structure of the Balance Sheet




 ■ASSETS: a measurable (monetary terms) economic resource under the control of the
    business.
         • £1,000 owed to the business by a credit customer who is unable to pay. NO

         • A patent, bought from an inventor, that gives the business the right to produce a new product.
         Production of the new product is expected to increase profits over the period during which
          the patent is held. YES

         • A recently hired marketing director who is confidently expected to increase profits by over
       30% during the next three years. NO

         • A recently purchased machine that will save the business £10,000 each year. It is already
         being used by the business but it has been acquired on credit and not yet paid for.YES/NO
        (depends if the company controls the asset — payment date is irrelevant)





                                                                                                                                                    77 / 106

--- Session 1, page 76 (combined page 76) ---
Structure of the Balance Sheet




 ■ASSETS: a measurable (monetary terms) economic resource under the control of the
    business.
         • The company’s brand reputation, built over decades, which customers strongly associate
          with quality. NO (unless the company is acquired — then we recognize this through
         goodwill)

         • A pool of millions of customers following the firm’s profile on Instagram, evaluated at £5 mi.
      NO (unless the company is acquired — then we recognize this through goodwill)

         • An unpaid invoice of £10,000 due from a customer, though there is some risk of default.
       YES (adjusted for credit risk)

         • A building leased for 10 years, giving the company exclusive rights to use the property
          during that period. YES





                                                                                                                                                    78 / 106

--- Session 1, page 77 (combined page 77) ---
Structure of the Balance Sheet





 ■ASSETS: a measurable (monetary terms) economic resource under the control of the
    business.
         • Current assets: those that are held for the short term.
              They are held for sale or consumption during the business’s normal operating cycle.
              They are expected to be sold within a year after the date of the relevant balance sheet.
              They are held principally for trading.
              They are cash, or near cash, short-term investments.

         • Trade receivables, inventories, and cash.





                                                                                                                                                    79 / 106

--- Session 1, page 78 (combined page 78) ---
Structure of the Balance Sheet





 ■ASSETS: a measurable (monetary terms) economic resource under the control of the
    business.
         • Non-current assets: those that do not meet the previous definition.
              Tend to be held for long-term operations.
            Can be either tangible (PP&E) or intangible.





                                                                                                                                                    80 / 106

--- Session 1, page 79 (combined page 79) ---
Structure of the Balance Sheet





 ■CLAIMS: an obligation of the business to provide cash, or some other form of benefit, to
    an outside party.
         • Equity: the claim of the owner(s) against the business.

         •  Liabilities: claims of other parties, apart from the owner(s).

         •  If a business wishes to acquire assets, it must raise funds from one or the other or both





                                                                                                                                                    81 / 106

--- Session 1, page 80 (combined page 80) ---
Structure of the Balance Sheet





 ■CLAIMS: an obligation of the business to provide cash, or some other form of benefit, to
    an outside party.
         • Current liabilities: amounts due for settlement in the short term.
              They are expected to be settled within the business’s normal operating cycle.
              They arise principally as a result of trading.
              They are due to be settled within a year after the date of the relevant balance sheet.
              There is no right to defer settlement beyond a year after the date of the relevant balance sheet.

         • Non-current liabilities: amounts due that do no meet the definition above — essentially
          longer-term liabilities.

--- Session 1, page 81 (combined page 81) ---
Back to Unilever... What about the balance sheet?





                                                                                                                                                    83 / 106

--- Session 1, page 82 (combined page 82) ---
What about current assets versus current liabilities?



  ■It is interesting to note that, for Unilever, the value of “current liabilities” exceeds the
    value of “current assets”.

  ■You’d be alarmed if you had less money in the bank than the amount of bills you have to
      pay...

 ■But this is part of Unilever’s business model: it is essentially financed by suppliers.
         • Cash from sales comes in quickly and, in practice, more quickly than obligations are due.

         • This is because these big companies have a lot of bargaining power to only pay suppliers
           after sales have happened, not in advance.

         • This means that Unilever can rely on suppliers to actively supply financing without the need
           to borrow additional money for operations.

 ■The low working capital ratio ( currentcurrent liabilitiesassets < 1) can actually be a sign of efficiency.





                                                                                                                                                    84 / 106

--- Session 1, page 83 (combined page 83) ---
The Shareholders’ Equity Statement





 ■We have just seen Unilever’s balance sheet. It tells us that shareholders’ equity was
    e18.1bn at the end of 2023 and e20.0bn at the end of 2024.

 ■But it does not tell us why  it moved.
         • Did the business generate wealth? Did the owners put money in? Did they take money out?

 ■That is the job of the statement of changes in equity (also called the shareholders’
     equity statement).
         •  It is the fourth primary statement, required both under IFRS (IAS 1) and under US GAAP.

         •  It reconciles the opening balance of equity with the closing balance, line by line.





                                                                                                                                                    85 / 106

--- Session 1, page 84 (combined page 84) ---
The Shareholders’ Equity Statement




  ■Its structure is as follows...
           1 We begin with the opening balance of equity (that is, the closing balance of last year’s
         balance sheet).

           2  Then we add the total comprehensive income for the period: the profit or loss from the
         income statement, plus other comprehensive income.

           3  Next, we record transactions with owners: dividends paid, shares issued, shares
         repurchased, treasury shares, share-based payments.

           4  By combining 1-3, the statement yields the closing balance of equity — which is exactly the
          equity line of this year’s balance sheet.


            Equityt   + Comprehensive income ± Transactions with shareholders =    Equityt+1
          balance| {zsheet}  (t)    |       performance{z          }    |             owners{z             }     balance|   sheet{z   (t+1)}





                                                                                                                                                    86 / 106

--- Session 1, page 85 (combined page 85) ---
Why do we care? Equity moves for two very different reasons



  ■Performance: wealth created by the business.
         • The profit or loss for the period (which comes straight from the income statement).

         • Plus other comprehensive income: gains and losses that IFRS deliberately keeps out of
             profit — currency translation of foreign subsidiaries, pension remeasurements, some hedges
        and equity investments.

         • They still change the owners’ wealth, so they have to land somewhere: they land directly in
           equity.

  ■Transactions with owners: no wealth is created — it is only moved between the firm
    and its shareholders.
         • Dividends and share buybacks take money out; share issues put money in.

 ■Two firms can report the same increase in equity for completely different reasons. This
    statement is what lets you tell them apart.





                                                                                                                                                    87 / 106

--- Session 1, page 86 (combined page 86) ---
Remember Paul?



  ■Paul’s equity statement would read as follows:


                £                            Monday  Tuesday

                Opening equity                          –      550
                    Capital introduced by the owner           400        –
                       Profit for the period                     150      180
                 Drawings (the equivalent of dividends)        –        –

                 Closing equity                        550      730


 ■Both the £400 and the £150 increase equity — but only one of them is performance.
  ■If Paul had taken £200 home on Tuesday, equity would have fallen even though the
    business was profitable.





                                                                                                                                                    88 / 106

--- Session 1, page 87 (combined page 87) ---
Structure of the Shareholders’ Equity Statement




 ■The statement is a grid: each row is an event, each column is a component of equity.
         • Called-up share capital: the nominal (par) value of the shares issued.

         • Share premium: what shareholders paid above nominal value when the shares were issued.

         • Other reserves: items sitting outside profit — the currency translation reserve, fair value
          reserves, treasury shares and shares bought back.

         • Retained profit: cumulative profits that have not been paid out. This is the column that links
         back to the income statement.

         • Non-controlling interests: the share of consolidated subsidiaries that the group does not
         own.


  Read across a row: one event.  Read down a column: the history of one type of claim.

--- Session 1, page 91 (combined page 91) ---
Back to Unilever... so what does this tell us?




 ■In 2024 Unilever paid out e4,320m in dividends and bought back e1,508m of its own
    shares.

 ■That is e5,828m returned to shareholders against e5,744m of profit in that period — a
    payout of slightly more than 100% of earnings.

 ■So equity did not grow because profits were retained — once buybacks are included,
     returns to shareholders exceed earnings.

  ■It grew because of other comprehensive income — mainly e1,297m of currency
     retranslation gains.

 ■Two companies can report the same equity growth for completely different reasons...

  ■If we consider underlying earnings (we saw this earlier) the payout ratio would be only
    78%.

--- Session 2, page 3 (combined page 108) ---
Introduction

--- Session 2, page 4 (combined page 109) ---
Motivation





 ■Today we will talk about revenue recognition...

 ■But why do we care about revenue?
         •  It is the top line item in the income statement, and usually is the single largest number in this
          statement.

         •  It is historically the most prevalent reason for accounting restatements.

         •  It is often used as a channel for earnings management since recognition involves
         substantial judgment and small timing shifts can have big effects.





                                                                                                                                                     3 / 118

--- Session 2, page 5 (combined page 110) ---
Example 1 - Qwest Communications International Inc.





 ■Revenue recognition has been a matter of fraud...

  ■“between 1999 and 2002, Qwest fraudulently recognized over $3.8 billion in revenue
    and excluded $231 million in expenses as part of a multi-faceted fraudulent scheme to
    meet optimistic and unsupportable revenue and earnings projections.”
         • They traded network capacity (rights to use their fiber-optic lines) with other phone
         companies and counted it as revenue, even though no new customer business was created.

         • They booked equipment “sales” upfront while hiding secret side deals associated with those
          sales (e.g., cancellations or future give-backs).

         • They back-dated paperwork so revenue appeared in an earlier quarter to hit targets.





                                                                                                                                                     4 / 118

--- Session 2, page 6 (combined page 111) ---
Example 2 - Under Armour Inc.





 ■Revenue recognition has been a matter of active manipulation...





                                                                                                                                                     5 / 118

--- Session 2, page 7 (combined page 112) ---
Example 2 - Under Armour Inc.




 ■Revenue recognition has been a matter of active manipulation...





                                                                                                                                                     6 / 118

--- Session 2, page 8 (combined page 113) ---
Example 3 - Luna Innovations Incorporated


 ■Revenue recognition has been a matter unintentional errors...

 ■“Luna Innovations Incorporated (NASDAQ: LUNA) (the “Company”), a global leader in
    advanced fiber optic-based technology, today announced that its Audit Committee of the
    Board of Directors (the “Audit Committee”), after consultation with the Company’s
    management and the Special Committee’s external legal and financial advisors,
    determined that its financial statements for the fiscal year ended December 31, 2022,
    and the interim periods ended March 31, 2022, June 30, 2022, and September 30,
    2022 and March 31, 2023, cannot be relied upon and need to be restated due to
     identified accounting errors relating to revenue recognition.”

 ■A final report on what were the specific errors was never published: Luna was delisted
    from Nasdaq in January 2025 and deregistered its stock, ending its reporting
     obligations.
         • Six quarterly reports and one annual report were never restated.

         • In February 2026, Luna settled a shareholders’ class action for $7.3mi — admitting no
         wrongdoing.

Review - The Basics



 ■Suppose a company sells inventories worth $ 100 for $ 130...





                                                                                                                                                     8 / 118

--- Session 2, page 10 (combined page 115) ---
Terminology



  ■Before we proceed, here are some important terms:


   1  Income: increases in assets or decreases in liabilities that increase equity, other than
     contributions from equity holders.
           1  Revenue: income from contracts with customers when control of promised goods or
          services transfers.

           2  Finance income: returns on financial assets (e.g., interest on loans/deposits, dividends,
           fair-value gains/losses).

           3  Lease income: income from leasing assets.

           4  Gains (other income): income arising from remeasurement or derecognition events, such
         as gain on disposal of assets and FX gain.

 ■Today we will focus solely on REVENUE. We will discuss other sources of income later
     in the ------    .



                                                                                                                                                     9 / 118

--- Session 2, page 11 (combined page 116) ---
Warm Up

--- Session 2, page 12 (combined page 117) ---
What is revenue?




  ■Let’s decide the type of income in each of the examples below...


    1 A retailer earns interest on its surplus cash held in a 6-month term deposit. →finance
    income

    2 A brand owner receives a sales-based royalty from licensing its trademark to a
     franchisee. →revenue

    3 A landlord receives fixed base rent from leasing out a shop unit for 10 years. →lease
    income

    4 A franchisor collects a non-refundable initial franchise fee at contract inception. →
    revenue





                                                                                                                                                    10 / 118

--- Session 2, page 13 (combined page 118) ---
What is revenue?





  ■Let’s decide the type of income in each of the examples below...


    1 A manufacturer charges a customer a contractually agreed termination fee for
     cancelling a bespoke order after work began. →revenue

    2 A retailer recognizes expected breakage on gift cards that are unlikely to be redeemed.
    →revenue

    3 An investor receives cash dividends from shares in a listed company. →finance income

    4 A logistics firm sells an old delivery truck for more than its carrying amount. →gain

--- Session 2, page 15 (combined page 120) ---
How (and when) to Recognize
       Revenues?

--- Session 2, page 16 (combined page 121) ---
Revenue Recognition Standard





 ■The asset-liability approach recognizes and measures revenue based on changes in
    assets and liabilities.
 ■Companies account for revenue based on the asset or liability arising from contracts
     with customers.
  ■Contracts indicate the terms of the transaction, provide the measurement of the
     consideration, and specify the promises that must be met by each party.





                                                                                                                                                    13 / 118

--- Session 2, page 17 (combined page 122) ---
Asset-Liability Approach





  ■Let’s take a simple example...

 ■A customer subscribes to a magazine subscription worth £120.

 ■The subscription is for 12 months.

 ■The customer pays for the subscription upfront.

 ■When should revenue be recognized?





                                                                                                                                                    14 / 118

--- Session 2, page 18 (combined page 123) ---
Asset-Liability Approach





                                                                                                                                                    15 / 118

--- Session 2, page 19 (combined page 124) ---
Asset-Liability Approach





                                                                                                                                                    16 / 118

--- Session 2, page 20 (combined page 125) ---
Asset-Liability Approach





                                                                                                                                                    17 / 118

--- Session 2, page 21 (combined page 126) ---
Recognizing sales - Historical Approach




 ■To better understand revenue recognition under this approach, let’s make a quick
     historical detour.

  ■Let’s focus on the most common source of revenue: sales of goods.

 ■The basic recognition criteria used to be:
           1  Revenue has been earned when the firm has satisfied its performance obligations. Typically,
          the firm has conveyed the goods to the buyer.

           2  Revenue is measurable when the firm has received cash, a receivable or some other asset
         capable of reasonable measurement.

 ■Revenue is recognized at the earliest time when these two conditions are met.
         • Note that this view is independent from the resulting changes in assets and liabilities over
          reporting periods.





                                                                                                                                                    18 / 118

--- Session 2, page 22 (combined page 127) ---
Recognizing sales - Historical Approach





 ■So when was a sale recognized?

  ■Historically, revenue from a sale could be recognized...
           1  At delivery (most common).

           2  Before delivery (when waiting until delivery distorts performance measures).

           3  After delivery (when some doubt exists about whether cash will be collected).

 ■The specific moment of recognition was dependent on the characteristics of that
     sale/industry.





                                                                                                                                                    19 / 118

--- Session 2, page 23 (combined page 128) ---
Recognizing sales - Historical Approach





                                                                                                                                                    21 / 118

--- Session 2, page 24 (combined page 129) ---
Recognizing sales - Historical Approach





                                                                                                                                                    22 / 118

--- Session 2, page 25 (combined page 130) ---
Recognizing sales - Does the approach matter?





  ■Let’s go back to our magazine subscription for a moment...

 ■Suppose that, on top of the £120, the customer also pays a non-refundable “joining
    fee” of £20 when signing up.
         • The fee covers opening the account and setting up the delivery.

         •  It is not refundable, and the customer does not pay it again if they renew.

 ■How much revenue should be recognized in the first month?

 ■The magazines are the easy part: £10, just as before. The question is what to do with
    the £20...





                                                                                                                                                    23 / 118

--- Session 2, page 26 (combined page 131) ---
Recognizing sales - Does the approach matter?





 ■Under the historical approach, we ask: has the firm earned it?
         • The account has been opened — the firm has done exactly what the fee was charged for.

         • Revenue in month 1: £20 + £10 = £30.

 ■Under the asset-liability approach, we ask something different: what do we still owe
    the customer?
         • Opening an account is not something the customer can consume on its own — our only
         promise is to deliver 12 issues.

         • So the £20 is simply an advance payment. The liability is £140, not £120.
         • Revenue in month 1: £140 ÷ 12 = £11.67.





                                                                                                                                                    24 / 118

--- Session 2, page 27 (combined page 132) ---
Recognizing sales - Current Approach




 ■Since 2018, every company disclosing under the IFRS standard is required to follow the
    “Five-Step Process” for revenue recognition.

 ■This new standard applies the same model and principles to every transfer of goods
     or services to customers.
         • That is: recognition of revenue is no longer tied to the specifics of a sale or industry.

 ■The key revenue recognition principle under this standardized procedure is:
         • Recognize revenue in the accounting period when the performance obligation is satisfied.

 ■And the organizing concept guiding this process is the one we previously discussed: the
     asset-liability approach.
         • That is: changes in assets and liabilities across reporting periods guide revenue recognition.





                                                                                                                                                    25 / 118

--- Session 2, page 28 (combined page 133) ---
The “Five-Step Process” - Overview





   1  Identify the contract with customers.

   2  Identify the separate performance obligations in the contract.

   3  Determine the transaction price.

   4  Allocate the transaction price to the separate performance obligations.

   5  Recognize revenue when each performance obligation is satisfied.





                                                                                                                                                    26 / 118

--- Session 2, page 29 (combined page 134) ---
The “Five-Step Process” - Example 5



  ■Airbus signs a contract to sell airplanes to Cathay Pacific Airlines for e100mi.


   1  Identify the contract with customers. →Airbus to deliver airplanes to Cathay

   2  Identify the separate performance obligations in the contract. →Single obligation:
     deliver airplanes.
         • A separate obligation would be created in case Airbus agreed to maintain the planes.

   3  Determine the transaction price. →e100mi.

   4  Allocate the transaction price to the separate performance obligations. →single
     obligation with price of e100mi.

   5  Recognize revenue when each performance obligation is satisfied. →revenue of
    e100mi recognized when control of the airplanes transfers to Cathay — normally on
     delivery.



                                                                                                                                                    27 / 118

--- Session 2, page 30 (combined page 135) ---
The “Five-Step Process” - Example 6



 ■Paula orders a Caffè Latte costing £4 from Starbucks. Paula hands £4, cash, to the
     barista, who then prepares the drink and hands it to Paula.

           1  Identify the contract with customers. →Paula agreed to pay the £4 for the drink, and
         Starbucks agreed to sell it.

           2  Identify the separate performance obligations in the contract. →single performance
           obligation: handing the drink to Paula.

           3  Determine the transaction price. →£4.

           4  Allocate the transaction price to the separate performance obligations. →single obligation
          with price of £4.

           5  Recognize revenue when each performance obligation is satisfied. →revenue of £4 should
        be recognized when Paula is handed the drink.





                                                                                                                                                    28 / 118

--- Session 2, page 31 (combined page 136) ---
The “Five-Step Process” - Example 6





 ■Paula orders a Caffè Latte costing £4 from Starbucks. Paula hands £4, cash, to the
     barista, who then prepares the drink and hands it to Paula.

 ■Would it make a difference if Paula paid for the drink with a credit card?

 ■No! That only affects the timing when cash changes hands (cash view), but not the
     timing when revenue is earned (accrual view) — that is: when the performance
     obligation is satisfied.





                                                                                                                                                    29 / 118

--- Session 2, page 32 (combined page 137) ---
The “Five-Step Process” - Example 7





 ■Paula now orders a Caffè Latte costing £4 and Egg Bites costing £5 pounds from
     Starbucks. Paula hands £9, cash, to the barista, who then prepares the drink and hands
        it to Paula.

 ■The barista then heats the Egg Bites, and hands it to Paula.

 ■When and how much revenue should be recognized in this case?

 ■Revenue of £4 is recognized when the drink is handed to Paula. Revenue of additional
    £5 is recognized when the Egg Bites are handed to Paula.





                                                                                                                                                    31 / 118

--- Session 2, page 33 (combined page 138) ---
The “Five-Step Process” - Example 8



  ■Starbucks decides to incentivize the sales of its Starbucks Expresso Roast. While a bag
     of these beans is usually sold for £12 and a expresso, for £3, Starbucks decides to sell
     the expresso for £1 when a customer also buys a bag of Expresso Roast.

 ■Paula takes advantage of this promotion. Upon paying £13 to the barista, Paula
     receives the bag of beans. The barista then prepares the drink and hands it to Paula.

 ■When and how much revenue should be recognized?

 ■The allocation of price between the performance obligations should be proportional to
     their original prices. This means that revenue of £10.40 should be recognized
    immediately when delivering the bag of beans, while the remaining £2.60 should be
    recognized when the coffee is handed to Paula.




 Note: original price of the bundle = £15, of which 80% comes from the bag of beans (£12) and 20%, from the expresso (£3).




                                                                                                                                                    32 / 118

--- Session 2, page 34 (combined page 139) ---
Step 1: Identifying the contract with customers




 ■A contract is an agreement between two or more parties that creates enforceable rights
     or obligations.
         • Contracts can be written, oral, or implied from customary business practice.

         • Revenue is recognized only when a valid contract exists.

 ■From an accounting perspective, a valid contract is characterized by the following:
           1  The contract has commercial substance.

           2  The parties have approved the contract.

           3  Identification of the rights of the parties is established.

           4  Payment terms are identified.

           5   It is probable that the consideration will be collected.





                                                                                                                                                    33 / 118

--- Session 2, page 35 (combined page 140) ---
Step 2: Identifying separate performance obligations





 ■A performance obligation is a promise (explicit, implicit, or based on customary
    business practice) to provide a product or a service to a customer.

 ■To determine whether a company has to account for multiple performance
     obligations, the company’s promise to sell the good or service to the customer must be
    separately identifiable from the other promises within the contract.
         • An “expresso” is a single performance obligation.

         • A “caffè latte” is also a single performance obligation: coffee and milk are not separately
           identifiable in this case because they are highly interdependent within the contract.





                                                                                                                                                    34 / 118

--- Session 2, page 36 (combined page 141) ---
Step 2: Identifying separate performance obligations



 ■What about the following cases?
           1 A company sells an automobile at a price that includes six months of telematics services
            (e.g., navigation system). The customer can renew the subscription for the services after the
           six months. Separate obligations.

           2 A company licenses a customer-relationship software to a customer. The company promises
          also promises to perform consulting services by customizing the software to the customer’s
          information technology environment. The total price includes both the license and the
          service. Single obligation.

           3 A telecom bundles a smartphone and a 24-month mobile service plan for one bundled price.
        The handset can be used with other carriers, and the mobile service is sold separately.
         Separate obligations.

           4 A SaaS provider charges a non-refundable set-up/activation fee together with monthly
          hosting for its platform. The set-up activities are necessary to provide the hosting. Single
          obligation.

           5 An equipment manufacturer sells a machine and grants the customer an option to buy spare
          parts for 12 months at a 40% discount. Separate obligations.


                                                                                                                                                    35 / 118

--- Session 2, page 37 (combined page 142) ---
Step 3: Determining the transaction price





 ■The transaction price is the amount of consideration that a company expects to receive
    from a customer in exchange for transferring goods and services.





                                                                                                                                                    36 / 118

--- Session 2, page 38 (combined page 143) ---
Step 3: Determining the transaction price



 ■In some cases...
         • The price of a good or service might depend on future events (e.g. price increases, volume
          discounts, rebates, credits, performance bonuses, royalties...).
             The company must estimate the amount to be received (either the expected value or the most
                     likely amount).
         • The price might need to account for the time value of money when the contract involves a
           significant financing component.
             The effects of financing are recorded as interest income (if the customer pays later) or interest
              expense (if the customer pays in advance).
         • The consideration received by the company is in the form of goods, services, or other
         non-cash consideration.
              Companies generally must recognize revenue on the basis of the fair value of what is received.
         • Companies might make payments to their customers as part of the agreement (e.g.
          discounts, volume rebates, coupons...).
              These elements reduce the consideration received and the revenue to be recognized.





                                                                                                                                                    37 / 118

--- Session 2, page 39 (combined page 144) ---
Step 4: Allocating the transaction price to separate performance
obligations




 ■For revenue arrangements with multiple performance obligations, a company might be
     required to allocate the transaction price to more than one performance obligation in the
     contract.

  ■If an allocation is needed, the transaction price is allocated to the various performance
     obligations based on their relative fair values.

 ■This is, usually, their standalone selling prices.

  ■If this information is not available, companies should use their best estimate of what the
    good or service might sell for as a standalone item.





                                                                                                                                                    38 / 118

--- Session 2, page 40 (combined page 145) ---
Step 5: Recognizing revenue when each performance obligation is satisfied




 ■A company has satisfied its performance obligation(s) when the customer has obtained
     control of the product(s) (good or service).

 ■Change in control is the deciding factor in determining when a performance obligation is
     satisfied.

 ■The below are indicators of when a customer controls the product or service...
           1  The company has a right to payment for the asset.

           2  The company has transferred legal title to the asset.

           3  The company has transferred physical possession of the asset.

           4  The customer has significant risks and rewards of ownership.

           5  The customer has accepted the asset.





                                                                                                                                                    39 / 118

--- Session 2, page 41 (combined page 146) ---
Example 9





  ■S-mobile offers a “free” handset with each 12-month data & call contract, for £35/month.

  ■Without the contract, the company would sell the handset for £330, and would provide
    the call & data services separately for £15/month in a 12-month contract.

 ■With regards to costs, the handset costs £200, while the services, £5/month.





                                                                                                                                                    40 / 118

--- Session 2, page 42 (combined page 147) ---
Example 9





  ■Let’s use the 5 steps we saw to determine revenue recognition...

 ■Step 1: Identify the contract with customers. (straight-forward)

 ■Step 2: Identify the separate performance obligations in the contract.
           1  Delivery of the handset.

           2  Delivery of the data & call services (monthly).

 ■Step 3: Determine the transaction price.
         • The transaction price is £420 (£35 × 12).





                                                                                                                                                    41 / 118

--- Session 2, page 43 (combined page 148) ---
Example 9





  ■Let’s use the 5 steps we saw to determine revenue recognition...

 ■Step 4: Allocate the transaction price to the separate performance obligations.
         • We perform this allocation based on the individual prices of each obligation...
         • The transaction price for the handset is £272 (£330/(£330 + £15 × 12) × £420).

         • The transaction price for the services is £148 (£420 - £272), which equals £12.25/month.

 ■Step 5: Recognize revenue when each performance obligation is satisfied.
         • Revenue of £272 is recognized immediately, as the handset is delivered to the customer.
          (gross profit of £72).

         • Revenue of £12.33 is recognized at the end of each month (gross profit of £7.33).





                                                                                                                                                    42 / 118

--- Session 2, page 44 (combined page 149) ---
Example 9





 ■The income statement at the end of the year (after the 12 months)...
         • Revenue ....................... £420 (£272 + £12.33 × 12)
         • Cost of sales ................. £260 (£200 + £5 × 12)
         • Gross profit ................... £160





                                                                                                                                                    43 / 118

--- Session 2, page 45 (combined page 150) ---
Example 9 - alternative!




 ■Suppose this example actually takes place in Turkey, where the effective monthly
     (risk-free) interest rate can be considered of 2.87% (≈40.5% annualy).

  ■S-mobile offers a “free” handset with each 12-month data & call contract, for £35/month.

  ■Without the contract, the company would sell the handset for £330, and would provide
    the call & data services separately for £15/month in a 12-month contract.

 ■With regards to costs, the handset costs £200, while the services, £5/month.

 ■What changes now?

 ■We need to account for a financing component when assessing the transaction!





                                                                                                                                                    44 / 118

--- Session 2, page 46 (combined page 151) ---
Example 9 - alternative!





  ■Let’s use the 5 steps we saw to determine revenue recognition...

 ■Step 1: Identify the contract with customers. (straight-forward)

 ■Step 2: Identify the separate performance obligations in the contract.
           1  Delivery of the handset.

           2  Delivery of the data & call services (monthly).





                                                                                                                                                    45 / 118

--- Session 2, page 47 (combined page 152) ---
Example 9 - alternative!





  ■Let’s use the 5 steps we saw to determine revenue recognition...

 ■Step 3: Determine the transaction price.
         • Nominal consideration: £420 (£35 × 12).

         • Effective monthly rate: 2.87%.

         • The transaction price will be determined by the present value of the 12 payments:
      PV= P12t=1 (1+0.0287)t35    ≈£351.11.
         • The difference between the nominal consideration and the PV is the financing component,
         which is recognized separately as interest income.

         • Interest income = £68.89.





                                                                                                                                                    46 / 118

--- Session 2, page 48 (combined page 153) ---
Example 9 - alternative!





  ■Let’s use the 5 steps we saw to determine revenue recognition...

 ■Step 4: Allocate the transaction price to the separate performance obligations.
         • The transaction price for the handset is £227.19 (£330/£510 × £351.11).

         • The transaction price for the services is £123.92 (£351.11 - £227.19), which equals
         £10.33/month.

 ■Step 5: Recognize revenue when each performance obligation is satisfied.
         • Revenue of £227.19 is recognized immediately, as the handset is delivered to the customer.

         • Revenue of £10.33 is recognized at the end of each month.





                                                                                                                                                    47 / 118

--- Session 2, page 49 (combined page 154) ---
Example 9 - alternative!





 ■The income statement at the end of the year (after the 12 months)...
         • Revenue ....................... £351.11 (£227.19 + £10.33 × 12)
         • Cost of sales ................. £260 (£200 + £5 × 12)
         • Gross profit ................... £91.11
         • Finance income ............ £68.89

 ■Note that the same final profit of £160 was recognized...

 ■However, it is now split between operating and finance income.
