--- Session 2, page 50 (combined page 155) ---
Revenue recognition issues





 ■Some issues might arise when recognizing revenue...

  ■Let’s go through examples of different problems!





                                                                                                                                                    49 / 118

--- Session 2, page 51 (combined page 156) ---
Revenue Recognition Issues - Sales Returns & Allowances



 ■Companies must recognize revenues net of returns and allowances.
         • This means that companies must estimate expected returns and allowances.

         • Set up a refund liability for the cash/credit it expects to refund.

         • Recognize an asset for the right to recover products — they should be measured at their
          previous carrying amount less expected recovery costs (e.g. shipment) and impairment.

 ■Amazon’s 2024 10-K report states:





                                                                                                                                                    50 / 118

--- Session 2, page 52 (combined page 157) ---
Revenue Recognition Issues - Sales Returns & Allowances




  ■Let’s see what this looks like in practice...

 ■Amazon sells 1,000 units of a product for $ 50 each. Each unit cost Amazon $ 30.
         • The customers pay on delivery.

         • From previous experience, Amazon expects 5% of the units to be returned.

         • The returned units can be resold, and the cost of recovering them is negligible.

 ■How much revenue should Amazon recognize?

 ■Not $ 50,000! Revenue is recognized net of the units Amazon expects to get back...

                  Revenue = $50, 000 −5% × $50, 000 = $47, 500





                                                                                                                                                    51 / 118

--- Session 2, page 53 (combined page 158) ---
Revenue Recognition Issues - Sales Returns & Allowances




 ■In the income statement, we recognize only the sales Amazon expects to keep...
         • Revenue ........................... $ 47,500   ($ 50,000 −5%)
         • Cost of sales .................... $ 28,500   ($ 30,000 −5%)

         • Gross profit ...................... $ 19,000

 ■In the balance sheet, the expected return shows up on both sides...
         • Cash ................................. +$ 50,000
         • Inventories ....................... −$ 30,000

         • Right to recover products .. +$ 1,500   (the goods we expect back, at cost)

         • Refund liability ................. +$ 2,500   (the cash we expect to repay)

 ■Assets rise by $ 21,500 and liabilities by $ 2,500 — the difference, $ 19,000, is exactly
    the gross profit.





                                                                                                                                                    52 / 118

--- Session 2, page 54 (combined page 159) ---
Revenue Recognition Issues - Non-refundable Upfront Fees



 ■Companies sometimes receive payments (upfront fees) from customers before they
     deliver a product or perform a service.

  ■Upfront payments generally relate to the initiation, activation, or setup of a good or
     service to be provided or performed in the future.

 ■When should revenue be recognized?

 ■In most situations, these payments are for future delivery of products and services and
    should therefore not be recorded as revenue at the time of payment.
         • Rather, they should be recorded over time, as products and services are delivered.

  ■Planet Fitness is an example: “Enrollment fees are charged to new members at the
    commencement of their membership. The Company recognizes enrollment fees ratably
    over the estimated duration of the membership life, which is generally two years.”





                                                                                                                                                    53 / 118

--- Session 2, page 55 (combined page 160) ---
Revenue Recognition Issues - Repurchase Agreements




 ■Companies sometimes enter into repurchase agreements:
         • They transfer an asset to a customer with an unconditional (forward) obligation or
          unconditional right (call option) to repurchase the asset at a later date.

 ■Did the company sell the asset?

 ■No! The customer does not obtain control.

 ■The way to account for these agreements depends on the relation between repurchase
     price and selling price...
           1   If repurchase price ≥original selling price, then the operation should be treated as
        borrowing — this is finance cost and interest expense should be recognized over time.

           2   If repurchase price < original selling price, then the operation should be treated as a
          lease.





                                                                                                                                                    54 / 118

--- Session 2, page 56 (combined page 161) ---
Revenue Recognition Issues - Repurchase Agreements




 ■This is not a rare arrangement: Stellantis (Fiat, Peugeot, Citroën, Jeep, Opel...)
     routinely sells vehicles to rental companies committing to buy them back later.

 ■In its 2025 annual report, Stellantis states:
         • “Vehicle sales to customers can include a repurchase obligation, whereby the Company is
          required to repurchase the vehicles at a given point in time. The Company accounts for such
          sales as an operating lease.”

 ■So what happens when the cars are delivered?

 ■No revenue at all!
         • The cars never leave Stellantis’ balance sheet: they sit in a current asset called “Assets sold
          with a buy-back commitment” (e3,616mi at the end of 2025).





                                                                                                                                                    55 / 118

--- Session 2, page 57 (combined page 162) ---
Revenue Recognition Issues - Principal-Agent Relationship



 ■In a principal-agent relationship...
         • The principal’s performance obligation is to provide goods or perform services for a
         customer.
         • The agent’s performance obligation is to arrange for the principal to provide these goods and
          services to the customer.
         • Amounts collected on behalf of the principal are not revenue to the agent!
              Revenue for the agent is the amount of the commission it receives for the sale (e.g. percentage of
                the agent’s revenue).

 ■Examples: Booking.com, Airbnb, Amazon (for goods that third-party sellers list on the
     marketplace)...

 ■What about Kayak? Is it an agent for airline companies?

 ■No! It only connects customers and airlines, but does not obtain revenue from the
     tickets sold.




                                                                                                                                                    56 / 118

--- Session 2, page 58 (combined page 163) ---
Revenue Recognition Issues - Principal-Agent Relationship





 ■A common example of principal-agent relationship is that of consignments...

  ■Manufacturers (or wholesalers) deliver goods but retain title to these goods until they
    are sold.

 ■The dealer does not record the merchandise as an asset on its books.

 ■The manufacturer or wholesaler carries the merchandise as inventory throughout the
    consignment, separately classified as “Inventory (consignments)”.

 ■Examples: Target, Nordstrom...





                                                                                                                                                    57 / 118

--- Session 2, page 59 (combined page 164) ---
Revenue Recognition Issues - Bill-and-Hold Arrangements




 ■A bill-and-hold arrangement is a contract under which an entity bills a customer for a
    product but the entity retains physical possession of the product until it is transferred to
    the customer at a point in the future.

 ■When should revenue from the sale be recognized?

 ■Only when control transfers to the customer. In practice the following conditions
    must be met:
           1  The reason for the bill-and-hold arrangement must be substantive.

           2  The product must be identified separately as belonging to the customer.

           3  The product currently must be ready for physical transfer to the customer.

           4  The seller cannot have the ability to use the product or to direct it to another customer.





                                                                                                                                                    58 / 118

--- Session 2, page 60 (combined page 165) ---
Revenue Recognition Issues - Warranties (we will revisit session 4)



 ■There are two types of warranties that can be provided:


   1  Assurance-type warranty: guarantee that the product meets agreed-upon
     specifications in the contract at the time the product is sold.
         • Quality guarantee that the product/service is free of defects at the point of sale.

         • The warranty and product/service form a single performance obligation, and so the
         warranty cost should be expensed in the period the goods are provided or the services are
         performed.

         • The company should also record a warranty liability with its estimated cost.

   2  Service-type warranty: guarantee providing additional service beyond the
    assurance-type warranty.
         • Represent a separate performance obligation.

         • The company recognizes revenue in the period that this warranty is in effect.




                                                                                                                                                    59 / 118

--- Session 2, page 61 (combined page 166) ---
Recognizing revenue over time



 ■When the obligation to provide goods or services spans more than one reporting period
      (i.e., 1 year), the total revenue generated is allocated over the relevant reporting periods.


   1 The customer consumes the benefits as the business performs its obligations.
         • Usually, long-term service contracts.

         • An accounting firm that undertakes employee payroll services for another business.

   2 The business creates, or develops, an asset of the customer.
         • Usually, construction contracts.

         • A builder undertakes the refurbishment of a shop owned by another business.

   3 The business creates an asset with no alternative use and the customer has agreed to
    pay for work carried out.
         • Usually, special orders.

         • An engineer produces a custom-made equipment for another business.



                                                                                                                                                    60 / 118

--- Session 2, page 62 (combined page 167) ---
Recognizing revenue over time




 ■In these cases, allocating revenue between the relevant reporting period requires some
    method of measuring progress.

 ■The process of recognizing revenue over time is known as the
    percentage-of-completion method.

  ■If conditions 1-3 in the previous slide are met but progress cannot be reliably measured,
   we apply the cost-recovery (zero-profit) method.

  ■If conditions 1-3 in the previous slide are not met, revenue is recognized at a point in
    time — not over time.
         • The business must only recognize revenue when control of each performance obligation is
          transferred to the customer.





                                                                                                                                                    61 / 118

--- Session 2, page 63 (combined page 168) ---
Percentage-of-completion method




 ■Under this method, a company recognizes revenues, costs, and gross profits as it
    makes progress towards the completion of a long-term contract.

 ■The key obstacle, however, is to effectively measure this progress toward completion.
    The most usual methods are:


   1 The cost-to-cost method: progress is measured by the percentage of the total
    estimated cost that has been incurred.
         • This can be literal (monetary) cost incurred, or labor hours worked, for example.

   2 The units-of-delivery method: progress is measured by the percentage of the total
    output expected to be delivered that have been produced.
         • Tons produced, floors of a building completed, miles of a highway completed...





                                                                                                                                                    62 / 118

--- Session 2, page 64 (combined page 169) ---
Example 10 - Boeing - 10-Q March 2025





 ■What basis for progress is used here? →cost-to-cost.

  ■“Parker is a Fortune 250 global leading manufacturer of motion and control technologies.
    For more than 100 years the company has engineered the success of its customers in a
    wide range of industrial and aerospace markets.”

 ■Some examples of goods offered by Parker:
         • Constant-pressure hose used in heavy equipment.

         • Standard and custom elastomer seals for fluid power and aerospace.

         • Valve that regulates refrigerant flow in AC and refrigeration systems.

         • High-efficiency compressed air filters for air/gas purification.

--- Session 2, page 66 (combined page 171) ---

 ■When should the “units-of-delivery” basis be used here?
 ■When the contract promises the transfer of homogeneous units of goods or services
    over time.
         • In this case, the “units-of-delivery” method can better reflect progress over time.

--- Session 2, page 67 (combined page 172) ---
Example 12





 ■London Engineering Ltd (LEL) has a contract with London NHS Trust to
     design-and-build a surgical suite on the customer’s site, in Whitechapel.

 ■The contract term is 24 months and evaluated at a price of £10mi.

 ■LEL estimates the cost at £7.5mi.

  ■According to the contract, the project is billed quarterly across the 8 quarters of the
     contract and based on invoices of £1.25mi, paid when billed.

 ■How to recognize revenue for this contract?





                                                                                                                                                    66 / 118

--- Session 2, page 68 (combined page 173) ---
Example 12




 ■LEL should use the “percentage-of-completion” method based on a “cost-to-cost”
     basis...
         • At the end of each year, LEL should recognize the fraction of revenue corresponding to the
           fraction of total costs incurred.

         • The difference between the recognized revenue and the amount paid by the customer at a
          point in time is charged as a “contract asset” (if positive) or as a “contract liability” (if
          negative).

 ■Suppose that, at the end of year 1, LEL incurred costs of £5mi for the contract.

 ■How much revenue should be recognized at year 1? £6.67mi (£5/£7.5 × £10).

 ■Is a “contract asset” or a “contract liability” created? A “contract asset” since the
    customer paid only £5mi by the end of year 1.





                                                                                                                                                    67 / 118

--- Session 2, page 69 (combined page 174) ---
Cost-recovery (zero-profit) method




 ■This method is applied when two conditions are met:


   1 A reliable measure of progress is not available.

   2  Costs are expected to be recoverable.
         • Contractually billable: the business has an enforceable right to payment for costs incurred to
          date.
         • Collectible: it is probable that the business will collect the amount from the customer.
                For example: credit risk doesn’t make the recovery doubtful.
         • No overriding loss expectation: expected consideration is at least equal to the expected
          costs of fulfilling what remains.
                Otherwise, the business must recognize a loss via an onerous contract provision.





                                                                                                                                                    68 / 118

--- Session 2, page 70 (combined page 175) ---
Example 13 - Petrofac - Annual Report 2023

 ■London Engineering Ltd (LEL) has a contract with City of London Corporation to build a
   new type of façade retrofit on the customer’s building.

 ■The contract price is fixed at £9mi.

 ■Because this is a new type of façade, LEL does not have a good estimate of its initial
     cost — although it expects to recover the full amount costs under the contract’s price.

 ■The contract term is 3 years, and the customer has agreed to pay £3mi at each
    year-end.

  ■Total cost will be reliably estimated by the end of year 2.

--- Session 2, page 72 (combined page 177) ---
Example 14





 ■At year 1, LEL incurred costs of £2mi.

 ■How much revenue should be recognized?

 ■LEL should recognize £2 mi — because no good estimate of progress can be
     established, revenue is recognized at the amount of costs actually incurred.

 ■The difference between the amount paid by the customer, of £3mi, and the recognized
    revenue, of £2mi, gives rise to a “contract liability” of £1mi.





                                                                                                                                                    71 / 118

--- Session 2, page 73 (combined page 178) ---
Example 14




  ■Let’s see what the statements look like at the end of year 1 (assuming this contract is
     LEL’s only activity)...

 ■Income statement:
         • Revenue .......................... £2mi

         • Cost of sales ................... £2mi

         • Profit ............................... £0

 ■Balance sheet:
         • Cash ................................. £1mi   (£3mi received −£2mi of costs paid)
         • Contract liability .............. £1mi   (£3mi billed −£2mi of revenue)

         • Equity ............................... £0





                                                                                                                                                    72 / 118

--- Session 2, page 74 (combined page 179) ---
Example 14




 ■At year 2, LEL incurred costs of £2.5mi. The company also estimates total costs at
    £7.5mi by the end of year 3.

 ■How much revenue should be recognized?

 ■LEL should recognize £3.4 mi — now we can use a measure of progress...
         • 60% ((£2mi + £2.5mi)/£7.5mi) have been incurred, so a total cumulative revenue of £5.4mi is
          recognized.
         • In year 1, only £2mi had been recognized — so the remaining £3.4mi should be currently
          recognized.

 ■The amount paid by the customer, of £6mi still exceeds the cumulative amount of
    revenue recognized, of £5.4, so LEL has to record a “contract liability” of £0.6mi.





                                                                                                                                                    73 / 118

--- Session 2, page 75 (combined page 180) ---
Example 14




 ■And at the end of year 2...

 ■Income statement:
         • Revenue .......................... £3.4mi

         • Cost of sales ................... £2.5mi

         • Profit ............................... £0.9mi

 ■Balance sheet:
         • Cash ................................. £1.5mi   (£6mi received −£4.5mi of costs paid)
         • Contract liability .............. £0.6mi   (£6mi billed −£5.4mi of revenue)

         • Equity ............................... £0.9mi

 ■Now that we can measure progress, LEL finally recognizes a profit.





                                                                                                                                                    74 / 118

--- Session 2, page 76 (combined page 181) ---
Example 14





 ■At year 3, LEL incurred costs of £2.7mi. This yields a total cost of £7.2mi, which is
    below its estimated cost in year 2, of £7.5mi.

 ■How much revenue should be recognized?

 ■LEL should recognize £3.6 mi, which is the remaining amount for the contract.

 ■What about the fact that incurred costs were lower than previously estimated?

 ■The “extra” amount of profit generated by the lower-than-expected costs increases the
      profit recorded for year 3 — no other adjustment is necessary.





                                                                                                                                                    75 / 118

--- Session 2, page 77 (combined page 182) ---
Example 14




 ■And, finally, at the end of year 3...

 ■Income statement:
         • Revenue .......................... £3.6mi

         • Cost of sales ................... £2.7mi

         • Profit ............................... £0.9mi

 ■Balance sheet:
         • Cash ................................. £1.8mi   (£9mi received −£7.2mi of costs paid)

         • Contract liability .............. £0   (the contract is complete)

         • Equity ............................... £1.8mi

 ■Over the three years, LEL recognized a total profit of £1.8mi — exactly the contract
     price of £9mi less the £7.2mi it ended up spending.





                                                                                                                                                    76 / 118

--- Session 2, page 78 (combined page 183) ---
Over time or at a point in time?




 ■Many contracts in the “real world” are much more complicated than the ones we saw...

 ■In particular, many contracts bundle licenses, services, goods, and options at the same
     time.

 ■Revenue recognition in these cases requires:
         • Identifying the separate performance obligations.

         • Identifying which of those should be recognized over time or at a point in time.

 ■Some examples of these more complex contracts are franchises, telecom bundles of
    devices and services, automotive bundles of vehicles with included maintenance plan...

  ■Let’s discuss the case of franchises.





                                                                                                                                                    77 / 118

--- Session 2, page 79 (combined page 184) ---
Example 15 - McDonald’s





  ■McDonald’s conventional franchises operate in the following way:
         • McDonald’s usually owns or leases the land and building where the franchise is located.

         • The franchisee then pays rent for the location, a sales-based royalty for the sale of products,
        and an initial fee.

         • The contract usually lasts 20 years.

 ■What are the performance obligations in this contract?
           1  Rent (lease income).

           2  Royalty.

           3  Non-refundable initial fee.





                                                                                                                                                    78 / 118

--- Session 2, page 80 (combined page 185) ---
Example 15 - McDonald’s



  ■McDonald’s conventional franchises operate in the following way:
         • McDonald’s owns or leases the land and building where the franchise is located.

         • The franchisee then pays rent for the location (operating lease), a sales-based royalty for the
          sale of products, and an initial fee.

 ■How should revenue for each of these performance obligations be recognized?
           1  Rent (lease income): recognized under IFRS 16 not IFRS 15 (the one we reviewed today) —
       we will discuss later in the ------    .
              Note that a “lease contract” is not a “contract for the transfer of a good or service”.
                 Rather, it is a contract that conveys the right to control the use of an identified asset for a period
                  of time in exchange for consideration.
             By identified it is meant: only one specific asset can fulfill the contract (e.g., serial number,
               apartment at a particular floor and number ...).

           2  Royalty: at a point in time, as sales occur.

           3  Non-refundable initial fee: over time! “(...) the initial franchise services are not distinct from
          the continuing rights or services offered during the term of the franchise agreement”.



                                                                                                                                                    79 / 118

--- Session 2, page 81 (combined page 186) ---
What about the recognition of expenses?





 ■The matching convention guides the recognition of expenses: expenses should be
    matched to the revenue that they helped generate.

 ■The general principle is that expenses associated with a particular item of revenue
    should be taken into account in the same reporting period as that in which the item of
    revenue is included.





                                                                                                                                                    80 / 118

--- Session 2, page 82 (combined page 187) ---
Trade Receivables and Uncertainty

--- Session 2, page 83 (combined page 188) ---
What is a trade receivable...





 ■A trade receivable is an entity’s right to consideration that is unconditional — only
     the passage of time is required before the payment is due.
         •  It typically arises from a revenue contract with a customer, once the entity has performed.

         •  If the right is still conditional on something other than time — usually further performance —
         then it is a contract asset, not a receivable.

 ■The key properties are that...
         • Trade receivables are less liquid than cash & cash equivalents.
         • Trade receivables carry credit risk.





                                                                                                                                                    81 / 118

--- Session 2, page 84 (combined page 189) ---
A bit of history




  ■Before 2018, under the old standard — IAS 39 — impairment followed an incurred-loss
    model.

 ■In this model, losses were only booked after objective evidence that a loss event had
    occurred (for example, delinquency of more than 90 days).

 ■In practice, assets remained very close to the gross carrying amount with only minimal
    allowance for credit risk.

  ■This was a big issue during the 2008 financial crisis since many financial assets
     (including trade receivables in distressed sectors) looked healthy on paper because the
     incurred-loss model deferred recognition of expected losses.





                                                                                                                                                    82 / 118

--- Session 2, page 85 (combined page 190) ---
A bit of history





 ■Since 2018, however, the new IFRS standard (IFRS 9) requires impairment follows an
    expected credit loss (ECL) adjustment.

 ■In the case of trade receivables, this means that every trade receivable must be
    recognized alongside an allowance for any potential losses due to credit risk (lifetime
    expected credit loss).

 ■The rationale is that because credit risk exists, the balance sheet should only recognize
     the net realizable value of the trade receivable.





                                                                                                                                                    83 / 118

--- Session 2, page 86 (combined page 191) ---
The mechanics



 ■A business sells a good for £100, with payment due in 90 days. The customer is
    expected to default with a probability of 10%.

 ■The net realizable value is thus £90, recorded as follows...

   1 INCOME STATEMENT
         • Revenue ............................. £100
         • Impairment loss ................. (£10)
         • Profit ................................ £90
   2 BALANCE SHEET — assets
         • Trade receivables (gross) ... £100
         • Less: loss allowance ......... (£10)
         • Trade receivables (net) ... £90
   3 BALANCE SHEET — equity
         • Retained earnings ............. £90



                                                                                                                                                    84 / 118

--- Session 2, page 87 (combined page 192) ---
The mechanics





 ■In 90 days, if the customer does pay for the good, the business reverses the unused
    allowance in the income statement through an “Impairment gain” of £10.

 ■In 90 days, if the customer does not pay for the good, the business records an
    “Impairment expense” of £90 in the income statement.





                                                                                                                                                    85 / 118

--- Session 2, page 88 (combined page 193) ---
Why this matters




 ■In practice, it is hard to estimate credit risk.

 ■There are methods designed to do so, with IFRS 9 specifically prescribing a
     provision-matrix framework.1
         • In a nutshell: under this method, the business applies different loss rates depending on the
        number of days that a trade receivable is past due (i.e. the “age” of the trade receivable).
         • Depending on the diversity of its customer base, the business needs to use appropriate
          groupings if its historical credit loss experience shows significantly different loss patterns for
            different customer segments.

  ■Although this method reduces discretion, human judgment still plays a role in this
     process.



     1If you are interested in learning more about this, check out this introductory note:
 https://www.iasplus.com/content/a3cc19fb-0b66-41bf-999c-96070c9873e8

                                                                                                                                                    86 / 118

--- Session 2, page 89 (combined page 194) ---
Why this matters



 ■In particular, managing allowances for credit risk is a way in which firms can practice
    earnings management.

 ■For instance, discretion can be exercised in...
         • Determining the appropriate groupings of customers (e.g. by geographic area).

         • Determining the period over which to consider historical loss rates (e.g. should the period
          include the financial crisis?).

         • Determining how to adjust historical loss rates to forward looking information (e.g. how will
          the FED’s policy affect delinquency?).

  ■Importantly, according to IFRS 9, the loss allowance must be remeasured at each
    reporting date to reflect current credit risk, updated historical loss experience, and
     forward-looking information.
         • This is really important to maintain the accuracy of estimates.
         • But it also gives space to discretion and analysts should bear this in mind.



                                                                                                                                                    87 / 118

--- Session 2, page 90 (combined page 195) ---
Case -----   : ?Accounting for the iPhone
         Upgrade Program?

--- Session 2, page 91 (combined page 196) ---
Reference





 ■These slides are based on the case “Accounting for the iPhone Upgrade Program (A)”
     distributed by Harvard Business Publishing, and its associated teaching notes.

 ■  --------   are required to read the case before -----  .





                                                                                                                                                    88 / 118

--- Session 2, page 92 (combined page 197) ---
Context





 ■What is the context of this case ----- ?
         • The case is set in September 2015, when Apple launched its new iPhone 6s and iPhone 6s
         Plus models and announced the “iPhone Upgrade Program”.

         • Even after the introduction of the new program and models, Apple’s revenue on the fiscal
          quarter ending March 2016 declined 13% on a YoY basis.





                                                                                                                                                    89 / 118

--- Session 2, page 93 (combined page 198) ---
The “iPhone Upgrade Program”





 ■Of what consisted the “Apple Upgrade Program” as announced in September 2015?
         • The program was only available through Apple’s retail stores in the US.

         •  It offered a 24-month payment plan for the unlocked iPhone 6s (6s Plus) and the AppleCare+
          service for $ 32.41 ($ 44.92) a month. The loan was provided by Citzens Bank.

         • Customers could upgrade to a new device after making 12 payments.

         • Customers could switch to new service carriers.

         • Upon exercise of the trade-in right and purchase of a new iPhone, the customer enrolled in a
        new 2-year 0% loan with Citzens. Apple then covered the outstanding balance of the original
           loan.





                                                                                                                                                    90 / 118

--- Session 2, page 94 (combined page 199) ---
Thinking strategically...





 ■Why do you think Apple introduced the “iPhone Upgrade Program”?
         • The average iPhone upgrade time in the US rising from 18 months in 2010 to 26 months in
         2015. →The program was meant to incentivize upgrading.

         • The program allowed Apple to enter the market for used iPhones, which could increase its
         presence in developing countries — particularly, China and India.

         • Consumers were starting to shy away from 2-year contracts with major wireless service
          providers in order to have the freedom to change carriers. This involved paying the full cost
            for the iPhone upfront and opened a market opportunity for Apple’s upgrade program to
          create loyalty across the consumer base.





                                                                                                                                                    91 / 118

--- Session 2, page 95 (combined page 200) ---
Thinking strategically...





 ■What were the benefits of Apple’s “iPhone Upgrade Program” to customers?
         • Low monthly payments combined with a 24-month 0% APR installment loan with Citzens.

         • The option to upgrade and obtain the latest iPhone model.

         • Better service with AppleCare+.

         • Unlocked phone, allowing the customer to switch carriers.

         • From a “brand” perspective, Apple could market 0% financing while shifting the credit risk to
          Citzens.





                                                                                                                                                    92 / 118

--- Session 2, page 96 (combined page 201) ---
Thinking strategically...





 ■What are the obligations that Apple undertook with the “iPhone Upgrade Program”?
         • Guarantee to take back the used phone and pay the outstanding balance of the loan on
          behalf of the customer.

         • The risk of the loan was concentrated on Citzens and Apple had to compensate the bank for
         such risk — but such compensation is not explained in Apple’s financial statements.

         • The risk that the used returned phone was in poor conditions (a risk mitigated by
         AppleCare+).

         • Risk of obsolescence if used or refurbished phones lose value.

         • Additional costs associated with the trade in of used phones (admin, interest on loan,
          refurbishment costs).





                                                                                                                                                    93 / 118

--- Session 2, page 97 (combined page 202) ---
Accounting lenses... iPhone - without upgrade program





  ■Let’s start with the basics...

 ■Suppose that you walk into an Apple store and buy an iPhone — without the upgrade
    program. How would Apple account for it?
         • iPhone 6s (includes software upgrades) ........................ $ 649.00
         • AppleCare+ (2 years coverage) ........................................ $ 129.00
         • Software upgrade (estimated) ............................................. $ 10.00

  ■Let’s use the 5 steps we saw today.





                                                                                                                                                    94 / 118

--- Session 2, page 98 (combined page 203) ---
Accounting lenses... iPhone - without upgrade program





   1  Identify the contract with customer. →straight-forward

   2  Identify the separate performance obligations. →iPhone, AppleCare+, software
    upgrades.

   3  Determine the transaction price. →$ 778.00

   4  Allocate the transaction price to the separate performance obligations. →iPhone = $
    639.00; AppleCare+ = $ 129.00; Software upgrade = $ 10.00.

   5  Recognize revenue when each performance obligation is satisfied.
         • iPhone: immediately.
         • AppleCare+: on a straight-line basis over the 2-year period.
         • Software upgrade: on a straight-line basis over the estimated period provision (?).





                                                                                                                                                    95 / 118

--- Session 2, page 99 (combined page 204) ---
Accounting lenses... iPhone - with upgrade program





 ■Now suppose that you decide to buy the iPhone with the upgrade program. How does
    Apple account for it?

  ■Monthly payments of $ 32.41 for 24 months at 0% APR, with a loan from Citizens Bank.
         • Upgrade after 12 payments: trade in your iPhone and enroll in a new 24-month 0% loan —
         Apple then covers the outstanding balance of your original loan.

         • AppleCare+ is included in the monthly payment.





                                                                                                                                                    96 / 118

--- Session 2, page 100 (combined page 205) ---
Accounting lenses... iPhone - with upgrade program




  ■AppleCare+ and software updates are still accounted for in the same way as before.

 ■What about the upgrade program specifically? Is it a lease or a sale?

  ■It is a sale (with right of return)! Why?
         • Control and ownership pass to the customer at delivery.
                  In a lease, the customer only has a right of use.

         • Apple has no contractually defined forward obligation or call option to repurchase the iPhone.
         • The trade-in right is conditional on buying the next new iPhone.
             The “return” is tied to a future sale!

         • The monthly payments are loan repayments paid to Citizens and not “rent” paid to Apple.

  ■Let’s use 5 steps we saw before...





                                                                                                                                                    97 / 118

--- Session 2, page 101 (combined page 206) ---
Accounting lenses... iPhone - with upgrade program





   1  Identify the contract with customer. →previous slides

   2  Identify the separate performance obligations. →iPhone, AppleCare+, software
    upgrades.

   3  Determine the transaction price. →$ 777.84 (24 × $ 32.41)

   4  Allocate the transaction price to the separate performance obligations. →iPhone = $
    638.84; AppleCare+ = $ 129.00; Software upgrade = $ 10.00.

   5  Recognize revenue when each performance obligation is satisfied.
         • iPhone: immediately, but net of expected returns.
         • AppleCare+: on a straight-line basis over the 2-year period.
         • Software upgrade: on a straight-line basis over the estimated period provision (?).





                                                                                                                                                    98 / 118

--- Session 2, page 102 (combined page 207) ---
Accounting lenses... iPhone - with upgrade program



 ■We just saw that Apple recognizes the revenue for the iPhone immediately, but net of
    expected returns...
 ■What does this mean?
 ■In terms of the balance sheet:
         • Apple expects to cover the outstanding balance of your loan when you trade in your
           original iPhone. →this requires Apple to create a “Refund Liability”

         • When (and if) you upgrade your phone, Apple will add your original handset to its inventory.
        →this requires Apple to create a “Right to Recover” determined by the former carrying
        amount of the used handset, less expected refurbishment costs and any expected decline
            in its value.
 ■In terms of the income statement:
         • Recognize revenue net of the refund liability.

         • Recognize cost of sales net of the right to recover asset.





                                                                                                                                                    99 / 118

--- Session 2, page 103 (combined page 208) ---
Accounting lenses... Challenge





 ■What is the key challenge involved in Apple accounting practices?

  ■It is highly dependent on estimates...
         • Number of customers exercising the option.

         • Timing of customers exercising the option.

         • Price of used/ refurbished phones.

         • Cost of phone repair (admin, material, etc...).





                                                                                                                                                   100 / 118

--- Session 2, page 104 (combined page 209) ---
Accounting lenses... Challenge





 ■Are these estimates reliable? What are the issues involved?

  ■Apple’s and competitors’ innovation cycles might affect...
         • The timing/number of customers exercising options.

         • The market value of used/refurbished phones.

 ■Consumer behavior and consequent pricing might differ a lot across countries.
         • How much profit can be obtained from selling refurbished US phones in India?

 ■The more successful the Upgrade Program is, the larger the supply of used phones,
    which might depress prices.





                                                                                                                                                   101 / 118

--- Session 2, page 105 (combined page 210) ---
Accounting lenses... Intuition





 ■Which scenario is better for Apple?
           1  The customer exercises the option at the beginning of month 7 (18 installments left).

           2  The customer exercises the option at the beginning of month 19 (6 installments left).

  ■Opposite forces...
           1  Positive: Apple saves more on the APR subsidy the more the consumer waits.
           2  Negative: but...
                This delays the next sale, which might worsen Apple’s position in the longer run.
               Apple faces greater risk on the returned phone (it is older and, possibly, has a lower associated
                 trade in value).





                                                                                                                                                   102 / 118

--- Session 2, page 106 (combined page 211) ---
Accounting lenses... Why does Apple save on the APR subsidy the more
the consumer waits?



 ■Suppose the iPhone is financed over 24 months: $ 720 in total, or $ 30 per month, at
   0% APR.

 ■But Citizens does not lend for free... Apple prepays the bank a subsidy when the loan
      is granted. Say 5% of the amount financed:

                         Subsidy = 5% × $720 = $36 per loan

 ■Here is the key: that $ 36 buys 24 months of financing, and it is paid upfront —
    whether or not the loan runs its full term.

 ■So what happens when the customer trades in early?
         • The old loan is settled and a new 24-month loan begins.

         • Apple pays a new $ 36 subsidy — and whatever was left of the old one is simply lost.





                                                                                                                                                   103 / 118

--- Session 2, page 107 (combined page 212) ---
Accounting lenses... Why does Apple save on the APR subsidy the more
the consumer waits?





  ■Let’s go back to our two scenarios... of the $ 36 prepaid, how much did Apple actually
    use?
         • Scenario 1 (exercises at the beginning of month 7): 6 of 24 months →$ 9 used, $ 27
         wasted.
         • Scenario 2 (exercises at the beginning of month 19): 18 of 24 months →$ 27 used, $ 9
         wasted.

 ■Apple believed this Upgrade would be beneficial for them.

 ■What would be your main concerns?

 ■As an analyst at the time, what information would you be looking for in Apple’s 10-K?

           2  Information on Apple’s assumptions regarding customers’ behavior.

 ■What would be the red flags you are looking for?
           1  Impairments in inventories (of old phones).

           2  Discretion in revenue recognition. Are Apple’s assumptions reasonable?

           3  Expenses related to AppleCare+ might be much higher now under the Upgrade Program
         (more customers using these services).

 ■In the short run, Apple’s Upgrade Program was not the success Apple had predicted.

         documenting a decrease of 9% in total revenue and of 13% in iPhone revenue (YoY). Apple’s
          stock price plummeted.

           2 On September 12 2016, an Apple customer sued Apple claiming that the program was
          misleading.

 ■There were good news as well:
           1  Samsung encountered quality problems that forced the company to recall the Galaxy Note 7,
          with customers switching to iPhones (survey evidence).
           2  Apple started to sell refurbished iPhones 6s Plus in the US.

--- Session 3, page 4 (combined page 233) ---
Motivation





 ■Today we will talk about cost capitalization, and subsequent depreciation and
     revaluation/impairment of assets...

 ■But why do we care about these topics?

 ■We care because they influence the way we measure investments, performance and
      risk...





                                                                                                                                                     3 / 121

--- Session 3, page 5 (combined page 234) ---
Motivation





 ■For example: two otherwise identical companies might display different
           1  performance metrics (e.g. earnings),

           2  investment efficiency (e.g. asset turnover),

           3  and debt-to-equity ratio,
     just because of different accounting choices (depreciation, valuation...) for valuing their
    PP&E.

 ■When comparing companies, we need to understand if the observed differences in
    performance are “material” in that they reflect actual differences in economic
    performance, or if they are purely a reflection of accounting choices.





                                                                                                                                                     4 / 121

--- Session 3, page 6 (combined page 235) ---
Motivation





 ■We also care about this because the valuation of PP&E and their depreciation is highly
    dependent on estimates.

 ■This is another channel through which companies might use discretion in “generating”
    the desired results.

 ■Around the 2000’s there were many cases of fraud involving the valuation of PP&E.

--- Session 3, page 10 (combined page 239) ---
Context





 ■Today we will focus on tangible assets (specifically Property, Plant, and Equipment,
   PP&E) — we will discuss intangible assets next week.

 ■We will discuss some key accounting issues regarding PP&E...
           1  Should an expenditure be capitalized (i.e. be treated as an asset) or expensed?

           2 How to measure the “cost” of an asset?

           3 How do we record the “use” of an asset over time? (depreciation & depletion)

           4 How do we record the sale of an asset? (disposal of fixed assets)

           5 How do we record changes in the value of an asset? (impairments & revaluations)





                                                                                                                                                     9 / 121

--- Session 3, page 11 (combined page 240) ---
Asset or Expense?

--- Session 3, page 12 (combined page 241) ---
What is PP&E?





 ■PP&E are tangible assets that are...
         • held for use in production or supply of goods and services, for rentals to others (e.g. rental
          cars or equipment — but land and buildings held to earn rentals are investment property), or
            for administrative purposes;

         • expected to be used during more than one reporting period.

  ■This type of asset...
         •  is acquired for use in operations,

         •  is long-term in nature and usually depreciated,

         • possesses physical substance.





                                                                                                                                                    10 / 121

--- Session 3, page 13 (combined page 242) ---
PP&E, inventory or investment property?





  ■Consider the case of a portfolio of buildings. . .

  ■If they are held for sale in the ordinary ------   of business (imagine a housebuilder such
    as Persimmon), this portfolio is considered inventory.

  ■If they are held to earn rentals or capital appreciation (imagine a landlord such as
     British Land), this portfolio is considered investment property.

  ■If they are used in operations (imagine a supermarket chain’s own stores, such as
     Tesco’s), this portfolio is considered PP&E.

     malls, hotels in Hong Kong and mainland China...

  ■Its main business is being a landlord with more than 80% of its revenues in 2025
    coming from rent from offices and malls.

--- Session 3, page 20 (combined page 249) ---
When to recognize PP&E?





 ■An expenditure should be capitalized (i.e. shown as an asset) if it meets the asset
    recognition criteria...
           1   It is probable that future economic benefits associated with the item will flow to the entity.

           2  The cost of the item can be measured reliably.

 ■and if the it is directly attributable to bringing the asset to the condition/location for
    intended use.





                                                                                                                                                    18 / 121

--- Session 3, page 21 (combined page 250) ---
Why to recognize PP&E?





 ■In practice, capitalization (when possible) is beneficial because it allows companies to
    spread costs over time.


    1  Capitalization:
         • Balance Sheet: increases long term assets.

         • Income Statement: charge depreciation over the useful life of the asset.

    2  Expensing:
         • Balance Sheet: reduces retained earnings via lower period profit.

         • Income Statement: charge the expenditure when incurred.





                                                                                                                                                    19 / 121

--- Session 3, page 22 (combined page 251) ---
How to recognize PP&E? (Initial Recognition)




 ■An expenditure should be capitalized (i.e. shown as an asset) if it meets the asset
    recognition criteria...
           1   It is probable that future economic benefits associated with the item will flow to the entity.

           2  The cost of the item can be measured reliably.

 ■and if the it is directly attributable to bringing the asset to the condition/location for
    intended use.

 ■This is the criteria for the initial recognition of an expenditure as an asset. We will talk
    about subsequent expenditures later!

  ■Let’s categorize some examples...





                                                                                                                                                    20 / 121

--- Session 3, page 23 (combined page 252) ---
Capitalize or expense?



   1 A company buys a completed office building using a bank loan. No refurbishment
    needed. It finances the acquisition with a loan. →Q: should borrowing costs be
     capitalized or expensed? Expensed, because the asset is ready to use — these costs
    are not necessary to get it ready.

   2 A company starts building a factory that will take 18 months to complete. It finances
     construction using a loan. →Q: should borrowing costs be capitalized or expensed?
    Capitalized, because the asset needs substantial time to be readied for its intended
    use (in practice, there is no defined rule for “substantial”, but 12 months is a common
     rule of thumb).

   3 A company acquires land to build a new HQ. However, the land has an old warehouse.
    The company pays to demolish it and remove debris before proceeding with the
     construction of the HQ. →Q: should demolition & debris removal costs be capitalized?
       If so, as part of the land or the HQ? Capitalized as part of the cost of the land,
    because the removal of the warehouse was necessary to get the land ready for its
    intended use (build a new HQ).

                                                                                                                                                    21 / 121

--- Session 3, page 24 (combined page 253) ---
Capitalize or expense?




   1 A new production line requires trial runs before it’s capable of intended use. Test runs
     cost $500k, of which $100k is the cost of the saleable items produced, which are sold
     for $300k. →Q: should test costs be capitalized? Capitalize $400k of testing costs.
    The $300k of sales is revenue, and the $100k cost of those items is cost of sales.

   2  Before starting up that new line, staff attend a two-week vendor training ------   costing
    $120k. →Q: should this cost be capitalized? No! It should be expensed. The asset
      itself is ready independently of the training of the staff.

   3 A stamping press that has been in use for two years is moved from Plant A to Plant B.
    The company pays £300k to dismantle, transport, re-install, and run test batches to get
        it back to normal output. →Q: should these costs be capitalized? No! They should be
    expensed, because only costs directly attributable to bringing the asset to the
     condition/location necessary for its initial intended use can be capitalized.





                                                                                                                                                    22 / 121

--- Session 3, page 25 (combined page 254) ---
How to recognize PP&E?





 ■An asset should be recorded under its historical cost in the first reporting period when
        it is recognized.
         • For an acquired asset: the acquisition should be recorded at cost.
         • For a self-constructed asset: the asset should be reported at the accumulated cost during
          the construction phase. Note that the asset’s value increases every year when it’s under
          contruction.

 ■In subsequent periods, firms can choose to switch to a fair value approach. (more on
     this later...)

      fees...).

 ■The construction ends in April 2019.

 ■Where do we see this? In the notes about PP&E!

--- Session 3, page 28 (combined page 257) ---
Example - Tottenham Hotspur Stadium



 ■Assets under construction (AUC) in the PP&E note, £m (years ending 30 June):


                                     2015   2016   2017   2018      2019

       AUC at 30 June (cost)          102.8   172.4   386.3   792.4       31.2

         Added during the year             36.4    69.6   214.1   497.5      408.7
           Transferred to completed PP&E   (14.9)      –      –   (39.2)   (1,157.0)

 ■Costs are capitalised in AUC for years, and not depreciated.

 ■When the stadium opens (April 2019), £1.16bn is transferred out of AUC: £1,064.5m to
    land & buildings and £92.5m to plant & equipment — and depreciation starts.

 ■The smaller transfers in 2015 and 2018 are other assets completed earlier (in 2018,
    mainly the new training centre).

--- Session 3, page 30 (combined page 259) ---
What about self-constructed assets?





 ■In this case, if construction takes a substantial period of time, borrowing costs should be
     capitalized.
         • A useful rule of thumb is whether the construction lasts for more than 12 months.

  ■Overheads shared with normal production (e.g. power, heat, insurance, supervision
     salaries) are also included: variable overheads based on actual use, fixed overheads
    based on the share of normal capacity.

 ■By normal capacity we mean the output the facilities are expected to achieve under
    normal circumstances.





                                                                                                                                                    28 / 121

--- Session 3, page 31 (combined page 260) ---
What about self-constructed assets? (parenthesis)





 ■When should a company classify a self-constructed asset as PP&E or inventory or
    investment property?
         • When the self-constructed asset is intended for sale as part of the firm’s ordinary ------   of
          business, it should be recognized as “inventory”. (e.g. a real estate company develops a
           building for sale)

         • Specifically for buildings: when the self-constructed asset is intended to earn rentals or
           capital appreciation, it should be recognized as “investment property”. (e.g. a company
         develops a building for rent or to hold for capital appreciation)

         • When the self-constructed asset is not intended for sale, it should be capitalized as PP&E.
           (e.g. a company develops a building for its new HQ)





                                                                                                                                                    29 / 121

--- Session 3, page 32 (combined page 261) ---
What about self-constructed assets (for sale)? (parenthesis)




  ■Last class, we discussed recognition of revenue from long-term contracts.

 ■Many times, these long-term contracts consist of constructing a tangible asset that
    would usually “be held for sale under the ordinary ------   of business”.

 ■When should expenditures on this self-constructed asset be recognized as inventory or
    expensed?


    1   If there is no contract for the sale of the asset, then the firm should capitalized its costs
    and treat the asset as part of inventory.

    2   If there is a contract for the sale of the asset and the asset has no alternative use for the
      seller, then the firm should recognize revenue and costs over time, as discussed last
     session.





                                                                                                                                                    30 / 121

--- Session 3, page 33 (combined page 262) ---
Example - ordinary ------   of business? (parenthesis)



 ■In our first session, we discussed Arsenal’s financial statements.

 ■An important note is that Arsenal set up a property development business to carry out
     the property developments linked to the Emirates Stadium project — which included the
    redevelopment of its old stadium.

 ■How should this asset be classified?
           1  Emirates Stadium.
             As PP&E.
           2  Highbury Square Apartments, intended for sale.
             As inventory.
                This is because both (1) these properties were intended for sale, not rentals (or holding for capital
                  appreciation), and (2) building and selling hundreds of flats made property development one of
                 Arsenal’s ordinary activities (setting up the property development business is evidence of this).
                Note: if selling these apartments did not qualify as “ordinary ------   of business”, they would be
                 allocated to “assets held for sale” and a one-off gain/loss would be obtained from their sale (not
                 revenue!).


                                                                                                                                                    31 / 121

--- Session 3, page 34 (combined page 263) ---
Some issues in asset valuation (back to PP&E...)





 ■What if the company purchases PP&E subject to cash discounts for prompt payment?
         • Usually: treat the discount, whether taken or not, as a reduction in the cost of the asset.

 ■What if the company purchases PP&E with a deferred-payment contract (e.g. using a
    note payable or a bond issued directly to the seller)?
         • In most cases, companies must account for the purchase based on the present value (not
          the face value!) of the long-term contract.

         • This is because part of the face value represents interest (time value of money) and should
        be recognized as an interest expense over time — not capitalized into the asset.

         • In some cases, if the asset need a substantial period to get ready, these borrowing costs can
          also be capitalized.





                                                                                                                                                    32 / 121

--- Session 3, page 35 (combined page 264) ---
Some issues in asset valuation





 ■What if the company purchases a group of assets at a single lump-sum price (that is, as
    a bundle)?
         • Companies allocate the transaction price across the separate assets based on their relative
             fair values.

 ■What if the company finances the purchase of PP&E by issuing shares?
         • The company should use the market price of the shares issued as a measure of the cost of
          the PP&E acquired.





                                                                                                                                                    33 / 121

--- Session 3, page 36 (combined page 265) ---
Some issues in asset valuation


 ■What if the company purchases PP&E using another non-monetary asset (such as
    PP&E)?
         • For example, a company obtains a semi-truck in exchange for some used trucks.

 ■The key factor for accounting purposes is whether this exchange has commercial
    substance.
         • An exchange has commercial substance if future cash flows (for the reporting party) change
         as a result of the transaction.
         • By “change” we mean that the expected risk, timing, or amount of the reporting firm’s cash
          flows is affected.

  ■If the exchange has a commercial substance: record the asset received at the fair value
     of the asset given up, and recognize gain/loss immediately based on the difference
    between the fair value of asset given up and the book value of the asset given up.

  ■If the exchange lacks a commercial substance: record the asset received at the book
    value of the asset given up.


                                                                                                                                                    34 / 121

--- Session 3, page 37 (combined page 266) ---
Example 4





 ■Suppose Company A gives an old hydraulic press (machine 1) and cash to Company B,
    which, in exchange, gives a used machine (machine 2) to Company A.

 ■The carrying amount of machine 1 in Company A’s balance sheet is $70, and its fair
    value is evaluated at $120.

 ■Company A also pays $30 cash to Company B.

 ■Note: when the exchange includes cash, you must adjust the amounts received/given
    up!





                                                                                                                                                    35 / 121

--- Session 3, page 38 (combined page 267) ---
Example 4


 ■Suppose machine 2 is an industrial 3D printer, which allows Company A to produce new
    types of goods.

 ■This means that the trade has commercial substance. So record it as...
         • Add “machine 2” to PP&E at the value of $150 (fair value of machine 1 $120 + cash paid
          $30).

         • Recognize a gain of $50 in the income statement.
                [       $120     −         $70                 ]
                   | {z }                     |{z}
              fair value of machine 1  book value of machine 1

 ■Suppose machine 2 is a similar hydraulic press and no significant changes in Company
     A’s operation are expected.

 ■This means that the trade has no commercial substance. So record it as...
         • Recognize no gain or loss in the income statement.

         • Add “machine 2” to PP&E at the value of $100. [    $70    +   $30     ]
                                                              |{z}          |{z}
                                       BV machine 1  cash paid

                                                                                                                                                    36 / 121

--- Session 3, page 39 (combined page 268) ---
Back to the Qwest example...





 ■In Session 2, we saw an accounting scandal regarding revenue recognition: Qwest
    swapped network capacity with other telecom companies and booked the swaps as
    revenue.

 ■This test of “commercial substance” curtails that!

 ■Each company ended up with capacity similar to what it gave up: its future cash flows
     did not change and no new customer business was created — so no revenue or profit
    should be recognized.

--- Session 3, page 40 (combined page 269) ---
Accounting for an asset’s additional costs during operation





 ■To be capitalized, an additional cost must:
           1  Either increase the asset’s service potential (e.g., capacity, functionality, durability...), replace
        a major component, or represent a major inspection/overhaul.

           2  Be reliably measured.

  ■Ordinary repairs and maintenance activities are those required to restore and maintain
     the assets in normal operating conditions.
         • Benefits generated are “consumed as incurred” (i.e., in the current reporting period).

         • These costs should be expensed as incurred.





                                                                                                                                                    38 / 121

--- Session 3, page 41 (combined page 270) ---
Accounting for an asset’s additional costs during operation





 ■To be capitalized, an additional cost must:
           1  Either increase the asset’s service potential (e.g., capacity, functionality, durability...), replace
        a major component, or represent a major inspection/overhaul.

           2  Be reliably measured.

 ■Major repairs (e.g., an overhaul): substantial, infrequent activities necessary to
    maintain the asset usable for a further, identifiable period.
         • Benefits are “consumed over time”.

         • These costs should be capitalized over the defined period (e.g., until next overhaul).





                                                                                                                                                    39 / 121

--- Session 3, page 42 (combined page 271) ---
Example 5





 ■A shipping company just bought a new ship for £200 million with a useful life of 20 years.

 ■Every 4 years, however, the ship must be dry-docked and a major overhaul done
    (estimated cost: £4 million).

 ■How to account for this?
         • The £4 million should be capitalized when incurred and depreciated over 4 years.

--- Session 3, page 46 (combined page 275) ---
Accounting for an asset’s additional costs during operation





 ■To be capitalized, an additional cost must:
           1  Either increase the asset’s service potential (e.g., capacity, functionality, durability...), replace
        a major component, or represent a major inspection/overhaul.

           2  Be reliably measured.

  ■Expenditures on significantly enhancing the asset, extending its useful life, or reducing
     operating costs should also be capitalized.
         • The company should derecognize the carrying amount of the replaced component and
          recognize a gain or loss, if any.

         • The company should capitalize the cost of the new component.





                                                                                                                                                    44 / 121

--- Session 3, page 47 (combined page 276) ---
Example 6





 ■A property company owns an office building recorded at £20 million.

 ■The building includes an elevator system that must be fully replaced every 10 years.

 ■At the end of Year 10, the company replaces the elevator system at a cost of £500,000.

 ■How to account for this?
         • Derecognize the carrying amount of the old elevator system (cost minus accumulated
          depreciation).

         • Capitalize the cost of the new elevator system (£500,000).

         • Depreciate the new system over its own useful life (10 years).





                                                                                                                                                    45 / 121

--- Session 3, page 48 (combined page 277) ---
Disposal of PP&E




 ■The carrying amount of an asset on the balance sheet is determined by its net book
    value:

        Net Book Value = Historical Cost or Fair Value −Accumulated Depreciation

 ■When an asset is disposed of, a gain/loss on disposal is recorded on the income
     statement...

          Gain or Loss on Disposal = Proceeds from Disposal −Net Book Value

 ■Hence, the gain/loss on disposal depends on accumulated depreciation.
         • Even though the total charge to the income statement over the year does not.
         • This is because the accumulated depreciation has been expensed through the income
         statement over the years.





                                                                                                                                                    46 / 121

--- Session 3, page 49 (combined page 278) ---
Example 7



 ■Suppose Company A buys a machine (capitalized as PP&E) at the end of year 0 for
    £1,000, cash. The company uses the historical cost approach throughout the years.

 ■The useful life of this machine is 5 years and it has no residual value. Company A uses
    the straight-line method of depreciation.
         • We will see more on this soon...
         • But this means that Company A should expense £200 of depreciation per year.

 ■Suppose Company A sells the machine at the end of year 3 for £800, in cash.

 ■What is the gain/loss on disposal?

 ■At the end of year 3, the net book value of the machine is £400.

 ■Hence, there is a gain of £400 on disposal.





                                                                                                                                                    47 / 121

--- Session 3, page 50 (combined page 279) ---
Example 7 - Income Statement


 ■Year 0:
         • no charge should be made.

 ■Year 1:
         • depreciation ................... (£200)

 ■Year 2:
         • depreciation ................... (£200)

 ■Year 3:
         • depreciation ................... (£200)
         • gain on disposal ............. £400

 ■Hence the net P&L impact for the asset over the years is: -(£200) × 3 + £400 = -£200
         • Note that this equals...

                        Net P&L Impact = Proceeds from Sale −Original Cost
                                              |                                                       {z        }   |                                                                          {z    }                                           £800                                                               £1,000
                .

                                                                                                                                                    50 / 121

--- Session 3, page 53 (combined page 282) ---
Depreciation & Depletion

--- Session 3, page 54 (combined page 283) ---
Treatment of costs over the asset life





  ■If a firm consumes the services of the asset over time in generating revenue...
         • Then the firm should write off a proportion of the cost each period to against revenues.

  ■If a firm does not consume the services of the asset...
         • Then the firm should carry the asset at cost (e.g., land).

 ■The process of writing off a proportion of the cost each period is called:
           1  Depreciation for tangible assets.

           2  Amortization for intangible assets (patents, copyrights..).

           3  Depletion for natural resources (oil, gas, coal...).





                                                                                                                                                    51 / 121

--- Session 3, page 55 (combined page 284) ---
Treatment of costs over the asset life




  ■Depreciation (amortization, or depletion) is a process of cost allocation, not asset
    valuation.
         •  It is not intended to track the asset’s declining market value (for that, we use impairments).

         •  It is intended to spread the cost of an asset over its period of use.

  ■Depreciation involves three estimates of future events:
           1  The expected useful life of the asset. (which might be different from its physical life due to
         obsolescence)

           2  The expected residual or salvage value of the asset. (which determines the depreciable
        base of an asset)

           3  The expected pattern of declining service potential of the asset. (which determines the
          depreciation method that should be used).





                                                                                                                                                    52 / 121

--- Session 3, page 56 (combined page 285) ---
Methods of depreciation



   1  Activity method (units of use or production): depreciation is a function of use or
     productivity (e.g. # units produced, or # hours worked), instead of the passage of time.

   2  Straight-line method: depreciation is a linear function of time.

   3  Diminishing (accelerated)-charge methods: provide a higher depreciation cost in the
     earlier years and lower charges in later periods.
         • (a) Sum-of-the-years’-digits method: fraction of depreciation is determined by

                                    year of estimated life remaining
                                                                                                                 .
                                                  total sum of years’ digits

         • (b) Declining-balance method: applies a constant percentage rate of depreciation to a
          declining book value each year.

 ■The method chosen depends on the pattern in which the asset’s future economic
     benefits are expected to be consumed by the company.


                                                                                                                                                    53 / 121

--- Session 3, page 57 (combined page 286) ---
Example 8



   1  Activity method (units of use or production).

 ■Suppose an asset is acquired for £1,000 at the end of year 0. It has a residual value of
    £100. So the depreciable base is £900.

 ■Suppose this asset is expected to produce 3,000 units of a good in its useful life.

 ■Suppose that it produces: 1,500 units in year 1, 500 units in year 2, and 1,000 units in
    year 3. Then the asset is retired.

  ■Depreciation at each year...
                               1,500
         • Year 1: £450 [ 3,000 × 900]
         • Year 2: £150 [ 3,000500 × 900]

                               1,000
         • Year 3: £300 [ 3,000 × 900]
         • Accumulated depreciation: £900.

--- Session 3, page 58 (combined page 287) ---

   2  Straight-line method.

 ■Suppose an asset is acquired for £1,000 at the end of year 0. It has a residual value of
    £100. So the depreciable base is £900.

 ■Suppose this asset’s useful life is estimated in 3 years.

  ■Depreciation at each year...
         • Year 1: £300 [ 9003  ]
         • Year 2: £300 [ 9003  ]
         • Year 3: £300 [ 9003  ]
         • Accumulated depreciation: £900.
