--- Session 3, page 59 (combined page 288) ---
Example 8





   3  Diminishing (accelerated)-charge methods: (a) Sum-of-the-years’-digits.

 ■Suppose an asset is acquired for £1,000 at the end of year 0. It has a residual value of
    £100. So the depreciable base is £900. Suppose this asset’s useful life is estimated in 3
     years.

  ■Depreciation at each year...
         • Year 1: £450 [ 1+2+33  × 900 = 36 × 900]
         • Year 2: £300 [ 26 × 900]
         • Year 3: £150 [ 16 × 900]
         • Accumulated depreciation: £900.





                                                                                                                                                    56 / 121

--- Session 3, page 60 (combined page 289) ---
Example 8




   3  Diminishing (accelerated)-charge methods: (b) Declining-balance method.

 ■Suppose an asset is acquired for £1,000 at the end of year 0. It has a residual value of
    £100. Suppose this asset’s useful life is estimated in 3 years (straight-line rate = 1/3).
    Suppose the rate of depreciation used by the company is double that rate (that is, 2/3).

 ■The asset is retired only when the residual value is reached. Depreciation is charged
    over the asset’s carrying amount.

  ■Depreciation at each year...
         • Year 1: £667 [ 32 × 1, 000]
         • Year 2: £222 [ 32 × (1, 000 −667)]
         • Year 3: £11 [min{ 23 × (1, 000 −667 −222), (book value −residual value)}]
         • Accumulated depreciation: £900.

--- Session 3, page 64 (combined page 293) ---
Changes in estimates and methods





 ■From an accounting perspective, companies are always allowed to change their
     depreciation policy and estimates, and must do it when the change allows for a better
     representation of how the assets are consumed over time.

 ■To make these changes, however, the company must always provide a clear and
     rational reason.

  ■Moreover, any change is applied only prospectively. (for both US GAAP and IFRS!)





                                                                                                                                                    61 / 121

--- Session 3, page 68 (combined page 297) ---
Component depreciation





 ■IFRS requires that depreciation be accounted for by major component: that is, each
    major component is treated as a different asset and depreciation is charged individually,
    by asset.
         • This allows for a better representation of costs, since components might have different useful
           lives or consumption patterns.

 ■Under US GAAP, component depreciation is permitted but not required, and, in practice,
    most companies use rules of depreciation by asset class.





                                                                                                                                                    65 / 121

--- Session 3, page 69 (combined page 298) ---
Analysis of discretion in depreciation





 ■When analyzing the reasons for any change in policy or estimates, in addition to the
      explicit reason given by management, consider less obvious reasons...
         • What effect will the change have on income?
         • What effect will the change have on leverage ratios?
         • What effect might this change have on other ratios?

  ■This changes will have effect on the current year, sure...

 ■But they could also have material impacts several years into the future!





                                                                                                                                                    66 / 121

--- Session 3, page 70 (combined page 299) ---
What about depletion?


  ■Unlike PP&E, mineral resources are consumed physically over the period of use and do
     not maintain their physical characteristics.
         • The accounting treatment is similar to PP&E...

 ■There are three types of expenditures associated with mineral resources:
           1  Pre-exploratory costs (or prospecting costs): those before the company has obtained legal
           rights to explore a specific area.

           2  Exploratory & Evaluation (E&E) costs: like the acquisition of rights to explore, exploratory
             drilling, activities in relation to evaluating the technical feasibility of extracting a mineral
           resource...

           3  Development costs: are composed of (1) tangible equipment costs, (2) intangible
         development costs, and (3) restoration costs.

 ■The question is: which costs should be included in the depletion base (“book value” at
     acquisition)?
         • Depletion base is the total capitalized cost that will be allocated over the life of the natural
          resource.


                                                                                                                                                    67 / 121

--- Session 3, page 71 (combined page 300) ---
Expensing or capitalizing?





   1  Pre-exploratory costs (or prospecting costs): those before the company has obtained
     legal rights to explore a specific area.

 ■These costs are considered speculative in nature and should be expensed as
    incurred.





                                                                                                                                                    68 / 121

--- Session 3, page 72 (combined page 301) ---
Expensing or capitalizing?




   2  Exploratory & Evaluation (E&E) costs: like the acquisition of rights to explore,
     exploratory drilling, activities in relation to evaluating the technical feasibility of
     extracting a mineral resource...

 ■IFRS offers flexibility: these costs can be either expensed as incurred or capitalized (but
     policy must be consistent over time and disclosed).

 ■Why? Because, in practice, it is hard to determine the “best” way to represent these
     costs...

 ■Assume that Royal Dutch Shell is exploring a well to determine the amount of the
     reserves.
         • The first well drilled is dry.
         • Shell then drills more holes and finds some dry and others with reserves.
         • Should Shell expense or capitalize the cost of the drilling of dry wells?




                                                                                                                                                    69 / 121

--- Session 3, page 73 (combined page 302) ---
Expensing or capitalizing?




   3  Development costs: are composed of (1) tangible equipment costs, (2) intangible
    development costs, and (3) restoration costs.

  ■Tangible equipment costs are capitalized. But, are only included in the depletion base if
    they do not have an alternative use.
         • For example, most heavy equipment can be moved to another site and used in its
          exploration — so they are not included in the depletion base.

  ■Intangible development costs (such as drilling costs, tunnels, shafts, wells...) are
     capitalized and included in the depletion base.

 ■Firms should recognize a provision1 for restoration costs at present value (fair value of
    the obligation to restore the property after extraction) and include this in the depletion
    base.

   1More on this next session!

                                                                                                                                                    70 / 121

--- Session 3, page 74 (combined page 303) ---
Depletion





 ■To compute depletion, after the depletion base is defined, a business should estimate
    the total number of units available (e.g. liters of oil).

  ■Periodic depletion is based on the ratio of units extracted during the period to the total
    estimated units available.

 ■Note: activity-based depletion is the usual fit for the consumption pattern of natural
    resources, but is not required.





                                                                                                                                                    71 / 121

--- Session 3, page 75 (combined page 304) ---
Impairments & Revaluations

--- Session 3, page 76 (combined page 305) ---
What are impairments?





  ■Definition: a long-lived tangible asset is impaired when a company is not able to recover
     the asset’s carrying amount either through using it or selling it.

  ■Firms must annually review assets for impairment indicators: a decline in the assets’
      ability to generate future cash flows through use or sale.

  ■If these indicators are present, firms must test the asset for impairment: compare the
    carrying amount to the recoverable amount.
         •  If carrying amount > recoverable amount: a write off of the asset’s value and an
         impairment loss should be recorded.
         •  If carrying amount ≤recoverable amount: no impairment should be recorded.





                                                                                                                                                    72 / 121

--- Session 3, page 77 (combined page 306) ---
Can impairments be reversed?





 ■Under IFRS: Yes! But to a limited extent: the amount of recovery of the loss is limited to
    the carrying amount that would result if the impairment had not occurred.

 ■Under US GAAP: No!

 ■The intuition is that IFRS treats impairment as an estimate that can be revised; US
   GAAP treats this written-down amount as the new cost of the asset.





                                                                                                                                                    73 / 121

--- Session 3, page 78 (combined page 307) ---
Example 12


 ■On 1 January 2016, a company purchased a building for $1 million.

 ■The company estimated:
         • Annual cash flows from the building to be $92,960 (at each of period).

         • Useful life to be 40 years and depreciation to be taken based on a straight-line.

         • Cost of capital of 9% per year.

         • No residual value.

 ■On 1 January 2020, as a result of a property decline due to the credit crisis, the
    company experienced a reduction in rent income. This compelled it to assess whether
    the building has been impaired.
         • The company has used historical cost valuation since 2016.

         • Data suggests that the building will generate income of only $60,000 for the next 20 years.
           After that, cash flow will return to the initially estimated $92,960.

         • The cost of capital increased to 10%.

 ■How should we account for these changes at the end of 2020? Let’s go step by step...

                                                                                                                                                    74 / 121

--- Session 3, page 79 (combined page 308) ---
Example 12




 ■We need to compute the carrying amount of the asset at 1st January 2020 and
    compare it to its fair value (present value of cash flows)...


   1  Calculate cumulative depreciation at 1st January 2020: CumDepJan2020...

                                   4
                     CumDepJan2020 =  × 1, 000, 000 = 100, 000
                                  40

   2  Calculate the carrying amount of the asset (CAJan2020). At this point, there have been 4
    years of straight-line depreciation at acquisition cost...

                     CAJan2020 = 1, 000, 000 −CumDepJan2020 = 900, 000

   3  Calculate the fair value of the asset as of 1 January 2020: present value of expected
    cash flows — 20 years at $60,000 and remaining 16 years at $92,960, all with cost of
     capital of 10%.
                                   PVJan2020 = 618, 921.14


         • Note that, now, the asset has been impaired: there was a significant decrease in the
         estimated value of the asset, below the carrying amount CAJan2020!

   4  Calculate new amount of depreciation starting at 1 January 2020... You need to
    consider the new value of the asset, PVJan2020, and divide it by the remaining years.

                                           PVJan2020
                            DepDec2020 =      = 17, 192.25
                                     36

    the PVJan2020...

                                                ˆ    impairment been conducted before: let’s call this CAJan2024...

                     CAJan2024                    ˆ    = CAJan2020 −4 × 1, 000, 000 = 800, 000

                                   PVJan2024 = 945, 507.36


         • Under IFRS: the previous impairment loss can be reversed!

   5  Considering IFRS: the reversal of the impairment is restricted to the book value of the
    asset as of 1 January 2024 had there not been the previous impairment:

                             BVJan2024 = CAJan2024ˆ    = 800, 000.00

                                                                                                                                                    82 / 121

--- Session 3, page 87 (combined page 316) ---
Revaluations (valuing assets under “fair value”)





 ■Under IFRS, upwards revaluations are permitted but not mandated. (under US GAAP
    they are not allowed! Revaluations are only recognized upon sale of the asset)

  ■Specifically: under IFRS...
           1  At acquisition, firms must record their assets using the “historical cost” approach we
         discussed earlier.

           2  In the next reporting periods, firms have the choice to switch from the “historical cost”
         approach to the “fair value” approach.





                                                                                                                                                    83 / 121

--- Session 3, page 88 (combined page 317) ---
Revaluations (valuing assets under “fair value”)



 ■But this accounting choice must apply consistently across asset classes.
         • An asset class is a grouping of items that have a similar nature and use in a company’s
          operations.

         • A firm can choose to revalue “buildings” but not “land”...

         • But then all buildings must be revalued.

  ■Revaluations need to be performed frequently.
         • Because of this, most companies choose not to revalue their assets.

 ■Can the firm switch back to the “historical cost” approach after choosing the “fair value”
    approach?
         • Yes, but this is allowed only if it results in more reliable and more relevant information.

         • In practice, this is hard to argue... Auditors and regulators expect a clear documented
         reasoning for this “switch back”.





                                                                                                                                                    84 / 121

--- Session 3, page 89 (combined page 318) ---
How revaluations are recorded on financial statements?




   1 Upwards revaluations: the firm records...
         • The asset at its new fair value on the balance sheet.
         • A revaluation surplus in “Other Comprehensive Income” (does not impact earnings; this is
        an unrealized gain).

   2 Downwards revaluations: the firm records...
         • The asset as its new fair value on the balance sheet.
         • A revaluation loss as an expense in the period (does impact earnings).

 ■Note 1: if the firm has a revaluation surplus and incurs subsequently a revaluation loss,
    then it only expenses what is in excess of the previous surplus.

 ■Note 2: if the firm recognized a revaluation loss and incurs subsequently a revaluation
     surplus, it recognizes the reversal in the P&L and only what is in excess of the previous
     loss goes to OCI.




                                                                                                                                                    85 / 121

 ■On 1 January 2016, a company purchased a building for $1 million.

 ■This company evaluates all of its buildings based on the “fair value” approach. So, at

 ■What do the income statement and balance sheet look like at year-end 2017?

                                         40
                                        |          {z          }                                          depreciable base
                                                useful life

 ■Hence, there is a revaluation surplus of...

                      RevSurplus2017 = FV2017 −CA2017,old = 30, 000

                                    38

  ■Instead of charging a loss, we first decrease this amount from the revaluation surplus
    from last period...

                    RevSurplus2018 = 30, 000 −19, 210.53 = 10, 789.47

                                                                                                                                                    92 / 121

--- Session 3, page 97 (combined page 326) ---
How to value PP&E (after initial recognition)?





 ■Why use the historical cost approach?
           1   It is simpler and, many times, less costly to implement.

           2  Numbers are more stable over time.

           3  But value can easily become out of date.

 ■Why use the fair value approach?
           1  More timely (especially over longer periods of time, or in inflationary contexts).

           2  Better reflects the “exit price” (disposal of asset).

           3  But introduces equity volatility and the cost of frequent revaluations.





                                                                                                                                                    93 / 121

--- Session 3, page 98 (combined page 327) ---
How to value PP&E (after initial recognition)?




 ■In practice, you will usually see companies measuring PP&E based on the cost
    approach.

 ■Some infrastructure-heavy companies (e.g., airports) use the fair value approach for
    land & buildings.

 ■Here are some examples of companies that use revaluation for certain PP&E assets:
         • Auckland International Airport (NZ) for land, runways, taxiways, aprons and infrastructure.

         • Stena AB (SE) for its ports.

         • Brookfield Corporation (CA) for utilities, transport, midstream, data and renewable power
          generating assets.

         • Network Rail Infrastructure (UK) for its railway network.





                                                                                                                                                    94 / 121

--- Session 3, page 99 (combined page 328) ---
Quick detour - Other Comprehensive Income



 ■In a nutshell (broad terms), the statement of “Other Comprehensive Income” has the
     objective of:


   1  Ensure that the balance sheet is measuring assets and liabilities at updated values
    (when this is required).
         • Under the revaluation approach, an increase in the price of a PP&E asset must be
          recognized.

   2  Avoid including charges to earnings (profit or loss) until they actually relate to
    performance.
         • The increase in the fair value of a PP&E asset is not a realized gain — this is an application
           of the prudence principle...

         • This gain is only treated as a “cushion” for the future, but not recognized as an increase in
        economic benefits.

         • This increase in the fair value of a PP&E asset only affects performance through increased
          depreciation expenses over time (i.e., this extra expense is recognized over time).


                                                                                                                                                    95 / 121

--- Session 3, page 100 (combined page 329) ---
Takeaways

--- Session 3, page 101 (combined page 330) ---
Key Takeaways





 Topic            Key takeaway                                      IFRS vs US GAAP

 Initial costs          Capitalize costs directly attributable to getting the asset ready  Same
                          for its intended use (including borrowing costs, if this takes
                        substantial time). Expense the rest (e.g., training, relocation).

 Costs during        Capitalize improvements, replacements of major          Same
 operation          components, and major overhauls. Expense ordinary repairs.

 Changes in         Allowed, and required when they better reflect how the asset  Same
 estimates              is used. Applied prospectively only.

 Impairment         Write down when carrying amount > recoverable amount.     Reversal: IFRS yes
                                                                                 (capped); US GAAP no

 Revaluation          Fair value, by asset class. Gains go to OCI (revaluation      IFRS option; not allowed
                       surplus); losses hit P&L once the surplus is used up.         under US GAAP

 Why it matters     These choices move earnings, asset turnover, and leverage.   –
                      Adjust before comparing companies (and watch for abuse:
                  WorldCom, Waste Management).

      Airways versus Lufthansa?

 ■These slides are based on the case “Depreciation Policy: British Airways versus

--- Session 3, page 104 (combined page 333) ---
Context





 ■What is the context of the case?
         • Set in 2003.

         • Analyses the different depreciation policies and their impact on statements of British Airways
        and Lufthansa.





                                                                                                                                                    98 / 121

--- Session 3, page 105 (combined page 334) ---
Depreciation Policies





  ■British Airways: straight-line basis over a period of 20 years after making allowance for
     residual values at 10% of cost.

  ■Lufthansa: straight-line basis over a period of 12 years after making allowance for
     residual values at 15% of cost.





                                                                                                                                                    99 / 121

                              20

                           AvAge

                                                                                                                                                   101 / 121

--- Session 3, page 108 (combined page 337) ---
Question 1



 ■When to use each method?

 ■Use the first method when you know the depreciation policy and it applies for the whole
     class of assets.

 ■The issue with the first method is that it tends to understate the average age of the fleet
    because it implicitly considers that each aircraft has a maximum usage of 12 years (i.e.,
    not used after that).

 ■Use the second method when you don’t know the exact depreciation policy, or when you
    want a quick response.

 ■The issue with the second method is that it tends to overstate the average age of the
     fleet because fully depreciated aircraft stay in accumulated depreciation but no longer
    add to the year’s charge.

    does this mean?

  ■Lufthansa is using some of its aircraft beyond their stated useful life!

   2 The residual value (as a fraction of cost) of BA’s fleet is lower than that of Lufthansa’s.

--- Session 3, page 111 (combined page 340) ---
Question 2 - possible reasons





   1  Shorter useful life might reflect a higher utilization rate of the aircraft by Lufthansa
     relative to BA.
   2  Differences in residual value might reflect different mix of owned aircraft (models,
     manufacturers...).
         • For example, BA’s fleet appears to be predominantly Boeings whereas Lufthansa relies
        more heavily on Airbus.

   3 BA might have a more stringent maintenance program, extending the useful life of its
     aircraft beyond those of Lufthansa.
   4  Differences in residual value might reflect local factors — for example, the availability of
    a close second-hand market, or customary business practices.
         • Much more room for this before IAS 16 (revised in 2003 and effective since 2005).

                                        20

                                                                                                                                                   109 / 121

--- Session 3, page 116 (combined page 345) ---
Question 4 - Balance Sheet (cont.)





 ■Remember the dual aspect convention: if assets increase (NBV for “aircraft” is now
     higher), something has to increase on liabilities or equity. . .

 ■The extra NBV for “aircraft” (9,501.74 - 6,529.9 = 2,971.84) is depreciation that
    Lufthansa charged in the past but wouldn’t have charged under BA’s policy!

 ■So this amount would have remained in retained earnings: equity would increase by
    2,971.84 (before taxes).

 ■Note: disposals are materially small, so we won’t account for that. But the most precise
    version of this exercise would have to adjust the depreciation on disposals.





                                                                                                                                                   110 / 121

--- Session 3, page 117 (combined page 346) ---
Question 4 - Balance Sheet (cont.)




 ■We will see how to account for income taxes in Session 5.

 ■But the idea is that all the lower depreciation charges over the years coming from BA’s
     policy mean that Lufthansa has recognized profits (2,971.84) that were not taxed yet —
     restating the books does not change the taxes Lufthansa actually paid!

 ■These taxes will have to be paid in the future: this is a deferred tax liability (DTL).
    Using the effective tax rate (taxes/PBT = 231/952 = 24.3%):

                     DTL = 2, 971.84 × 24.3% = 722.16

 ■Hence, equity increases by 2, 971.84 −722.16 = 2, 249.68 (to a total of 6,421.7).

--- Session 4, page 3 (combined page 362) ---
Intangible Assets

--- Session 4, page 4 (combined page 363) ---
Motivation





 ■We will start discussing accounting for intangible assets.

  ■Understanding how to treat intangible assets is really important today: for many
    companies, what drives value is intangibles (IP, software, customer relationships...) and
    not PP&E (factories, machines...).

 ■Take Bayer as an example: it has a lot of PP&E that allows it to produce
    pharmaceuticals and perform R&D, for example, but most of its value is in intangibles...





                                                                                                                                                     3 / 123

--- Session 4, page 5 (combined page 364) ---
Example 1





                                                                                                                                                     4 / 123

--- Session 4, page 6 (combined page 365) ---
Definition





  ■Intangible assets have some main characteristics:
           1  They are identifiable: an intangible asset is either separable from the company (can be sold
          or transferred), or it arises from a contractual or legal right from which economic benefits will
          flow to the company.
           A software is an example of the former.
           A patent is an example of the latter.

           2  They lack physical substance.

           3  The are not monetary assets: monetary items are cash or assets/liabilities to be
          received/paid in a fixed or determinable number of currency units.
           4  They are not financial assets.1 (more on this later)




   1These would fit characteristics 1 through 3, but is excluded from the scope under IAS 38

                                                                                                                                                     5 / 123

--- Session 4, page 7 (combined page 366) ---
Recognition of intangible assets





   1  Identifiability: the asset must be separable from the business (sold or rented on its
    own) or it must arise from contractual/legal rights (even if not separable).

   2  Control: the entity must have the power to obtain future economic benefits from the
    asset and restrict others’ access to those benefits.

   3  Probable future economic benefits: there must be evidence that the asset will
    generate inflows (cost savings, sales, licensing income...).

   4  Costs can be reliably measured.

 ■Why does internally generated goodwill fail these criteria? Fails (1) and (4).





                                                                                                                                                     6 / 123

--- Session 4, page 8 (combined page 367) ---
Valuation of purchased intangible assets




 ■Under IFRS, intangible assets are recorded at cost (similar to PP&E):
         • The cost includes all purchase price plus expenditures directly attributable to making the
          asset ready for its intended use.

         •  If the asset is purchased in exchange for non-monetary consideration (e.g. share), use the
         procedure discussed in the last session...

         • Check if the exchange has commercial substance (CS): apply either the fair value of the
          asset given up (if CS) or the carrying amount of the asset given up (no CS).

  ■Differently from PP&E assets however...

 ■Companies can only choose the revaluation approach to record an intangible asset if
    there is an active market for that asset.
         • What is an active market? An active market is characterized by (i) homogeneous goods, and
                (ii) publicly available prices.





                                                                                                                                                     7 / 123

--- Session 4, page 9 (combined page 368) ---
Digital Assets


 ■A good example of intangible asset with an active market is cryptocurrency (except
   when it is traded OTC)... (more on this later)

 ■Under IFRS, in this case, companies have the choice between the cost approach and
    the revaluation approach. Which would you choose?





                                                 Bitcoin to USD, daily (closing price)




                                                                                                                                                     8 / 123

--- Session 4, page 10 (combined page 369) ---
Example 2




 ■Meitu Corporation, a Chinese technology company that produces smartphones and
     selfies apps decided for the cost approach.





                                                                                                                                                     9 / 123

--- Session 4, page 11 (combined page 370) ---
Example 3





 ■Nexon Corporation, a South Korean video game developer and publisher, opted for the
     revaluation approach.





                                                                                                                                                    10 / 123

--- Session 4, page 12 (combined page 371) ---
Valuation of internally created intangible assets (IFRS)





 ■Under IFRS, internally created intangible assets (just as with PP&E) are initially valued
    based on the cumulative capitalized costs that were incurred to develop the asset.
         • Some intangible assets (such as goodwill) are never recognized because they do not satisfy
          the recognition criteria...

 ■The key issue is determining which costs can be capitalized and which must be
    expensed.





                                                                                                                                                    11 / 123

--- Session 4, page 13 (combined page 372) ---
R&D expenditures




 ■To deal with recognition problem, IAS 38 classifies the generation of an intangible asset
     into two phases...


   1  Research phase: this phase precedes the development phase and may have no direct
    connection to the development of a particular good or service.
         • Research activities include original and planned investigation undertaken with the prospect
           of gaining new scientific or technical knowledge and understanding.

   2  Development phase: this is a more advanced phase and covers activities like the
     design/construction/testing of prototypes/models/pilot plants.
         • Development activities consist of applying research findings or other knowledge to plan or
         design the production of new or substantially improved products/services before the start
          of commercial production or use.





                                                                                                                                                    12 / 123

--- Session 4, page 14 (combined page 373) ---
Capitalizing development expenditures



 ■For an intangible asset arising from development expenditures to be recognized, the
    business must demonstrate the:


   1  Technical feasibility of completing that intangible asset for use or sale.

   2  Intention to complete the intangible asset for use of sale.

   3  Ability to use or sell the intangible asset.

   4 How the intangible asset will generate probable future economic benefits.

   5  Availability of adequate technical, financial and other resources to complete the
     intangible asset for use or sale.

   6  Ability to measure expenditure, attributable to the intangible asset, reliably.





                                                                                                                                                    13 / 123

--- Session 4, page 15 (combined page 374) ---
Capitalizing development expenditures




 ■Only development costs incurred after the 6 conditions in the previous slide have been
    met can be capitalized.

 ■From these development expenditures, only costs that are directly attributable to
    producing the asset or getting it ready for use may be capitalized.

 ■In practice, this usually includes...
         • Materials and services consumed in creating the asset.

         • Employee costs.

         • Legal fees.

         • Depreciation (amortization) of patents and licenses used in the development of the asset.

 ■But expenses like selling, administrative or general overhead costs are not included.





                                                                                                                                                    14 / 123

--- Session 4, page 16 (combined page 375) ---

 ■How Vodafone accounts for internally generated software?





                                                                                                                                                    15 / 123

--- Session 4, page 17 (combined page 376) ---

                                                                                                                                                    16 / 123

--- Session 4, page 18 (combined page 377) ---
Example 5


 ■How does Gaumont account for the feature films it produces?

    knowledge, designs, software associated with an engine...)?





                                                                                                                                                    18 / 123

--- Session 4, page 20 (combined page 379) ---
Example 6



 ■How does Rolls Royce account for its “engine programmes” (that is, the technical
    knowledge, designs, software associated with an engine...)?





                                                                                                                                                    19 / 123

--- Session 4, page 21 (combined page 380) ---
Example 6



 ■How does Rolls Royce account for its “engine programmes” (that is, the technical
    knowledge, designs, software associated with an engine...)?





                                                                                                                                                    20 / 123

--- Session 4, page 22 (combined page 381) ---
Capitalize or expense?



 ■The key to capitalization is that economic viability has been “proved” and that the costs
    are directly attributable to getting the asset ready to use.

 ■Suppose a company incurs some start-up costs related to opening a new plant to
    produce using its internally developed method of production. Should these start-up
    costs be capitalized to the internally generated intangible? No! They are costs to get
    the asset “plant” ready; not the intangible.

 ■Suppose a company incurs in initial operating losses as its trying to launch its new
     internally developed software. Should these losses be capitalized to the software? No!
    The asset is already ready to use; these are not necessary costs to get it ready.

 ■Suppose a company hires celebrities to advertise its internally generated intangible,
     therefore increasing their value. Should the hiring costs of this advertisement be
     capitalized to the asset? No! The asset is already ready to use; these are not
    necessary costs to get it ready.



                                                                                                                                                    21 / 123

--- Session 4, page 23 (combined page 382) ---
Valuation of internally created intangible assets (US GAAP)





 ■Under US GAAP, all research and development costs are expensed as incurred.

  ■This is a key difference between IFRS and US GAAP!

 ■The main exception is software:
         • Software to be sold: costs are capitalized once technological feasibility is reached (in
           practice, close to release, so little is capitalized).

         • Software for internal use: costs are capitalized once the project is approved and funded and
              its completion is probable.





                                                                                                                                                    22 / 123

--- Session 4, page 24 (combined page 383) ---
Amortization





  ■Just as we saw last session: the allocation of the cost of intangible assets is called
    amortization.

 ■The amortization schedule allocates the cost of an intangible asset with limited-life
    over its useful life.
         • For example: copyrights on movies or licenses related to branded products.

  ■If no factors (e.g. legal, regulatory, contractual...) limit the useful life of an intangible
     asset, a company consider its useful life as indefinite and there is no amortization.
         • For example: a trademark (indefinite renewals for periods of 10 years each), or goodwill from
        a business combination (more on this soon!)





                                                                                                                                                    23 / 123

--- Session 4, page 25 (combined page 384) ---
Impairments & revaluations




 ■In practice, most intangible assets are valued using the cost approach and, hence, must
    be periodically tested for impairment.

  ■Specifically, just as for PP&E assets: an impairment loss should be recognized (and
    expensed through the P&L statement) for the amount that the “carrying amount” of the
    asset is greater than the recoverable amount.
         • Recoverable amount is the greater of the fair value less costs to sell or value-in-use.

         • Impairment reversals are allowed as long as justified by estimates.

 ■IFRS: In the more rare case where the company uses the revaluation approach for its
     intangibles, the revaluations follow the same process discussed for PP&E:
         • Revaluation gains do not affect earnings — specifically, they affect equity via OCI.
         • Revaluation losses affect earnings — in the amount net of any previous revaluation surplus.





                                                                                                                                                    24 / 123

--- Session 4, page 26 (combined page 385) ---
When to recognize goodwill?




  ■Goodwill is only recorded when a company acquires control of another business
       (i.e., when consolidation happens).

  ■Goodwill is the exception for intangible asset valuation:
         • Instead of computing how much it is worth directly, its value is obtained through a residual.

         • The value of goodwill is the difference between the fair value of the business’ assets (the
          net tangible and identifiable intangible assets) and the price (cost) of acquiring that
         business.

 ■Suppose Company A acquires Company B for £10mi. The fair value of Company B’s
     identifiable net assets is £9.5mi. Then, implicitly, Company A acquires Company B’s
     goodwill for £500k.





                                                                                                                                                    25 / 123

--- Session 4, page 27 (combined page 386) ---
How to recognize goodwill?





  ■Goodwill is always valued at cost (the residual) — it can never be subject to the
     revaluation approach.

 ■Given the cost approach, goodwill must be regularly tested for impairment.

 ■In order to do so, a company must assign goodwill to some cash generating unit and
     test the whole unit for impairment.

 ■This is because goodwill only generates cash flows in combination with other assets
     (the “cash generating unit”).

  ■Let’s see what this means!





                                                                                                                                                    26 / 123

--- Session 4, page 28 (combined page 387) ---
Cash-generating unit





  ■Definition: a cash-generating unit (CGU) is the smallest identifiable group of assets
     that generates cash flows independently of the cash flows of other assets.

  ■Loosely speaking: if every asset in the company is shut down except a given group,
    would this group still generate cash inflows? If yes, then it is a CGU.

  ■Consider a retail group that owns two stores, one in Dallas and one in Boston. Would
    you say that each store is a CGU?

  ■Consider a mining company that owns a mine and processing plant. Would you say the
    mine is a CGU?





                                                                                                                                                    27 / 123

--- Session 4, page 29 (combined page 388) ---
Example 7





 ■Many pharmaceuticals companies define the CGU at the product level... Here is
    AstraZeneca’s guidance in their 2024 annual report:





                                                                                                                                                    28 / 123

--- Session 4, page 30 (combined page 389) ---
How to impair goodwill?





   1  At a given reporting period, the company must estimate the recoverable amount for that
   CGU. This is the maximum between:
         • The fair value less cost of disposal.

         • The value-in-use (roughly, the estimated present value of future cash flows of that CGU).

   2  The company must then compare this recoverable amount to the carrying amount of the
   CGU (i.e., the sum of the book value of all the net assets that integrate that CGU).

   3   If this carrying amount is larger than the recoverable amount, the CGU has been
     impaired.
         • The impairment loss is first allocated to goodwill!

         • After the value of goodwill reaches zero, the loss is allocated to the assets that make up the
      CGU based on their relative carrying amounts.





                                                                                                                                                    29 / 123

--- Session 4, page 31 (combined page 390) ---
How to impair goodwill?





 ■Note 1: the impairment of goodwill can never be reversed.

 ■Note 2: if an individual asset within the CGU shows indicators of impairment,2 the
    company should first test this specific asset for impairment and charge an impairment
     loss to it.
         • Only after “fixing” individual assets do you test the CGU as a whole.

         • The idea is that impairment of the CGU should reflect not the impairment of its individual
          assets, but rather of their joint operation (their combination).





   2Note 3: some assets, like indefinite-life intangibles, must be tested at least annually for impairment.

                                                                                                                                                    30 / 123

--- Session 4, page 32 (combined page 391) ---
Example 8



  ■Bayer’s annual report (2024) is a good example of the practices regarding goodwill and
      its potential impairment...





                                                                                                                                                    31 / 123

--- Session 4, page 33 (combined page 392) ---
Example 8



  ■Bayer’s annual report (2024) is a good example of the practices regarding goodwill and
      its potential impairment...





                                                                                                                                                    32 / 123

--- Session 4, page 34 (combined page 393) ---
Example 9





 ■Suppose Company A owns two types of products (baby wipes and dog food), each
    associated with a strong individual brand and, hence, each representing a separate
   CGU.

 ■Company A is acquired by Company B by the full price of £5mi. The total fair value of
    net identifiable assets for Company A £4mi. What is the total goodwill in this case?

 ■How to allocate this goodwill between the two CGUs?





                                                                                                                                                    33 / 123

--- Session 4, page 35 (combined page 394) ---
Example 9





 ■The total goodwill of £1mi should be allocated based on the expected synergies that
     arise from the combination of Company A with Company B.

 ■Suppose that Company B owns a veterinarian clinics. In this case, it is possible that all
    the expected synergies arise from the combination of Company B with the dog food
    segment of Company A — in which case the £1mi should be allocated to this CGU.

  ■It might be that Company B expects synergies to arise in proportion to the value of each
   CGU — say baby wipes have a fair value of £3mi, and dog food, the remaining £1mi. In
     this case, 75% of goodwill is allocated to baby wipes.





                                                                                                                                                    34 / 123

--- Session 4, page 36 (combined page 395) ---
Example 9





 ■Now suppose that the CGU “dog food” is composed by several assets: the plant that
    produces the dog food, the machines within this plant, the brand reputation, the specific
    formulas used for the food, goodwill...

 ■Suppose that there is a new technology in the market and the machines used to
    produce dog food are now outdated, and their recoverable value decreases significantly.

 ■Should we impair the CGU or the machines?





                                                                                                                                                    35 / 123

--- Session 4, page 37 (combined page 396) ---
Example 9





 ■Now suppose that the CGU “dog food” is composed by several assets: the plant that
    produces the dog food, the machines within this plant, the brand reputation, the specific
    formulas used for the food, goodwill...

 ■Suppose that there is a large and persistent decline in the value of dog food —
    consumers just don’t want to give their dogs industrialized food anymore and are
    cooking the food themselves.

 ■Should we impair the CGU?

--- Session 4, page 38 (combined page 397) ---

--- Session 4, page 39 (combined page 398) ---
Reference





 ■These slides are based on the Harvard Business School case “MicroStrategy:
    Accounting for Cryptocurrency” by Jonas Hesse and Annelena Lobb.





                                                                                                                                                    37 / 123

--- Session 4, page 40 (combined page 399) ---
Setting





 ■What is the setting of the case?
         • This case is set in early 2021, after MicroStrategy has bought substantive amounts of bitcoin.

         • The case is centered around the question of whether this strategic choice by MicroStrategy
          (holding reserves in bitcoin) has “changed materially” the fundamentals of this business.





                                                                                                                                                    38 / 123

--- Session 4, page 41 (combined page 400) ---
MicroStrategy, in a nutshell





  ■MicroStrategy was founded in 1989 as a software company that provided solutions of
    data mining to its clients.

 ■The company went public in 1998, and shortly after had issues with improper
    accounting that led its earnings to be restated and its stock price to fall.

  ■Although apparently maintaining the sale of business intelligence and software as its
    main activities, MicroStrategy decided to invest heavily in digital assets starting in 2020.





                                                                                                                                                    39 / 123

--- Session 4, page 42 (combined page 401) ---
The strategy shift





  ■MicroStrategy noted in its 2020 10-K filing (filled in February 2021) that:

      “One strategy is to grow our enterprise analytics software business and the other
                            strategy is to acquire and hold Bitcoin”

  ■Its executive chairman even said that:

     “Bitcoin is swarm of cyber hornets serving the goddess of wisdom, feeding on the fire of
          truth, exponentially growing ever smarter, faster, and stronger behind a wall of
                                   encrypted energy”

 ■To fund this new strategy, the company invested a substantial amount of its reserves of
    cash & equivalents, and issued convertible debt.

                                          Share of total assets over time

--- Session 4, page 44 (combined page 403) ---
Bitcoin


  ■Bitcoin (BTC) is a decentralized digital currency that runs on a public blockchain.
         • There is no central issuer (like traditional fiat currencies, such as the dollar).

         • The supply is fixed and the issuance is capped at 21 million BTC.

         • The price is market-driven and highly volatile.





                                                 Bitcoin to USD, daily (closing price)

--- Session 4, page 45 (combined page 404) ---
Why classify Bitcoin as an intangible asset?




 ■Remember the definition we saw earlier in -----  ...

  ■Intangible assets have three characteristics:
           1  They are identifiable. ✓
           2  They lack physical substance. ✓
           3  They are not monetary or financial assets. ✓Why?
              Because BTC is not cash or cash equivalent — it is not issued by a central bank (so it is not cash)
              and its price is volatile (so it is not a cash equivalent).3
              Because BTC also is not a financial asset as it does not give no contractual right to receive a
                 fixed/determinable number of currency units.
                   Let’s jump to the next slide...




    3Note: cash equivalents must have insignificant risk of changes in value.

                                                                                                                                                    44 / 123

--- Session 4, page 46 (combined page 405) ---
Why classify Bitcoin as an intangible asset?




  ■According to IAS 32, a financial asset is:
         • Cash.

         • An equity instrument of another company.

         • A contractual right to receive cash or another financial asset (e.g. a trade receivable or a
          bond).

         • A contract to exchange financial assets or financial liabilities with another party on terms that
          are potentially favorable (a derivative).

         • Certain own-equity-settled contracts. (we won’t discuss this)

  ■Bitcoin is none of the above...

  ■Note: for broker-traders, bitcoin can be treated as inventory. But this is not the case for
     MicroStrategy...





                                                                                                                                                    45 / 123

--- Session 4, page 47 (combined page 406) ---
Accounting treatment under US GAAP





 ■US GAAP does not allow companies to record their intangible assets at fair value...

  ■This was the context when this case was written.

  ■However, effective December 15 2024, companies that are subject to the US GAAP
    must record their digital assets using fair value.

  ■This treatment differs from the one under IFRS since it allows upwards revaluations to
     affect earnings (i.e. revaluation gains are not recorded in OCI like under IFRS, but in the
    P&L).
