                                                                                                                                                    50 / 123

--- Session 4, page 52 (combined page 411) ---
Is MicroStrategy an investment entity?





 ■No!

 ■An investment entity is primarily engaged in the business of investing for returns from
     capital appreciation and/or investment income, and reports substantially all investments
     at fair value with changes in earnings.

  ■MicroStrategy does not fit this definition.

 ■So even though it holds large amounts of wealth in a highly volatile asset, it is not
     subject to any of the requirements imposed to these entities (like leverage limitations)...





                                                                                                                                                    51 / 123

--- Session 4, page 53 (combined page 412) ---
Provisions & Contingent Liabilities

--- Session 4, page 54 (combined page 413) ---
Liabilities





  ■Until now, we have focused on assets. Now we turn to the other side of the balance
     sheet: liabilities.

 ■What is a liability?
           1   It is a present obligation of the entity (legal or constructive),

           2  to transfer an economic resource (usually cash) to another entity, and

           3  that arises from a past event (the obligating event).





                                                                                                                                                    52 / 123

--- Session 4, page 55 (combined page 414) ---
Is there a liability?





   1 The company received a delivery of raw materials last week. The supplier’s invoice is
    payable in 60 days. Yes. The obligating event is the delivery, not the payment.

   2  In December, the company signed a contract to buy raw materials throughout next year.
    Nothing has been delivered and nothing has been paid. No. Neither party has
    performed. The obligation arises as deliveries occur.

   3 The company sold products this year with a two-year warranty. Historically, 3% of units
    are returned. Yes. The obligating event is the sale. Uncertainty over timing and amount
    does not prevent recognition — it makes this a provision.

   4 A customer paid in advance for goods the company will deliver next year. Yes. The
     obligating event is the receipt of cash; the resource to be transferred is the goods.





                                                                                                                                                    53 / 123

--- Session 4, page 56 (combined page 415) ---
Is there a liability?



   5 The company expects its loss-making division to lose another £5m next year. No.
    Future operating losses arise from future trading — there is no past event.

   6  Regulation requires the company to overhaul its aircraft engines every three years. The
    next overhaul falls due in two years. No. The company could avoid the expenditure by
     selling the aircraft, so there is no present obligation. The overhaul is capitalized and
    depreciated as a component instead.

   7 New legislation coming into force next year will require the company to retrain its staff.
    No. The company can avoid the cost by changing how it operates. Note that it would
    have an obligation for any fines already incurred.

   8  The board declared a dividend on 20 December. It will be paid on 15 January. Yes. The
     obligating event is the declaration.

 ■Note the recurring theme: if the company can still avoid the outflow through its own
     future actions, there is no present obligation.


                                                                                                                                                    54 / 123

--- Session 4, page 57 (combined page 416) ---
Legal vs constructive obligations




 ■A legal obligation is an obligation that derives from a contract, legislation or other
    operation of law.

 ■A constructive obligation is an obligation that derives from an entity’s actions where:
           1  By an established pattern of past practice, published policies or a sufficiently specific current
          statement, the entity has indicated to other parties that it will accept certain responsibilities;
        and

           2  As a result, the entity has created a valid expectation on the part of those other parties that it
             will discharge those responsibilities.

  ■Within IAS 37, both legal and constructive obligations are treated as liabilities.
         • Within US GAAP, constructive obligations are, in some cases, not recognized as liabilities.





                                                                                                                                                    55 / 123

--- Session 4, page 58 (combined page 417) ---
IFRS vs US GAAP





 ■Suppose that a company contaminates a river during its operations but operates in a
    country where the law does not require it to clean this contamination.

 ■The company, however, has a long-standing policy of cleaning up any contamination it
    causes, and the public expects it to do so.

 ■Under IFRS, the company has a constructive obligation to clean up the contamination
    and must recognize a liability for this obligation.

 ■Under US GAAP, the company has no legal obligation and, hence, does not recognize a
      liability for this obligation.





                                                                                                                                                    56 / 123

--- Session 4, page 59 (combined page 418) ---
Current vs non-current liabilities





 ■A current liability is a liability that satisfies either of the following criteria:
           1   liability expected to be settled within 12 months of the reporting date (or within the normal
          operating cycle), or

           2   liability for which the entity does not have the right at the end of the reporting period to defer
          settlement of the liability for at least 12 months, or

           3  holds the liability primarily for the purpose of trading (e.g. derivatives — we won’t see this in
            this ------   ).

 ■But what is an operating cycle?





                                                                                                                                                    57 / 123

--- Session 4, page 60 (combined page 419) ---
Current vs non-current liabilities




 ■Operating cycle: the time between the acquisition of assets for processing and their
     realization in cash or cash equivalents.
         •  It might differ from 12 months for some companies, depending on the nature of their
          business.

         • Diageo: by law, Scotch whisky must mature in oak casks for at least three years, and its
        premium single malts mature for 10 to 18 years or more. The cycle from distillation to cash is
        measured in years.

         • Barratt Redrow: a UK housebuilder typically takes three to five years to go from acquiring
          land, through planning permission and construction, to selling the finished homes.

         • In both cases, inventories — and the trade payables that finance them — are classified as
          current, even though they will not convert into cash within 12 months.





                                                                                                                                                    58 / 123

--- Session 4, page 61 (combined page 420) ---
A special case: unearned revenue





 ■Unearned revenue is a current liability that arises when a company receives cash in
    advance for goods or services that have not yet been delivered or performed.

  ■It represents an obligation to transfer goods or services to the customer in the future.

 ■As the company fulfills its obligations, the unearned revenue is recognized as earned
    revenue and the liability is reduced.

  ■This is a case where analysts might read a high current liability balance as a positive
     sign of sales and profitability.
         • When sales grow, the company might receive cash in advance for future deliveries, which
          increases unearned revenue.





                                                                                                                                                    59 / 123

--- Session 4, page 62 (combined page 421) ---
A special case: unearned revenue




  ■Let’s look at Salesforce as an example.

  ■Salesforce is a US enterprise software company (San Francisco, founded 1999), and
     the world’s largest provider of customer relationship management (CRM) software.

 ■They sell access to software over the internet — the “software as a service” (SaaS)
    model. In FY2025, $35.7bn of $37.9bn in revenues (94%) came from subscriptions and
     support.

  ■Salesforce invoices its customers in advance, in annual instalments, but delivers
     the service continuously over the year.
         • The cash arrives on day 1, but the performance obligation is satisfied over time →revenue is
         recognized month by month.

         • Everything that is billed but not yet delivered sits in unearned revenue.





                                                                                                                                                    60 / 123

--- Session 4, page 63 (combined page 422) ---
A special case: unearned revenue





  ■Salesforce (CRM) — current liabilities as reported:



            $ million                              Jan 2024   Jan 2025   Jan 2026

             Accounts payable, accrued expenses & other      6,111      6,658      8,253
              Operating lease liabilities, current                 518       579       548
            Unearned revenue                            19,003     20,743     24,317
              Debt, current                                 999         –      4,000

               Total current liabilities                        26,631     27,980     37,118

            Unearned revenue / total current liabilities     71.4%     74.1%     65.5%





                                                                                                                                                    61 / 123

--- Session 4, page 64 (combined page 423) ---
Provisions & Contingent Liabilities





 ■We have just seen what a liability is.

 ■But when does an uncertain future outflow qualify as a liability?

 ■This is what determines whether a company should recognize a provision (a liability) or
    a contingent liability (disclosed only; not in the balance sheet).





                                                                                                                                                    62 / 123

--- Session 4, page 65 (combined page 424) ---
Provisions





  ■Provisions are liabilities of uncertain timing or amount.

 ■They are recognized when:
           1  The entity has a present obligation (legal or constructive) as a result of a past event (the
           obligating event).

           2   It is probable that an outflow of resources embodying economic benefits will be required to
           settle the obligation.

           3 A reliable estimate can be made of the amount of the obligation.





                                                                                                                                                    63 / 123

--- Session 4, page 66 (combined page 425) ---
Probable?




 ■Under IFRS, “probable” means more likely than not (i.e., a probability greater than 50%).


         • This threshold is applied to the CLASS of events that could give rise to a liability, not to the
           individual events themselves.

         • Think about warranties: the company does not know which specific units will be returned,
          but it knows that a certain percentage of the total units sold will be returned.

 ■Under US GAAP, the threshold is considerably higher: “probable” means likely to occur
     (usually interpreted as a probability of 70% or higher).

 ■This difference in thresholds can lead to different recognition of provisions under IFRS
    and US GAAP.
         • IFRS recognizes provisions earlier and more often than US GAAP.





                                                                                                                                                    64 / 123

--- Session 4, page 67 (combined page 426) ---
What is not a provision?





 ■A provision is not the same as an accrual.
         • An accrual is a liability for an obligation that is certain in timing and amount, such as
           salaries payable or accounts payable.

         • A provision is a liability for an obligation that is uncertain in timing or amount, such as a
          warranty provision or a legal claim.

 ■A provision is not the same as a mere expectation of future costs.
         • A mere expectation of future costs does not create a present obligation.

         • For example, a company may expect to incur costs for future research and development, but
            this does not create a liability until the company has a legal or constructive obligation to pay
         those costs.





                                                                                                                                                    65 / 123

--- Session 4, page 68 (combined page 427) ---
When to recognize a provision?





    1  The company’s employees worked through December. Salaries of £1.8m will be paid on
    5 January. No — this is an accrual. A present obligation, but with virtually no
     uncertainty as to amount or timing.

    2 A customer is suing the company for £2m after being injured by one of its products.
    Lawyers believe the company will more likely than not lose, and estimate the cost at
    £1.5m. Yes. All three criteria are met.

    3 The same lawsuit, but lawyers now believe the company will probably win — a loss is
     possible, but not probable. No. Criterion 2 fails →contingent liability, disclosed in the
    notes only.

    4  The board has approved a £3m refurbishment of the company’s own head office for next
     year. No — and no liability at all. The company can still cancel the work or sell the
     building: a mere expectation of future costs.





                                                                                                                                                    66 / 123

--- Session 4, page 69 (combined page 428) ---
When to recognize a provision?





    5 The audit of the year just ended has been completed, but the audit firm’s invoice for
    £250k has not yet arrived. No — this is an accrual. The service has already been
     received and the amount is known.

    6 The company chooses not to insure its delivery fleet. No accident has occurred by the
     reporting date. No — and no liability at all. Losses are near-certain over time, but no
     past event has occurred yet.

    7 The company has contaminated land and local legislation requires it to clean it up. The
     cost is estimated at £8m, to be incurred in six years. Yes. A legal obligation arising from
    a past event →provision, measured at present value.





                                                                                                                                                    67 / 123

--- Session 4, page 70 (combined page 429) ---
Why must provisions be tied to a past event?





 ■Why must provisions be tied to a past event?

  ■Earnings smoothing: provisions are often used to smooth earnings over time, by
     recognizing a provision in a good year and releasing it in a bad year.

 ■A “cookie jar reserve”!

  ■Let’s see an example...





                                                                                                                                                    68 / 123

--- Session 4, page 71 (combined page 430) ---
Alpha plc — year 1: a very good year




  ■Alpha plc has an excellent year: operating profit of £100m. Management fears that next
    year will be much weaker...

 ■So it decides to recognize a provision of £20m for “reorganisation costs that may be
     incurred in the future”.

 ■The entry: Dr Reorganisation expense £20m   / Cr Provision £20m.

 ■Income statement: the £20m is charged as an expense →reported profit falls from
   £100m to £80m.

 ■Balance sheet: a new £20m liability appears, and retained earnings are £20m lower.

 ■Note that no cash has moved. Nothing has been “set aside”: this is purely an
    accounting entry.





                                                                                                                                                    69 / 123

--- Session 4, page 72 (combined page 431) ---
Alpha plc — year 2: the costs are actually incurred





 ■Year 2 is indeed weaker: operating profit of £60m, before any reorganisation costs.

  ■During the year, Alpha spends £12m of cash on the reorganisation.

 ■The entry: Dr Provision £12m   / Cr Cash £12m.

 ■Income statement: nothing at all. The £12m never appears as an expense →
     reported profit stays at £60m.

 ■Balance sheet: cash is £12m lower, and the provision falls from £20m to £8m.

 ■Had the provision not existed, the £12m would have been an expense and profit would
    have been only £48m.





                                                                                                                                                    70 / 123

--- Session 4, page 73 (combined page 432) ---
Alpha plc — year 3: the provision is reversed




 ■Year 3: operating profit of £70m. The reorganisation is now complete, and the
    remaining £8m will not be needed.

 ■The entry: Dr Provision £8m   / Cr Reorganisation expense £8m.

 ■Income statement: an £8m credit to expenses →reported profit rises from £70m to
    £78m.

 ■Balance sheet: the provision falls from £8m to zero, and retained earnings are £8m
     higher.

 ■The credit normally goes back to the same expense line it came from →there is often
   no visible “provision release” anywhere in the accounts.





                                                                                                                                                    71 / 123

--- Session 4, page 74 (combined page 433) ---
Alpha plc — the three years side by side





                  £ million                    Year 1   Year 2   Year 3   Total

                          Profit without the provision       100      48      70    218
                        Profit as reported               80      60      78    218

                      Provision balance at year end      20       8       –


 ■The total is identical: smoothing does not create earnings, it only moves them
    between years.
 ■But the volatility is gone: £100m / 48m / 70m becomes £80m / 60m / 78m.
 ■Hence the past event requirement: in year 1 there was no obligating event, so that
     provision could never have been recognized in the first place.





                                                                                                                                                    72 / 123

--- Session 4, page 75 (combined page 434) ---
A real example of the “cookie jar reserves”



 ■Take W.R. Grace, a large US chemicals and healthcare group, as an example.

 ■Between 1991 and 1995, its Health Care Group (the subsidiary National Medical Care)
    was growing much faster than management wanted to report...

 ■So Grace held the excess profits back in reserves that had no basis under GAAP, and
    released them in the weaker years, bringing reported earnings in line with its internal
     targets.

 ■What did investors see? A remarkably steady growth in the earnings of the Health Care
    Group.

 ■And what was actually happening? Real growth swung from a decline of 8% to an
    increase of 61%!

 ■The SEC settled proceedings with Grace in 1999: a cease-and-desist order, plus a $1m
    fund for programmes to educate the public about... reading financial statements.




                                                                                                                                                    73 / 123

--- Session 4, page 76 (combined page 435) ---
Measuring a provision




 ■The amount recognized as a provision should be the best estimate of the expenditure
     required to settle the present obligation at the reporting date.

 ■Imagine that Toyota has a warranty provision for its cars. The company estimates that
   3% of cars sold will be returned under warranty, and the average cost of repair is
    £1,000. If Toyota sold 100,000 cars, the best estimate of the provision would be:

                         100, 000 × 3% × $1, 000 = $3, 000, 000

 ■Note that: if the obligation is a large population of items (e.g., Toyota’s warranty
     obligations), the best estimate is usually the expected value (the probability-weighted
    average of all possible outcomes).





                                                                                                                                                    74 / 123

--- Session 4, page 77 (combined page 436) ---
Measuring a provision




  ■If, however, the obligation is a single item (e.g., a specific lawsuit), the best estimate is
    the most likely outcome.

 ■For example, if a company is being sued for £10m, and the lawyers believe there is a
   60% chance of losing £10m and a 40% chance of losing nothing, the best estimate of
    the provision would be:

                        Provision = $10m  (the most likely outcome)

 ■Note that: if the obligation is a single item, the expected value is not the best estimate.
         • Here, the expected value would be 0.6 × $10m + 0.4 × $0 = $6m — but that is not the
        amount we recognize.





                                                                                                                                                    75 / 123

--- Session 4, page 78 (combined page 437) ---
Time value of money and discounting





  ■If the effect of the time value of money is material, the amount of a provision should be
     the present value of the expenditures expected to be required to settle the obligation.

 ■Over time, the provision is increased to reflect the passage of time (the unwinding of the
     discount) and this increase is recognized as a finance cost.





                                                                                                                                                    76 / 123

--- Session 4, page 79 (combined page 438) ---
Time value of money and discounting - an example



  ■Imagine that Beta plc has contaminated the land around one of its plants. Legislation
     requires the company to clean it up, and the work will be done in 5 years, at an
    estimated cost of £10m.

 ■Is there a provision here? Yes! A legal obligation, arising from a past event (the
     contamination), probable and reliably estimated.

 ■So should we recognize a provision of £10m? No! Paying £10m in five years is not as
    onerous as paying it today...

 ■Using a pre-tax discount rate of 5%, the provision is the present value of that £10m:

                               $10m
                                Provision =    = $7.835m
                                           1.055

 ■The entry: Dr Clean-up expense £7.835m   / Cr Provision £7.835m.





                                                                                                                                                    77 / 123

--- Session 4, page 80 (combined page 439) ---
Time value of money and discounting - an example




 ■One year goes by. Nothing has changed about the clean-up, but the payment is now
    one year closer...

 ■So the provision must grow. This growth is the unwinding of the discount:

                           $7.835m × 5% = $0.392m

 ■The entry: Dr Finance cost £0.392m   / Cr Provision £0.392m →the provision is now
    £8.227m.

 ■Note that this is not an operating expense! It sits alongside interest, below operating
      profit.

 ■And so it goes on, year after year, until the provision reaches £10m...





                                                                                                                                                    78 / 123

--- Session 4, page 81 (combined page 440) ---
Time value of money and discounting - an example





            £ million   Opening provision   Unwinding (finance cost)   Closing provision

             Year 1                  7.835                      0.392               8.227
             Year 2                  8.227                      0.411               8.638
             Year 3                  8.638                      0.432               9.070
             Year 4                  9.070                      0.454               9.524
             Year 5                  9.524                      0.476             10.000

              Total                                              2.165


 ■In year 5, Beta pays the £10m: Dr Provision £10m   / Cr Cash £10m — and nothing
    touches the income statement.
 ■So what was the total expense? £7.835m of operating expense at the start, plus
    £2.165m of finance cost along the way = £10m.





                                                                                                                                                    79 / 123

--- Session 4, page 82 (combined page 441) ---
The recognition asymmetry





      Likelihood of the event   A possible outflow          A possible inflow

      Virtually certain           Recognize a provision            Recognize the asset
     Probable (> 50%)         Recognize a provision           Disclose only (contingent asset)
      Possible, but not probable   Disclose only (contingent liability)   Nothing
     Remote                    Nothing                          Nothing


 ■Look at the two highlighted cells: at the same probability, a possible outflow is
     recognized, but a possible inflow is only disclosed!
 ■An inflow must be virtually certain before we recognize an asset →this is
    conservatism.
 ■Note that IAS 37 only defines “probable” (> 50%). “Virtually certain” and “remote” are
    not given numerical thresholds — in practice, think of roughly 90% and above, and
   5–10% or below.



                                                                                                                                                    80 / 123

--- Session 4, page 83 (combined page 442) ---
Applications





 ■The most common applications of provisions are:
         • Warranties (assurance-type)

         • Restructuring provisions

         • Litigation provisions

         • Environmental provisions

         • Onerous contracts

         • Consideration payable to a customer (e.g., volume rebates, coupons, discounts, free
          products, etc.)





                                                                                                                                                    81 / 123

--- Session 4, page 84 (combined page 443) ---
Warranty Provisions





 ■In our session today, we saw for Toyota a “assurance-type” warranty (the standard
     type of warranties), which is recognized as a provision.
         • Toyota does not charge a premium for this warranty.

         • The warranty is not a separate performance obligation!

         • The cost of fulfilling it is recognized as a provision, and the revenue is recognized when the
          car is sold.

         • These warranties are a quality guarantee that the product was free of defects at the point of
           sale.





                                                                                                                                                    82 / 123

--- Session 4, page 85 (combined page 444) ---
Warranty Provisions





 ■In session 2, we saw what is called a “service-type” warranty for Apple (AppleCare+).
         • Apple charges a premium for this warranty, and the revenue is recognized over the life of the
          warranty.

         • The warranty is a separate performance obligation: the amount of the transaction price
          allocated to it is deferred as a contract liability, and the cost of fulfilling it is expensed as
           incurred.

         • These warranties provide a service in addition to the assurance that the product meets
           specifications.





                                                                                                                                                    83 / 123

--- Session 4, page 86 (combined page 445) ---
Litigation and Environmental Provisions





 ■When reading the notes and disclosures, analysts should be aware that:
         • The obligating event is identifiable, but the amount is really uncertain.

         • Management has a lot of discretion in estimating the provision.

         • The amounts can be material, and can have a big impact on reported earnings.

         • IAS 37 lets a company withhold details that would “prejudice seriously” its position in a
          dispute — which is exactly when the exposure is largest!

  ■Let’s see an example...





                                                                                                                                                    84 / 123

--- Session 4, page 87 (combined page 446) ---
Volkswagen — September 2015: the obligating event




 ■On 18 September 2015, the US Environmental Protection Agency issued a Notice of
    Violation of the Clean Air Act: Volkswagen had installed software that detected when a
     car was being tested, and cut emissions only during the test.

  ■Volkswagen admitted that around 11 million vehicles worldwide carried the affected
   EA 189 engines.

 ■Is there a provision here? Yes! A present obligation, from a past event (selling those
     cars), and an outflow that is clearly probable.

 ■So the hard criterion is the third one: can the amount be reliably estimated? What
     exactly does VW have to estimate?

  ■Recalls and technical fixes, vehicle buybacks, regulatory fines, criminal penalties,
    customer compensation and litigation... across dozens of jurisdictions!





                                                                                                                                                    85 / 123

--- Session 4, page 88 (combined page 447) ---
Volkswagen — 22 September 2015: the first estimate





 ■Four days after the Notice of Violation, Volkswagen announced that it planned “to set
    aside a provision of some e6.5 billion recognized in the profit and loss statement in the
     third quarter”.

 ■The entry: Dr Expense e6.5bn   / Cr Provision e6.5bn →and the quarterly result
    turned into a loss.

 ■Remember that this is the best estimate of the expenditure required to settle the
     obligation...

  ■...made four days into a crisis. How good could that estimate possibly be?





                                                                                                                                                    86 / 123

--- Session 4, page 89 (combined page 448) ---
A parenthesis...





 ■Note that the provision is reassessed at each reporting date.

 ■Each reassessment creates a new entry in the accounts, and the movement is
     disclosed in the notes.

  ■If the best estimate increases, the provision is increased and the expense is recognized
     in the income statement.

  ■If the best estimate decreases, the provision is decreased and the expense is reversed
     in the income statement.





                                                                                                                                                    87 / 123

--- Session 4, page 90 (combined page 449) ---
Volkswagen — the 2015 accounts: three months later





 ■By the time the 2015 annual accounts were published, the diesel provision had been
    reassessed and the total diesel charge recognized in the income statement had
    reached e16.2bn.

  ■This was primarily e7.8bn for field activities (recalls and service measures) and vehicle
    repurchases, plus e7.0bn for legal risks.

 ■So the estimate was two and a half times larger — and still within the same financial
     year!

 ■And it kept going: a further charge of e3.2bn in 2018 alone. By the end of 2018,
    Volkswagen reported total diesel special items of e29.0bn over four years.





                                                                                                                                                    88 / 123

--- Session 4, page 91 (combined page 450) ---
Restructuring provisions and the “big bath”





 ■Of all provisions, restructuring provisions are the classic vehicle for a “big bath”. Why?
         • Management chooses the date: the obligating event is the announcement of the
           restructuring plan, and management decides when to make it.

         • The charge is “non-recurring”: analysts routinely strip it out of underlying earnings... so it
             is a charge that “does not count”.

         •  It relieves future years twice: costs incurred later are charged against the provision (never
          touching the P&L), and anything over-provided can be released back into profit.

 ■And the incentives peak at one particular moment: when a new CEO arrives...

  ■Let’s see a real example.





                                                                                                                                                    89 / 123

--- Session 4, page 92 (combined page 451) ---
Sunbeam — 1996: the bath





 ■In July 1996, the board of Sunbeam (a US consumer appliances maker) hired Al
    Dunlap, a famous turnaround specialist, to restructure the company.

 ■At year end 1996, management created around $35m of improper restructuring and
     other “cookie jar reserves” — including $18.7m of restructuring costs that the SEC
    found did not conform to GAAP.

 ■Because of these entries, the 1996 loss was bigger.

 ■Why would a new CEO want a worse first-year result? Because 1996 was still his
    predecessors’ year — and every dollar available in a reserve would be a dollar available
     to smooth his first full year...





                                                                                                                                                    90 / 123

--- Session 4, page 93 (combined page 452) ---
Sunbeam — 1997: the release





 ■During 1997, those reserves were reversed into income, creating the picture of a
    spectacular turnaround.

 ■Sunbeam reported $189m of earnings from continuing operations for 1997 — against a
    reported net loss of $228.3m in 1996.

 ■The SEC later concluded that at least $60m of that came from accounting fraud →
    almost a third of the reported profit.





                                                                                                                                                    91 / 123

--- Session 4, page 94 (combined page 453) ---
Sunbeam — the consequences





 ■Dunlap was fired in June 1998. In May 2001, the SEC sued him, the CFO, three other
    former officers, and the external audit partner.

 ■So what should we take from this?

 ■The pattern is not unique to Sunbeam: the academic evidence finds that incoming
   CEOs systematically take large write-offs, shifting the blame for weak earnings onto
     their predecessors — and earnings rebound in the following year (Strong & Meyer,
    1987; Pourciau, 1993; Wells, 2002).

 ■So whenever you see a large restructuring charge, ask: who arrived recently, and what
    happens to that provision over the next two years?





                                                                                                                                                    92 / 123

--- Session 4, page 95 (combined page 454) ---
IFRS vs US GAAP on restructuring provisions




 ■Remember that IFRS recognizes a restructuring provision on the basis of a
    constructive obligation: a detailed formal plan, plus a valid expectation raised in those
     affected.
 ■Under US GAAP, a commitment to a plan creates no liability at all. Each cost is
    recognized only when it is incurred:
         • termination benefits, when the plan is communicated to employees;

         • contract termination costs, when the contract is terminated;

         • other associated costs, as they are incurred.

 ■So under US GAAP the expense is recognized later and in pieces.

  ■Let’s see an example...





                                                                                                                                                    93 / 123

--- Session 4, page 96 (combined page 455) ---
IFRS vs US GAAP — Gamma plc announces a restructuring




 ■Gamma plc will close a factory. The plan is detailed and its cost is evaluated at £30m:
         • Employee termination benefits: £18m (staff leave at once; no further service is required of
          them).
         • Penalty to exit a supplier contract: £8m.
         • Closure and site-clearance costs at the factory: £4m.
 ■Step 1 — 15 March: the board approves the detailed plan and announces its main
     features to the affected employees.
 ■IFRS: recognize the whole £30m now.
 ■US GAAP: this is the communication date →recognize a provision for the £18m of
     termination benefits.





                                                                                                                                                    94 / 123

--- Session 4, page 97 (combined page 456) ---
IFRS vs US GAAP — Gamma plc: the next two steps





 ■Step 2 — 30 June: Gamma formally terminates the supplier contract and pays the £8m
     penalty.
 ■IFRS: nothing new in the income statement. Dr Provision £8m   / Cr Cash £8m.
 ■US GAAP: the contract termination cost is incurred now →recognize £8m of expense.
 ■Step 3 — 30 September: the factory closes, and the £4m of closure and site-clearance
    costs are incurred.
 ■IFRS: again nothing new — the cash is charged against the provision.
 ■US GAAP: recognized as incurred →£4m of expense.





                                                                                                                                                    95 / 123

--- Session 4, page 98 (combined page 457) ---
IFRS vs US GAAP — Gamma plc: side by side





              Restructuring expense (£m)   15 March   30 June   30 September   Total

             IFRS                            30        –             –     30
          US GAAP                        18        8             4     30


 ■The total is the same. What differs is when the expense is recognized.





                                                                                                                                                    96 / 123

--- Session 4, page 99 (combined page 458) ---
Long-term contract losses





  ■Long-term contracts are many times subject to revision of estimates of expected costs
    and revenues.

 ■This may lead to worsening estimates (increase in expected costs or decrease in
    expected revenues) which must be recorded.

 ■There are two types of losses that can occur in long-term contracts...





                                                                                                                                                    97 / 123

--- Session 4, page 100 (combined page 459) ---
Long-term contract losses





   1 A temporary loss in the current period on a contract that is still profitable overall
    because costs increased.
         • The business records the change in estimates and uses the “percentage-to-completion”
        method based on the new cost estimate.

         • The adjustment flows through revenue, with no separate loss account.





                                                                                                                                                    98 / 123

--- Session 4, page 101 (combined page 460) ---
Long-term contract losses





   2  Loss in a onerous/unprofitable contract.
         • A contract is onerous when the unavoidable costs of meeting the obligation exceed the
        economic benefits expected.

         • The unavoidable costs are the minimum between (a) the costs to fulfill the contract and (b)
          the costs to exit the contract.

         • The business must recognize immediately (once the contract becomes onerous)...
                the expected loss in the income statement as an expense, under “Loss on onerous contracts”,
              and create a provision for the entire expected loss in the balance sheet (liabilities).

         • As the loss materializes through actual performance, the business “releases” the provision
          (reducing the liabilities) — so that losses are not double counted.





                                                                                                                                                    99 / 123

--- Session 4, page 102 (combined page 461) ---
Analysis of Intangibles & Provisions

--- Session 4, page 103 (combined page 462) ---
From accounting to analysis





 ■Whenever you read the statements thinking through intangibles and liabilities of a
    company, ask four questions:
           1  Is anything missing from the balance sheet?

           2  Are the numbers comparable across companies — and across IFRS and US GAAP?

           3 Where has management used discretion?

           4  Is every liability bad news?

  ■Let’s take them one at a time...





                                                                                                                                                   100 / 123

--- Session 4, page 104 (combined page 463) ---
Is anything missing from the balance sheet?


 ■Remember: research is always expensed!
 ■In practice, a pharmaceutical company expenses its development costs until approval is
     (highly) probable. So where is all that R&D?

            FY2025                         AstraZeneca  Novo Nordisk       Shell

             Market capitalization (31 Dec 2025)      $287.7bn   DKK 1,445bn   $210.8bn
               Equity (31 Dec 2025)                    $48.7bn   DKK 194.0bn   $174.4bn
              Price-to-book                          5.9×          7.4×      1.2×
          ROE (net income/ avg. equity)            22.9%         60.7%     10.1%

          R&D expensed, 2021–2025              $58.2bn   DKK 174.4bn     $5.4bn
                         . . . as % of equity                    120%         90%      3%

  ■AstraZeneca has expensed more R&D in five years than its entire book equity. It
    does report $37.8bn of intangibles... but all acquired or in-licensed. Its own
    development? “At 31 December 2025, no amounts have met the recognition criteria”.
 ■So equity is understated →price-to-book and ROE look high. How much of that is
    performance, and how much is accounting? Let’s see an example...


                                                                                                                                                   101 / 123

--- Session 4, page 105 (combined page 464) ---
Delta plc



  ■Delta plc is a pharmaceutical company. Every year, it earns an operating profit of £100m
    before R&D and, like most pharmaceutical companies, it expenses all of its R&D.

 ■For many years, Delta has spent £30m a year on R&D. Then it steps up: £45m in year
     1, and £60m a year from year 2 to year 5. In year 6, it cuts R&D back to £30m.

  ■Delta’s other net operating assets (operating assets - operating liabilities) are £200m,
    and it pays out all of its profits as dividends.

 ■So its capital employed (total assets - current liabilities) is £200m. (Ignore taxes.)
 ■As analysts, we believe that each year’s R&D creates an asset that benefits the
     following three years. So we undo the expensing:
           1  Capitalize each year’s R&D, and amortize it evenly over the next three years.
           2  Adjusted EBIT = reported EBIT + R&D expense −amortization of capitalized R&D.

           3  Adjusted capital employed = reported capital employed + unamortized R&D (the “R&D
           asset”).



                                                                                                                                                   102 / 123

--- Session 4, page 106 (combined page 465) ---
Delta plc





   £ million                           Year 0   Year 1   Year 2   Year 3   Year 4   Year 5   Year 6

  R&D spent in the year                  30      45      60      60      60      60      30
   Amortization of capitalized R&D           30      30      35      45      55      60      60

   Reported EBIT (100 −R&D spent)        70      55      40      40      40      40      70
   Adjusted EBIT (100 −amortization)       70      70      65      55      45      40      40

  R&D asset at year end                  60      75     100     115     120     120      90
   Reported capital employed (year end)     200     200     200     200     200     200     200
   Adjusted capital employed (year end)     260     275     300     315     320     320     290

   Reported ROCE                   35.0%   27.5%   20.0%   20.0%   20.0%   20.0%   35.0%
   Adjusted ROCE                   26.9%   26.2%   22.6%   17.9%   14.2%   12.5%   13.1%

 ■Year 0 is the steady state before the step-up (R&D of £30m a year for many years).
 ■ROCE = EBIT / average capital employed. For example, adjusted ROCE in year 1
   = 70/[(260 + 275)/2] = 26.2%.



                                                                                                                                                   103 / 123

--- Session 4, page 107 (combined page 466) ---
Delta plc



 ■While R&D is growing (years 1 to 4), reported EBIT is below adjusted EBIT: expensing
    charges the whole investment to the year it is made.

 ■In steady state (year 5), EBIT is £40m under both views... but reported ROCE is 20%
     against 12.5% adjusted. The R&D asset is missing from the denominator →reported
   ROCE is overstated.

 ■In year 6, Delta cuts R&D: reported EBIT jumps from £40m to £70m, and ROCE from
   20% to 35%. Did anything improve? No! Adjusted EBIT is still £40m.

 ■This is the “hidden reserve” created by conservative accounting: it builds up while
    investment grows, and it is released into earnings when investment slows down
    (Penman & Zhang, 2002).

 ■And managers know it: in a survey of more than 400 executives, 80% said they would
     cut discretionary spending (R&D, advertising, maintenance) to meet an earnings target
    (Graham, Harvey & Rajgopal, 2005).



                                                                                                                                                   104 / 123

--- Session 4, page 108 (combined page 467) ---
Are the numbers comparable? Development costs in the car industry



 ■Remember: under IFRS, development costs must be capitalized once the six criteria
    are met; under US GAAP, R&D is expensed as incurred. Let’s compare two car makers
    under each:


       FY2025                            Volkswagen   BMW       Ford     GM
                                                IFRS    IFRS  US GAAP  US GAAP

          Total R&D spent                           e19.4bn   e8.3bn      $9.4bn      $8.5bn
               . . . of which capitalized                       e9.3bn   e3.4bn          –          –
         Capitalization ratio                      48%    41%      0%      0%
         Amortization of capitalized development        e8.4bn   e2.0bn          –          –

      R&D expense in the income statement      e18.4bn   e6.8bn     $9.4bn     $8.5bn

  ■Almost half of Volkswagen’s R&D goes to the balance sheet first — for Ford and GM,
     nothing. So are their R&D expenses comparable? And their operating profits?

 Volkswagen: Automotive Division (amortization includes impairment losses). BMW: Group.



                                                                                                                                                   105 / 123

--- Session 4, page 109 (combined page 468) ---
Putting Volkswagen on a US GAAP basis



 ■To compare Volkswagen with Ford and GM, we can expense everything, just as US
   GAAP would:

       As if expensed = Reported −Capitalized development costs + Amortization



                  Volkswagen, Automotive Division (e million)      2024    2025

                    Operating result, as reported                     16,300    5,279
                   −Capitalized development costs                  (10,244)   (9,343)
            + Amortization of capitalized development costs      7,209    8,365

                  Operating result, as if all R&D were expensed    13,265    4,301
                    Difference                           −19%   −19%

 ■Or the other way round: capitalize Ford’s and GM’s R&D, as we did for Delta. What
    matters is that all companies are on the same basis.




                                                                                                                                                   106 / 123

--- Session 4, page 110 (combined page 469) ---
Knock-on effects: cash flows and EBITDA





 ■Where do capitalized development costs go in the cash flow statement?
         • In investing activities: Volkswagen reports about e9bn of “additions to capitalized
         development costs” there in 2025.

         • For Ford and GM, all R&D is paid out of operating cash flow.

         • So Volkswagen’s operating cash flow is higher just because of where the spending is
           classified.

 ■And EBITDA? It adds back amortization... so the capitalized development costs never
    reduce Volkswagen’s EBITDA, whereas every dollar of R&D reduces Ford’s.

 ■Hence: EBITDA multiples of IFRS and US GAAP car makers are not comparable.





                                                                                                                                                   107 / 123

--- Session 4, page 111 (combined page 470) ---
Red flags of discretion (to inflate earnings) in capitalization



  ■Capitalization requires judgment: when are the six criteria met? What is “directly
     attributable”? Over how many years should we amortize?


            Capitalization ratio    2019    2020    2021    2022    2023    2024    2025

           Volkswagen         36.1%   46.6%   50.3%   51.4%   51.2%   48.7%   48.1%
       BMW              33.2%   36.6%   36.5%   39.3%   33.6%   38.8%   41.2%

 ■What should make an analyst look twice?
         • A capitalization ratio drifting up (Volkswagen: from 36% in 2019 to above 50% in
         2021–2023).
         • Capitalized costs growing faster than total R&D, or than sales.
         • Longer amortization periods (check the accounting policies note).
         • Impairments of previously capitalized projects (Volkswagen, 2025: project costs at Porsche).
       Was too much capitalized?
 ■Note that: a red flag is not a proof! A rising ratio may just reflect the product cycle
    (many models close to production). Read the management report before concluding.


                                                                                                                                                   108 / 123

--- Session 4, page 112 (combined page 471) ---
Goodwill: too little, too late?




 ■Remember: goodwill is never amortized. It sits on the balance sheet until an
    impairment test says otherwise...

  ■... and that test relies on management’s own forecasts of the cash flows of the CGU,
    and on its own discount rate.

 ■What does the evidence say? Impairments tend to come late:
         • Since goodwill stopped being amortized, goodwill balances have become inflated and
         impairments untimely (Li & Sloan, 2017).

         • Many companies whose market value stays below their book value for two years in a row
                still do not impair their goodwill (Ramanna & Watts, 2012).

 ■So do not wait for the impairment; try to see it coming...





                                                                                                                                                   109 / 123

--- Session 4, page 113 (combined page 472) ---
Goodwill: what to check?





   1  Goodwill / equity: how much of the book value is goodwill?

   2  Market capitalization vs book value: if the market values the company below its book
     value, part of the goodwill is probably impaired already.

  ■Let’s see a real example...





                                                                                                                                                   110 / 123

--- Session 4, page 114 (combined page 473) ---
Bayer & the Monsanto goodwill


 ■Remember Bayer? In June 2018, it acquired Monsanto for e48.0bn and most of the
     goodwill went to Crop Science.

  e billion       Market cap.   Equity   Price-to-book   Goodwill   Goodwill /   Goodwill impaired
  31 December                                            (Group)       equity       (Crop Science)

  2018                  56.5     46.0            1.23        38.1      83%                 –
  2019                  71.5     47.3            1.51        39.1      83%                 –
  2020                  47.3     30.5            1.55        36.1      118%                   2.2
  2021                  46.2     33.0            1.40        40.1      122%                 –
  2022                  47.5     38.8            1.23        39.6      102%                   0.7
  2023                  33.0     32.9            1.00        32.3      98%                   6.7
  2024                  19.0     31.9            0.60        30.0      94%                   3.3
  2025                  36.4     25.9            1.40        28.1      108%                 –

 ■The market moved first: from e71.5bn (end of 2019) to e19.0bn (end of 2024).
  ■Impairments followed in pieces: e12.9bn of Crop Science goodwill in 2020–2024.
 ■End of 2024: a price-to-book of 0.6... and the Group’s goodwill (e30.0bn, of which
    e13.8bn in Crop Science) was still 94% of equity.


                                                                                                                                                   111 / 123

--- Session 4, page 115 (combined page 474) ---
Bayer: what did the notes say?



 ■IFRS requires an interesting disclosure for goodwill and indefinite-lived intangible
     assets: the sensitivity of the recoverable amount to changes in key assumptions.

 ■Red flag: if a small change in assumptions wipes out the headroom (difference
    between recoverable amount and carrying amount).
 ■For the Crop Science goodwill...
         • End of 2019: the recoverable amount would fall to the carrying amount if cash flows were
      8% lower, or if the discount rate (WACC) were 0.5 percentage points higher. A thin
         headroom, even while the market valued Bayer at e71.5bn!

         • End of 2024: a 10% cut in cash flows would reduce the recoverable amount by about
         e3.5bn, and a 10% higher WACC by about e3.9bn — “each of which would have led to an
           additional impairment loss on goodwill”. No headroom at all.

 ■In 2025, Bayer reversed impairments on several Crop Science intangibles (e.g., e838m
    on Soybean Seed & Traits)... but not a single euro of goodwill. Why? An impairment of
     goodwill can never be reversed!



                                                                                                                                                   112 / 123

--- Session 4, page 116 (combined page 475) ---
Reading the provisions note (IFRS)




 ■Under IFRS, companies are required to disclose a “movement” table for each class of
     provision. Under US GAAP, the disclosure is less detailed.

   1  Additions vs amounts used: over time, in a stable business, the two should be
    roughly similar.
         • Additions persistently above use →the provision is building up. A cookie jar?
         • Additions persistently below use →under-provisioning, and charges still to come (remember
         Volkswagen & the scandal with the emissions test).

   2  Reversals / reported profit: how much of this year’s profit comes from releasing past
     provisions?

   3  Provision / sales (e.g., warranty provision / sales): compare it over time and with peers.
   A falling ratio boosts margins, but is the product really getting better?





                                                                                                                                                   113 / 123

--- Session 4, page 117 (combined page 476) ---
Is every liability bad news?



 ■Remember Salesforce: unearned revenue was two thirds of its current liabilities
    ($24.3bn of $37.1bn in January 2026). Should this worry an analyst?
 ■No! This liability is very different from a bank loan:
         •  It is settled by delivering the service, not by paying cash — and delivering the service
          costs Salesforce much less than the $24.3bn.

         • Customers pay in advance and charge no interest: they are financing the business.

         • In FY2026, Salesforce’s operating cash flow was $15.0bn, against a net income of $7.5bn —
         twice as much.

 ■So when we value Salesforce, unearned revenue is not treated as debt.

  ■It tells us something about the future: today’s unearned revenue is tomorrow’s revenue.
    For Salesforce, for example, about half of the FY2026 revenue came from the unearned
    revenue balance of January 2025.





                                                                                                                                                   114 / 123

--- Session 4, page 118 (combined page 477) ---
Is every liability bad news? Billings



  ■Analysts often track billings (what was invoiced to customers in the year): Billings =
    Revenue + ∆Unearned revenue.


                     Salesforce ($ million)         FY2024   FY2025   FY2026

                   Revenue                        34,857    37,895    41,525
             + Change in unearned revenue     1,627     1,740     3,574

                        Billings                        36,484    39,635    45,099

                   Revenue growth                    –     8.7%     9.6%
                        Billings growth                    –     8.6%    13.8%

  ■Billings growing faster than revenue →more revenue in the pipeline. Good news?
    Generally speaking, yes.
 ■Note that: unearned revenue is seasonal (most annual contracts are billed in the fourth
     quarter). So, we should compare the same quarter across years.

                                                                                                                                                   116 / 123

--- Session 4, page 121 (combined page 480) ---
Key Takeaways — provisions & liabilities





 Topic            Key takeaway                                      IFRS vs US GAAP

 Constructive     A liability, just like a legal obligation (e.g., a long-standing    US GAAP: in some cases,
 obligations          policy of cleaning up any contamination).                   not a liability

 “Probable”        More likely than not (>50%), assessed for the whole class of  US GAAP: likely to occur
                        obligations.                                       (≈70%+) →IFRS
                                                                               recognizes earlier and
                                                              more often

 Restructuring     A provision for the whole plan once there is a detailed formal  US GAAP: each cost when
                     plan and a valid expectation in those affected.                incurred →later and in
                                                                               pieces (same total)

 Provisions note   A “movement” table for each class of provision: additions,    US GAAP: only for some
                  amounts used, reversals, unwinding of the discount.               liabilities (e.g., warranties,
                                                                                          restructuring)

 Why it matters     These differences move earnings, assets, liabilities and      –
                          ratios. Put companies on the same basis before comparing
                  them (remember Volkswagen vs Ford).
