                                                                                                                                                     2 / 115

--- Session 5, page 3 (combined page 492) ---
Income Taxes

--- Session 5, page 4 (combined page 493) ---
Motivation





 ■Today we will talk about accounting for income taxes...

 ■But why do we care about this?

  ■Important effect on firm’s earnings: for example the average corporate tax in 2024 was
     of 23.5% around the globe.

 ■Large variation geographically...





                                                                                                                                                     3 / 115

--- Session 5, page 5 (combined page 494) ---
Motivation





                                                                                                                                                     4 / 115

--- Session 5, page 6 (combined page 495) ---
Fundamentals



 ■Companies file income tax returns following the guideline developed by the appropriate
     tax authority.

 ■But IFRS and tax regulations often differ, creating a wedge between the tax expense
    and the tax payable.
         • This might be because the pretax financial income differs from the taxable income.

         • Common causes of this divergence in income are differences in depreciation schedules, or
          installment plans.

         •  It is also possible that some tax benefit changes the amount that is actually due to payment
           (tax payable) at a certain point in time.

 ■These wedges between the tax expense and the tax payable create assets or liabilities
     for the future, which we refer to as deferred tax asset or liability.

  ■Usually, these wedges are temporary and reflect temporal differences in the recognition
    and payment of taxes.



                                                                                                                                                     5 / 115

--- Session 5, page 7 (combined page 496) ---
Terminology




 ■From the tax rules:
         • Statutory rate: this is the tax rate set by law. It is currently 25% in the UK and 21% in the US.

         • Taxable income: Amount of the firm’s income that is subject to tax.

         • Current tax expense: tax payable on this year’s taxable income.

 ■From IFRS (or US GAAP):
         • Pre-tax (“book”) income: Income before taxes according to GAAP.

         • Tax expense: Taxes reported in the income statement.

         • Effective Tax Rate (ETR):

                                                   Tax expense
                                        Effective tax rate =
                                                        Pre-tax income





                                                                                                                                                     6 / 115

--- Session 5, page 8 (combined page 497) ---
Why the effective rate differs from the statutory rate?





 ■Here is a list of the main drivers of differences between pre-tax (book) income and
     taxable income:
         • Permanent differences between pre-tax income (book) and taxable income (more on this
             later).

         • Foreign rate differentials: profit earned in countries with lower/higher rates than the parent’s
          statuatory rate.

         • Tax credits and incentives.

         • Changes in recognized deferred tax assets (DTAs; more on this later).

 ■Note that a low ETR is not automatically good news. Ask whether it is sustainable
     (structural) or one-off.





                                                                                                                                                     7 / 115

--- Session 5, page 9 (combined page 498) ---
Example 1



 ■Suppose ABC Company reports $10 million of pre-tax income in 2012, consisting of the
     following:
         • $1 million: interest from municipal bonds (fully tax exempt);

         • $2 million: dividends from other corporations (70% tax exempt);

         • $7 million: operating income (fully taxable).

  ■Taxable income = $7 million + 30% of $2 million = $7.6 million.

  ■Current tax expense = 7.6 × 35% = $2.66 million  (35%: US federal statutory rate at
    the time).

 ■Tax expense = current tax + deferred tax = 2.66 + 0 = $2.66 million. Both differences
    are permanent, so there is no deferred tax.

  ■Effective tax rate:
                               Tax expense    2.66
                  ETR =          =    = 26.6%
                                  Pre-tax income    10



                                                                                                                                                     8 / 115

--- Session 5, page 10 (combined page 499) ---
Permanent vs temporary differences...





 ■When comparing pre-tax (book) income to taxable income, two types of differences
     arise:

           1  Permanent differences:
                Certain revenue items on the book are never taxable. (e.g. interest earned on municipal bonds)
                Certain expense items on the book are never deductible for tax purposes. (e.g. fines imposed by
                the government)

           2  Temporary differences: book income includes revenues/expenses in one period whereas
          taxable income includes them in another period.
              These create deferred tax assets and liabilities (DTA, DTL).





                                                                                                                                                     9 / 115

--- Session 5, page 11 (combined page 500) ---
Deferred Tax Liability





  ■Let’s start with DTLs...

 ■A deferred tax liability represents the increase in taxes payable in future years (an
     obligation) that results from taxable temporary differences between the tax expense
    and the tax payable at the end of the current year.

  ■Usually this arises because income is recognised in the books before it is taxed (or
    an expense is deducted for tax before it is expensed in the books).

 ■The tax expense already reflects this income, but the tax will only be payable in the
     future, creating an obligation.





                                                                                                                                                    10 / 115

--- Session 5, page 12 (combined page 501) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan





 ■For installment plans with a significant financing component (usually, more than 1 year),
        it is common that DTLs are created.

 ■This is because for these long-term installment plans, tax authorities generally only
    charge tax when the installment is actually received by the company.

 ■We will discuss short-term installment plans later.





                                                                                                                                                    11 / 115

--- Session 5, page 13 (combined page 502) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan


  ■Consider that Company X made a sale of a good in 1 January 2020 based on a
    long-term installment plan:
         • The customer pays, in total, $20,000 for the good: one installment of $8,000 in 1 January
         2021, and one installment of $12,000 in 1 January 2022.

         • The cost of capital is 10%.

         • The COGS for this item is $10,000.

         • The customer is not expected to default on any of the payments.

         • Corporate income tax is 30% and is only charged once installments are received by the
         company.

 ■How much revenue is recognized in 2020 (year-end)?

 ■We need to compute the PV of the payments...

                                      8, 000   12, 000
                   PV =    +     = 17, 190.08
                                    1.1      (1.1)2



                                                                                                                                                    12 / 115

--- Session 5, page 14 (combined page 503) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan





 ■Note that by 31 December 2020, 1 year of financing has been provided. How much
     interest income should be recognized?

 ■We need to compute the amount of interest accumulated in the principal...

                            Interest2020 = 17, 190.08 × 10% = 1, 719.01





                                                                                                                                                    13 / 115

--- Session 5, page 15 (combined page 504) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan




 ■How much taxes are expensed in the income statement?

 ■The “Pretax Income” from this sale is:

               Pretax Income = 17, 190.08 −10, 000 + 1, 719.01 = 8, 909.09

 ■Note that no installment has yet been paid, so there is still no taxes payable. We
    recognized income that has still not been taxed, so we owe taxes in the future in the
    amount of: 30% × 8, 909.09 = 2, 672.73.

 ■These taxes are recognized in 2020’s income statement, but their payment is deferred
    (DTL).

 ■What do the income statement and balance sheet look like for 2020 (year-end)?





                                                                                                                                                    14 / 115

--- Session 5, page 16 (combined page 505) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan





   1  Income Statement (2020):

 ■Revenue ....................... $17,190.08

 ■COGS ........................... $10,000

  ■Interest Income ............. $1,719.01

 ■Tax Expense ................. $2,672.73

 ■Net Income ................... $6,236.36





                                                                                                                                                    15 / 115

--- Session 5, page 17 (combined page 506) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan





   1  Balance Sheet (2020):

 ■Accounts Receivable (carried at present value) ......... $18,909.09 [$17,190.08 +
     interest]

  ■Inventory ..................................................................... ($10,000)

 ■DTL ............................................................................. $2,672.73

  ■Retained Earnings ...................................................... $6,236.36





                                                                                                                                                    16 / 115

--- Session 5, page 18 (combined page 507) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan





 ■Now suppose that at 31 December, there is a 20% chance of the customer defaulting
    on each of the installments.

 ■What changes?

 ■Would the amount of revenue recognized in 2020 change?

 ■No! But an allowance for this loss (contra-asset) would be created on the balance
     sheet...

 ■And an impairment loss of the asset “Trade Receivable” in the same amount would be
    recorded in the income statement.





                                                                                                                                                    17 / 115

--- Session 5, page 19 (combined page 508) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan





 ■At 31 December 2020, the trade receivable is worth the present value of the transaction
     plus 1 year of interest received...

                 Trade Receivable = 17, 190.08 + 1, 719.01 = 18, 909.09
                                       |present{z value}   | interest{z   }

                    Loss Allowance = 20% × 18, 909.09 = 3, 781.82





                                                                                                                                                    18 / 115

--- Session 5, page 20 (combined page 509) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan




 ■Now suppose that 31 December, there is a 20% chance of the customer defaulting on
    each of the installments.

 ■Would the amount of DTL change?

 ■Yes! The taxable base is still the same, but the receivable’s carrying amount is now
    lower (net of the allowance), so the temporary difference and the DTL shrink...

          Pretax Income = 17, 190.08 −10, 000 −3, 781.82 + 1, 719.01 = 5, 127.27

   So the tax expense is 30% × 5, 127.27 = 1, 538.18.

 ■The DTL is now recorded at $1,538.18.





                                                                                                                                                    19 / 115

--- Session 5, page 21 (combined page 510) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan





  ■Important note: in this example we only have temporary differences between taxable
    income and book income relating to the timing when we recognize income for book and
     tax purposes...

 ■In this case, the ETR and the statutory rate are equal!!!

 ■That is why in the example in the previous slides, the tax expense was exactly equal to
     pre-tax income × statutory tax rate.

 ■This is not always the case, and we will shortly see the correct method to calculating tax
     expense...





                                                                                                                                                    20 / 115

--- Session 5, page 22 (combined page 511) ---
Deferred Tax Asset (DTA)





 ■DTAs represent the increase in taxes refundable (or saved) in future years as a
     result of deductible temporary differences existing at the end of the current year.
         • DTAs can only be recorded if it is probable that this future deductible (or saved) amounts
             will be used.

         • “Probable” means that there is more than a 50% probability of realizing that deductible
         amount. (prudence principle in action!)

         •  If only some portion of this deductible amount is expected to be realized, then only this
          portion should be recognized as DTA.





                                                                                                                                                    21 / 115

--- Session 5, page 23 (combined page 512) ---
Example 3





 ■Think about a company that has a warranty obligation to a client...

 ■The company’s pretax income as of the sale will be lower than its taxable base. Why?
         • Because a warranty provision will be expensed and most tax codes don’t allow for this
         deduction in income until this cash is actually paid to the client.

 ■Can this DTA be recognized?

 ■Only if it is probable that sufficient taxable profits will be available to use the deduction.





                                                                                                                                                    22 / 115

--- Session 5, page 24 (combined page 513) ---
Example 4 - short-term installment plan



 ■For short-term installment plans (credit sales), DTAs might be created.

 ■This is because for these short-term installment plans, tax authorities generally charge
    the full amount of tax once the sale is recognized.

 ■Suppose Company X sells 10 units of a good in 1 July 2024, each for a different client.
    The total revenue is $1,000, and the total COGS is $300.
         • All of the units are sold on credit, with clients paying equal installments of $10 for 10 months,
           starting at 1 July 2024.
         • All of the 10 clients paid for the installments until December 2024.
         • The company expects 2 clients to default on the remaining amount of payment due in 2025.
         • Corporate tax is of 15%.
         • Company expects to sell 20 new units in 2025 (COGS $3 each, Price $10 each) with more
         than 50% probability.

 ■What is the DTA in 2024?



                                                                                                                                                    23 / 115

--- Session 5, page 25 (combined page 514) ---
Example 4 - short-term installment plan




  ■First, we need to calculate the amount of default expense expected for 2025...

                 2 customers × $10 × (10 months −6 months) = $80

 ■Second, we need to calculate how much tax deduction this would generate in 2025...

                           15% × 80 = $12

  ■Third, we need to ensure that this amount is only recognized in 2024 if it is probable
     that it will be deducted in 2025...
         • Because the company expects to make 20 new sales of units in 2025, the expected taxable
            profit in 2025 is: (10 −3) × 20 = 140 > 80.

 ■So a DTA of $12 can be recognized in 2024.





                                                                                                                                                    24 / 115

--- Session 5, page 26 (combined page 515) ---
Example 4 - short-term installment plan





 ■What would change if only 10 new units were expected to be sold in 2025?

 ■Only the deductible portion would be recognized as DTA (not the full $12)...

                         DTA2024 = 15% × (10 −3) × 10 = 10.50





                                                                                                                                                    25 / 115

--- Session 5, page 27 (combined page 516) ---
Example 4 - short-term installment plan



 ■In the case where we recognize the full DTA ($12), the journal entries are simply:
         • Dr DTA ... 12
         • Cr Tax expense ... 12

 ■What happens when the company will only use $10.50 of the DTA?

 ■Under IFRS:
         • Dr DTA ... 10.50
         • Cr Tax expense ... 10.50

 ■Under US GAAP:
         • Dr Gross DTA ... 12
         • Cr Valuation Allowance ... 1.50 [contra-asset account]
         • Cr Tax expense ... 10.50
         • Note that the Net DTA in the balance sheet is still worth $10.50 as under IFRS.





                                                                                                                                                    26 / 115

--- Session 5, page 28 (combined page 517) ---
DTAs - US GAAP vs IFRS




 ■For a given company, the same net DTA will be shown whether it reports under US
   GAAP or under IFRS, and this final number will involve the same managerial
     choices/level of discretion...

 ■The key advantage of US GAAP is that this discretion is more easily observed.
         • Under IFRS, you would have to work through the numbers to try and reconstruct the gross
       DTA amounts.

 ■Why is there no valuation allowance under IFRS?

 ■This is due to historical path-dependence... In 2009, IASB proposed adopting the US
    method along with a wider reform, but the draft was dropped.





                                                                                                                                                    27 / 115

--- Session 5, page 29 (combined page 518) ---
Why defer at all? Flow-through vs liability method



 ■There are potentially two ways of measuring tax expense...

  ■Flow-through method: report only the current tax expense — what is actually owed to
     the tax authority this year.

  ■Liability method: report current and deferred tax expense. Deferred tax represents tax
     to be paid (or saved) in future because of this year’s activity.
         • An increase in a DTL →deferred tax expense; an increase in a DTA →deferred tax benefit.

         • Deferred taxes arise only from temporary differences.

 ■Only the liability method is permitted under IFRS and US GAAP. Why?
           1 A DTL (DTA) meets the definition of a liability (asset): a real obligation (benefit) arising from
          past events (income/expenses already recognized).

           2  Accrual accounting: like any other cost, tax is recognised when incurred — when the income
             is earned — not when it is paid.





                                                                                                                                                    28 / 115

--- Session 5, page 30 (combined page 519) ---
Temporary Differences in Book Basis vs Tax Basis of an Asset/Liability





 ■Revenues or gains that are taxable after they are recognized in financial income create
    deferred tax liabilities.
         • Sales accounted for on accrual basis for financial reporting purposes but cash basis for tax
         purposes. (e.g. the long-term installment example).

         • Contracts accounted for under the percentage-of-completion method for financial reporting
         purposes but under the cost-recovery method for tax purposes.

         • Unrealised holding gains on investments measured at fair value through profit or loss, taxed
          only when the investment is sold.
             The same DTL arises if the gain goes to OCI (FVOCI), but the deferred tax then also goes to OCI.





                                                                                                                                                    29 / 115

--- Session 5, page 31 (combined page 520) ---
Temporary Differences in Book Basis vs Tax Basis of an Asset/Liability





 ■Expenses or losses that are deductible after they are recognized in financial income
     create a deferred tax asset.
         • For example, the ones we discussed before: warranty liabilities, bad-debt expense.

         • Unrealised holding losses on investments measured at fair value through profit or loss,
          deductible only when the investment is sold.
             The same DTA arises if the loss goes to OCI (FVOCI), but the deferred tax then also goes to OCI.





                                                                                                                                                    30 / 115

--- Session 5, page 32 (combined page 521) ---
Temporary Differences in Book Basis vs Tax Basis of an Asset/Liability





 ■Revenues or gains that are taxable before they are recognized in financial income
     create deferred tax assets.
         • The advance payment of a good or service creates a liability, but is usually immediately
          taxed.

         • The future sacrifices to provide goods or perform services that settle this liability will result in
          tax deductible future amounts.

         • For example: subscriptions received in advance.





                                                                                                                                                    31 / 115

--- Session 5, page 33 (combined page 522) ---
Temporary Differences in Book Basis vs Tax Basis of an Asset/Liability





 ■Expenses or losses that are deductible before they are recognized in financial income
     create a deferred tax liability.
         • A common case is that depreciation/amortization/depletion follows a different method for tax
         purposes than for financial reporting purposes.

         • Suppose for tax purposes an accelerated method is used versus a straight-line for financial
          reporting services.

         • Then, in the first periods, more deduction will be applied for tax purposes than for financial
          reporting purposes... This creates a DTL.





                                                                                                                                                    32 / 115

--- Session 5, page 34 (combined page 523) ---
Permanent Differences in Book Basis vs Tax Basis of an Asset/Liability





 ■Some differences are permanent between the book basis and the tax basis, and so they
   do not create neither DTLs nor DTAs.

 ■One example is charitable donations: they are recognized as an expense for financial
     reporting purposes, but sometimes not deductible for tax purposes.

  ■Another example is fines or penalties — recognized as an expense for financial
     reporting purposes, but usually not deductible for tax purposes.

 ■Yet another example is non-deductible (tax purposes) entertainment, like taking clients
     to dinner, a football match, a golf day...





                                                                                                                                                    33 / 115

--- Session 5, page 35 (combined page 524) ---
Permanent vs temporary differences





 ■The key point: permanent differences move the ETR; temporary differences do not
     (they only move the split between current and deferred).





                                                                                                                                                    34 / 115

--- Session 5, page 36 (combined page 525) ---
Expected change in future taxation





 ■Even if a company expects changes in future taxation that might affect current deferred
     taxation, this can only be accounted for financial reporting purposes if the change has
    been enacted.

 ■When a taxation change has been enacted, companies must recognize the change
    immediately in their deferred taxes as an adjustment to the tax expense in the current
     period.





                                                                                                                                                    35 / 115

--- Session 5, page 37 (combined page 526) ---
How do we compute deferred taxes?




 ■There are two ways to compute deferred taxes:
           1  The income-statement approach works from the flows: compare this year’s book income
          with this year’s taxable income.

           2  The balance-sheet approach works from the stocks: compare the carrying amount of each
         asset and liability in the books with its tax base.

 ■We will start with the most intuitive method — the income-statement approach — and
    then see where it goes wrong.

 ■The correct method is the balance-sheet approach: it is the one required by IAS 12 and
   US GAAP (ASC 740).

 ■In simple cases both methods give the same answer. They differ when tax rates change,
     or when deferred taxes arise outside the income statement.





                                                                                                                                                    36 / 115

--- Session 5, page 38 (combined page 527) ---
The income-statement approach





 ■This approach looks at this year’s gap between book income and taxable income (only
      its temporary part).

           DTLend = DTLbeg + (Book income −Taxable income)temporary × tax rate

  ■Strip out permanent differences first (e.g. tax-exempt interest): they never reverse, so
     they create no deferred tax. A DTA is simply a negative DTL.

  ■It only works if the tax rate is stable over time and if the temporary differences flow
    through the income statement (key issues: revaluation of assets through OCI and
    business combinations!).





                                                                                                                                                    37 / 115

--- Session 5, page 39 (combined page 528) ---
The income-statement approach





  ■Let’s see an example. Suppose that for financial reporting purposes, a company
    charges straight-line depreciation of £250 per year for an asset worth £1,000 with 4
    years of useful life and no residual value.

 ■For tax reporting purposes, however, the company uses the accelerated method of
     depreciation. Here is the schedule: 400 in year 1, 300 in year 2, 200 in year 3, and 100
     in year 4.

 ■The statutory tax rate is 25%.





                                                                                                                                                    38 / 115

--- Session 5, page 40 (combined page 529) ---
The income-statement approach - example





        Year   Accounting          Tax     Timing   Deferred tax  Cumulative
               depreciation   depreciation   difference      (×25%)       DTL

         1           250          400       +150         +37.5          37.5
         2           250          300        +50         +12.5          50.0
         3           250          200       −50        −12.5          37.5
         4           250          100      −150        −37.5           0.0





                                                                                                                                                    39 / 115

--- Session 5, page 41 (combined page 530) ---
The balance-sheet approach




 ■For each asset and liability, you compare its carrying amount in the books with its tax
    base.
           DTL = (Carrying amount −Tax base) × tax rate   (for an asset)

 ■Tax base of an asset/liability is the amount that tax authorities attribute to it.

 ■The deferred tax expense is then simply the change in the balance:

                         Deferred tax expense = DTLend −DTLbeg

 ■This is the method required by IAS 12 and US GAAP (ASC 740).

 ■Because it recomputes the whole balance every year, it handles tax rate changes
     automatically; it also already takes the revalued value of assets into account.





                                                                                                                                                    40 / 115

--- Session 5, page 42 (combined page 531) ---
Tax base





 ■For an asset, the tax base is the amount that will deductible for tax against the taxable
    economic benefits the asset produces when you recover its carrying amount.

 ■For example, for a sale whose revenue has been recognized but not yet taxed, COGS
    determines the taxable base.
 ■As another example, interest receivable of 100 taxed only when received in cash has a
     tax base of 0.
         • Nothing is deductible against the £100 when it comes in, so all of it will be taxed then.





                                                                                                                                                    41 / 115

--- Session 5, page 43 (combined page 532) ---
Tax base





 ■For a liability, the tax base is the carrying amount minus any amount deductible for tax
   when the liability is settled.
 ■For example, for revenue received in advance, the tax base is the carrying amount
    minus any of that revenue that won’t be taxed in future periods.
         • 600 deferred revenue, already taxed on receipt: tax base = 600 - 600 = 0.
         • 600 deferred revenue, taxed only when recognised: tax base = 600 - 0 = 600.





                                                                                                                                                    42 / 115

--- Session 5, page 44 (combined page 533) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan - revisited




  ■Consider that Company X made a sale of a good in 1 January 2020 based on a
    long-term installment plan:
         • The customer pays, in total, $20,000 for the good: one installment of $8,000 in 1 January
         2021, and one installment of $12,000 in 1 January 2022.

         • The cost of capital is 10%.

         • The COGS for this item is $10,000.

         • The customer is not expected to default on any of the payments.

         • Corporate income tax is 30% and is only charged once installments are received by the
         company.

 ■How to compute the DTL in year-end 2020?





                                                                                                                                                    43 / 115

--- Session 5, page 45 (combined page 534) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan - revisited





 ■We know that “trade receivable” is: 18,909.09 (PV 17,190.08 + interest 1,719.01).

 ■The tax base of this receivable is determined by COGS (the amount deductible for tax
     purposes...): 10,000

 ■So the DTL in year-end 2020 is: DTL2020 = (18, 909.09 −10, 000) × 30% = 2, 672.73





                                                                                                                                                    44 / 115

--- Session 5, page 46 (combined page 535) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan - revisited





 ■On 1 January 2021 the customer pays the first installment of 8,000.

 ■How much tax does the company pay in 2021 and how much does it expense?

 ■Assume that each installment is taxed net of its share of the cost, so that in 2021:
     8, 000/20, 000 × 10, 000 = 4, 000.

  ■Taxable income in 2021 is therefore 8, 000 −4, 000 = 4, 000 (the installment received
    minus its share of the cost). So the company pays taxes of 4, 000 × 30% = 1, 200.





                                                                                                                                                    45 / 115

--- Session 5, page 47 (combined page 536) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan - revisited





 ■And what is the amount of the DTL?

 ■The asset “trade receivable” is now worth 18, 909.09 −8, 000 + 1, 090.91 = 12, 000: we
    received the first installment, and the remaining balance earned 2021’s interest
     (10, 909.09 × 10% = 1, 090.91).

  ■Its tax base is 12, 000/20, 000 × 10, 000 = 6, 000...

 ■So the DTL is (12, 000 −6, 000) × 30% = 1, 800.





                                                                                                                                                    46 / 115

--- Session 5, page 48 (combined page 537) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan - revisited





 ■So what about the tax expense in the income statement?

                  Tax Expense = tax payable + DTLclosing −DTLopening

                 Tax Expense2021 = 1, 200 + 1, 800 −2, 672.73 = 327.27
  ■Intuition: of the 1,200 paid, 872.73 is tax on 2020’s profit (already expensed, held in the
    DTL); only 327.27 relates to 2021.





                                                                                                                                                    47 / 115

--- Session 5, page 49 (combined page 538) ---
Example 2 - long-term installment plan - revisited





 ■Now what about 2022 when the final installment of 12,000 is paid?

  ■Taxable income in 2022 is 12, 000 −6, 000 = 6, 000 (the installment received minus its
    share of the cost). So:

                             tax payable = 6, 000 × 30% = 1, 800

 ■The new closing balance of the DTL is 0 because there is no trade receivable anymore.
    So:
                      tax expense = tax payable + DTLclosing −DTLopening

                            tax expense = 1, 800 + 0 −1, 800 = 0

--- Session 5, page 50 (combined page 539) ---
In practice: one row per balance sheet item





  ■Deferred tax is never computed on the total. A firm builds a schedule: for every asset
    and liability, carrying amount versus tax base, difference × the rate expected on
     reversal.

 ■In the notes of the financial statements, you will find a breakdown of these deferred
     taxes.

  ■Let’s look at Unilever (note 6B, 2024 annual report)





                                                                                                                                                    49 / 115

--- Session 5, page 51 (combined page 540) ---
Example, Unilever, 1st table = roll foward

--- Session 5, page 52 (combined page 541) ---
Example, Unilever, 2nd table = breakdown DTL/DTA





                                                                                                                                                    51 / 115

--- Session 5, page 53 (combined page 542) ---
Example, Unilever





 ■The largest component in Unilever’s deferred taxes is "Goodwill and intangible assets".
 ■This holds mainly DTLs on brands that Unilever acquired through acquisition of the
     target’s shares.
 ■For book purposes, the target’s brand at acquisition is measured at fair value. For tax
    purposes, this target’s brand is recognized at its original tax value...
 ■For internally generated brands that is usually close to zero!
 ■So large DTLs are created, and they remain in the books until the brand is sold or
     impaired.





                                                                                                                                                    52 / 115

--- Session 5, page 54 (combined page 543) ---
Example, Unilever





 ■What about goodwill?
 ■For accounting purposes, companies are not allowed to recognize deferred taxes on
     goodwill upon acquisition of another business.
 ■The exception is tax-deductible goodwill! In some jurisdictions and deal structures,
     goodwill is amortized for tax purposes, but not for book purposes.
 ■This creates a temporary difference between the book value of goodwill and its tax
     value, and a DTL is booked.
 ■This DTL reverses on sale or impairment.





                                                                                                                                                    53 / 115

--- Session 5, page 55 (combined page 544) ---
Example 5 - XYZ Inc. - a DTL and a DTA over three years




  ■Let’s now put everything together now!

 ■XYZ Inc. has operating revenues of $20,000 per year and receives $5,000 per year of
     interest from municipal bonds (tax-exempt). In year 1, it buys a machine for $24,000.
         • For financial reporting: the machine is depreciated straight-line over 3 years ($8,000 per
           year), and a provision for doubtful debts of $2,000 is charged every year.

         • For tax purposes: the machine is depreciated straight-line over 2 years ($12,000 per year),
        and only actual bad debts are deductible. There are none in years 1 and 2, and $6,000 in
         year 3.

         • The statutory tax rate is 40% in all years.

 ■What are the tax expense, the tax payable and the deferred taxes in each year?





                                                                                                                                                    54 / 115

--- Session 5, page 56 (combined page 545) ---
Example 5 - XYZ Inc. - spotting the differences



 ■Before computing anything, let’s identify the differences between book income and
     taxable income...

  ■Municipal bond interest: in book income every year, but never taxed. Permanent or
    temporary?
         • Permanent! It never reverses, so it creates no deferred tax.

  ■Depreciation: the tax return deducts the machine faster ($12,000 vs $8,000 in years 1
    and 2), and then has nothing left to deduct in year 3. DTL or DTA?
         • DTL! XYZ pays less tax now and more tax in year 3. (Governments often allow faster tax
          depreciation to encourage investment.)

 ■Doubtful debts: the books expense $2,000 every year, but the tax return only deducts
    the $6,000 when the customers actually default, in year 3. DTL or DTA?
         • DTA! XYZ pays more tax now and less tax in year 3. (Tax authorities do not accept
          provisions because they are management’s estimates — remember the cookie jar reserves
         from last session.)



                                                                                                                                                    55 / 115

--- Session 5, page 57 (combined page 546) ---
Example 5 - XYZ Inc. - two sets of books




            Income statement (books)                Year 1       Year 2       Year 3

            Revenues                                20,000       20,000       20,000
                Interest from municipal bonds                 5,000        5,000        5,000
               Depreciation                                 (8,000)       (8,000)       (8,000)
               Provision for doubtful debts                   (2,000)       (2,000)       (2,000)

              Pre-tax income                           15,000       15,000       15,000


            Tax return                               Year 1       Year 2       Year 3

            Revenues                                20,000       20,000       20,000
                Interest from municipal bonds                  –           –           –
             Tax depreciation                            (12,000)      (12,000)           –
           Bad debts written off                          –           –       (6,000)

             Taxable income                            8,000        8,000       14,000
            Tax payable (current tax) @ 40%            3,200        3,200        5,600

 ■The business is exactly the same every year, but the tax bill jumps in year 3. So which
     tax number should go in the income statement?


                                                                                                                                                    56 / 115

--- Session 5, page 58 (combined page 547) ---
Example 5 - XYZ Inc. - what goes in the income statement?





                                                        Year 1   Year 2   Year 3

                    Pre-tax income                          15,000   15,000   15,000

                  Flow-through: tax expense = current tax   (3,200)   (3,200)   (5,600)
                 Net income                             11,800   11,800    9,400
                     Effective tax rate                       21.3%   21.3%   37.3%

                     Liability method: tax expense              (4,000)   (4,000)   (4,000)
                 Net income                             11,000   11,000   11,000
                     Effective tax rate                       26.7%   26.7%   26.7%

  ■Flow-through: net income falls by $2,400 in year 3, yet the business did not change.
  ■Liability method: tax expense is 40% × (15, 000 −5, 000) = 4, 000 every year. We
     strip out the permanent difference, and nothing else.
 ■ETR of 26.7% every year: below 40% only because of the tax-exempt interest
    (40% × 5, 000/15, 000 = 13.3%). Permanent differences move the ETR; temporary
     differences do not.


                                                                                                                                                    57 / 115

--- Session 5, page 59 (combined page 548) ---
Example 5 - XYZ Inc. - the income-statement approach





           From book income to taxable income             Year 1   Year 2   Year 3

               Pre-tax income (books)                           15,000   15,000   15,000
               Municipal interest (permanent)                       (5,000)   (5,000)   (5,000)
               Depreciation: + book −tax (temporary)              (4,000)   (4,000)    8,000
               Doubtful debts: + provision −write-off (temporary)    2,000    2,000   (4,000)

             Taxable income                                   8,000    8,000   14,000

               Current tax (40%× taxable income)                  3,200    3,200    5,600
              Deferred tax: depreciation (∆DTL)                   1,600    1,600   (3,200)
              Deferred tax: doubtful debts (−∆DTA)                 (800)     (800)    1,600
               Total tax expense                                 4,000    4,000    4,000

             Closing DTL (machine)                            1,600    3,200       0
             Closing DTA (receivables)                         800    1,600       0

 ■Each temporary line × 40% is that year’s deferred tax: taxable income $4,000 lower →
    $1,600 less tax today →DTL up by $1,600.
 ■Adding up the flows year after year gives the balances.


                                                                                                                                                    58 / 115

--- Session 5, page 60 (combined page 549) ---
Example 5 - XYZ Inc. - the balance-sheet approach




            Machine                                 Year 1      Year 2      Year 3

               Carrying amount (books)                    16,000       8,000          0
              Tax base                                  12,000          0          0
               Carrying amount −tax base                  4,000       8,000          0
           DTL (× 40%)                               1,600       3,200          0

             Receivables
               Carrying amount (books)∗           R −2,000  R −4,000  R −6,000
              Tax base                           R       R  R −6,000
               Carrying amount −tax base                   (2,000)      (4,000)          0
           DTA (× 40%)                              800       1,600          0

              Deferred tax expense = ∆DTL −∆DTA        800        800      (1,600)

 ∗R = receivables before the allowance and before any write-off. In year 3, the $6,000 is written off: in the books against the

 allowance, and for tax as a deduction.
 ■Same balances and same deferred tax as the income-statement approach.
 ■Why do they agree? The tax rate never changes and every difference goes through the
    income statement.

                                                                                                                                                    59 / 115

--- Session 5, page 61 (combined page 550) ---
Example 5 - XYZ Inc. - journal entries



 ■Years 1 and 2 (each year)...
         • Dr Tax expense 4,000 ; Dr DTA 800 ; Cr DTL 1,600 ; Cr Tax payable 3,200

 ■Year 3, when both differences reverse...
         • Dr Tax expense 4,000 ; Dr DTL 3,200 ; Cr DTA 1,600 ; Cr Tax payable 5,600

 ■Over the three years, total tax expense and total tax paid are the same:

                   3 × 4, 000 = 3, 200 + 3, 200 + 5, 600 = 12, 000

  ■Deferred taxes do not change how much tax is paid. They only allocate it to the year in
    which the profit is earned.

 ■And both balances are back to zero at the end of year 3: every temporary difference
    has reversed.

--- Session 5, page 62 (combined page 551) ---
Lufthansa revisited



  ■Let’s go back to Lufthansa versus British Airways (Session 3, Question 4): we restated
     Lufthansa’s financial statements as if it used BA’s depreciation policy (20 years and
   10% residual value, instead of 12 years and 15%).

 ■Back then, we booked a deferred tax liability of 722.16 and an extra tax expense of
     92.1.

 ■Now we can see exactly where these numbers come from. As in Session 3:
         • We restate the books, not the tax return: Lufthansa still pays the same taxes (231, all
           current). What changes is the tax expense, through deferred taxes.

         • The tax rate is Lufthansa’s ETR: 231/952 = 24.3%. The case does not give the statutory
           rate, so the ETR is our best estimate.

         • Alternatively, we could have checked Germany’s statutory rate in 2002 and used that instead.
           (correct approach)

                                                                                                                                                    66 / 115

--- Session 5, page 68 (combined page 557) ---
Deferred taxation and impairment




 ■In Session 4, we discussed that, before 2025, MicroStrategy was required to carry their
      digital assets at historical cost.

 ■In 2022 there was a strong decrease in the price of bitcoin, leading MicroStrategy’s
      digital assets to be impaired.

 ■For financial reporting purposes, an impairment loss was charged in the income
    statement.

 ■However, for tax purposes, impairment losses are not accounted for until they are
     realized (through the disposal of the asset).

 ■What does this mean? DTL or DTA?





                                                                                                                                                    67 / 115

--- Session 5, page 69 (combined page 558) ---
Deferred taxation and impairment





 ■DTA!

 ■The tax payable is higher than the tax expense (financial reporting) because the asset’s
     tax base is larger than its carrying amount.

 ■Under IFRS: if it is probable that the taxable income in future periods will be large
    enough to deduct the deferred taxation created by the impairment, recognize a DTA.

 ■Under US GAAP: use valuation allowance to reduce the DTA to the amount that is more
      likely than not to be realized.





                                                                                                                                                    68 / 115

--- Session 5, page 70 (combined page 559) ---
Deferred taxation and impairment



 ■This is what happened to MicroStrategy’s DTs between December 2024 and March
    2025 (in millions USD)...





 ■The DTL increased and the DTA decreased. Why?
  ■MicroStrategy switched to the fair value model and their digital assets were revalued
    upwards.
         • The previous impairment loss was reversed, and the carrying amount of the asset increased
          reversing the DTA.
         • The carrying amount of the asset is now higher than its tax base, creating a DTL.


                                                                                                                                                    69 / 115

--- Session 5, page 71 (combined page 560) ---
Net Operating Losses & loss carryforward





 ■A net operating loss (NOL) occurs for tax purposes in a year when tax-deductible
    expenses exceed taxable revenues.

 ■Under certain circumstances, tax law allows taxpayers to use losses of one year to
     offset the profits of future years.

 ■In this way, companies can use their losses to cover future taxes payable.
         • IFRS: The caveat is that companies can only recognize this future benefit if it is probable
           that they use this benefit before its expiration.

         • US GAAP: Use the valuation allowance to reduce the DTA to the amount that is more likely
         than not to be realized.





                                                                                                                                                    70 / 115

--- Session 5, page 72 (combined page 561) ---
Loss carryback





  ■Although less common, some jurisdictions allow taxpayers to carry back losses to offset
     profits of prior years.

 ■This generates an immediate refund of taxes paid in prior years.

 ■Note that there is an accounting difference between a carryback and a carryforward:
         • A carryback generates an immediate refund, a tax receivable, recognised in full. No
           recoverability test.

         • A carryforward generates a DTA, recognised only to the extent future taxable profit is
          probable.





                                                                                                                                                    71 / 115

--- Session 5, page 73 (combined page 562) ---
Carryback and carryforward rules vary by jurisdiction



 ■US (post-2017): no carryback for ordinary NOLs; carryforward indefinite but usable
     against only 80% of taxable income. (The 2020 CARES Act briefly revived a 5-year
    carryback for 2018–2020 losses; that has expired.)
 ■UK: 1-year carryback (temporarily extended to 3 years for 2020–2022); carryforward
     indefinite but restricted to 50% of profits above a £5m allowance.
 ■Germany: 2-year carryback (since 2022), capped at e1m and for corporate income tax
     only (not trade tax); carryforward indefinite but restricted to e1m plus 70% of income
    above it (2024–2027; 60% from 2028, and always 60% for trade tax).
 ■France: optional 1-year carryback, capped at the lower of e1m and the prior year’s
      profit — it creates a tax credit, used against tax over 5 years and refunded if unused;
     carryforward indefinite but restricted to e1m plus 50% of profits above it.
 ■Canada: 3-year carryback and 20-year carryforward, with no annual cap.

 Rules for corporations as of 2026. Sources: PwC Worldwide Tax Summaries; Tax Foundation (2026).

--- Session 5, page 74 (combined page 563) ---

  ■Let’s see an example...

 ■Suppose that in 2022 a company experiences a net operating loss of $200,000 and
    takes advantage of the carryforward provision.

 ■The corporate income tax is 20%.

 ■Assume the company expects a taxable income of $250,000 in 2023.

 ■What does this company’s income statement look like in 2022?
         • Operating profit (loss) before income taxes .......... $(200,000)
         • Income tax expense (benefit)
                Deferred ................................................................. $(40,000)

         • Net Income ........................................................... $(160,000)





                                                                                                                                                    73 / 115

--- Session 5, page 75 (combined page 564) ---
Example 6




 ■What does this company’s income statement look like in 2023?
         • Operating profit (loss) before income taxes .......... $250,000
         • Income tax expense (benefit)
                Current ................................................................. $10,000
                Deferred ................................................................. $40,000

         • Net Income ........................................................... $200,000

 ■In words:
         • Current tax payable is $10,000 because the company uses the DTA of $40,000 from last
          period to offset part of the taxes (total taxes: 20% × 250, 000 = 50, 000).

         • The DTA asset account (worth $40,000 last period) is reduced to zero — thus there is an
        expense in deferred taxes (negative change in DTA) of $40,000.





                                                                                                                                                    74 / 115

--- Session 5, page 76 (combined page 565) ---
Example 6 - what if we had not recognised the DTA?





 ■Now suppose that, at the end of 2022, management judges that future taxable profit is
    not probable. So no DTA is recognised.

 ■What does this company’s income statement look like in 2022?
         • Operating profit (loss) before income taxes .......... $(200,000)

         • Income tax expense (benefit) ........................... $0

         • Net Income ........................................................... $(200,000)

 ■The full loss hits net income: there is no tax benefit to soften it.





                                                                                                                                                    75 / 115

--- Session 5, page 77 (combined page 566) ---
Example 6 - what if we had not recognised the DTA?




 ■But 2023 turns out to be a good year after all: a taxable income of $250,000 (before
    using the loss). What does the income statement look like in 2023?
         • Operating profit (loss) before income taxes .......... $250,000
         • Income tax expense (benefit)
                Current ................................................................. $10,000
                Deferred ................................................................. $0

         • Net Income ........................................................... $240,000

 ■In words:
         • Current tax is still $10,000: for tax purposes, the loss shelters 2023’s profit whether or not
       we recognised a DTA: 20% × (250,000 −200,000) = 10,000.

         • But there is no DTA to reverse, so there is no deferred tax expense.





                                                                                                                                                    76 / 115

--- Session 5, page 78 (combined page 567) ---
Example 6 - what if we had not recognised the DTA?





                                         Recognise in 2022  Do not recognise
                                               (Example 6)

                 2022 tax expense (benefit)               (40,000)                –
                 2022 net income (loss)                (160,000)           (200,000)

                 2023 tax expense                       50,000             10,000
                 2023 net income                     200,000            240,000

                 Two-year total net income              40,000             40,000
                 2023 effective tax rate                20%           4%

 ■Now 2023 looks even better: net income is 20% higher and the ETR is only 4%.





                                                                                                                                                    77 / 115

--- Session 5, page 79 (combined page 568) ---
Recognition timing as an earnings lever



 ■So when the DTA is recognised is not neutral: it moves earnings from one year to
     another.

 ■Can management simply choose?

 ■Not officially! Under IFRS, a DTA on a tax loss must be recognised if future taxable
      profit is probable (IAS 12.34).

 ■But “probable” is a judgement... And after a loss, IAS 12.35 asks for convincing
    evidence of future profits. So in a bad year, not recognising the DTA is easy to justify.

 ■The judgement is reassessed every year (IAS 12.37): once future profits look probable,
    the DTA is recognised and the benefit lands in that year.

 ■Under US GAAP, this same judgement/discretion happens through the valuation
    allowance.



                                                                                                                                                    78 / 115

--- Session 5, page 80 (combined page 569) ---
Recognition timing as an earnings lever





 ■Why would management not recognise the DTA?
 ■A “big bath”: a company already reporting a large loss has little to lose from a slightly
     larger one. The benefit is banked for a year when earnings matter more (our 2023:
    $240,000 instead of $200,000).
 ■Why would management recognise it straight away?
 ■To increase capital (the DTA increases equity), or to offset a large charge already
    being taken (e.g. a restructuring).





                                                                                                                                                    79 / 115

--- Session 5, page 81 (combined page 570) ---
Example 6 - alternative!



 ■Suppose now that the tax law also allows losses to be carried back one year.

 ■In 2022 the company experiences a net operating loss of $200,000.

 ■In 2021 the company was profitable: it had a taxable income of $150,000, on which it
    paid $30,000 of taxes.

 ■The corporate income tax is 20% (in all years).

 ■Assume the company expects a taxable income of $250,000 in 2023.

 ■What changes now?

 ■The company first carries the loss back against 2021’s profit, and gets back taxes it has
    already paid.

 ■Only what is left of the loss is carried forward.




                                                                                                                                                    80 / 115

--- Session 5, page 82 (combined page 571) ---
Example 6 - alternative!




 ■How is the $200,000 loss used?
         • Carried back: $150,000 (all of 2021’s taxable income) →refund of
       20% × 150,000 = $30,000. This is a tax receivable, recognised in full.
         • Carried forward: 200,000 −150,000 = $50,000 →DTA of 20% × 50,000 = $10,000.
         Recognised only because a taxable income of $250,000 in 2023 is probable.

 ■What does this company’s income statement look like in 2022?
         • Operating profit (loss) before income taxes .......... $(200,000)
         • Income tax expense (benefit)
                Current ................................................................. $(30,000)
                Deferred ................................................................. $(10,000)

         • Net Income ........................................................... $(160,000)

 The refund is tax paid in 2021, so it is computed at 2021’s rate; the DTA uses the rate expected when the loss is used (2023). Here,

 both are 20%.





                                                                                                                                                    81 / 115

--- Session 5, page 83 (combined page 572) ---
Example 6 - alternative!



 ■What does this company’s income statement look like in 2023?
         • Operating profit (loss) before income taxes .......... $250,000
         • Income tax expense (benefit)
                Current ................................................................. $40,000
                Deferred ................................................................. $10,000

         • Net Income ........................................................... $200,000

 ■In words:
         • Current tax payable is $40,000 because only $50,000 of the loss is left to offset 2023’s profit:
       20% × (250,000 −50,000) = 40,000.

         • The DTA (worth $10,000 last period) is reduced to zero — thus there is an expense in
          deferred taxes of $10,000.

         • The $30,000 refund is not in 2023’s income statement: it was already recognised in 2022.
       When it is received, it is just cash (Dr Cash ; Cr Tax receivable).





                                                                                                                                                    82 / 115

--- Session 5, page 84 (combined page 573) ---
Example 6 vs Example 6 - alternative!





                                                 Example 6        Alternative
                                                  (carryforward only)   (carryback first)

              2022 current tax expense (benefit)                   –           (30,000)
              2022 deferred tax expense (benefit)               (40,000)           (10,000)
              2022 net income (loss)                        (160,000)          (160,000)

               Tax receivable, 31 Dec 2022                        –            30,000
              DTA, 31 Dec 2022                              40,000            10,000

              2023 current tax expense                        10,000            40,000
              2023 deferred tax expense                      40,000            10,000
              2023 net income                             200,000          200,000

 ■Same net income in both years! So what changed?
 ■The asset behind the benefit.





                                                                                                                                                    83 / 115

--- Session 5, page 85 (combined page 574) ---
Example 6 vs Example 6 - alternative!





 ■What if a taxable income in 2023 were not probable?
         • Example 6: no DTA can be recognised →2022 net loss of $(200,000).

         • Alternative: no DTA on the $50,000 carried forward, but the $30,000 refund is still
         recognised →2022 net loss of $(170,000).

 ■The cash from the refund comes back immediately, exactly when the company is losing
    money...

 ■That is why governments extended carrybacks during COVID (the CARES Act in the
    US, the temporary 3-year carryback in the UK).





                                                                                                                                                    84 / 115

--- Session 5, page 86 (combined page 575) ---
Why choose a carryforward over a carryback?





 ■Given the choice, which one would you pick?

  ■Usually the carryback! The refund is:
         • Cash now, not when (and if) profits come back;

         • The cash comes in exactly when the company is losing money.

 ■So why would a company carry forward instead? Three reasons...
           1  The tax rate is expected to rise.

           2  The carryback may crowd out tax credits used in the prior year.

           3  The company may want to keep control over when the benefit shows up in earnings.





                                                                                                                                                    85 / 115

--- Session 5, page 87 (combined page 576) ---
The three parts of the income tax footnote





 ■Under both IFRS and US GAAP, the income tax note has three parts.
           1  Tax expense summary: the tax expense in the income statement, split into current and
          deferred. (US companies also split it into federal, state and foreign.)

           2  DTA/DTL summary: the temporary differences and carryforwards that have not yet reversed
           at the balance sheet date, by source. Under US GAAP, the DTAs are shown gross, with the
          valuation allowance separately.

           3  Rate reconciliation: starts at the statutory rate and walks, line by line, to the ETR.

  ■Let’s now go through all three for Walmart’s latest annual report (fiscal year to 31
    January 2026).





                                                                                                                                                    86 / 115

--- Session 5, page 88 (combined page 577) ---
Walmart FY2026 - 1. tax expense summary





  ■“Provision” here is just a naming convention in the US: it is the total tax expense, not a
    cash reserve.

Walmart FY2026 - 2. DTA/DTL summary





 ■Net DTL is the value of DTL minus the value of DTAs, net of valuation allowances.





                                                                                                                                                    89 / 115

--- Session 5, page 91 (combined page 580) ---

 ■The largest DTL is property and equipment, which grew by $1,819m in a single year.
   We will come back to this in a minute...
 ■Under US GAAP we can see the valuation allowance: $4.4bn, almost as large as the
     loss and tax credit carryforwards ($4.6bn). What does that suggest?
 ■That Walmart does not expect to use most of its carryforwards. Under IFRS, we would
     only see the net amount.
