                                                                                                                                                    92 / 115

--- Session 5, page 94 (combined page 583) ---
Backing out data: inferring tax depreciation




 ■The tax footnote also lets you recover numbers the company never discloses... For
    example: how much depreciation does Walmart deduct for tax?

 ■Remember the income-statement approach, applied to property and equipment:

           DTLend = DTLbeg + (Tax depreciation −Book depreciation) × tax rate

  ■Rearranging:

                                                    DTLend −DTLbeg
                Tax depreciation = Book depreciation +
                                                                tax rate

 ■This income-statement approach only works if the tax rate does not change, and the
   DTL moves only because of depreciation (no acquisitions, disposals or revaluations).





                                                                                                                                                    93 / 115

--- Session 5, page 95 (combined page 584) ---
Backing out data: Walmart FY2026




 ■From the tax footnote: the DTL on property and equipment goes from $4,303m to
    $6,122m (up $1,819m).
 ■From the cash flow statement: depreciation and amortization of $14,203m.
 ■Tax rate: 21% federal + 2.6% state and local = 23.6% (from the rate reconciliation).
 ■So: tax depreciation ≈14,203 + 1,819/0.236 = 14,203 + 7,708 ≈$21.9bn. More than
   50% above book depreciation!
 ■Why? Walmart keeps investing (gross property and equipment up from $231.6bn to
    $256.4bn), and since 2025 new assets can be deducted immediately for tax (100%
    bonus depreciation).

 This is an approximation: D&A also includes some amortization, and part of the DTL relates to non-US assets taxed at other rates.





                                                                                                                                                    94 / 115

--- Session 5, page 96 (combined page 585) ---
Case -----   : ?Deferred Taxes and the Valuation
     Allowance at Lucent Technologies?

--- Session 5, page 97 (combined page 586) ---
Reference





 ■These slides are based on the Harvard Business School case “Deferred Taxes and the
    Valuation Allowance at Lucent Technologies, Inc.” (A) by Gregory S. Miller and Jacob
    Cohen.





                                                                                                                                                    95 / 115

--- Session 5, page 98 (combined page 587) ---
Setting





 ■What is the setting of the case?
         • The case is set in July 2002. Lucent is closing its third fiscal quarter (to 30 June 2002).

         • The preliminary results show another losing quarter, and the short-term outlook for the
         telecom industry remains weak.

         • At the last year end (September 2001), Lucent’s balance sheet carried $7.7bn of deferred
          tax assets, net of a valuation allowance of only $742m...

         • Before releasing the results, their management has to decide whether Lucent should
          increase its valuation allowance.





                                                                                                                                                    96 / 115

--- Session 5, page 99 (combined page 588) ---
Lucent, in a nutshell




 ■Lucent was formed from AT&T’s systems and technology units (including the famous
     Bell Labs) and was spun off from AT&T in 1996.

  ■It became a leading provider of communications networks (systems, services and
     software) to the world’s largest telecom service providers. Competitors included Alcatel,
     Cisco, Ericsson, Motorola and Nortel.

 ■Key point: Lucent’s sales depend on its customers’ capital spending. So if telecom
    operators stop investing, Lucent stops selling...

 ■And after 2000, the telecom bubble burst, many of the newer operators went bankrupt,
    and the large ones consolidated (and gained buying power).

  ■Lucent’s share price fell from above $80 at the end of 1999 to $3.50 by March 2002.





                                                                                                                                                    97 / 115

--- Session 5, page 100 (combined page 589) ---
The problem: current outlook of the income statement





 $ million, year to 30 Sep.       2001   2000   1999      ■What jumps out?

 Net sales                     21,294  28,904  26,993      ■In a single year, sales fall by 26%,
 Gross margin                   2,058  11,714  12,969
                                                         the gross margin falls from 40.5%   as % of sales               9.7%  40.5%  48.0%
 Restructuring and impairment  (10,157)      –      –            to 9.7%, and Lucent takes
 Operating income (loss)        (19,029)   2,366   3,786          $10.2bn of restructuring and
 Income (loss) before taxes∗    (19,904)   2,357   3,825          impairment charges.
 Provision (benefit) for taxes      (5,734)    924   1,456
 Effective tax rate              28.8%  39.2%  38.1%       ■Result: a pre-tax loss of $19.9bn,
                                               and a net loss of $16.2bn.
 Net income (loss)             (16,198)   1,219   4,789
∗Continuing operations. Source: case Exhibit 1.           ■A benefit of $5.7bn softens the
                                                                loss. (more on this soon)





                                                                                                                                                     98 / 115

--- Session 5, page 101 (combined page 590) ---
The problem: current outlook of the income statement (cont.)





 ■Is 2001 a one-off? Unfortunately, it does not look like it...
         • The losses continue in fiscal 2002: $423m in the first quarter, $495m in the second, and the
          preliminary third-quarter results show another loss.

         • Customers face tight capital-spending budgets, and several of them have gone bankrupt.

         • Lucent competes with large, established rivals for the little business that is left and with new
          entrants that compete on price.

  ■Lucent has been restructuring since January 2001: cutting staff, centralising functions,
     selling divisions that do not fit, and outsourcing manufacturing to cut fixed costs.

 ■But management still expects Lucent to be profitable by fiscal 2003...





                                                                                                                                                    99 / 115

--- Session 5, page 102 (combined page 591) ---
The problem: where does the tax benefit come from?





 $ million                     2001   2000     ■The whole benefit is deferred: the losses
 Income (loss) before taxes      (19,904)   2,357        and charges of 2001 create DTAs.
 Current tax expense              201    433     ■And where do these DTAs come from?
 Deferred tax expense (benefit)   (5,935)    491           Let’s look at their breakdown...
 Total tax expense (benefit)     (5,734)    924

 Effective tax rate              28.8%  39.2%

Continuing operations. Source: case Exhibit 2.





                                                                                                                                                    100 / 115

--- Session 5, page 103 (combined page 592) ---
The problem: deferred taxes and the valuation allowance





 DTAs, $ million                         2001    2000          DTLs, $ million               2001    2000

 Bad-debt and customer financing reserves   1,004      82           Pension                       1,971   2,480
 Inventory reserves                        685     314            Property, plant and equipment       5     417
 Business restructuring reserves             632       –           Other                        521     734
 Other operating reserves                  536     407            Total DTLs                    2,497   3,631
 Postretirement and other benefits            2,386   2,352
 Loss and credit carryforwards            2,538     240          Net DTA (DTL)                 5,178     (69)
 Other                                  636     364                                                          30 September. Source: case Exhibit 2.
 Total DTAs (gross)                       8,417   3,759
 Valuation allowance                        (742)    (197)
 DTAs, net                                7,675   3,562

 ■Gross DTAs more than double in one year ($3.8bn →$8.4bn). What drives this?
 ■The 2001 losses themselves (carryforwards: $0.2bn →$2.5bn), and the reserves for
    bad debts, inventory and restructuring: customers going bankrupt, unsold inventory,
      layoffs... All expensed in the books, but deductible for tax only when paid or written off.
  ■Meanwhile, the DTLs fall. Overall, Lucent goes from a net DTL of $69m to a net DTA of
    $5.2bn!


                                                                                                                                                   101 / 115

--- Session 5, page 104 (combined page 593) ---
The problem: deferred taxes and the valuation allowance (cont.)





 ■A net DTA of $5.2bn is almost half of Lucent’s shareholders’ equity ($11.0bn)!

 ■But a DTA is only an asset if Lucent has future taxable income to use it against. How
   much does it need?

 ■At a 35% tax rate, $5.2bn of tax savings requires roughly $15bn of future taxable
    income. In its best recent year (1999), Lucent earned $3.8bn before taxes...

 ■In 2001, the valuation allowance was only $742m meaning that management concluded
     that the rest of the DTAs would more likely than not be realised. Is this still credible in
     July 2002?





                                                                                                                                                   102 / 115

--- Session 5, page 105 (combined page 594) ---
Should Lucent increase its valuation allowance?




 ■Should Lucent increase its valuation allowance? If so, by how much?
 ■The case for a small increase:
         • management expects to be profitable by fiscal 2003, and the restructuring is cutting fixed
          costs;

         • most carryforwards do not expire until after 2019: almost two decades to use them;
         • the allowance already rose almost four times in 2001 ($197m →$742m), while gross DTAs
          only doubled.
 ■The case for a large increase:
         • cumulative losses are objective evidence;

         • the losses keep coming, and nobody knows when telecom operators will start investing
          again.





                                                                                                                                                   103 / 115

--- Session 5, page 106 (combined page 595) ---
Should Lucent increase its valuation allowance? (cont.)




 ■How would an increase in the valuation allowance affect Lucent’s third-quarter financial
    statements?
         • Dr Deferred tax expense ; Cr Valuation allowance (a contra-asset).
         • Income statement: pre-tax income does not change, but the tax expense increases →a
           larger net loss.

         • Balance sheet: net DTAs and shareholders’ equity fall by the same amount.

         • Cash flow statement: nothing! It is a non-cash charge, added back in operating cash flows.

 ■What are the incentives?
         • The quarter is already a loss, so a “big bath” now means that future profits will come with
         almost no tax expense.

         • But a large charge may look like admitting that Lucent will not be profitable for years to come.





                                                                                                                                                   104 / 115

--- Session 5, page 107 (combined page 596) ---
The solution



 ■On 23 July 2002, Lucent took a non-cash charge of $5.83bn to provide a full valuation
    allowance on its remaining net DTAs (i.e. 100% of the DTAs).

 ■By year end (30 September 2002), the valuation allowance had grown to $10.0bn (from
   $742m a year earlier).

 ■In the earnings conference call, Lucent’s CFO said this reflected the continuing decline,
     uncertainty and lack of visibility in the telecom market, a significant drop in revenues,
    and the need for further restructuring actions to drive profitability.

 ■Does this mean that Lucent lost these assets?
         • No! The tax losses are still there for tax purposes. So when Lucent makes taxable profits
          again, it will use them to pay less tax.

         • And after a period of sustained profitability, Lucent would be able to reverse the valuation
         allowance and recognise the DTAs again.





                                                                                                                                                   105 / 115

--- Session 5, page 108 (combined page 597) ---
How’s Lucent doing now?

                               (loss)  allowance            close: by October 2002 the share price

 2002         12,321      (11,753)       9,989              until 2003.

                                 ■So, did Lucent reverse the valuation

                                       was still above $7bn in 2006.





                                                                                                                                                    106 / 115

--- Session 5, page 109 (combined page 598) ---
How’s Lucent doing now? (cont.)




 ■Why keep the allowance after returning to profit?
         • Much of the profit came from a pension credit that is not taxable ($868m in fiscal 2004) and
         from outside the US.

         • Even after leaving its cumulative loss position (in fiscal 2005), Lucent concluded that there
        was “not sufficient positive evidence” to release the allowance on its US DTAs.

 ■The largest reversal ($816m, fiscal 2004) actually came from a loss carryback: the IRS
    agreed that Lucent could carry its 2001 loss back to 1996, when it was still part of AT&T.

 ■In November 2006, Lucent merged with Alcatel. Under IFRS, Alcatel-Lucent recognised
      its US DTAs only gradually, as US earnings improved (e1.9bn by 2015).

 ■In 2016, Nokia bought Alcatel-Lucent — including Bell Labs, today Nokia Bell Labs.





                                                                                                                                                   107 / 115

--- Session 5, page 110 (combined page 599) ---
Analysis of Deferred Taxes

--- Session 5, page 111 (combined page 600) ---
What about releasing the allowance?





 ■We just saw how Lucent increased its valuation allowance in 2002. What about the
     opposite: releasing it?

  ■Let’s look at Ford...





                                                                                                                                                   108 / 115

--- Session 5, page 112 (combined page 601) ---
Ford in 2011: releasing the valuation allowance




 ■In 2011, Ford reports net income of $20.2bn, an exciting result! It was more than three
    times 2010’s $6.6bn!

  ■... but look at the income before taxes: $8.7bn in 2011, against $7.1bn in 2010. “Only” a
   21% increase.

 ■Why the difference? Taxes!


                 $ million                  2010    2011  Change

                  Net income                 6,561   20,213   +208%
                  Income before income taxes   7,149    8,681    +21%





                                                                                                                                                   109 / 115

--- Session 5, page 113 (combined page 602) ---
Ford in 2011: the tax footnote





             $ million                                  2010     2011

             Income before income taxes (excluding affiliates)   6,611     8,181

               Current tax expense                           215      270
               Deferred tax expense (benefit)                   377   (11,811)
                Total tax expense (benefit)                    592   (11,541)

  ■Current tax expense is only about 3.3% of income before taxes (270/8,181).

 ■And deferred tax is a benefit of $11.8bn. This is more than Ford’s entire 2011 income
    before taxes!!!

 ■Where does it come from?





                                                                                                                                                   110 / 115

--- Session 5, page 114 (combined page 603) ---
Ford in 2011: why?





 ■From Ford’s 2011 10-K...

     “At the end of 2011, our U.S. operations had returned to a position of cumulative profits for the
    most recent three-year period. We concluded that this record of cumulative profitability in recent
     years, our ten consecutive quarters of pre-tax operating profits, our successful completion of
     labor negotiations with the UAW, and our business plan showing continued profitability, provide
     assurance that our future tax benefits more likely than not will be realized. Accordingly, at
     year-end 2011, we released almost all of our valuation allowance against net deferred tax assets
      for entities in the United States, Canada, and Spain.”

 ■The valuation allowance had been created in the loss years, and releasing it essentially
     transfered profits from those previous years into 2011.





                                                                                                                                                   111 / 115

--- Session 5, page 115 (combined page 604) ---
The valuation allowance as a discretion account





 ■“More likely than not” is a management estimate and changes in the valuation
    allowance go straight to the tax expense. So the allowance gives managers substantial
     discretion...

 ■How could a manager use it?
         • By not providing an allowance in a year of poor “true” earnings →report higher net income.
         • By providing an allowance in a year of good “true” earnings →report lower net income.
         • By over-providing the allowance →shift earnings from good years to bad years.
         • And when DTAs covered by an allowance are finally realised →higher net income. (this is
         what Ford did in 2011)





                                                                                                                                                   112 / 115

--- Session 5, page 116 (combined page 605) ---
Will they reverse? Deferred taxes as equity




 ■The key question for an analyst: will the DTAs and DTLs reverse in the future? That is,
      will they ever have cash consequences?

  ■If not, they are not really an asset or a liability. For analysis, treat them as part of equity:
         • a DTA that will never be realised reduces shareholders’ equity;

         • a DTL that will never be paid increases shareholders’ equity.

  ■This adjustment matters for forecasting cash flows and for valuation because these
    DTAs/DTLs can be large.

  ■Analysts need to look at each component of the DTLs (and DTAs) and decide, case by
    case, whether it is likely to reverse. Factors to consider (future tax rates, tax laws and
    accounting standards; the firm’s growth rate...)

                                                                                                                                                     3 / 116

--- Session 6, page 5 (combined page 615) ---
Accounting for investments: US GAAP vs IFRS





 ■There has been convergence in fair value measurement of financial assets between
    IFRS and US GAAP.

 ■Most of what we learn in this session under IFRS largely translates to US GAAP.





                                                                                                                                                     4 / 116

--- Session 6, page 6 (combined page 616) ---
Accounting for investments





 ■Two criteria determine how financial assets should be classified and measured...


   1 What is the company’s business model for managing its financial asset?
         • Intention of trading versus holding the asset?

   2 What are the contractual cash flow characteristics of the financial investment?
         • Are the cash flows only determined by the repayment of principal and interest?

         • For example: debt versus equity.





                                                                                                                                                     5 / 116

--- Session 6, page 7 (combined page 617) ---
Debt Investments

--- Session 6, page 8 (combined page 618) ---
Definition



 ■Debt securities represent a creditor relationship with another company.
         • Government securities, municipal securities, company bonds, convertible debt, and
         commercial paper.

  ■Accounting for debt depends on the nature of the company’s business model for
    managing this asset.
           1  Held-for-collection: the debt security is held in order to collect the contractual cash flows.

           2  Held-for-collection and selling: the debt security is held with the objective of both
           collecting contractual cash flows and selling.

           3  Trading: a debt security not held for collection or collection and selling; the security is
         bought and held primarily for sale in the near term to generate income on temporary price
           differences.

  ■Decisions about accounting method/business model are taken at the portfolio level —
    each debt portfolio can be accounted for in a different way if managed in a different way.




                                                                                                                                                     6 / 116

--- Session 6, page 9 (combined page 619) ---
Summary





                                                                                                                                                     7 / 116

--- Session 6, page 10 (combined page 620) ---
Debt held-for-collection




 ■Debt investments that are only held-for-collection are measured using amortized
    cost.

  ■Intuition: using fair value in this situation would distort the measurement of the asset.
         • Because the business is holding the asset for collection only the cost of the asset (payment
           at acquisition) and interest paid matter, not the market value of the asset.

 ■A debt investment measured at amortized cost is carried on the balance sheet at its
      initial amount adjusted over time using the effective-interest method.
         •  Initial amount = fair value plus directly attributable transaction costs

 ■The interest revenue recognized in the income statement is calculated based on the
     effective interest rate.





                                                                                                                                                     8 / 116

--- Session 6, page 11 (combined page 621) ---
Effective interest method





 ■Companies must amortize premiums or discounts on bonds using the effective-interest
    method.
         • A bond is bought at a premium (discount) if you pay more (less) for it today than its par/face
          value.

 ■This method allocates the interest income at a constant periodic rate on the opening
     carrying amount of the security each period.

 ■This constant periodic rate (the effective interest rate) is the rate that equates the
    present value of the instrument’s expected future cash flows to its initial carrying
    amount.





                                                                                                                                                     9 / 116

--- Session 6, page 12 (combined page 622) ---
Example


 ■Assume that Robinson SA purchased e100,000 of 8% bonds of Evermaster AG on
    January 1 2025, at a discount, paying e92,278.

 ■Bonds mature January 1 2030 (5 years) and yield 10%.

  ■Interest is payable each July 1 and January 1.

 ■What is the initial amount for this investment (if we recorded it at 1 January 2025)?
         • The amount paid for the asset: e92,278.

         • Debit “debt investments” and credit “cash”.

 ■What is the effective interest rate we should use for amortization?
         • The yield: 10% (5% per half year period).

         •  It is the rate that equates the present value of all the bond’s cash flows to the price paid:

                                                            10
                                                        4, 000   100, 000
                                     92, 278 = X    +
                                                        1.05t     1.0510
                                                         t=1


                                                                                                                                                    10 / 116

--- Session 6, page 13 (combined page 623) ---
Example




 ■By July 1 2025, Robinson receives an interest payment (cash) in the amount of

                                1
                     8% ×  × 100, 000 = 4, 000
                                2

 ■But how much is the actual interest revenue it should consider given the effective
     interest method?
                                 1
                       10% ×  × 92, 278 = 4, 614
                                 2
 ■This means that, in this first half year, there was an amortization of e614, so the
    amortized value of the bond (its carrying amount by July 1 2025) is...

                                  92, 278 + 614 = 92, 892

--- Session 6, page 14 (combined page 624) ---
Example




 ■By December 31 2025, Robinson accrues an interest receivable in the amount of
    e4,000 (it will be paid only on 1 January 2026).

 ■But how much is the actual interest revenue it should consider given the effective
     interest method?
                                 1
                       10% ×  × 92, 892 = 4, 645
                                 2
 ■This means that, in this second half year, there was an amortization of e645, so the
    amortized value of the bond (its carrying amount by December 31 2025) is...

                                  92, 892 + 645 = 93, 537

                                                                                                                                                    13 / 116

--- Session 6, page 16 (combined page 626) ---
Example





 ■By December 31 2025, when Robinson discloses its financial statements, it records the
     following operations...
         • Income Statement: record the interest revenue in “Other income and expense”. The amount
             is
                                              9, 259 = 4, 614 + 4, 645

         • Balance Sheet: record the debt investment under “Long-term investments” at e93,537.

         • Balance Sheet: record the interest receivable (to be paid on 1 January 2026) under “Current
          assets” at e4,000.
         • Balance Sheet: cash increases by e4,000 (coupon received on 1 July 2025).





                                                                                                                                                    14 / 116

--- Session 6, page 17 (combined page 627) ---
Example




  ■Intuition: when we account for debt investments that were bought under discount
    using amortized cost, amortization “increases" the value of our asset every year.

 ■In the first 6 months, Robinson receives e4,000 as interest payment, while (given the
     effective interest rate of 10%) the actual interest revenue is e4,614.

 ■These extra e614 are treated as an accretion that is capitalized, increasing the value of
    the bond asset in our balance sheet.

 ■The original discount at the bond (e100,000 - e92,278) is “earned back” over time via
     amortization.
         •  If the bond was bought at a premium, the carrying amount would be amortized downwards
          over time.





                                                                                                                                                    15 / 116

--- Session 6, page 18 (combined page 628) ---
Debt held-for-collection and selling





 ■Debt investments that are held-for-collection and selling are measured using FVOCI
      (fair value through OCI).

  ■Intuition: because the asset can likely be sold, amortized cost would not be the best
    measurement since it wouldn’t reflect changes in market values.

 ■A debt investment measured at FVOCI is always carried at fair value and revaluation
    gains/losses are recognized in OCI.
         • Because the key intention is not trading the asset, recording revaluation changes in OCI
          avoids unnecessary earnings volatility.





                                                                                                                                                    16 / 116

--- Session 6, page 19 (combined page 629) ---
Debt held-for-collection and selling




 ■When the debt asset is sold, the company recognizes a gain or a loss in the income
    statement by “realizing" the gains/losses that were stored in OCI over time.

 ■So, the OCI acts as a temporary holding place for unrealized fair value gains or losses.

  ■If the company ends up holding the asset to maturity, the accumulated OCI goes back
     to zero on its own: at maturity, both the FV and the amortized cost are equal to the face
    value of the bond.
         • So there is nothing to recycle: the unrealized gains/losses stored in OCI reverse over time
        and never affect earnings.

         • Only through a sale can companies recognize these gains or losses in the income
          statement.

         • This is different from equity investments at FVOCI, which we will see later: there is no
          recycling and the OCI can be transferred directly to retained earnings.





                                                                                                                                                    17 / 116

--- Session 6, page 20 (combined page 630) ---
Example




 ■Assume that Robinson SA purchased e100,000 of 8% bonds of Evermaster AG on
    January 1 2025, at a discount, paying e92,278.

 ■Bonds mature January 1 2030 (5 years) and yield 10%.

  ■Interest is payable each July 1 and January 1.

  ■Previously, when this debt investment was held for collection only, Robinson recorded in
      its balance sheet the amortized value of the asset at year-end December: e93,537.

 ■What about now (when it is held for collection and selling)? What value should be
    recorded on the balance sheet at year-end December 2025?
         • The fair value of this asset!





                                                                                                                                                    18 / 116

--- Session 6, page 21 (combined page 631) ---
Example



 ■Suppose the fair value of the asset at 31 December 2025 is e94,000.

 ■What is the interest revenue to be recorded this year?
         • The exact same amount we had before: e9,259 (4,614+4,645)!

         • Nothing changed in the way interest is calculated throughout the year: it is still based on the
         amortized cost.

 ■But by the end of the year we must revalue the asset according to its FV and compute
    any revaluation gain or loss.

 ■How do we compute this gain/loss?
         • We compare the fair value with the carrying amount under amortized cost...

                                   94, 000 −93, 537 = 463 (gain, OCI)

         • So we record 463 in the OCI (statement of P&L and OCI).
         • OCI reserve (equity) is also e463 (it started at zero when we bought the bond).


                                                                                                                                                    19 / 116

--- Session 6, page 22 (combined page 632) ---
Example



 ■Now suppose the fair value of the asset at 31 December 2026 is e94,500.

 ■How should we compute interest revenue for the year?
         • We should consider the original schedule of interest revenue...

         • That is, interest revenue is computed based on amortized cost, not fair value.

         • This is because interest revenue represents the coupon plus the amortization of the original
          discount on the bond, not market value gains/losses.
         • Based on the previous schedule: interest revenue is 4,677+4,711=e9,388 (amortization of
         677+711=1,388) and amortized cost is e94,925.

 ■How should we compute the revaluation gain/loss?
         • The new OCI reserve is determined by the difference between the FV and the amortized
            cost... 94, 500 −94, 925 = −425
         • The OCI flow is thus... −425 −(+463) = −888
         • Intuition: FV went up by e500 but amortized cost by e1,388... 500 −1, 388 = −888



                                                                                                                                                    20 / 116

--- Session 6, page 23 (combined page 633) ---
Example



 ■Now suppose that Robinson sells the debt asset at 1 July 2027 by e96,000. What are
    the recordings by 31 December 2027?

 ■Income Statement: interest revenue for 2027 (accrued until 1 July 2027): e4,746.
    Amortized cost at 1 July 2027: e95,671.

 ■How do we compute the gain/loss on the sale and the “recycling”?

 ■Step 1: just before the sale, we revalue the asset to its FV (the selling price), as we do
     at every year-end...
         • The new OCI reserve is... 96, 000 −95, 671 = 329
         • The OCI flow is thus... 329 −(−425) = 754 (gain, OCI)

 ■Step 2: we sell the asset and “recycle” the OCI reserve into the income statement...
         • Income Statement: gain from disposal of debt asset of e329 (the OCI reserve is “realized”).
         • OCI: −329 (reclassification adjustment), so the OCI reserve goes back to zero.



                                                                                                                                                    21 / 116

--- Session 6, page 24 (combined page 634) ---
Example





 ■So, what are the recordings by 31 December 2027?
         • Income Statement: interest revenue of e4,746 and gain from disposal of debt asset of e329.
         • OCI: 754 −329 = +425 (the OCI reserve goes from −425 to zero).

         • Balance Sheet: OCI reserve and debt investment go to zero; cash increases by
         4,000+4,000+96,000 (coupons of 1 January and 1 July 2027, and selling price).

  ■Notice that the gain from disposal is exactly what we would have under amortized cost
     (held-for-collection only): 96, 000 −95, 671 = 329.
         • Not a coincidence: under FVOCI, the income statement always looks like amortized cost.
        The OCI only “stores” the difference between FV and amortized cost until the sale.





                                                                                                                                                    22 / 116

--- Session 6, page 25 (combined page 635) ---
FVOCI: computing OCI and recycling



 ■At every year-end t (amortized cost ACt from the effective interest schedule, fair value
     FVt):
         • Income Statement: interest revenue = effective interest rate × amortized cost (exactly as in
           held-for-collection).
         • OCI reserve (equity): Rt = FVt −ACt
         • OCI flow (statement of P&L and OCI): OCIt = Rt −Rt−1  (= change in FV −amortization of
          the year)

 ■When the asset is sold at a price P:
         • Step 1, revalue to FV: Rsale = P −ACsale, so the OCI flow is Rsale −Rt−1
         • Step 2, recycle: gain/loss from disposal (income statement) = Rsale = P −ACsale, and OCI
      = −Rsale

  ■Robinson: OCI reserve +463 (2025) →−425 (2026) →+329 (sale) →0; OCI flows
   +463, −888 and +754 −329 = +425.




                                                                                                                                                    23 / 116

--- Session 6, page 26 (combined page 636) ---
FVOCI



 ■There is a key difference between FVOCI and the revaluation approach for
     tangible/intangible assets under IFRS. What is it?

  ■Revaluation approach for tangibles/intangibles: revaluation upwards go through OCI,
     but revaluation downwards go through the income statement — except up to the
     revaluation surplus of that asset, which is reduced first through OCI.

 ■But in FVOCI, every revaluation change goes through OCI (apart from ECL, more on
     this soon)... Why?

  ■It is because of the conceptual nature of these assets...
         • Financial assets (FVOCI) are inherently market-based instruments. Both upward and
        downward movements are seen as temporary unrealized changes in value — hence, they
              all go through OCI.
         • PP&E are productive, non-financial assets. Decreases in value (“impairments”) are
         immediately recognized as losses in P&L because they affect future economic benefits.




                                                                                                                                                    24 / 116

--- Session 6, page 27 (combined page 637) ---
ECL and impairment




 ■For debt assets carried either at amortized cost or FVOCI, companies need to use an
    impairment model to estimate ECL (expected credit loss).
         • Similar methods of estimating ECL to those we saw with trade receivables in Session 2.

  ■Intuition for ECL on FVOCI: even though FV could already incorporate the ECL, the
     gains/losses go through OCI so they are not recognized in income.
         • ECL, in contrast, is recognized as a loss in income! It is an impairment loss.

 ■FVOCI: “split” the revaluation loss between “loss due to impairment” (ECL, income) and
     “loss due to changes in market values” (“true” fair value change, OCI).

 ■As discussed in Session 2: there is room for discretion when estimating ECL.

                                                                                                                                                    27 / 116

--- Session 6, page 30 (combined page 640) ---
Debt held for trading





 ■Debt investments that are held for trading are measured using FVPL (fair value
    through P&L statement).

  ■Intuition: because the company’s strategy is to keep track of market values, revaluations
    should be part of its P&L.

 ■Mechanisms are similar to previous example, except that every revaluation change
    goes through earnings.
         • Hence there is no “recycling” of OCI at sale.
         • No ECL is necessary (already captured in the income statement through revaluation
         changes).





                                                                                                                                                    28 / 116

--- Session 6, page 31 (combined page 641) ---
Debt held for trading





  ■Fair-value option: even if a financial asset qualifies for measurement at amortized cost
     or FVOCI, a company may irrevocably elect, at initial recognition, to measure it using
    FVPL.

 ■This election is typically made to eliminate an accounting mismatch.
         • Example: using the debt investment for hedging.

 ■Once chosen, the FVPL treatment must be applied consistently until the asset is
    derecognized (it is sold or matures).





                                                                                                                                                    29 / 116

--- Session 6, page 32 (combined page 642) ---
Debt investments in the notes



 ■What do companies disclose about their debt investments in the notes (IFRS 7 and
    IFRS 13)?
         • Accounting policies: business model, measurement category (amortized cost, FVOCI,
        FVPL) and how ECL is estimated.

         • Financial assets by category: carrying amounts under each measurement category.

         • Fair values: also for the assets carried at amortized cost (this is where “hidden” gains and
          losses show up!).

         • Fair value hierarchy: Level 1 (quoted prices), Level 2 (observable inputs) and Level 3
          (models).

         • Credit risk: credit quality of the counterparties and the ECL allowance.

         • Market and liquidity risk: sensitivity to interest rates and maturities.

         • OCI: revaluation gains/losses and the amounts “recycled” to the income statement.

 ■US GAAP notes are similar.

--- Session 6, page 39 (combined page 649) ---
Example - Unilever




 ■What do we learn from Unilever’s notes?
         • Debt investments are small: other financial assets of e2.9 billion versus total assets of
        e79.8 billion.
         • They are mostly at amortized cost (deposits, fixed income securities: e1.3 billion) or at FVPL
        (money market funds, marketable securities: e0.8 billion). The e600 million at FVOCI are
          equity investments.

         • Fair value equals carrying amount: these are mostly short-term instruments, so there are no
          “hidden” gains or losses.
         • Level 3 (model-based) assets of e963 million (586 + 377), mainly unlisted investments: here
          the valuation requires judgment.

         • Credit risk: a limited number of counterparties with secure credit ratings, each with its own
             limit.

--- Session 6, page 43 (combined page 653) ---
Parenthesis: valuation of non-current liabilities




 ■We just saw how the valuation of debt investments works.

  ■Valuation of non-current liabilities works in just the same way as debt
     held-for-collection...

 ■Companies carry the amortized cost of the debt and the amortization is calculated
    based on the effective interest method.

 ■The most important types of non-current liabilities are bonds payable1 and long-term
     notes.2



   1Long-term debt securities issued by a company to multiple investors, promising periodic interest payments and
 repayment of principal at maturity.
    2Written promises to pay a specific amount (plus interest) to a single lender or institution, due in more than one
 year.

                                                                                                                                                    41 / 116

--- Session 6, page 44 (combined page 654) ---
Equity Investments (EI)

--- Session 6, page 45 (combined page 655) ---
Equity investments





  ■Definition: an equity investment represents ownership interest (e.g. ordinary,
     preference, or other capital shares).

  ■It also includes rights to acquire or dispose of ownership interests at an agreed-upon or
    determinable price, such as in warrants and rights.

 ■The cost of equity investments is measured at the purchase price of the security.
         • Broker’s commissions and other fees incidental to the purchase are recorded as expense.

 ■The level of influence of one investor over the business determines the accounting
    treatment that this investor should use when recording the investment.





                                                                                                                                                    42 / 116

--- Session 6, page 46 (combined page 656) ---
Equity investments





 ■The most usual measure of “level of influence” is the percentage of ownership (PO) of
    the investor over the business.


   1 PO < 20%: indicates little or no influence over the business.
         • Use fair value for valuation.

   2 20% ≤PO ≤50%: indicates significant influence over the business, but not control.
         • Use the equity method for valuation.

   3 PO > 50%: indicates control over the business.
         • Use the consolidation for valuation.





                                                                                                                                                    43 / 116

--- Session 6, page 47 (combined page 657) ---
Little or no influence (EI)

--- Session 6, page 48 (combined page 658) ---
Equity investments with little or no influence





 ■The accounting treatment when the investor has little or no influence over the business
    depends on the investor’s intention with the investment.

  ■If the investment is aimed at trading the asset: valuation should follow FVPL.

  ■If the investment is aimed at holding the asset: valuation can follow FVOCI — but this
    choice is irrevocable until disposal of the shares.

 ■In practice, most investments with little or no influence use FVPL.

  ■Important: because the investor has little or no influence over the business, the
     business’ economic performance is not recognize in the investor’s statements...





                                                                                                                                                    44 / 116

--- Session 6, page 49 (combined page 659) ---
Equity investments with little or no influence





 ■Suppose company A buys equity shares in company B and has total ownership of 15%.

 ■Company B earns £100k in net income. How much of this net income should be
    recognized by company A?

 ■Only its share (15%) of the earnings that B pays in dividends... plus the change in the
      fair value of its shares (through P&L under FVPL, through OCI under FVOCI).





                                                                                                                                                    45 / 116

--- Session 6, page 50 (combined page 660) ---
A couple of notes




 ■Note 1: the FV gain or loss in one reporting period is the difference between the new
     fair value and the implied carrying amount (without the new valuation).

 ■Note 2: This implied carrying amount in a reporting period is the book value at the end
     of the previous reporting period (previous FV) adjusted for any transactions: disposals
     of shares, acquisition of additional shares...
         • The carrying amount is NOT adjusted for dividends received.

         • Dividends are a return on the investment.

 ■Note 3: companies report loss/gain at the aggregate of their portfolio of equity
     investments.

 ■Note 4: when using FVOCI, there is no recycling of the OCI upon sale of equity... (this is
     different from FVOCI for debt investments)





                                                                                                                                                    46 / 116

--- Session 6, page 51 (combined page 661) ---
A couple of notes - intuition





 ■Note 4: when using FVOCI, there is no recycling of the OCI upon sale of equity... (this is
     different from FVOCI for debt investments)
         • The intuition is that debt investments have contractual cash flows and, upon disposal,
          recycling OCI through P&L ensures that the total lifetime return (interest + realized price
           effects) is ultimately realized in profits.

         • Equity has no contractual yield and basically all of the returns comes from price movements.

         • Because equity using FVOCI is not for trading these price movements should not get
         recognized ultimately in the P&L.

         • Recycling could have the effect of incentivizing equity holders to sell stocks that they
           theoretically do not hold for trading in specific times, just to manage earnings.





                                                                                                                                                    47 / 116

--- Session 6, page 52 (combined page 662) ---
Example




 ■Suppose company X acquires e259,700 worth of shares in Burberry on November 3
    2025.

 ■Company X holds the shares for trading (FVPL).

 ■Company X paid for the shares in cash.

  ■Burberry has not distributed any dividends by December 31 2025, but fair value of these
    shares has risen to e275,000. →gain of e15,300.

 ■What are the journal entries?
         • Cr Cash 259,700 ; Cr Equity 15,300 (via FV gain to net income)

         • Dr Equity Investments 275,000

Example





 ■Now we are in 2026: company X has not sold its shares of Burberry.

  ■Burberry paid e5,000 in cash dividends in 2026.

 ■The fair value of equity held on Burberry is e300,000. →gain of e25,000.

 ■What are journal entries?
         • Dr Cash 5,000 ; Cr Equity 5,000 (other income to net income)

         • Dr Equity Investments 25,000 ; Cr Equity 25,000 (FV gain to net income)

--- Session 6, page 54 (combined page 664) ---
Example





 ■Now we are in 2027: company X has sold its shares of Burberry by e260,000 — the FV
     of the investment at the time of sale.

 ■For reporting purposes, does it matter when in the year the sale takes place?

 ■No! The journal entries depend only on the FV as of the time of the sale.
         • Cr Equity Investments: 300,000

         • Dr Cash/AR: 260,000

         • Dr Equity: 40,000 (disposal loss to net income)





                                                                                                                                                    50 / 116

--- Session 6, page 55 (combined page 665) ---
Example - portfolio




 ■When you have more than one equity investment you track each stock individually, but
    records only the net gain/loss and aggregate asset value.

 ■For example: suppose you hold the following stocks on 2025 year end, and that FV in
    2025 and 2026 were...
         • Burberry: FV in 2025 = 300,000 ; FV in 2026 = 350,000

         • Nestlé: FV in 2025 = 320,000 ; FV in 2026 = 300,000

         • St. Regis Pulp Co. FV in 2025 = 150,000 ; FV in 2026 = 90,000

 ■There were no transactions on these stocks that we need to account for in 2026.

  ■Total equity investment in 2026 is e740,000.

  ■Total loss from FV in 2026 is e30,000.





                                                                                                                                                    51 / 116

--- Session 6, page 56 (combined page 666) ---
US GAAP vs IFRS





 ■Under US GAAP, companies can only use FVPL! There is no choice between FVOCI
    and FVPL for equity investments intended to be held (not traded).





                                                                                                                                                    52 / 116

--- Session 6, page 57 (combined page 667) ---
Significant influence (EI)

--- Session 6, page 58 (combined page 668) ---
Equity investments with significant influence





 ■The accounting treatment when the investor has significant influence (but not control)
    over the business is the equity method.

 ■The investor recognizes a portion of the business’ net income in the income statement
     (“share of profit in associate”) and of the business’ net assets in the balance sheet
    (“investment in associate”).

 ■The intuition is that the economic performance of the business is now part of the return
    on investment earned by the investor.

 ■So the investor should record a fraction of the earnings generated by the business.





                                                                                                                                                    53 / 116

--- Session 6, page 59 (combined page 669) ---
Equity method





  ■Definition: under the equity method, the investor originally records the investment at
     cost of the shares acquired but subsequently adjusts the amount each period for
    changes in the investee’s net assets.

 ■The investor’s proportionate share of the earnings (or losses) of the investee
     periodically increases (decreases) the investment’s carrying amount.

  ■All dividends received by the investor also decrease the investment’s carrying amount.





                                                                                                                                                    54 / 116

--- Session 6, page 60 (combined page 670) ---
Example





 ■Suppose Maxi Company acquires 20% of Mini Company’s ordinary shares on January
    2 2025 by £480,000.

 ■By the end of 2025, Mini Company reports net income of £200,000.

 ■By the end of 2025, the FV of Maxi’s shares of Mini has increased to £576,000.

 ■Mini has not distributed dividends in 2025.

 ■How should we record this?





                                                                                                                                                    55 / 116

--- Session 6, page 61 (combined page 671) ---
Example





 ■The book value of Maxi’s investment in Mini by the end of 2025 is determined by the
    purchase price adjusted for Mini’s net income:

                          480, 000 + 20% × 200, 000 = 520, 000

 ■In Maxi’s income statement, one should record in “other income and expense”:
         • Share of associate’s profit = +40,000

 ■What if Maxi did not have significant influence over Mini and used FVPL?
         • Then Maxi would record the book value as the new fair value, £576,000.
         • The income statement would include a FV gain of 576, 000 −480, 000 = 96, 000.





                                                                                                                                                    56 / 116

--- Session 6, page 62 (combined page 672) ---
Example





 ■Now suppose that on January 28 2026, Mini announced and paid a cash dividend of
    £100,000 — Maxi received 20% of that.

 ■For the year of 2026, Mini reported a net loss of £50,000.

 ■At the end of 2026, the FV of Maxi’s shares at Mini is £528,000.

 ■How should we record this?





                                                                                                                                                    57 / 116

--- Session 6, page 63 (combined page 673) ---
Example




 ■The book value of Maxi’s investment in Mini by the end of 2026 is determined by the
    book value in 2025 adjusted for Mini’s net income and dividends:

                520, 000 + 20% × (−50, 000) −20% × 100, 000 = 490, 000



         • Receipt of dividends decreases the carrying amount of the investment. Why?
         • Because the payment of dividends decreases Mini’s net assets!
         • In exchange, Maxi’s cash increases by £20,000: Dr Cash 20,000 ; Cr Investment in Mini
         20,000 (no effect on net income).

 ■In Maxi’s income statement, one should record in “other income and expense”:
         • Share of associate’s profit = -10,000 (−50, 000 × 20%)





                                                                                                                                                    58 / 116

--- Session 6, page 64 (combined page 674) ---
Example





 ■The equity method might be costly for investors in that they need to “absorb” the
    performance of their investee’s.

 ■In 2000, Amazon disclosed equity stakes in Basis Technology, Drugstore.com,
    Eziba.com, Greenlight.com and Kozmo.com.

 ■These equity investees reported losses that made Amazon’s already bad bottom line
    even worse, accounting for up to 22% of its reported loss in that year alone...
         • In their 10-K (2000), Amazon stated a net loss of $1,411.3m with an “equity-method” loss of
         $304.6m.





                                                                                                                                                    59 / 116

--- Session 6, page 65 (combined page 675) ---
A parenthesis about influence

--- Session 6, page 66 (combined page 676) ---
Defining influence



 ■The thresholds based on ownership percentages to define the level of influence of the
     investor on the investee are just a presumption.

  ■If they show material evidence of their influence (or lack of) in the other company, they
    should use the appropriate accounting treatment.
         • For example: use EM for less than 20% interest, and use FV with more than 20% interest.

 ■Examples of indicators of influence:
           1  Representation on the board of directors or equivalent governing body.

           2  Participation in policy-making processes (e.g., budget approval, strategy committees).

           3  Material transactions between investor and investee.

           4  Interchange of managerial personnel.

           5  Provision of essential technical information or critical dependence (IP, franchising,
          technology).

           6  Substantive potential voting rights (e.g., options/convertibles that are currently exercisable).



                                                                                                                                                    60 / 116

--- Session 6, page 67 (combined page 677) ---
Example





 ■BP acquired 19.75% interest in Rosneft in 2013. Should this be classified as little/no
     influence?

 ■In their 2020 filing„ BP says:

    “bp owns 19.75% of the voting shares of Rosneft which are listed on the MICEX stock
    exchange in Moscow and its global depository receipts are listed on the London Stock
                                     Exchange.”

     and...

      “bp classifies its investment in Rosneft as an associate because, in management’s
                   judgement, bp has significant influence over Rosneft”





                                                                                                                                                    61 / 116

--- Session 6, page 68 (combined page 678) ---
Example




 ■For years Ryanair held just under 30% of Aer Lingus but did not apply the equity
    method!

  ■It classified the stake as an available-for-sale financial asset, explicitly stating it did
    not have the power to exercise any influence over Aer Lingus...
           1  Ryanair held no board seat or appointment rights.

           2  Ryanair had no participation in policy making.

           3  Ryanair and Aer Lingus had no material transactions or personnel interchange.

           4  There was opposition from a major shareholder (the Irish government) to Ryanair’s
           acquisition of shares.

           5 EU competition decisions confirmed the absence of control from Ryanair over Aer Lingus.





                                                                                                                                                    62 / 116

--- Session 6, page 69 (combined page 679) ---
Example




 ■Remember Amazon in 2000? It held 11% of Basis Technology, 5% of Greenlight.com
    and 16% of Kozmo.com. Should these be classified as little/no influence?

 ■In their 10-K (2000), Amazon says:

    “Although the Company’s ownership percentage for Basis Technology, Greenlight.com
    and Kozmo.com is below 20%, the Company’s representation on the investees’ Boards
     of Directors and the impact of commercial arrangements result in the Company having
                     significant influence over the operations of each investee.”

 ■Two of our indicators of influence are present: representation on the board of directors
    and material transactions with the investees (commercial arrangements).

 ■So Amazon used the equity method and absorbed its share of their losses!





                                                                                                                                                    63 / 116

--- Session 6, page 70 (combined page 680) ---
A parenthesis about vendor financing

--- Session 6, page 71 (combined page 681) ---
Vendor financing



 ■We just saw that material transactions between investor and investee are an indicator of
     influence (Amazon 2000).

 ■Now think about the extreme case: the investee is your own customer, and the money
    you put in comes back to you as sales...

  ■Definition: vendor financing is when a company finances its own customers so that
     they can buy its products.
         • Loans or notes to customers (a debt investment for the vendor).

         • Guarantees of the customer’s bank loans or leases.

         • Equity stakes in customers (an equity investment for the vendor).

  ■It is very common: aircraft (Boeing, Airbus), cars and machinery (Ford Credit,
     Caterpillar Financial), telecom equipment in the late 1990s (Lucent, Nortel, Motorola)
    and AI chips today (NVIDIA).




                                                                                                                                                    64 / 116

--- Session 6, page 72 (combined page 682) ---
Vendor financing





 ■Why would a company lend money to its own customers?
         • Customers might not have access to cheap credit (new entrants, emerging markets), so no
          financing means no sale.

         • The vendor might know the product (and its value as collateral) better than a bank does.

         • Competitors offer it, so if you don’t, they get the sale.

         •  It allows the vendor to offer different terms to different customers (e.g. a price discount
         hidden in a cheap loan).

 ■None of this is a problem in itself... But, as outsiders, we need to understand what the
     vendor’s revenue and assets actually represent.





                                                                                                                                                    65 / 116

--- Session 6, page 73 (combined page 683) ---
Example




 ■Suppose Vendor SA sells equipment to Client AG for e100m on January 2 2026. The
    equipment cost Vendor SA e60m to produce.

  ■Client AG is a young company with no cash, and no bank is willing to lend to it...

 ■So Vendor SA lends e100m to Client AG (a 5-year loan at a market interest rate), and
     Client AG uses this cash to pay for the equipment.

 ■What are the journal entries?
         • The loan: Dr Loan to Client AG (debt held-for-collection, amortized cost) 100 ; Cr Cash 100

         • The sale: Dr Cash 100 ; Cr Equity 100 (revenue to net income)

         • The cost of the sale: Dr Equity 60 (COGS to net income) ; Cr Inventory 60





                                                                                                                                                    66 / 116

--- Session 6, page 74 (combined page 684) ---
Example





 ■Now suppose that, instead of a loan, Vendor SA buys e100m of new shares in Client
   AG (a 10% stake, little or no influence, FVPL).

  ■Client AG again uses this cash to pay for the equipment.

 ■What are the journal entries?
         • The investment: Dr Equity investments (FVPL) 100 ; Cr Cash 100

         • The sale: Dr Cash 100 ; Cr Equity 100 (revenue to net income)

         • The cost of the sale: Dr Equity 60 (COGS to net income) ; Cr Inventory 60

  ■Exactly the same as before, except for the type of investment!





                                                                                                                                                    67 / 116

--- Session 6, page 75 (combined page 685) ---
Example

                                                                                                                                                    68 / 116

--- Session 6, page 76 (combined page 686) ---
So what is the problem?





   1 Revenue quality: the e100m of sales are not evidence of independent demand as
    Vendor SA paid for them. The e40m of profit is only real if the claim on Client AG is
     really worth e100m.
         • (a) If Client AG does not repay: ECL, an impairment loss in income.

         • (b) If Client AG’s shares lose value: a FV loss in income (FVPL).

   2 Cash flow classification: the cash goes out as investing and comes back as operating.
    Measures such as “free cash flow = operating cash flow −capex” are contaminated,
    since they don’t capture the financing component.

   3 Wrong-way risk: both the value of the asset and the vendor’s future sales depend on
    the customer’s success. If Client AG fails, Vendor SA loses twice: the sales stop, and
    the loan or the shares are written off.





                                                                                                                                                    69 / 116

                                                                                                                                                     71 / 116

--- Session 6, page 79 (combined page 689) ---
Example - Lucent





 ■What do we learn from Lucent?
           1  Revenue quality: part of the sales growth of 1999–2000 was financed by Lucent itself.

           2  Off-balance sheet: most of the exposure was in commitments not yet drawn, and in
         guarantees.

           3 Wrong-way risk: the borrowers were the customers that could not get financing elsewhere,
        and their ability to pay depended on the same telecom boom as Lucent’s own sales.

           4  Timing: the reserves came only once the customers were already in trouble...





                                                                                                                                                    72 / 116

--- Session 6, page 80 (combined page 690) ---
Example - Lucent and Winstar



 ■One of Lucent’s largest borrowers was Winstar, a new telecom operator.

  ■Although the loans to Winstar had protections, like collateral (a lien on Winstar’s assets)
    and covenants (e.g. any new bank loans had to be used to repay Lucent), Winstar went
    bankrupt in April 2001 and Lucent had to write off (make a 100% reserve for) the
   $737m it had lent.

 ■Why did the protections not help?
         • The collateral was worth very little once the telecom bubble burst.

         • And using the covenants backfired: in December 2000, Winstar had to pass $188.2m of a
       new bank loan to Lucent. The court later judged that Lucent was an insider of Winstar,
        because it “had come to dominate the parties’ relationship”.

 ■So Lucent had to give the $188.2m back to Winstar’s bankruptcy estate, for the benefit
     of Winstar’s other creditors.
         • Similar to influence we saw with equity.



                                                                                                                                                    73 / 116

--- Session 6, page 81 (combined page 691) ---
Example - Motorola and Telsim




  ■Motorola also financed its customers in the late 1990s. Its largest borrower was Telsim,
    a Turkish mobile operator controlled by the Uzan family.

 ■The loans financed “equipment purchases and working capital, of which a portion was
    used for license payments”.

 ■By March 2001, about $2.0bn of Motorola’s $2.9bn of gross long-term finance
     receivables related to this one customer. (concentration risk!)

 ■As security, 66% of the shares of Telsim were pledged to Motorola. What do you think of
     this collateral?
         •  If Telsim cannot pay, how much are Telsim’s shares worth? The collateral is worth the least
          exactly when Motorola needs it (wrong-way risk again!).

         • And it got worse: Telsim diluted the pledged stake from 66% to 22%...





                                                                                                                                                    74 / 116

--- Session 6, page 82 (combined page 692) ---
Example - Boeing



 ■What does vendor financing look like when it is part of the business model? Boeing has
    financed its customers (airlines) for decades, through Boeing Capital (BCC).

 ■In Boeing’s words: “BCC seeks to ensure that Boeing customers have the financing
    they need to buy and take delivery of their Boeing product while managing overall
     financing exposure.”

 ■In 2003, Boeing had $13.0bn of customer financing on its balance sheet (26% of
    revenue), with an allowance for losses of $452m.

 ■Then the airlines went bankrupt after 2001: United (December 2002, $1.2bn of BCC’s
     portfolio), Hawaiian (March 2003), ATA (October 2004)...
         • In 2003 Boeing recorded an additional $191m of expense “due to deteriorated airline credit
           ratings and depressed aircraft values”.

 ■What is the collateral here?
         • The aircraft. Unlike Telsim’s shares, an aircraft can be taken back and leased to another
            airline. This is only a problem when the airline industry is in crisis...


                                                                                                                                                    75 / 116

■Today, we have the equity version: NVIDIA, the
   main supplier of AI chips, has been investing in                     invests (investing outflow)
    its own customers.

■In September 2025, NVIDIA signed a letter of
