                                                                                                                                                    76 / 116

--- Session 6, page 84 (combined page 694) ---
Example - NVIDIA, a mix of all our cases



  ■NVIDIA’s situation mixes all of the cases we saw before: Lucent, Motorola and Boeing.

  ■It has financed its customers by acquiring equity stakes, by giving them longer payment
    terms, and by guaranteeing their data center leases.
         • Some of these exposures have physical assets behind them: the data centers and the chips
            (like Boeing’s aircraft).

         • Others do not: the equity stakes and the receivables are claims on the customer itself (like
          Motorola’s collateral).

 ■The key risk is correlation: NVIDIA’s revenue, the value of its equity stakes and the
    value of the assets behind its guarantees all depend on the successful development of
    the AI industry.
         • Lucent’s customers all depended on the same telecom boom.

         • Motorola’s collateral was the customer itself.

         • Boeing reported “depressed aircraft values” when airlines were failing.




                                                                                                                                                    77 / 116

--- Session 6, page 85 (combined page 695) ---
A parenthesis about goodwill

--- Session 6, page 86 (combined page 696) ---
Measuring and recording goodwill




 ■As we discussed, goodwill is recognized whenever CONTROL is obtained (in many
    cases, more than 50% ownership).

  ■Goodwill is defined as the residual between what was paid for the acquisition of shares
    and the value of identifiable net assets of the acquired company.

 ■When control is not obtained (e.g. ownership of less than 50%), what happens to the
     difference between FV of the books and the price paid for the acquisition of shares?
           1 No significant influence: the investor measures the financial asset at FV (market value of the
          shares) and this already captures any goodwill! (no need for impairment)
           2  Significant influence: any positive residual is included as part of "investment in associate".
         Impairment of "goodwill" in this case occurs through impairment of the whole investment in
          associate.





                                                                                                                                                    78 / 116

--- Session 6, page 87 (combined page 697) ---
Example





 ■Suppose company X acquires shares from business Y.

 ■The market value of each share is £100 and there is a total of 10,000 shares
     outstanding.

 ■Suppose the fair value of Y’s net assets is £800,000.

  ■If X acquires 100% of the shares, what is the value of goodwill?
         • 100% × 10, 000 × 100 −100% × 800, 000 = 200, 000





                                                                                                                                                    79 / 116

--- Session 6, page 88 (combined page 698) ---
Example



 ■What if X acquires 60% of the shares?

 ■Under IFRS, X has a choice between the “full-goodwill” method or the “partial-goodwill”
    method. Under US GAAP, only full-goodwill is allowed.

  ■Full-goodwill:
         • 100% × 10, 000 × 100 −100% × 800, 000 = 200, 000

         • Under the full-goodwill method, company X shows the full goodwill in its consolidated
         balance sheet (we will see this soon!) but acknowledges that part of it belongs to
          non-controlling interests.

         • Specifically, NCI is 40% of the fair value of the acquired company:
       40% × 10, 000 × 100 = 400, 000.

  ■Partial-goodwill:
         • 60% × 10, 000 × 100 −60% × 800, 000 = 120, 000
         • NCI only absorbs 40% of the FV of net identifiable assets (i.e., not including goodwill).



                                                                                                                                                    80 / 116

--- Session 6, page 89 (combined page 699) ---
Example




 ■Suppose company X acquires shares from business Y.

 ■The market value of each share is £100 and there is a total of 10,000 shares
     outstanding.

 ■Suppose the fair value of Y’s net assets is £800,000.

 ■What if X acquires 40% of the shares? How much goodwill should be recorded?
         • None!
         • Company X records an initial “investment in associate” of 40% × 10, 000 × 100 = 400, 000

         • Company X then adjusts this amount over time according to dividends distributed by Y and
          Y’s net income...

         • Any goodwill is embedded in this initial investment.





                                                                                                                                                    81 / 116

--- Session 6, page 90 (combined page 700) ---
Control (EI)

--- Session 6, page 91 (combined page 701) ---
Equity investments under full control





 ■When companies acquire control of another company (e.g. ownership of more than
    50%), they should use the method of consolidation in their statements.

  ■Consolidation is the accounting treatment used for groups of companies...
         • Groups of companies arise when a company starts a new activity within itself (a new
          subsidiary is founded), or

         • when a company takes over an existing one — which then becomes its subsidiary.





                                                                                                                                                    82 / 116

--- Session 6, page 92 (combined page 702) ---
Equity investments under full control





                                                                                                                                                    83 / 116

--- Session 6, page 93 (combined page 703) ---
Equity investments under full control





                                                                                                                                                    84 / 116

--- Session 6, page 94 (combined page 704) ---
Control or not?





 ■Suppose company A owns 60% of the shares of company B, which in turn owns 60% of
    the share of company C.

 ■This means that company A has an effective interest of 36% in company C.

                             60% × 60%

 ■Is company C a subsidiary of company A?

 ■Yes! This is because A controls B, which in turn controls C...





                                                                                                                                                    85 / 116

--- Session 6, page 95 (combined page 705) ---
Control or not?




 ■Suppose company A owns 40% of the shares of company B and 40% the shares of
    company C.

 ■The other 60% of the voting shares of company C are owned by company B.

 ■Note company A has an effective interest of 64% in company C.

                          40% + 40% × 60%

 ■Is company C a subsidiary of company A?

 ■No! This is because A does not control B and, therefore, cannot control B’s shares in C.





                                                                                                                                                    86 / 116

--- Session 6, page 96 (combined page 706) ---
Control or not?





 ■When we have a group of companies (e.g. a parent and a subsidiary), we observe
     multiple balance sheets:
           1  The subsidiary’s balance sheet.

           2  The parent’s balance sheet, which includes the investments made in the shares of the
          subsidiary companies.

           3  The consolidated balance sheet, which includes the net assets of both directly.





                                                                                                                                                    87 / 116

  ■Consider that company A purchased 100% of the shares of company B by a total of
    £1mi.

 ■The fair value of company B’s net assets was £800k.

 ■So there is 200k of goodwill to be recognized.

--- Session 6, page 99 (combined page 709) ---
Going through the consolidation method...



  ■Consider that company A purchased only 60% of the shares of company B by a total of
    £600k.

 ■Using the partial-goodwill method, there is 120k of goodwill to be recognized.

 ■When there is less than 100% ownership, we still consider 100% of B’s net assets in the
    consolidated statement.
         • But we include a line within equity capturing other investors: “non-controlling interests”.

         • We also include a line within the income statement capturing other investors: “profits
           attributable to non-controlling interests”.

 ■Note that goodwill arising from consolidation is treated differently from all other assets
    (under partial-goodwill).
         • Because goodwill is a residual relative to the 60% ownership, we only consider 60% of total
          goodwill (total goodwill was 200k in the previous example).

--- Session 6, page 101 (combined page 711) ---
Going through the consolidation method...




  ■Consider that company A purchased 100% of the shares of company B by a total of
    £1.3mi.

 ■The fair value of company B’s net assets was £1.1 mi.

 ■So there is 200k of goodwill to be recognized.3

 ■Also suppose that company B has debt investments held-for-collection whose carrying
    amount at acquisition date is £200k, but whose fair value is 300k.

 ■Upon acquisition, the values that company A will consider for its financial statements
    are based on the fair value of B’s net assets!



   3We ignore taxes here: in practice, the step-up of the debt investments creates a deferred tax liability (25k at a
 25% rate), which increases goodwill to 225k.

--- Session 6, page 103 (combined page 713) ---
A couple of notes



 ■Note 1: Inter-company assets and liabilities must be eliminated before consolidation.
         • Intuition: the business cannot owe itself money.

 ■Note 2: Just as we saw in the last example, we need to adjust the subsidiary’s balance
    sheet to fair value on the date of acquisition.
         • Over time, the subsidiary will keep depreciating/amortizing its assets based on the original
         books.

         • But, the parent, in its consolidated statement, will consider the new value of assets as the
          depreciable base.

         • Hence, we might see different depreciation/amortization charges in their income statements.

 ■Note 3: stepping up assets to fair value usually creates new DTLs in the consolidated
    statements (the tax base does not change), which increase goodwill. The amount
    depends on the deal structure (buying shares vs. buying assets) and local tax law.





                                                                                                                                                    94 / 116

--- Session 6, page 104 (combined page 714) ---
One last note on goodwill...




  ■Goodwill captures benefits of the business combination that do not meet identifiability.
   Some examples...
         • Synergies (e.g. cost savings or economies of scope).

         • Assembled workforce.

         • Location/market position advantages.

 ■In practice, goodwill represents a large share of acquisition prices.

  ■Regulators “worry” that companies might use this goodwill in their advantage...
         • By “inflating” goodwill relative to the fair value of net assets, companies tend to reduce the
        amount of depreciation due every period.

         • By “inflating” goodwill relative to the fair value of net assets, companies tend to minimize
         impairment effects over valuation since goodwill is harder to impair than other assets.





                                                                                                                                                    95 / 116

--- Session 6, page 105 (combined page 715) ---
Business combination under
 common control (BCUCC)

--- Session 6, page 106 (combined page 716) ---
Business combination under common control (BCUCC)




 ■During the last bit of the session, we discussed the acquisition method.

 ■We implicitly assumed that the transferring business and the receiving entity were not
     controlled by the same party.

  ■Acquisitions under common control fall out of the scope of IFRS 3 (business
    combinations) that we just discussed.

 ■Under IFRS, a company acquiring another under common control can use either the
    predecessor method or the acquisition method.
         • In practice, the predecessor method is the most commonly used under IFRS and common
           control.

         • Under US GAAP, the predecessor method for BCUCC is mandatory.





                                                                                                                                                    96 / 116

--- Session 6, page 107 (combined page 717) ---
Common control?




 ■A parent acquires a business from its own subsidiary. Is this a BCUCC?
         • Yes! The same ultimate parent controls both before & after.

  ■Subsidiary A acquires a business from subsidiary B. Both have the same ultimate
     parent. Is this a BCUCC?
         • Yes! These is a combination from sister-to-sister within the same group.

 ■The parent conducts a reorganization and creates a new holding company above itself.
     Is this a BCUCC?
         • Yes! The ultimate control has not changed.

 ■The parent buys a business from an unrelated third party. Is this a BCUCC?
         • No! The ultimate control of the acquired business has changed.





                                                                                                                                                    97 / 116

--- Session 6, page 108 (combined page 718) ---
Choosing between methods





 ■Under common control, there is no specific IFRS guidance: companies can either use
    the predecessor method or the acquisition method.

 ■However, once companies choose a method, they must use it consistently over time.





                                                                                                                                                    98 / 116

--- Session 6, page 109 (combined page 719) ---
The “predecessor method”



 ■The predecessor method consists of carrying over the assets and liabilities of the
    acquired company to the receiving company at their book value (carrying amounts).

 ■No new goodwill is recognized!
         • Recall that goodwill is the residual between the FV of the net assets acquired and the price
           paid.
         • But, in the predecessor method, there is no computing of the FV of net assets and no
         consequent goodwill.
         • Any premium between the book value of net assets and the payment is absorbed in equity
           (e.g. as “merger reserve” or “common control reserve” or “APIC”).

 ■Companies often restate their previous statements as if the acquired company had
    been under control since the earliest year presented (or since common control began).
         • Suppose that a company’s annual report for 2025 would compare the year’s results with
         those of 2024 and 2023.
         • Then the statements for 2024 and 2023 would be restated as if the acquired company was
          already under control.


                                                                                                                                                    99 / 116

--- Session 6, page 110 (combined page 720) ---
The “predecessor method” vs the “acquisition method”





   1  Predecessor method:
         • Less costly to implement since no updating of the books is required.

         • Easier to compare over time since previous statements are adjusted.

         • Reflects the fact that, ultimately, control has not changed.

         • Issue: this approach does not recognize the market value of the operation and, thus, might
          not be a faithful representation of the combination to outside investors.4

   2  Acquisition method: solves the main issue at the cost of the other mentioned benefits.





    4That is: those who have no control over the rest of the group.

                                                                                                                                                   100 / 116

--- Session 6, page 111 (combined page 721) ---
A note...




 ■The acquisition method under common control might lead to an interesting situation...

 ■Suppose that Parent owns subsidiary A and subsidiary B.

 ■Suppose that subsidiary A acquires subsidiary B, but remains under Parent’s control (so
    Parent still controls both subsidiaries).

 ■From Parent’s point of view, nothing changed! Parent will still present a consolidated
    statement with both A and B included.

  ■However, if A uses the acquisition method, A might recognize goodwill in the acquisition
     of B and will “update” B’s books to fair value.
         • Meanwhile, this “updating” and the recognized goodwill will not be reflected in Parent’s
         books.





                                                                                                                                                   101 / 116

--- Session 6, page 112 (combined page 722) ---
Case -----   : ?Huaneng Power International:
 Acquisition Or the Predecessor Method??

--- Session 6, page 113 (combined page 723) ---
Reference





 ■These slides are based on the Singapore Management University case “Huaneng
    Power International: Acquisition or the Predecessor Method? A Decision on a Business
    Combination under Common Control” by Pearl Tan, Tracey Chunqi Zhang, and
    Chu-Yeong LIM.





                                                                                                                                                   102 / 116

--- Session 6, page 114 (combined page 724) ---
The setting





 ■What is the setting of the case?
         • Huaneng Power International (HPI) acquired 10 businesses under common control (all
         under control of the Huaneng Group).

         • HPI was incorporated in the People’s Republic of China and, following local standards (PRC
        GAAP), used the predecessor method to record the acquisitions.

         • HPI has several international investors (e.g. being listed on the HKEX and SSE) and
           voluntarily applied the acquisition method in its IFRS reporting.





                                                                                                                                                   103 / 116

--- Session 6, page 115 (combined page 725) ---
What were the benefits of the combination?





 ■What were the benefits of the combination?
         • Multifaceted growth: HPI grew in many fronts due to the BCUC... Assets, production scale,
          revenues, and geographical coverage.

         • Reduced intra-group competition.

         • With the anticipated easing of demand, the BCUCC allowed HPI to conduct more
          coordinated decisions and to operate at a more efficient level.

         • The BCUCC allowed the sellers (Huaneng Group and HIPDC) to unlock the value created in
          the 10 businesses by realizing in cash both the fair value of the assets and the internally
         generated goodwill.





                                                                                                                                                   104 / 116

--- Session 6, page 116 (combined page 726) ---
What is the difference?





 ■Can someone tell me what were the key differences between the methods?
         • Under the predecessor method, carry books at carrying amounts and do not recognize
           goodwill.
         • Under the acquisition method, update books to fair value and recognize goodwill (if any).





                                                                                                                                                   105 / 116

--- Session 6, page 117 (combined page 727) ---
What were the actual differences between the statements?





 RMB million                 IFRS   PRC GAAP     ■What jumps out?
                           (acquisition)  (predecessor)
                             ■On average, each of the acquired
 Identifiable net assets          10,412          7,042
                                                   businesses held a FV of net assets   measured at                     fair value     book value
 Non-controlling interests        (1,935)          (1,247)       24% higher than the BV of the net
 Goodwill                       1,170             –           assets.
 Cash paid                     9,647          9,647     ■Summing these FV and BV across

                                                                                                                                                     106 / 116

--- Session 6, page 118 (combined page 728) ---
Where did the RMB 3.85bn go?



 ■Under IFRS, the numbers add up: 10,412 −1,935 + 1,170 = 9,647. Under PRC GAAP,
     they don’t: 7,042 −1,247 = 5,795...

 ■So HPI paid RMB 3,852m above the book value of its share of the net assets
    (9,647 −5,795). Where did this premium go?

 ■Under IFRS (acquisition method), it becomes assets:
         • Fair value step-ups on HPI’s share of the net assets: (10,412 −1,935) −5,795 = 2,682.

         • Goodwill: 1,170.

         • Total: 2,682 + 1,170 = 3,852.

 ■Under PRC GAAP (predecessor method), it reduces equity:
         • No step-ups and no goodwill: the RMB 3,852m is charged directly to capital reserves.

         • That is roughly 5.5% of HPI’s equity attributable to shareholders (RMB 70.1bn at the end of
          2014).




                                                                                                                                                   107 / 116

--- Session 6, page 119 (combined page 729) ---
Did HPI grow in 2015?



 ■Under IFRS, HPI reported revenues of RMB 128.9bn in 2015, up 2.8% from RMB
    125.4bn in 2014. Did HPI grow in 2015?
 ■Only thanks to the acquisition! The acquired businesses contributed RMB 15.5bn of
    revenues in 2015.
         • Without them, revenues would have been RMB 113.4bn: a decrease of 9.6%!

         • Same for assets: total assets grew by 12.2% in 2015, but the acquisition alone added 10.5%
        on the acquisition date.

 ■Why? Under the acquisition method, comparatives are not restated: 2015 includes the
    10 businesses, but 2014 does not.

 ■Under the predecessor method (PRC GAAP), 2014 is restated as if HPI had always
     controlled the 10 businesses. The “growth” from the acquisition disappears, and we
    compare like with like.

 ■As an analyst, which growth rate would you use to forecast HPI’s revenues?



                                                                                                                                                   108 / 116

--- Session 6, page 120 (combined page 730) ---
Acquisition or predecessor method?




 ■So, what method do you think more accurately represents this acquisition?
 ■The case for the predecessor method:
         • From Huaneng Group’s point of view, nothing changed: it already controlled the 10
          businesses.

         • Book values are less subjective, and cheaper to produce, than fair values.
 ■The case for the acquisition method:
         • From HPI’s point of view, control did change: HPI paid RMB 9.6bn in cash for 10 businesses.

         • The deal had real economic effects (remember the benefits we discussed).

         • Other shareholders own 48.69% of HPI and need to know what HPI paid for. Under the
         predecessor method, the RMB 3.85bn premium simply reduces equity...
 ■Key point: whose point of view should HPI’s statements reflect? Huaneng Group’s, or
     HPI’s own investors’?

Next Steps

--- Session 7, page 3 (combined page 743) ---
Introduction

--- Session 7, page 4 (combined page 744) ---
Motivation (1)


 ■Leasing is a growing form of financing for firms worldwide.

  ■According to data from the World Leasing Yearbook (Solifi Global Leasing Report 2022),
     the annual volume of new business leases has grown an average of 8% per year since
    2010.





                                                                                                                                                     3 / 109

--- Session 7, page 5 (combined page 745) ---
Motivation (2)





 ■Leasing has also been an important source of off-balance sheet financing...

 ■Before IFRS 16 / ASC 842, operating leases were not recognized on the balance
    sheet: they did not create assets or liabilities.

  ■Instead, lease payments were simply recorded as operating expenses in the income
    statement.

 ■This allowed firms to keep their leverage ratios low, even when they had significant
    lease obligations.





                                                                                                                                                     4 / 109

--- Session 7, page 6 (combined page 746) ---
Motivation (2)





        Source: “Intermediate Accounting for Decision Makers, IFRS Edition” (chart from the book, with data from 2016)




                                                                                                                                                     5 / 109

--- Session 7, page 7 (combined page 747) ---
Motivation (3)



 ■How big was this off-balance sheet financing? In 2016, the IASB looked at retailers that
     later failed (Chapter 11, liquidation or bankruptcy).





    Source: IASB, “Effects Analysis: IFRS 16 Leases” (2016), p. 23 (averages over the five years before each company failed)


  ■Their leases were about 4 to 66 times their debt! Circuit City had $3.3bn of leases off
    the balance sheet... and only $50m of debt.


                                                                                                                                                     6 / 109

--- Session 7, page 8 (combined page 748) ---
Impact of lease accounting changes





  ■Consider debt as “non-current liabilities”...

  ■Tesco’s debt-to-equity ratio changed from 0.91 to 1.67 in 2019 based on the
    restatement of the company’s financial position following IFRS 16.

 ■The non-current liabilities were valued at £22,493mi in the restated statement for 2019
     relative to an original amount of £13,533mi.





                                                                                                                                                     7 / 109

--- Session 7, page 9 (combined page 749) ---
What about now?





 ■There is still scope for off-balance sheet financing through leases, though much less
    pronounced than before.

 ■As we will see, there are certain exceptions within IFRS 16 and ASC 842 that allow for
     off-balance sheet treatment.

 ■But in general, the key message today is that almost all leases go through the balance
     sheet: creating both an asset and a liability.





                                                                                                                                                     8 / 109

--- Session 7, page 10 (combined page 750) ---
Example - Oracle, AI data centers (2026)


 ■Big tech is signing huge leases for AI data centers. Are they on the balance sheet?





          Source: Oracle, Form 10-Q for the quarter ended August 31, 2026 (Note 6: Leases and Other Commitments)

 ■Not yet! Oracle has $44bn of lease liabilities... and $288bn more of leases that have not
     started. A lease only goes on the balance sheet when it starts (when the data center is
    ready to use).


                                                                                                                                                     9 / 109

                                                                                                                                                      10 / 109

--- Session 7, page 12 (combined page 752) ---
Leases

--- Session 7, page 13 (combined page 753) ---
Definition





 ■A lease is “a contract that conveys the right to control the use of an identified asset for
    a period of time in exchange for consideration”
         • A lease is a contractual agreement between a lessor (the owner of an asset) and a lessee
          (the user of the asset).

         • The lessee pays the lessor for the right to use the asset for a specified period of time.

         • In return for the use of the property, the lessee makes periodic lease payments to the lessor.

 ■Key element: the lessee must control the use of the asset during the lease term.
         • The lessee gets substantially all the economic benefits from using the asset.

         • The lessee can direct how and for what purpose the asset is used.





                                                                                                                                                    11 / 109

--- Session 7, page 14 (combined page 754) ---
What kinds of assets can be leased?


 ■In general, tangible, non financial assets can be leased: basically, property, plant and
    equipment.





                                                                                                                                                    12 / 109

--- Session 7, page 15 (combined page 755) ---
Why lease? Lessee perspective




    1 100% financing at fixed rates.
         • No initial cash outflow (e.g. downpayment).

         • Lease payments are usually fixed, which provides certainty over future cash flows.

    2  Protection against obsolescence.
         • In many cases, the risk of residual value is passed to the lessor.

         • The lessee can upgrade to newer assets at the end of the lease term.

         • Sometimes, this upgrade can happen even before the end of the lease term.

    3  Flexibility.
         • Leases can be structured to meet the lessee’s specific needs.

         • Usually, lease agreements contain less restrictive provisions than other debt agreements
         because the lessor can reclaim the asset more easily than a bank can liquidate a loan.

    4  Tax benefits making it a less costly financing option.



                                                                                                                                                    13 / 109

--- Session 7, page 16 (combined page 756) ---
What tax benefits? Lessee perspective





  ■If the lessor is the tax owner of the asset, it can claim tax depreciation deductions on
     the asset.

  ■This tax shield can be passed on to the lessee in the form of lower lease payments.

  ■This is especially important for companies that would not be able to take advantage of
     the depreciation deductions had they bought the asset outright (like loss-making firms,
     startups).





                                                                                                                                                    14 / 109

--- Session 7, page 17 (combined page 757) ---
Why lease? Lessor perspective





    1  Profitable interest margins.

    2  Stimulate sales of a lessor’s product.

    3  Tax benefits.
         • Example soon...

    4  Profit on residual values.
         • Example soon...





                                                                                                                                                    15 / 109

--- Session 7, page 18 (combined page 758) ---
What tax benefits? Lessor perspective





  ■Mainly deductions for tax depreciation...

 ■For example, Airbus might sell a plane to a wealthy investor, who leases it to a foreign
      airline.
         • Airbus gains from selling the plane.

         • The investor gains from tax depreciation deductions on the plane.

         • The airline gains from using the plane without having to buy it outright.





                                                                                                                                                    16 / 109

--- Session 7, page 19 (combined page 759) ---
Profit on residual values?




 ■A key component of lease pricing is the expected residual value of the asset at the end
     of the lease term.

  ■If the lessor can sell the asset for more than its expected residual value, it makes a
      profit.

 ■For example, if a car leased for 3 years is expected to be worth $15,000 at the end of
    the lease, but the lessor ends up selling it for $18,000.
         • Hence, the lessor makes a $3,000 profit on the residual value.

 ■Lessors often use conservative estimates of residual values to protect themselves
     against market fluctuations.
         • So even if the lessor expects to make a profit on the residual value, it may not be explicitly
          factored into the lease pricing.





                                                                                                                                                    17 / 109

--- Session 7, page 20 (combined page 760) ---
Example - Triton





                                                                                                                                                    18 / 109

--- Session 7, page 21 (combined page 761) ---
Example - AeroCentury Corporation




 ■But this residual risk may cut both ways... AeroCentury Corporation had a loss on
     disposal of previously leased assets.





                                                                                                                                                    19 / 109

--- Session 7, page 22 (combined page 762) ---
Example - Hertz (2024)





 ■Hertz is a famous lessor. To meet customer demand for EV vehicles, Hertz assembled
    over the years a large EV fleet including for example an order of 100k Teslas in 2021.

 ■But the value of its used electric cars fell dramatically!

 ■In January 2024, in a filing to the SEC, Hertz announced that the reduction in residual
    values of its EV fleet would increase net depreciation in approx. $245m.

 ■And it did not stop there! More incremental net depreciation of $195m in early 2024,
    and a $1 bn impairment on its whole fleet later in 2024 “largely due to the decline in
     fleet residual values”.





                                                                                                                                                    20 / 109

--- Session 7, page 23 (combined page 763) ---
Leasing around the world




 ■Leasing is more prevalent in some countries than others.

 ■This is mostly due to differences in the regulatory, tax and legal environments in each
     country.

 ■Key aspects are:
         • The tax treatment of lease payments and depreciation. For example, Brazil charges a
          service tax (ISS) on financial lease payments...

         • The legal protections for lessors, making it easier or harder to repossess leased assets in
         case of default.

         • The regulatory conditions. For instance, in India, most independent lessors are NBFC, facing
          higher costs of funds and tighter regulations than deposit-taking banks.





                                                                                                                                                    21 / 109

--- Session 7, page 24 (combined page 764) ---
Leasing around the world





                   Percentage of investment in a country financed by leasing in 2020. Source: Solifi, GLR.





                                                                                                                                                    22 / 109

--- Session 7, page 25 (combined page 765) ---
Lessee accounting (IFRS 16)

--- Session 7, page 26 (combined page 766) ---
Capitalize leases?





 ■Under IFRS 16, all leases should be capitalized on the balance sheet — i.e., recognized
    as both a right-of-use asset and a lease liability.

 ■This eliminates the distinction between operating and finance leases for lessees.

 ■There are three exceptions...





                                                                                                                                                    23 / 109

--- Session 7, page 27 (combined page 767) ---
Capitalize leases?




 ■The exceptions are:
           1  Short-term leases (lease term of 12 months or less) with no purchase option.

           2  Leases of low-value assets (e.g., personal computers, small office furniture). The prescribed
          threshold is $5,000.

           3  Leases with rent payment dependent on future sales or usage.

 ■In cases (1)-(2), lease payments can be recognized as an expense on a straight-line
    basis over the lease term, or capitalized if the lessee prefers.

 ■In case (3), lease payments are expensed when incurred.
         • The intuition is that these leases do not create a liability (present obligation) for the lessee,
          since the payments depend on future performance.

         • So these payments are treated as an operational cost and not as debt.





                                                                                                                                                    24 / 109

--- Session 7, page 28 (combined page 768) ---
Determining lease term





 ■The lease term is the non-cancellable period for which the lessee has the right to use
    the asset, plus:
         • Periods covered by an option to extend the lease if the lessee is reasonably certain to
          exercise that option.

         • Periods covered by an option to terminate the lease if the lessee is reasonably certain not to
          exercise that option.

 ■The intuition is that the lease term should reflect all the period over which the lessee
    has (or is reasonably certain to have) the right to use the asset.





                                                                                                                                                    25 / 109

--- Session 7, page 29 (combined page 769) ---
Example




 ■Suppose a company (the lessee) rents an office space for 12 months. Should this be
    categorized as a short-term lease?

  ■It depends...

  ■If there is an option to extend the lease for another 12 months and the company is
    reasonably certain to exercise it. →capitalize.

  ■If there is an option to terminate the lease after 6 months and the company is
    reasonably certain to exercise it. →expense.

  ■If there is no option to extend or terminate the lease, but the lessee is reasonably certain
     that it will renew the lease (at possibly new conditions) for another term. →capitalize.





                                                                                                                                                    26 / 109

--- Session 7, page 30 (combined page 770) ---
Determining lease payments





 ■The amount of lease payments (lease liability) to be included is the present value of the
     following payments:
         • Fixed payments, less any lease incentives receivable.

         • Variable lease payments that depend on an index or a rate, initially measured using the
          index or rate at the commencement date.

         • Amounts expected to be payable by the lessee under residual value guarantees.

         • The exercise price of a purchase option if the lessee is reasonably certain to exercise that
           option.

         • Payments of penalties for terminating the lease, if the lease term reflects the lessee
          exercising an option to terminate the lease.





                                                                                                                                                    27 / 109

--- Session 7, page 31 (combined page 771) ---
Example





 ■What is the lease liability for the example below?

 ■Lease of 5 years with annual fixed payment of £100,000 and implicit interest rate of 6%.

 ■The present value of these payments... £421,236.


                                          5
                                    100, 000
           X      = 421, 236
                                           (1.06)t
                                       t=1

                                                                                                                                                    32 / 109

--- Session 7, page 36 (combined page 776) ---
The discount rate




 ■To compute the liability, lease payments must be discounted to present value.

 ■The discount rate is the interest rate implicit in the lease.
         • This is the rate r such that...

                                       Lease payments   Unguaranteed residual value
                Fair value of leased asset = X          +
                                                (1 + r)t                 (1 + r)T
                                             t≤T

  ■If this rate is not readily determinable, the lessee should use its incremental borrowing
     rate.
         • Rate to borrow over a similar term the funds necessary to obtain an asset of similar value to
          the ROU asset.





                                                                                                                                                    33 / 109

--- Session 7, page 37 (combined page 777) ---
Carrying the liability over time





 ■The lease liability is carried at amortized cost.

 ■This means that, over time, the lease liability is increased by the interest expense and
    decreased by the lease payments made.

 ■The effective interest rate is used to calculate the interest expense — this rate is either
    the implicit rate in the lease or the lessee’s incremental borrowing rate.

 ■The schedule follows the same logic as for bonds, which we saw last session.

Example





  ■Let’s see an example of how to look at the dynamics of the lease liability over the lease
     term...

  ■Consider that company X has entered into a lease contract with a term of 5 years in
    January 1 2024.

 ■There are annual fixed payments of $100,000 that occur at the end of the year, and
    company X’s incremental borrowing rate is 6%.

Example





 ■What is the impact of the amortization of the lease liability on the income statement?
         • Interest expense is recognized on the P&L.

 ■What is the impact on the cash flow statement?
         • Lease payments: principal is recognized in the financing activities section; interest follows
          the company’s policy choice (CFF or CFO).





                                                                                                                                                    38 / 109

--- Session 7, page 42 (combined page 782) ---
Example





 ■What would change if there was a guarantee of residual value in place?

 ■From the lessee’s point of view, the guaranteed residual value is an additional amount
    due to payment at the termination of the lease.

 ■However, (a portion of) this payment is only warranted if the actual residual value at
     termination is lower than the guaranteed value.

 ■The lease liability at commencement will be only affected by the expected amount of
    payment at termination brought to present value.





                                                                                                                                                    39 / 109

--- Session 7, page 43 (combined page 783) ---
Off-balance sheet leases?





 ■The exceptions to capitalization of leases are:
           1  Short-term leases (lease term of 12 months or less).

           2  Leases of low-value assets (e.g., personal computers, small office furniture). The prescribed
          threshold is $5,000.

           3  Variable lease payments based on activity (e.g., a rent that is a percentage of store sales).

 ■To perform “off-balance sheet” financing, companies might try to structure leases to fit
     within these exceptions.





                                                                                                                                                    40 / 109

--- Session 7, page 44 (combined page 784) ---
Off-balance sheet financing?





 ■The retail industry is the most common user of off-balance sheet financing for a couple
     of reasons...

  ■Retailers often lease small-value assets, such as point-of-sale terminals, display
     fixtures, and office equipment.

 ■By leasing these assets instead of purchasing them, retailers can keep their balance
    sheets lighter and maintain better financial ratios.





                                                                                                                                                    41 / 109

--- Session 7, page 45 (combined page 785) ---
Off-balance sheet financing?





 ■The retail industry is the most common user of off-balance sheet financing for a couple
     of reasons...

  ■Retailers also commonly use leases that depend on sales performance, such as
    percentage rent leases for store locations.

 ■In these leases, the rent payments are tied to a percentage of the store’s sales revenue.

 ■Since these payments are variable and depend on future sales, they are expensed as
     incurred and do not create a lease liability on the balance sheet.

 ■In practice, these leases can be quite significant for large retailers.

                                                                                                                                                      45 / 109

--- Session 7, page 49 (combined page 789) ---
What about the accounting for the ROU asset?




 ■The right-of-use asset is initially measured at the amount of the lease liability, plus:
         • Any lease payments made at or before the commencement date, less any lease incentives
          received.

         • Any initial direct costs incurred by the lessee.

         • An estimate of costs to be incurred by the lessee in restoring the underlying asset to the
          condition required by lease.

 ■Over time, the ROU asset is depreciated (usually on a straight-line basis) over the lease
     term.
         • The residual value of the ROU asset is usually zero.

         • The depreciation expense is recognized on the income statement.

         • But there are exceptions...





                                                                                                                                                    46 / 109

--- Session 7, page 50 (combined page 790) ---
What about the accounting for the ROU asset?





  ■If the lease transfers ownership of the underlying asset to the lessee by the end of the
    lease term the ROU asset is depreciated over the useful life of the underlying asset.
         •  If the lessee is reasonably certain to exercise a purchase option, the ROU asset is also
          depreciated over the useful life of the underlying asset.

  ■If the ROU asset is classified as “investment property”, the lessee can choose to
    measure the ROU asset at fair value instead of depreciating it.
         • In this case, changes in the fair value of the ROU asset are recognized in P&L.

         • This is usually the treatment for leases of real estate held for rental income.





                                                                                                                                                    47 / 109

--- Session 7, page 51 (combined page 791) ---
Example



  ■Let’s use the same lease example as before, and compute the ROU asset depreciation
     schedule...

  ■Consider that company X has entered into a lease contract with a term of 5 years in
    January 1 2024.

 ■There are annual fixed payments of $100,000 that occur at the end of the year, and
    company X’s incremental borrowing rate is 6%.

  ■First you should determine the lease liability, as we did before.

 ■Then, to get at the initial ROU asset value, you add any initial direct costs and lease
    payments made at or before commencement date...

  ■Depreciation is straight-line over the lease term (5 years).

--- Session 7, page 54 (combined page 794) ---
Front-loading of expenses





 ■A key implication of IFRS 16 is that the total lease expense recognized in the income
    statement is front-loaded over the lease term.

 ■This is because the interest expense on the lease liability is higher in the earlier years of
    the lease (and the depreciation expense on the ROU asset is constant).

 ■This means that profit depends on the age of the lease portfolio!

     to the financial statements, including:
         • A maturity analysis of lease liabilities.

         • Reconciliation of the opening and closing balances of ROU assets and lease liabilities.

         • The amount of lease expenses recognized in the income statement.

         • Information about variable lease payments, extension and termination options, and residual
          value guarantees.

                                                                                                                                                    56 / 109

--- Session 7, page 60 (combined page 800) ---
Lessor accounting (IFRS 16)

--- Session 7, page 61 (combined page 801) ---
Lessor accounting





  ■Differently from lessee accounting, lessor accounting under IFRS 16 remains largely
    unchanged from previous standards...

 ■This means that lessors still classify leases as either operating leases or finance
    leases.

 ■The classification depends on whether the lease transfers substantially all the risks and
    rewards incidental to ownership of the underlying asset.

  ■If it does, the lease is classified as a finance lease; otherwise, it is an operating lease.





                                                                                                                                                    57 / 109

--- Session 7, page 62 (combined page 802) ---
Finance leases




 ■A finance lease is a non-cancellable lease that meets one or more of the following
      criteria:
           1  The lease transfers ownership of the underlying asset to the lessee by the end of the lease
          term.

           2  The lessee has a purchase option that is reasonably certain to be exercised.

           3  The lease term is for the major part of the economic life of the underlying asset. (e.g. 75% or
         more)

           4  The present value of the lease payments amounts to at least substantially all of the fair value
           of the underlying asset. (e.g. 90% or more)

           5  The underlying asset is of such a specialized nature that only the lessee can use it without
         major modifications.





                                                                                                                                                    58 / 109

--- Session 7, page 63 (combined page 803) ---
Finance versus operating leases





 ■In practice, finance leases transfer not only the control of the asset, but also most of
    the risks and rewards of ownership to the lessee.

 ■In contrast, operating leases keep these risks and rewards with the lessor: the lessee
     only gets the right to use the asset for a limited period of time.

 ■Any lease that does not meet the criteria for a finance lease is classified as an operating
     lease.

 ■As we will see, the accounting treatment for finance and operating leases is quite
     different for lessors.





                                                                                                                                                    59 / 109

--- Session 7, page 64 (combined page 804) ---
Finance or operating lease?





 ■Suppose company A leases a machine to company B with annual payments and an
     implicit rate of 5%.

 ■The fair value of the machine is £500,000, and its economic life is 10 years.

 ■What would make this lease a finance lease?
         •  If the lease term is 8 years or more (80% of economic life).

         •  If the present value of lease payments is £450,000 or more (90% of fair value).

         •  If there is a purchase option that company B is reasonably certain to exercise.

         •  If ownership transfers to company B at the end of the lease term.

         •  If the machine is so specialized that only company B can use it without major modifications.





                                                                                                                                                    60 / 109

--- Session 7, page 65 (combined page 805) ---
Accounting for finance leases





 ■At the commencement date, the lessor recognizes a lease receivable at an amount
    equal to the net investment in the lease.

 ■The lease receivable is measured at the present value of the lease payments plus any
    unguaranteed residual value.

 ■The original underlying asset is derecognized from the lessor’s balance sheet.

 ■There are two types of finance leases: direct financing leases and sales-type
     leases...





                                                                                                                                                    61 / 109

--- Session 7, page 66 (combined page 806) ---
Accounting for finance leases





 ■The difference between direct financing leases and sales-type leases is that the latter
     involves not only the “loan” component of the lease, but also the “sale” component.

  ■It usually occurs when manufacturers or dealers lease their own products to customers.

 ■The “sale” component is recognized immediately! This involves recognizing:
         • Revenue in the P&L from this sale.

         • COGS in the P&L associated with the sale.

         • The lease receivable in BS for the financing component.

         • Derecognition of the underlying asset from the BS (inventory in the amount of COGS).





                                                                                                                                                    62 / 109

--- Session 7, page 67 (combined page 807) ---
Accounting for finance leases




 ■Over time, the “loan” component of the lease is treated similarly independently of
    whether it is a direct financing lease or a sales-type lease.

 ■The lessor recognizes interest income on the lease receivable using the effective
     interest method.

 ■Lease payments received reduce the lease receivable balance.

  ■Specifically:
         • The lease payment affects the cash flow statement under investing activities.

         • Interest income is recognized in the P&L.

         • The lease receivable is carried at amortized cost on the balance sheet.





                                                                                                                                                    63 / 109

--- Session 7, page 68 (combined page 808) ---

 ■Suppose a lessor enters into a lease contract of 5 years with annual payments of
    £25,000 at the end of each year.

 ■There is an implicit rate of 6%.

 ■What is the value of the lease receivable?

 ■The present value of the lease payments (there is no residual value)... £105,309.


                                          5
                                      25, 000
           X     = 105, 309
                                           (1.06)t
                                       t=1

--- Session 7, page 69 (combined page 809) ---

--- Session 7, page 70 (combined page 810) ---

                                                                                                                                                    66 / 109

--- Session 7, page 71 (combined page 811) ---
What about residual values in finance leases?




  ■If there is an unguaranteed residual value, it is included in the lease receivable at
    commencement date.

 ■Over time, the lessor should reassess the expected residual value of the underlying
     asset.

  ■If the expected unguaranteed residual value changes, the lease receivable should be
     adjusted accordingly.
         • Decreases in this value result in immediate loss, reducing the recognized interest income in
        P&L.
         • Increases are recognized over the remaining lease term as an adjustment to interest income.

 ■An impairment of the lease receivable occurs when there is expected credit loss (i.e. the
    lessee might default on payments).





                                                                                                                                                    67 / 109

--- Session 7, page 72 (combined page 812) ---
Example




 ■Suppose a lessor enters into a lease contract of 5 years with annual payments of
    £25,000 at the end of each year.

 ■There is an implicit rate of 6%, and an unguaranteed residual of £20,000.

 ■What is the value of the lease receivable?

 ■The present value of the lease payments plus the present value of the unguaranteed
     residual... £120,254.


                        5
                       25, 000   20, 000
      X     +     = 105, 309 + 14, 945 = 120, 254
                          (1.06)t    (1.06)5
                      t=1

 ■Note that I put all annual cash receipts on CFI, but this is a policy choice...

  ■Principal reductions (receipt −interest) usually goes to the CFI (the exception is when
     leasing is your main business activity).

 ■But the interest income might be categorized as either CFO or CFI — this is a policy
    choice that must be applied consistently to a company’s finance leases.





                                                                                                                                                    71 / 109

--- Session 7, page 76 (combined page 816) ---
Accounting for operating leases





 ■For operating leases, the lessor keeps the underlying asset on its balance sheet.

 ■Lease payments received are recognized as rental income on a straight-line basis over
     the lease term.

 ■The underlying asset is accounted for similarly to other property, plant and equipment.
     In general:
         • The lease payment affects the cash flow statement under operating activities.

         • Depreciation expense is recognized in the P&L.

         • The underlying asset is carried at cost less accumulated depreciation on the balance sheet.





                                                                                                                                                    72 / 109

--- Session 7, page 77 (combined page 817) ---
Accounting for operating leases





 ■Note 1: land and buildings (“property”) held for operating leases are often classified as
    “investment property” and carried at fair value.
         • In this case, changes in fair value are recognized in P&L.

         • No depreciation expense.

 ■Note 2: plant and equipment held for operating leases might be subject to the
     revaluation model instead of cost model.
         • In this case, changes in fair value are recognized in OCI.

         • Depreciation expense is still recognized in P&L.

         • We saw this in detail in Session 3.





                                                                                                                                                    73 / 109

--- Session 7, page 78 (combined page 818) ---
Example





 ■Now let us consider an operating lease... The carrying amount of the underlying asset
     at commencement is £500,000.

 ■There is still a useful life of 10 years for this asset, and a residual value of £50,000.

 ■The lease term is 5 years, with annual receipts according to the following schedule:
    £40,000 in year 1, £50,000 in year 2, £60,000 in year 3, £70,000 in year 4, and £80,000
     in year 5.

 ■So the total receipts equal £300,000 — and hence a lease income of £60,000 per year
    (300, 000/5).

 ■Lessors have to disclose certain information about their leases in the financial
    statements.

 ■For instance, the nature of the leasing activities, significant leasing arrangements, and
    the accounting policies adopted for leases.

  ■Additionally, lessors must provide quantitative information on the nature, timing, and
    amounts of future rentals.

--- Session 7, page 84 (combined page 824) ---
Sale-and-leaseback

--- Session 7, page 85 (combined page 825) ---
Definition





 ■A “sale-and-leaseback” is a transaction in which an entity (the seller-lessee) transfers
    an asset to another party (the buyer-lessor) and then leases that same asset back.





                                                                                                                                                    80 / 109

--- Session 7, page 86 (combined page 826) ---
Example - EasyJet, BOCCOMM (2020)





                                                                                                                                                    81 / 109

--- Session 7, page 87 (combined page 827) ---
Example - MGM, Blackstone (2021)





                                                                                                                                                    82 / 109

--- Session 7, page 88 (combined page 828) ---
Example - AT&T, Reign Capital (2025)





                                                                                                                                                    83 / 109

--- Session 7, page 89 (combined page 829) ---
Why sale and leaseback?





  ■Liquity needs are a major reason why companies enter into sale-leaseback transactions.

 ■By selling an asset and leasing it back, companies can free up cash that was tied up in
     the asset.

  ■This is often more attractive than taking on additional debt, especially if the company
    has limited borrowing capacity.





                                                                                                                                                    84 / 109

--- Session 7, page 90 (combined page 830) ---
Accounting for sale and leaseback



 ■The accounting treatment for sale-leaseback transactions depends on whether a “sale”
     effectively occurs. Specifically: does the effective ownership of the asset transfer to the
     buyer-lessor?
         • A finance lease, for example, often fails the sale criteria, since effective ownership does not
           transfer to the buyer-lessor at commencement.

  ■If the transfer of the asset meets the criteria for a sale under IFRS 15, then the
     seller-lessee...
         • Recognizes a gain or loss on the sale of the asset over the rights transferred to the
          buyer-lessor.

         • De-recognizes the asset from its balance sheet, and creates a right-of-use asset and lease
              liability for the leaseback.

  ■If the transfer does not meet the criteria for a sale, then the transaction is accounted for
    as a financing arrangement, and the leaseback is treated as a debt obligation.
         • The lessee continues to recognize the asset on its balance sheet, and recognizes a financial
              liability for the proceeds received from the sale.


                                                                                                                                                    85 / 109

--- Session 7, page 91 (combined page 831) ---
Lease accounting under US GAAP
          (ASC 842)

--- Session 7, page 92 (combined page 832) ---
Lessor accounting under US GAAP




 ■Lessor accounting under US GAAP (ASC 842) is largely similar to IFRS 16...

 ■Lessors classify leases as either finance leases or operating leases, using the criteria
   we saw before.

 ■The accounting treatment for finance leases and operating leases is also similar to
     IFRS...
         • Finance leases: recognize a lease receivable and derecognize the underlying asset.
         • Operating leases: continue to recognize the underlying asset and recognize lease income
        on a straight-line basis.

 ■Finance leases should be classified into sales-type leases and direct financing leases,
     with the former resulting in a profit or loss recognition at the time of sale.





                                                                                                                                                    86 / 109

--- Session 7, page 93 (combined page 833) ---
Lessee accounting under IFRS - recap




 ■We saw that lessee accounting under IFRS 16 requires all leases to be capitalized on
    the balance sheet, with some exceptions...
         • Short-term leases.

         • Leases of low-value assets.

         • Variable lease payments linked to sales or usage.

 ■At commencement, lessees recognize a ROU asset and a lease liability.

 ■Then, over time, the P&L recognizes interest expense on the lease liability and
     depreciation expense on the ROU asset, separately.

 ■The lease liability is carried at amortized cost, while the ROU asset is usually
    depreciated on a straight-line basis over the lease term.





                                                                                                                                                    87 / 109

--- Session 7, page 94 (combined page 834) ---
Lessee accounting under US GAAP - what changes?




 ■Under US GAAP (ASC 842), lessees also have to capitalize most leases on the balance
     sheet.
         • The exception is short-term leases and variable lease payments linked to sales or usage,
         which can be expensed as incurred.

         • Low-value asset leases are not exempted under US GAAP.

 ■The key difference is that lessees must classify leases as either finance leases or
    operating leases.
         • The classification criteria are similar to those for lessors.

         • Accounting for finance leases under US GAAP is just like that for all leases under IFRS.

         • Accounting for operating leases under US GAAP differs from IFRS: the lease liability and
      ROU asset are not separately recognized in P&L...





                                                                                                                                                    88 / 109

--- Session 7, page 95 (combined page 835) ---
Lessee accounting under US GAAP - operating leases





 ■The key difference for operating leases under US GAAP is in the P&L recognition...

 ■Lessees recognize a single lease expense in the P&L, based on a straight-line basis
    over the lease term.

 ■This lease expense combines both the interest expense on the lease liability and the
     depreciation expense on the ROU asset.

 ■This lease expense affects EBITDA!





                                                                                                                                                    89 / 109

--- Session 7, page 96 (combined page 836) ---
Example




 ■Suppose a company (the lessee) enters into a 5-year operating lease with annual
    payments of $10,000, and an implicit interest rate of 6%. The contract starts on January
     1, 2020.

 ■Payments are made at the end of each year.

 ■What is the initial measurement of the lease liability and ROU asset at commencement
    date (January 1, 2020)?
         • The present value of the lease payments is $42,124 — this determines the initial
        measurement of the lease liability and ROU asset.

         • At commencement, the company recognizes a lease liability and ROU asset of $42,124 on
              its balance sheet.





                                                                                                                                                    90 / 109

--- Session 7, page 97 (combined page 837) ---
Example - IFRS




  ■Depreciation expense is constant over time: straight-line over 5 years = $8,425 per
     year.

 ■Lease liability carried at amortized cost, with interest expense recognized using the
     effective interest method.





                                                                                                                                                    91 / 109

--- Session 7, page 98 (combined page 838) ---
Example - US GAAP




  ■Depreciation expense is varying over time: specifically, it is the residual between the
    lease payment and the interest expense for each year.

 ■Lease liability carried at amortized cost, with interest expense recognized using the
     effective interest method.





                                                                                                                                                    92 / 109

--- Session 7, page 99 (combined page 839) ---
Example - some notes





 ■Note 1: relative to US GAAP, IFRS front-loads the expense recognition (higher
    expenses in earlier years).

 ■Note 2: in terms of cash flows, both standards result in similar total cash outflows over
    the lease term.

 ■Note 3: there is a small impact on the balance sheet over time since depreciation is
    constant under IFRS, but varying (and increasing) under US GAAP.





                                                                                                                                                    93 / 109

--- Session 7, page 100 (combined page 840) ---
Summary




 ■Lessee accounting:
         • IFRS: all leases go through the balance sheet and create interest expense & depreciation
          (exceptions: activity-based payments, short-term leases, low-value leases).

         • US GAAP: split between finance and operating leases (exceptions: activity-based payments,
          short-term leases). Both go through the balance sheet. For operating leases, there is a
          single lease expense in the P&L; for finance leases there is interest expense and
          depreciation.

 ■Lessor accounting:
         • Both IFRS and US GAAP: split between finance and operating leases.
         • Finance: derecognize underlying asset; recognize lease receivable.
         • Operating: keep underlying asset in balance sheet; record lease income in the P&L each
          period.

--- Session 7, page 107 (combined page 847) ---
Where has management used discretion?





 ■Which judgments change the size of the lease liability?

 ■Lease term: when is an extension “reasonably certain”? Hugo Boss only counts an
     extension once it is signed, so e390m of extension options stay out of its liability.

  ■Discount rate: a higher rate means a smaller liability for the same payments. AA uses
    7.5% for its operating leases; IAG’s lease interest is about 5.5% of its lease liability.

  ■Contract design: variable payments and short-term leases keep the liability small.

--- Session 7, page 111 (combined page 851) ---
American Airlines





 ■AA was one of the largest airlines in the world prior to the COVID-19 pandemic.

 ■As of year-end 2019, AA had 133,700 employees, operated a fleet of 942 aircraft, and
    served over 350 destinations worldwide.

 ■AA leased a wide range of property and equipment, including aircraft, engines, and
     airport facilities.
         • The leasing arrangements regarding aircraft and engines were mostly classified as operating
          leases with fixed payments.
         • However, many leases of airport facilities (like terminals and gates), although classified as
          operating leases, included variable payments based on activity.

--- Session 7, page 114 (combined page 854) ---
AA’s liabilities





                                                                                                                                                   106 / 109

--- Session 7, page 115 (combined page 855) ---
Leverage?




 ■Are the variable-activity leases materially relevant for AA’s leverage position as we think
    about the potential impacts of COVID?
         • Yes: they represent more than half of AA’s total lease expense in 2019, which, in turn,
         represented 11% of AA’s total operating expense. This has a significant impact on AA’s
          cash-flow/coverage ratios (like cash burn).

         •  If an analyst were to “capitalize” these variable-activity leases (at present value of expected
           future payments, for example), they would find a material impact on AA’s liability position
          given the sheer size of these variable lease expenses.

         • As a side note: operating lease liabilities alone represented 15% of AA’s total liabilities as of
         year-end 2019...

         • No: they are activity-dependent and their weight on AA’s position is limited by the size of
          operations — the COVID hit should drop AA’s activities significantly, therefore reducing
         these payments too...





                                                                                                                                                   107 / 109

--- Session 7, page 116 (combined page 856) ---
What about the COVID impact?





  ■Variable lease expenses dropped by 30% in 2020 relative to 2019.

 ■As comparison: drop of 62% in operating revenues, and of 35% in operating expenses
     excluding variable lease expenses.

Analyzing Cash Flows

--- Session 8, page 4 (combined page 863) ---
Motivation (1)





 ■In the early 1970s, W.T. Grant Co., was among the largest U.S. retailers.

 ■The company paid dividends consistently since 1906 and reported profits for many
     years.

 ■As of 1973, Grant’s stock traded at roughly 20 × earnings!





                                                                                                                                                     3 / 124

--- Session 8, page 5 (combined page 864) ---
Motivation (1)





                                                                                                                                                     4 / 124

--- Session 8, page 6 (combined page 865) ---
Motivation (1)





 ■What investors did not realize is that Grant’s cash flow situation was deteriorating.

 ■By 1975, the company was forced to file for bankruptcy.

 ■The key issue is that W.T. Grant was having trouble effectively collecting cash from
    customers.

 ■So even though revenues were recognized on an accrual basis, cash flows kept
    worsening.

 ■Cash is king!





                                                                                                                                                     7 / 124

--- Session 8, page 9 (combined page 868) ---
Motivation (2)





 ■Cash is king!

 ■Cash can be a especially large obstacle for small companies...

  ■According to Intuit QuickBooks (2019, “The State of Small Business Cash Flow”):
         • Almost one in seven UK small business owners have been left unable to pay employees.

         • Around 57% of UK small business owners have experienced problems with cash flow.

         • 38% of UK small business owners who have had cash flow issues have been left unable to
         pay debts.

         • UK small business owners lose £26,000 on average by being forced to turn down work due
           to cash flow problems.

--- Session 8, page 10 (combined page 869) ---
Motivation (3)





 ■Why do we care about the cash flow statement?
           1   It tells us about the company’s ability to generate future cash flows.

           2   It also indicates the company’s ability to pay dividends and meet obligations.

           3   It reconciles the difference between cash flows and profit.

           4   It helps assess the company’s investing and financing activities.

  ■It was acquired by Microsoft in 2023.

 ■One of the strongest elements of Activision’s financial performance has been its ability
