--- Session 8, page 17 (combined page 876) ---
Example - Activision Blizzard, Inc.





                                                                                                                                                    16 / 124

--- Session 8, page 18 (combined page 877) ---
Classification of cash flows




  ■Operating: cash effects of transactions that enter into the determination of net income.
         • Indicates the ability to generate cash from operations to pay bills and dividends, and to fund
          investing activities without external financing.

  ■Investing: cash effects generally involving non-current assets.
         • Indicates the extent to which expenditures have been made to maintain or expand the
          operating capacity of the company.

         • Includes making and collecting loans and acquiring and disposing of investments and
          productive long-lived assets.

  ■Financing: cash effects generally involving non-current liabilities and equity.
         • Indicates the extent to which cash has been obtained from or repaid to owners and creditors.





                                                                                                                                                    17 / 124

--- Session 8, page 19 (combined page 878) ---
The cyclical nature of cash flows





 ■When analysing cash flows, it is important to consider the cyclical nature of business
      activities.

  ■Operating cash flows may fluctuate due to seasonal demand, economic cycles, or
     industry-specific factors.

  ■Investing and financing activities may also be influenced by strategic decisions, market
     conditions, and capital structure considerations.





                                                                                                                                                    18 / 124

--- Session 8, page 20 (combined page 879) ---
The life cycle of cash flows



  ■Dickinson (2011) shows that the signs of CFO, CFI and CFF reveal a firm’s life-cycle
     stage:


  Stage      CFO   CFI  CFF  What is going on

   Introduction   −   −   +   Few customers and high start-up costs: operations burn cash, the firm
                                         invests heavily, and outside investors and lenders fund both.
  Growth     +   −   +    Sales and margins rise, so operations generate cash, but not enough to
                                     cover investment: the firm keeps raising funds.
   Maturity     +   −   −    Operating cash flow peaks and investment slows to maintenance.
                                     Surplus cash repays debt and goes to shareholders.
  Decline     −   +   +/−   Sales fall and operations burn cash again. The firm sells assets to repay
                                     debt or keep operating.




 Source: Dickinson, V. (2011). Cash flow patterns as a proxy for firm life cycle. The Accounting Review, 86(6), 1969–1994.





                                                                                                                                                    19 / 124

--- Session 8, page 21 (combined page 880) ---
The life cycle of cash flows





 Stylized illustration of the cash flow patterns in Dickinson, V. (2011). Cash flow patterns as a proxy for firm life cycle. The

 Accounting Review, 86(6), 1969–1994.


                                                                                                                                                    20 / 124

--- Session 8, page 22 (combined page 881) ---
Example - Netflix, Inc.



 ■Since its IPO in 2002, Netflix has transitioned from a DVD rental service to a leading
     global streaming platform.

 ■Around 2016...
         • Netflix ramped up its investment in original content production.

         • The company also launched in 130 new countries, requiring the securing of additional
          licensing agreements and partnerships.

  ■Following US GAAP, Netflix classifies payments for content (licensed and produced) in
    CFO.

 ■But for Netflix, content works more like an investment: it is paid upfront and generates
    revenue over several years. So let’s reclassify these payments to CFI.

 ■Most of this growth was financed through debt issuances.




                                                                                                                                                    21 / 124

--- Session 8, page 23 (combined page 882) ---
Example - Netflix, Inc.





 Payments for content (additions to content assets less the change in content liabilities) are moved from operating to investing cash
 flows. Free cash flow is unchanged. Source: Netflix, Inc., Form 10-K filings.


                                                                                                                                                    22 / 124

--- Session 8, page 24 (combined page 883) ---
Cash or Earnings?





 ■Many analysts “prefer” to look at cash flows rather than earnings because the former is
     less manipulable than the latter.

  ■Although it may be harder to “manage” cash than earnings, keep in mind that there is
    room for discretion when preparing the cash flow statement.

 ■As practitioners often note: many companies that reported profits without backing them
    up with cash have gone bankrupt. Investors focus on CFO, and companies know it, so
    they try to show the best CFO possible.





                                                                                                                                                    23 / 124

--- Session 8, page 25 (combined page 884) ---
Discretion in cash flows?





 ■The key “source” of discretion is that companies might have flexibility in how they
     classify certain cash flows.

 ■This means that companies might include some receipts or exclude some payments
    from the CFO to paint a better picture.

 ■This is especially important for IFRS that allows “interest or dividends received” to be
     classified as CFO or CFI, while “interest or dividends paid” can be classified as either
   CFO or CFF.





                                                                                                                                                    24 / 124

--- Session 8, page 26 (combined page 885) ---
IFRS





 ■The rationale for this flexibility under IFRS is to be able to adapt the disclosure to
     different business models.

 ■For banks, the interest and dividends received/paid are part of “ordinary operations”
    and it makes sense to include them in the CFO.

 ■For industrials, these cash flows may look more like cost of financing or return on
     investment...





                                                                                                                                                    25 / 124

--- Session 8, page 27 (combined page 886) ---
IFRS





 ■Companies choose a policy and must remain consistent with it over time.

 ■Any policy change must have a good reason and requires restatement of previous
     results.

 ■Note: under IFRS 18 (annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027), the
     options disappear for most companies: interest and dividends paid are classified in CFF,
     interest and dividends received in CFI. Banks, insurers and other companies whose
    main business is investing or lending follow their income statement classification.





                                                                                                                                                    26 / 124

--- Session 8, page 28 (combined page 887) ---
Example - Nestle





                                                                                                                                                    27 / 124

--- Session 8, page 29 (combined page 888) ---
CFO under US GAAP vs IFRS



 $ million               US GAAP IFRS 18    ■US GAAP: interest paid is always part of CFO.

 Operating cash before interest      1,000    1,000     ■IFRS 18 (from 2027): interest paid is classified
 −Interest paid                     (200)       –
                                                                  in CFF for most companies. CFO                           800    1,000
 Interest paid in CFF                 –    (200)    ■Same business, same cash, but IFRS CFO is
                                                  higher by the full amount of interest paid. Total cash flow                  800     800
Illustrative example: the same company reporting under each    ■The gap is largest for indebted companies
framework.                                       (many retailers and industrials). Leases also
                                                 widen it (under IFRS 16, lease payments also
                                                                             sit in CFF, but under US GAAP operating
                                                     leases sit in CFO).

                                        ■Before comparing CFO or FCF across
                                                     frameworks, put them on the same basis (e.g.,
                                                   deduct interest paid from IFRS CFO).




                                                                                                                                                      28 / 124

--- Session 8, page 30 (combined page 889) ---
Cash vs Earnings



 ■Over short periods of time, cash is not a good measure of economic performance!
    (Dechow, 1994 JAE)
         • This is because cash depends on timing and on investing and financing decisions.

  ■Rather, it is a measure of how healthy and sustainable this performance is.

  ■Accrual accounting captures at its core the idea of “economic performance”: how much
      profit were you able to generate based on what you actually earned as revenue and
     incurred as expense.

 ■The cash basis helps you understand the quality of this economic performance.
         • This is the main advantage of the indirect method... It allows you to look at “earnings quality”.

         • For instance, a large CFO that reflects depletion of inventories is most likely not persistent...

         • For instance, earnings that grow much faster than CFO are a red flag for low earnings
            quality.




                                                                                                                                                    29 / 124

--- Session 8, page 31 (combined page 890) ---
Example - Adidas


 ■A jump in CFO is not necessarily a sign of good performance.

 ■In 2023, Adidas’s CFO rose to e2.6 billion while pre-tax income fell to e65 million: a
    one-time working capital release (mainly clearing the excess inventory built up in 2022).





                                                                                                                                                    30 / 124

--- Session 8, page 32 (combined page 891) ---
Cash flow analysis: what to pay attention to?



 ■When analyzing cash flows there are four key elements to look at...


   1  Cumulative FCF vs cumulative NI (earnings quality?): over time they should roughly
     track each other. NI persistently running ahead of cash is a red flag.

   2 FCF over time (investing for growth?): persistently low FCF driven by heavy
     investment, without growth in economic performance, may signal overinvestment, an
    agency problem (more on this soon).

   3  Negative CFO persistence (liquidity?): when CFO is negative for a long time, the
    company’s liquidity capacity might be at stake.

   4  Decomposing CFO (sources of cash?): you want the generation of cash to be
     sustainable over time; in addition, you must understand where cash is coming from.
     (e.g. depletion of inventories or stretching of payables)




                                                                                                                                                    31 / 124

--- Session 8, page 33 (combined page 892) ---
On agency and cash flows





 ■The seminal work of Michael Jensen (1986) provides the theoretical foundations for how
   we think about managerial incentives in cash management still today.

 ■The idea is that when there is cash flow in excess of what is needed to fund all projects
     with positive net present value (all worthwhile investments), a conflict of interest
    between shareholders and managers arises...

  ■Shareholders want this “excess” cash to be distributed...

 ■But managers might prefer to invest them in negative NPV projects — overinvest in the
    company beyond its optimal size.





                                                                                                                                                    32 / 124

--- Session 8, page 34 (combined page 893) ---
On agency and cash flows





  ■Empirical evidence suggests that this is indeed a problem.

  ■Blanchard, Lopez-de-Silanes and Shleifer (1994) examined companies that received
     large windfalls of cash from legal settlements.

 ■They found that firms kept the cash rather than paying it out: they spent about as much
    as the award on acquisitions (often unrelated, and many sold within a few years),
    borrowed more, and raised managers’ pay.





                                                                                                                                                    33 / 124

--- Session 8, page 35 (combined page 894) ---
On agency and cash flows





 ■More recently, von Beschwitz (2018) studied a German tax reform that made sales of
    corporate equity stakes tax-free from 2002: firms holding such stakes could sell them
    and receive a cash windfall unrelated to their investment opportunities.

 ■These companies did not distribute the “extra” cash to shareholders...

 ■They instead significantly increased their acquisition activity through more M&A deals
    than their peers.

 ■At least from the market’s perspective, those were not “worthwhile” deals — these firms
    experienced negative returns on their stocks at the announcement of these acquisitions.





                                                                                                                                                    34 / 124

--- Session 8, page 36 (combined page 895) ---
On agency and cash flows



  ■According to the theory, one way to mitigate these conflicts of interest is through
    disciplined payout policies.

  ■Dividends have kept growing, but buybacks have grown faster: since 2010, S&P 500
     firms have spent more on buybacks than on dividends every year.





 Source: S&P Dow Jones Indices. Quarterly data, Q4 1998 to Q3 2025 (Q3 2025 is preliminary).


                                                                                                                                                    35 / 124

--- Session 8, page 37 (combined page 896) ---
On agency and cash flows



 ■S&P 500 companies returned a record $1.6 trillion to shareholders in 2024, 60% of it
    through buybacks.

 ■But buybacks are more discretionary: between 2007 and 2009 they fell 77% (dividends
    21%), and in 2020 they fell 29% while dividends were flat.





 Source: S&P Dow Jones Indices.

                                                                                                                                                    36 / 124

--- Session 8, page 38 (combined page 897) ---
Equity

--- Session 8, page 39 (combined page 898) ---
Equity





  ■Equity is the residual claim in the assets of the company after liabilities are settled.

  ■Usually, equity is composed of two “types” of capital...
           1  Contributed (paid-in) capital: the total amount provided by shareholders for use in the
          business.

           2  Earned capital: the amount that arises from profitable operations and that has not been
           distributed to shareholders.

  ■Different jurisdictions might place different requirements/restrictions on how these
     different capital accounts are used.





                                                                                                                                                    37 / 124

--- Session 8, page 40 (combined page 899) ---
Equity




  ■Equity is divided across subcomponents...


   1  Share capital (ordinary and preference).

   2  Share premium (ordinary and preference).

   3  Retained earnings.

   4  Accumulated other comprehensive income.

   5  Treasury shares.

   6  Non-controlling interest.





                                                                                                                                                    38 / 124

--- Session 8, page 41 (combined page 900) ---
Issuance of shares





 ■When issuing shares, a company usually issues “par-value” shares.

 ■The “par-value” of a share has no connection with its fair value and is usually very low.

  ■It is the legal (nominal) value of the share.

 ■This reflects the fact that companies are not allowed to issue shares at a discount, only
     at par value or at a premium.





                                                                                                                                                    39 / 124

--- Session 8, page 42 (combined page 901) ---
Issuance of shares





 ■The “share capital” account reflects the par value of the corporation’s issued shares.

 ■While the “share premium” (APIC) account indicates any excess over par value paid by
    shareholders in return for the shares.

 ■The direct costs incurred to sell the shares (e.g. underwriting costs, accounting and
     legal fees...) reduce the amounts paid in...

  ■Essentially, a lower value is added to the share premium.





                                                                                                                                                    40 / 124

--- Session 8, page 43 (combined page 902) ---
Equity





 ■There is a lot of regulation around what is the “legal capital” from which companies can
     distribute dividends from.

 ■For example, in the US, states generally allow companies to distribute capital that is
    held within “share premium” (subject to some tests).

 ■In contrast, in the UK, the amounts in the “share premium” account are non-distributable
    by law.





                                                                                                                                                    41 / 124

--- Session 8, page 44 (combined page 903) ---
Reacquisition of shares



 ■Companies often purchase their outstanding shares...


   1  This is often a form of distributing excess cash to shareholders in a tax-efficient way.
         • Usually, taxes are only charged when the shareholder actually sells the share while
          dividends are taxed presently.

   2  By buying back shares, firms reduce both shares outstanding and equity, which might
    boost some ratios.

   3  Firms might want to use those shares for employee compensation contracts.

   4  This might also help reducing the number of shareholders and preventing “outsiders”
    from gaining control.

   5  To establish a “floor” on the price of the shares.




                                                                                                                                                    42 / 124

--- Session 8, page 45 (combined page 904) ---
Reacquisition of shares





  ■After buying back the shares, the company might either retire them or hold them in the
     “treasury” for reissue.

 ■Note that when a company buys back some of its shares, it has not acquired an asset!

  ■It actually reduces net assets because cash is used to pay for this reacquisition.





                                                                                                                                                    43 / 124

--- Session 8, page 46 (combined page 905) ---
Example - The Home Depot



 ■Home Depot spent $14.6 billion on buybacks in the two years to January 2024.

 ■Why is its equity only $1.0 billion, despite $83.7 billion of retained earnings?



                        Stockholders’ equity ($ millions)         Jan 2023   Jan 2024

                Common stock                           90        90
                         Paid-in capital                           12,592     13,147
                      Retained earnings                       76,896     83,656
                     Accumulated other comprehensive loss       (718)       (477)
                       Treasury stock, at cost                    (87,298)    (95,372)

                          Total stockholders’ equity                   1,562      1,044


  ■Treasury stock is a contra-equity account: the $95.4 billion paid for 804 million shares
    (45% of all shares issued) reduces equity.

 Source: The Home Depot, Inc., Form 10-K for the fiscal year ended January 28, 2024.


                                                                                                                                                    44 / 124

--- Session 8, page 47 (combined page 906) ---
Payouts and equity



  ■Neither dividends nor buybacks go through the income statement! Dividends reduce
     retained earnings; buybacks increase treasury stock.

 ■This is because they are not a cost of earning income. They are a choice between
    keeping the cash in the company or distributing it to shareholders.

  ■Treasury stock may sometimes be so large that total equity becomes negative (e.g., The
   Home Depot in 2019).

 ■For a profitable company, this reflects a capital structure choice: it is funding part of
      its payouts with debt.

 ■This is not necessarily a problem, as long as the company’s operations generate
    enough cash to service the debt.

 ■But ratios based on book equity (e.g., ROE, debt-to-equity) become meaningless.



                                                                                                                                                    45 / 124

--- Session 8, page 48 (combined page 907) ---
Share splits




 ■When companies experience significant increase in the price per share, they might
    perform a stock split in order to decrease the individual price of each share.

  ■It is as if the company’s “pieces” were even more divisible now, which facilitates selling
    them individually in the stock market (i.e., improves liquidity).

 ■How do we account for stock splits?

 ■The total value of the company’s equity does not change (mkt cap)!

 ■The amount of outstanding shares increases, and the par value of each share and its
    market price both decrease.

 ■Note that each shareholder still holds the same proportion of total shares.





                                                                                                                                                    46 / 124

--- Session 8, page 49 (combined page 908) ---
Stock-based compensation



 ■Many companies pay their employees partly in shares (e.g. restricted stock units) or
    share options.

 ■Under both IFRS and US GAAP, this is an expense: it is measured at the fair value of
    the award on the grant date and recognized over the vesting period.

 ■Note that no cash leaves the company! One side of the entry is an expense, which
    reduces retained earnings; the other is an increase in additional paid-in capital (US
   GAAP) or in a share-based payment reserve (IFRS).

 ■Why should analysts care?
           1   It is a non-cash expense, so it is added back in cash flows from operations: CFO looks
           better than earnings.

           2 When the awards vest or are exercised, the number of shares increases and existing
         shareholders are diluted. Companies often buy back shares to offset this...

           3  Many companies exclude it from their “adjusted” earnings. But it is a real cost to
          shareholders, even if it is not paid in cash!


                                                                                                                                                    47 / 124

--- Session 8, page 50 (combined page 909) ---
Ratio Analysis

--- Session 8, page 51 (combined page 910) ---
Why ratio analysis?





 ■One key impediment to effective analysis of financial statements is the lack of
     comparability of the data contained in them.

 ■The most simple source of discrepancy is the size of the business, which varies both
    over time and across companies.

  ■Ratio analysis overcomes this problem by expressing the items in the financial
    statements as a proportion of one another.
         • For example, we can compare the profitability of a small company with that of a large
        company by looking at their profit margins, even though their absolute profit numbers are
            really different...





                                                                                                                                                    48 / 124

--- Session 8, page 52 (combined page 911) ---
Common-size statements





 ■The simplest way to remove the effect of size is to build common-size statements.

 ■Each item of the balance sheet is expressed as a percentage of total assets, and each
    item of the income statement as a percentage of revenue.

 ■This allows us to compare the structure of companies of very different sizes (or of the
   same company over time):
         • What does the company invest in?

         • How is it financed?

         • Where does its revenue go?





                                                                                                                                                    49 / 124

--- Session 8, page 53 (combined page 912) ---

% of total assets     Home Depot  Microsoft          % of total assets      Home Depot  Microsoft

Receivables                      4.3       11.1                  Deferred revenue                   3.6       11.8
Inventories                     27.4         0.2                Debt                            57.6       10.1
PP&E                         34.2       26.5                  Operating lease liabilities          10.6         3.7
Operating lease assets          10.3         3.7                 Other liabilities                   13.6       17.7
Goodwill & intangibles           15.8       28.7                   Equity                              1.4       52.4

                                                                   Total liabilities & equity         100.0      100.0

 ■Home Depot invests in inventory, PP&E and leased stores (72% of its assets) and is financed mostly by debt.
      Equity is only 1% of assets after years of buybacks!
  ■Microsoft has almost no inventory, customers often pay upfront (deferred revenue), and equity finances half
       of its assets.




 Sources: Form 10-K filings of The Home Depot (fiscal year ended January 28, 2024) and Microsoft (fiscal year ended June 30,
 2024). Columns may not add up due to rounding.


                                                                                                                                                    50 / 124

--- Session 8, page 54 (combined page 913) ---
Categories of ratios




 ■There are five main categories of ratios that can be used to analyse a company’s
     financial statements:
           1  Profitability ratios: measure the ability of a company to generate profit relative to sales,
          assets, equity, and other relevant bases.

           2  Efficiency ratios: measure how effectively a company utilizes its assets and manages its
          operations, including ratios like days inventory on hand or days receivables outstanding.

           3  Liquidity ratios: evaluate a company’s ability to meet its short-term obligations using
          metrics such as the current ratio and quick ratio.

           4  Financial gearing ratios: assess the level of a company’s financial leverage and its ability
           to meet long-term obligations, using ratios such as the debt-to-equity ratio and interest
         coverage ratio.

           5  Investment ratios: assess the attractiveness of a company’s shares to investors by
          evaluating metrics such as earnings per share, price-to-earnings ratio, and dividend yield.





                                                                                                                                                    51 / 124

--- Session 8, page 55 (combined page 914) ---
Benchmarks for interpreting ratios





 ■But ratios don’t speak for themselves... We always need to interpret them relative to a
    benchmark.

 ■Among the most common benchmarks are:
           1  Trend analysis (time series analysis): compare the company’s ratios over time to identify
          trends and patterns.

           2  Cross-sectional analysis: compare the company’s ratios with industry averages or peers to
         assess relative performance.

           3  Company’s own targets: if available, compare the company’s ratios with its own guidance
          or targets set before the fiscal year.





                                                                                                                                                    52 / 124

--- Session 8, page 56 (combined page 915) ---
Example - targeting ROCE





 ■ROCE (return on capital employed) is one of the most widely used target ratios for
      profitability (of non-financial businesses...).

 ■Companies many times make these targets public...
           1 BASF targets a ROCE of around 10% in 2028.

           2 BP targets a return on average capital employed above 16% in 2027.

           3 ABB has an annual target of a ROCE above 20%.

           4  Siemens targets a ROCE of 15% to 20% over a mid-term cycle.

           5 BMW targets a ROCE of at least 18% in its automotive business.

 ■One way of comparing profitability of non-financial firms across countries is comparing
     their ROCE ratios.





                                                                                                                                                    54 / 124

--- Session 8, page 58 (combined page 917) ---
Limitations





  ■Although ratio analysis is a powerful tool for financial statement analysis, it has several
     limitations.

 ■These should be kept in mind when interpreting ratios, and potentially addressed
    through additional analysis or adjustment of the relevant ratios.





                                                                                                                                                    55 / 124

--- Session 8, page 59 (combined page 918) ---
Limitations (1)





 ■One of these limitations comes from inflation, which might be very relevant in some
    cases.

  ■It distorts the historical cost values versus current revenues reported in the financial
    statements and hence affects ratios based on those values.

 ■For example, a company operating in a high-inflation environment may report higher
    revenues (at current prices) but have assets recorded at historical cost, leading to
     inflated profitability ratios.

  ■Similarly, deflation can distort ratios by reducing revenues while assets remain at
     historical cost.





                                                                                                                                                    56 / 124

--- Session 8, page 60 (combined page 919) ---
Example - hyperinflation




 ■Under IAS 29, companies whose functional currency is hyperinflationary are required to
     restate their financial statements to reflect the effects of inflation.

 ■They do so by adjusting non-monetary assets and liabilities, equity, and the related
    income/expense by a general price index from their recognition dates; monetary items
    are not restated.

 ■For instance, revenues are restated from the date they were recorded to year-end
     prices (a small adjustment), while depreciation is restated from the date the asset was
    bought (a large adjustment for old assets).

 ■A commonly used rule-of-thumb is that a currency is considered hyperinflationary if its
    cumulative inflation rate over three years approaches or exceeds 100%.





                                                                                                                                                    57 / 124

--- Session 8, page 61 (combined page 920) ---
Example - hyperinflation





  ■Turkey has passed that threshold in 2022, and companies with Turkish Lira had to adapt
     their disclosures accordingly.

 ■One example is Migros Ticaret A. ¸S., a leading Turkish retailer; in their annual
     presentation for 2023 they showed results with and without IAS 29 adjustments.

  ■Without IAS 29: net income was TRY 5,954 million, and equity was TRY 11,632 million.
   So ROE on year-end equity was 51%.

 ■With IAS 29: net income was TRY 8,905 million, and equity was TRY 36,254 million. So
   ROE on year-end equity was 25%.





                                                                                                                                                    58 / 124

--- Session 8, page 62 (combined page 921) ---
Limitations (2)





  ■Another limitation arises from accounting choices.

 ■These choices affect ratios and usually changes in these policies are only made
     prospectively — thus making the trend analysis more difficult.

 ■So always be careful when analysing ratios over long periods of time...

 ■For example, a change in depreciation method, inventory valuation method, or lease
    accounting treatment can significantly impact profitability and efficiency ratios.





                                                                                                                                                    59 / 124

--- Session 8, page 63 (combined page 922) ---
Limitations (2) - continued





  ■Accounting policies also affect the cross-sectional analysis of ratios.

 ■For example, companies may choose different depreciation methods, inventory
     valuation methods, or revenue recognition policies, leading to non-comparable ratios.

 ■On Session 3, we discussed how different depreciation methods blurred the comparison
    between Lufthansa and British Airways.

 ■On Session 7, we saw that different lease accounting between IFRS and US GAAP also
     affected the cross-sectional comparison between IAG and American Airlines.





                                                                                                                                                    60 / 124

--- Session 8, page 64 (combined page 923) ---
Profitability Ratios

--- Session 8, page 65 (combined page 924) ---
Profitability ratios





  ■Profitability ratios measure the ability of a company to generate profit (return) relative to
     sales, assets, equity, and other relevant bases.

 ■They are used by investors and creditors to assess the company’s ability to generate
     returns and create value over time.





                                                                                                                                                    61 / 124

--- Session 8, page 66 (combined page 925) ---
Profitability ratios





 ■We will cover the following profitability ratios:
           1  Return on ordinary shareholders’ equity (ROE).

           2  Return on capital employed (ROCE).

           3  Gross profit margin (GPM).

           4  Operating profit margin (OPM).





                                                                                                                                                    62 / 124

--- Session 8, page 67 (combined page 926) ---
A note on averages





 ■When computing these ratios, we usually use averages for balance sheet items (i.e.,
     assets, equity) to smooth out fluctuations that may occur during the period under
     analysis.

 ■A common approach is to take the average of the opening and closing balances for the
     period.

  ■This is because the balance sheet shows a snapshot at a point in time, while income
    statement items (e.g., revenues, expenses) are accumulated over the period.





                                                                                                                                                    63 / 124

--- Session 8, page 68 (combined page 927) ---
Return on ordinary shareholders’ equity (ROE)




 ■ROE measures the return generated on the equity invested by ordinary shareholders...

                                       Profits to ordinary shareholders
             ROE =
                           Avg. equity attributable to owners (ordinary)

 ■A higher ROE indicates that the company is effectively using shareholders’ funds to
    generate profits.

 ■To obtain the balance of ordinary shareholders’ equity, we subtract preferred equity (if
    any) from total equity and also non-controlling interest (if any).

 ■Net income attributable to ordinary shareholders is obtained by subtracting any
     preferred dividends from net income (attributable to owners of parent company).





                                                                                                                                                    64 / 124

--- Session 8, page 69 (combined page 928) ---
Return on capital employed (ROCE)




 ■ROCE measures the return generated on the total capital employed in the business,
    which includes both equity and debt.

                                        EBIT
                   ROCE =
                                     Avg. Capital Employed

 ■ROCE measures the relationship between operating profit generated during a period
    (“output”) and the average long-term capital invested in the business (“input”).

  ■Capital employed is typically defined as total assets minus current liabilities, or
     equivalently as equity plus non-current liabilities.

 ■A higher ROCE indicates that the company is efficiently using its capital to generate
      profits.





                                                                                                                                                    65 / 124

--- Session 8, page 70 (combined page 929) ---
Return on capital employed (ROCE)





 ■ROCE measures the return generated by operations relative to the capital required to
    run those operations, independently of how those operations are financed.

 ■That is why we exclude financing costs from the numerator (EBIT).

 ■And that is why we include both equity and long-term liabilities in the denominator.





                                                                                                                                                    66 / 124

--- Session 8, page 71 (combined page 930) ---
Return on capital employed (ROCE)





 ■Sometimes we might use EBITDA instead of EBIT...

 ■EBIT is preferred when we want to include the effect of depreciation and amortization,
    while EBITDA is used when we want to focus on cash-generating ability.

 ■The key point is that we do not use net income for ROCE because it includes
    non-operating items such as interest, which can distort the analysis of operational
    performance.





                                                                                                                                                    67 / 124

--- Session 8, page 72 (combined page 931) ---
Operating profit margin





  ■Operating profit margin measures the proportion of revenue that remains after
    deducting operating expenses.

  ■It is calculated as...
                                              EBIT
                           Operating Profit Margin =
                                             Revenues
 ■A higher operating profit margin indicates that the company is effectively controlling its
    operating costs relative to its sales.





                                                                                                                                                    68 / 124

--- Session 8, page 73 (combined page 932) ---
Example - targeting operating profit margins





 ■Many auto companies publish target operating margins (each defines its own)...

 ■In 2025, most cut or withdrew their guidance after the US auto tariffs of April 2025:
           1  Volkswagen: 5.5%–6.5%, cut to 4%–5% (tariffs), then to 2%–3%.

           2  Mercedes-Benz (cars): 6%–8%, cut to 4%–6% (tariffs).

           3 BMW (automotive): 5%–7%, narrowed to 5%–6%.

           4  Ford and Nissan withdrew or suspended their guidance.

           5  Ford’s long-term target fell from 10% (set in 2023) to 8% by 2029.

Gross profit margin





 ■Gross profit margin measures the proportion of revenue that remains after deducting
     cost of goods sold (COGS).

  ■It is calculated as...

                                       Revenues −COGS
                      Gross Profit Margin =
                                           Revenues

 ■A higher gross profit margin indicates that the company is effectively managing its
    production costs relative to its sales.

--- Session 8, page 76 (combined page 935) ---
Efficiency Ratios

--- Session 8, page 77 (combined page 936) ---
Efficiency ratios





  ■Efficiency ratios measure how effectively a company utilizes its assets and manages its
     operations.

 ■They are used by investors and creditors to assess the company’s operational efficiency
    and asset management.

 ■We will look at five efficiency ratios: days inventory on hand, days receivable
     outstanding, days payable outstanding, capital employed turnover, cash conversion
     cycle.





                                                                                                                                                    72 / 124

--- Session 8, page 78 (combined page 937) ---
Days inventory on hand





 ■Days inventory on hand (also called inventory days or inventory turnover days)
    measures the average number of days it takes for a company to sell its inventory.

  ■It is calculated as...

                                                 Avg. Inventory
                Days Inventory on Hand (DIO) =         × 365
                                   COGS

 ■A lower number of days indicates that the company is efficiently managing its inventory
    and converting it into sales quickly.





                                                                                                                                                    73 / 124

--- Session 8, page 79 (combined page 938) ---
Days sales outstanding





 ■Days sales outstanding measures the average number of days it takes for a company to
     collect payment from its customers.

  ■It is calculated as...

                                            Avg. Accounts Receivable
           Days Sales Outstanding (DSO) =                × 365
                                             Revenues

 ■A lower number of days indicates that the company is efficiently managing its
     receivables and collecting payments quickly.





                                                                                                                                                    74 / 124

--- Session 8, page 80 (combined page 939) ---
Days sales outstanding





 ■Note 1: if the company has contract assets, these should be added to accounts
     receivable when computing this ratio.
         • This is because they also represent amounts owed by customers for services already
           delivered.
         • Example: as in Session 2, think about a construction project for which the client will only be
          invoiced in the next milestone achievement.

 ■Note 2: consider only “current” trade receivables.
         • “Non-current” receivables may distort the analysis since they possibly reflect a significant
          financing component.





                                                                                                                                                    75 / 124

--- Session 8, page 81 (combined page 940) ---
Example - DSO in the cross section





 ■This ratio — the DSO — varies a lot by industry.

 ■For example, supermarkets tend to have very low DSO because they sell mostly to end
    consumers who pay immediately.

 ■In contrast, construction companies may have high DSO because they sell to other
    businesses that may take longer to pay.





                                                                                                                                                    76 / 124
