           1  Sainsbury (UK-based grocery and general merchandise retailer): DSO of 2 days (fiscal year
        ended March 2025).

           2  Pfizer (US-based, large biopharma): DSO of 66 days.

           3 ACS Group (Spain-based, global construction and infrastructure group): DSO of 76 days.

           4  Leonardo S.p.A. (based in Italy, multinational in aerospace, defense and security): DSO of
        155 days.

           5  Bharat Electronics (Indian, electronics company in defense and aerospace): DSO of 203
         days (fiscal year ended March 2025).





                                                                                                                                                    77 / 124

--- Session 8, page 83 (combined page 942) ---
Days payable outstanding





 ■Days payable outstanding measures the average number of days it takes for a company
     to pay its suppliers.

  ■It is calculated as...

                                              Avg. Accounts Payable
           Days Payable Outstanding (DPO) =              × 365
                                    COGS

 ■A higher number of days indicates that the company is effectively managing its payables
    and taking advantage of credit terms offered by suppliers.





                                                                                                                                                    78 / 124

--- Session 8, page 84 (combined page 943) ---
Capital employed turnover (CET)





  ■Capital employed turnover (CET) measures how efficiently a company uses its capital
    employed (total asset minus current liabilities) to generate revenue.

                                             Revenues
              Capital Employed Turnover (CET) =                    where                                              Avg. Capital Employed,

                   Capital Employed = Total Assets −Current Liabilities, or

                      Capital Employed = Equity + Non-Current Liabilities

 ■A higher ratio indicates that the company is effectively utilizing its assets to generate
     sales.





                                                                                                                                                    79 / 124

--- Session 8, page 85 (combined page 944) ---
Capital employed turnover (CET)





 ■Bear in mind that a very high ratio may indicate underinvestment in assets, which could
      limit future growth.

 ■One way to check for whether this ratio is “too” high, is to look at the company’s capital
    expenditure (Capex) relative to depreciation expense over time.
         •  If this ratio is low, it may suggest that the company is not investing enough in maintaining or
         expanding its asset base.

         • A rule-of-thumb is that a ratio of Capex-to-depreciation persistently below 0.8 is “low”.





                                                                                                                                                    80 / 124

--- Session 8, page 86 (combined page 945) ---
Example - Sears





 ■Since 2006, Sears’ CET has been increasing steadily, from 2.63 in 2006 to 4.75 in 2017.

 ■This indicates that Sears was generating more revenue per unit of capital employed
    over this period.

 ■At the same time, the company had a very low capex-to-depreciation ratio, which also
    worsened throughout the period from 0.44 in 2006 to 0.24 in 2017.

 ■The “apparent efficiency” of Sears was not sustainable and the company filed for
    bankruptcy in 2018.





                                                                                                                                                    81 / 124

--- Session 8, page 87 (combined page 946) ---
Example - Sears





                                                                                                                                                    82 / 124

--- Session 8, page 88 (combined page 947) ---
Example - Target





  ■Let’s take Target — which was “similar” to Sears — over the same horizon.

 ■Over the same period, Target’s CET has shown a less pronounced increase, from 2.30
     to 2.73.

 ■However, Target’s capex-to-depreciation ratio has remained above 0.5 all the time —
     with an average of 1.38 (well-above the threshold of 0.8).





                                                                                                                                                    83 / 124

--- Session 8, page 89 (combined page 948) ---
Example - Target





                                                                                                                                                    84 / 124

--- Session 8, page 90 (combined page 949) ---
DuPont analysis




 ■A “DuPont analysis” consists of breaking profitability within different components...
    Margin and efficiency (and, in the case of ROE, financial gearing too).

 ■We can break ROCE down into its components:

                      EBIT           Revenue
          ROCE =      ×                = OPM × CET
                    Revenue   Average Capital Employed

 ■This tells us that ROCE can be improved either by:
           1  Increasing operating profit margin (e.g., by reducing operating costs or increasing prices).

           2  Increasing capital employed turnover (e.g., by using assets more efficiently to generate
           sales).





                                                                                                                                                    85 / 124

--- Session 8, page 91 (combined page 950) ---
Example - DuPont Analysis: luxury vs non-luxury



  ■Although ROCE might be similar across businesses, there is a lot of variation in the
    components that form this profitability ratio...

 ■For instance: supermarkets tend to have low OPM but high CET, while luxury goods
    companies tend to have high OPM but low CET.

 ■In 2024:
           1  Prada: ROCE = 23.6% × 0.83 = 19.6%.
           2  Moncler: ROCE = 29.5% × 0.73 = 21.6%.
           3  Walmart: ROCE = 4.3% × 4.20 = 18.1% (fiscal year ended January 2025).
           4  Costco: ROCE = 3.6% × 7.29 = 26.6% (fiscal year ended September 2024).





 Figures may not multiply exactly due to rounding. Sources: Prada 2024 annual results, Moncler 2024 annual report, and Form 10-K
 filings of Walmart and Costco.


                                                                                                                                                    86 / 124

--- Session 8, page 92 (combined page 951) ---
Example - DuPont Analysis: landlord vs retail



 ■Simon Property Group owns shopping malls and rents space to retailers. Macy’s is one
     of its tenants.

 ■In 2024, they earned a similar ROCE in opposite ways:


            Company                 OPM   CET   ROCE

              Simon Property Group (landlord)    51.9%     0.19     9.8%
                Macy’s (retailer)                  4.0%     1.95     7.7%

 ■Simon keeps about half of its rent as operating profit, but needs a huge asset base (the
     malls): each $1 of capital employed generates only about $0.19 of revenue.
 ■Macy’s keeps only about 4 cents of each $1 of sales as operating profit, but generates
    almost $2 of sales per $1 of capital employed.
 Capital employed is averaged over the year. Simon does not classify its balance sheet, so we treat its accounts payable, accrued
 expenses, intangibles and deferred revenues as current liabilities. Macy’s fiscal year ended February 1, 2025. Sources: Form 10-K
 filings of Simon Property Group and Macy’s, Inc.


                                                                                                                                                    87 / 124

--- Session 8, page 93 (combined page 952) ---
Cash conversion cycle (CCC)





 ■The cash conversion cycle (CCC) measures the time it takes for a company to convert
      its investments in inventory and other resources into cash flows from sales.

  ■It is calculated as...

                Cash Conversion Cycle (CCC) = DIO + DSO −DPO

 ■A lower CCC indicates that the company is efficiently managing its working capital and
     converting its investments into cash quickly.





                                                                                                                                                    88 / 124

--- Session 8, page 94 (combined page 953) ---
Example - CCC across industries





                                                                                                                                                    89 / 124

--- Session 8, page 95 (combined page 954) ---
Liquidity Ratios

--- Session 8, page 96 (combined page 955) ---
Liquidity ratios





  ■Liquidity ratios measure a company’s ability to meet its short-term obligations.

 ■They are used by investors and creditors to assess the company’s short-term financial
     health.

 ■We will look at three liquidity ratios: current ratio, acid test ratio, and cash generated
    from operations to maturing obligations ratio.





                                                                                                                                                    90 / 124

--- Session 8, page 97 (combined page 956) ---
Current ratio





  ■Current ratio measures a company’s ability to pay its short-term liabilities with its
    short-term assets.
                                             Current Assets
                            Current Ratio =
                                            Current Liabilities
 ■A higher current ratio indicates that the company has more short-term assets available
     to cover its short-term liabilities.

 ■A current ratio below 1 may indicate potential liquidity issues...

 ■But a very high current ratio may suggest inefficient use of assets.





                                                                                                                                                    91 / 124

--- Session 8, page 98 (combined page 957) ---
Acid test ratio




 ■The Acid test ratio (also called quick ratio) is a more stringent measure of liquidity
    because it excludes inventory from current assets.

  ■It is calculated as...

                                         Current Assets - Inventory
                       Acid Test Ratio =
                                             Current Liabilities

 ■A higher acid test ratio indicates that the company has more liquid assets available to
    cover its short-term liabilities.

  ■Similar to the current ratio, an acid test ratio below 1 may indicate potential liquidity
     issues.





                                                                                                                                                    92 / 124

--- Session 8, page 99 (combined page 958) ---
Acid test ratio





 ■Why exclude inventories?

 ■Because they may not be easily convertible to cash in the short term, especially if it is
     specialized or perishable.





                                                                                                                                                    93 / 124

--- Session 8, page 100 (combined page 959) ---
Cash generated from operations to maturing obligations ratio





 ■Cash generated from operations to maturing obligations ratio measures a company’s
      ability to meet its short-term obligations using cash generated from its operations.

  ■It is calculated as...

                              Cash Generated from Operations
                CFO-CL Ratio =
                                            Current Liabilities

 ■A higher ratio indicates that the company is effectively generating cash to cover its
    short-term obligations.





                                                                                                                                                    94 / 124

--- Session 8, page 101 (combined page 960) ---
Example - liquidity across companies





  ■Industries with low working capital requirements can operate with low liquidity ratios.

 ■Many times, we do not worry about low current ratios in retail because of the fast
     inventory turnover and cash sales model.

 ■One way to check this is to look at the cash conversion cycle (CCC): if the CCC is low
     (e.g., less than 30 days), it may suggest that the company can manage with a lower
     current ratio.





                                                                                                                                                    95 / 124

--- Session 8, page 102 (combined page 961) ---
Example - liquidity across companies



 ■None of the companies below are considered to have liquidity issues, but, even so,
   some might have low liquidity ratios.

 ■Where liquidity ratios are low, we might want low (and negative, if possible) cash
    conversion cycles...

 ■The red font indicates the “best” company in each category.



         Company    Current ratio    Acid test    CFO-CL   CCC (days)

         LVMH                 1.41         0.71        0.57           210
            Sainsbury             0.74         0.57        0.12         −32




 CFO-CL uses net cash from operating activities and average current liabilities. Sources: LVMH 2024 annual financial report and J
 Sainsbury plc annual report (fiscal year ended March 1, 2025).


                                                                                                                                                    96 / 124

--- Session 8, page 103 (combined page 962) ---
Financial Gearing Ratios

--- Session 8, page 104 (combined page 963) ---
Financial gearing





  ■Financial gearing is the degree to which a company uses debt to finance its operations
    and growth.

 ■There are two main reasons why companies use financial gearing:
           1  To increase returns to shareholders through leverage.

           2  To benefit from other advantages of debt, such as tax deductibility of interest and avoiding
            dilution.

 ■However, high financial gearing also increases the risk of insolvency, as the company
    must meet its debt obligations regardless of its financial performance.





                                                                                                                                                    97 / 124

--- Session 8, page 105 (combined page 964) ---
Financial gearing ratios





  ■Financial gearing ratios measure a company’s financial leverage and its ability to meet
    long-term obligations.

 ■They are used by investors and creditors to assess the company’s long-term financial
     health.

 ■We will look at three financial gearing ratios: gearing ratio, interest coverage ratio, debt
     to EBITDA.





                                                                                                                                                    98 / 124

--- Session 8, page 106 (combined page 965) ---
Gearing ratio




 ■The gearing ratio measures the proportion of a company’s capital that is financed
    through debt.
                                                      Total Debt
                         Gearing Ratio =
                                                 Total Debt + Equity

 ■The definition of “Total Debt” is not exact, but most commonly it includes both
    borrowings (current & non-current) and lease liabilities (current & non-current).

 ■A higher gearing ratio indicates that the company is more leveraged and has a higher
     level of financial risk.

 ■A gearing ratio above 50% is generally considered high, but this can vary by industry.





                                                                                                                                                    99 / 124

--- Session 8, page 107 (combined page 966) ---
Interest coverage ratio





  ■Interest coverage ratio measures a company’s ability to meet its interest payments on
    outstanding debt.

  ■It is calculated as...

                                               EBIT
                           Interest Coverage Ratio =
                                                        Interest Expense

 ■A higher interest coverage ratio indicates that the company is more capable of meeting
      its interest obligations.

 ■An interest coverage ratio below 1.5 may indicate potential financial distress.





                                                                                                                                                   100 / 124

--- Session 8, page 108 (combined page 967) ---
Debt to EBITDA




 ■Debt to EBITDA compares a company’s debt with the operating earnings available to
    repay it.

  ■It is calculated as...
                                                       Total Debt
                            Debt to EBITDA =
                                       EBITDA
  ■It can be read as the number of years of operating earnings needed to repay the debt.
     Unlike the gearing ratio, it does not depend on book equity.

 ■Lenders and rating agencies rely heavily on this ratio (e.g., in loan covenants). As a
    rough guide, below 2–3× is moderate and above 4–5× is high, but this varies by
     industry.





                                                                                                                                                   101 / 124

--- Session 8, page 109 (combined page 968) ---
Example - financial gearing ratios



  ■Let’s go back to Home Depot and Microsoft...


  Company     Total debt    Equity  Gearing ratio  Interest coverage  Debt to EBITDA

  Home Depot    $52.2bn    $1.0bn        98%            11.2×            2.1×
   Microsoft       $97.9bn  $268.5bn        27%            37.3×            0.7×

 ■Home Depot’s gearing is almost 100%! This is mostly because years of buybacks
     (partly funded with debt) have shrunk its equity. But relative to its earnings, its debt is
    moderate: about 2 times EBITDA, and its EBIT covers its interest expense 11 times.





 Total debt includes borrowings and lease liabilities (current and non-current). EBITDA is EBIT plus depreciation and amortization.
 Sources: Form 10-K filings of Home Depot (FY ended January 28, 2024) and Microsoft (FY ended June 30, 2024).


                                                                                                                                                   102 / 124

--- Session 8, page 110 (combined page 969) ---
Investment Ratios

--- Session 8, page 111 (combined page 970) ---
Investment ratios





  ■Investment ratios assess the attractiveness of a company’s shares to investors.

 ■They are used by investors to evaluate metrics such as earnings per share,
     price-to-earnings ratio, and dividend yield.

 ■We will look at five investment ratios: dividend-payout ratio, dividend yield, earnings per
     share, CFO per share, and price-to-earnings ratio.





                                                                                                                                                   103 / 124

--- Session 8, page 112 (combined page 971) ---
Dividend-payout ratio





  ■Dividend-payout ratio measures the proportion of earnings that a company distributes to
      its shareholders as dividends.

                                       Dividends announced for the year
                Dividend Payout Ratio =
                                        Earnings available for dividends

 ■A higher dividend-payout ratio indicates that the company is returning more profits to
    shareholders.

 ■A lower ratio suggests that the company is retaining more earnings for growth and
     reinvestment.





                                                                                                                                                   104 / 124

--- Session 8, page 113 (combined page 972) ---
Dividend-payout ratio





  ■Usually, this ratio is computed using net income attributable to ordinary shareholders
      (i.e., after preferred dividends, if any) in the denominator.

  ■Similarly, dividends used in the numerator should be only those paid to ordinary
    shareholders (i.e., excluding any preferred dividends).

 ■Companies disclose separately the dividends paid to NCI (if any) in the notes to the
     financial statements, so we need to be careful to exclude those amounts when
    computing this ratio.





                                                                                                                                                   105 / 124

--- Session 8, page 114 (combined page 973) ---
Dividend yield





  ■Dividend yield measures the return on investment that shareholders receive from
     dividends.
                                          Dividends per Share
                          Dividend Yield =
                                               Price per Share

 ■A higher dividend yield indicates that the company is providing a higher return to
    shareholders through dividends.

 ■Note that market price per share can fluctuate significantly over time, so this ratio may
    vary accordingly.





                                                                                                                                                   106 / 124

--- Session 8, page 115 (combined page 974) ---
Earnings per share (EPS)



  ■Earnings per share (EPS) measures the profitability of a company on a per-share basis.

                              Net income attributable to ordinary shareholders
           Earnings-per-Share =
                                    Outstanding shares (weighted average)

 ■A higher EPS indicates that the company is generating more profit for each share held
    by shareholders.

 ■We use a weighted average of the number of shares outstanding during a period,
    weighted by their time of issuance.
         • Because earnings accrues over time, we weight shares by the fraction of time of the
          reporting period that those shares were actually outstanding.

  ■Listed companies report basic and diluted EPS on the income statement and disclose
    the weighted average number of shares in the notes (IAS 33 / ASC 260).





                                                                                                                                                   107 / 124

--- Session 8, page 116 (combined page 975) ---
Earnings per share (EPS)





 ■EPS is very important because it is widely used by investors to assess a company’s
      profitability and to compare it with other companies in the same industry.

  ■It is also a key input in valuation models, such as the price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio.

 ■But it is not all! Warren Buffett said in 1979: “The primary test of managerial economic
    performance is the achievement of a high earnings rate on equity capital employed
     (without undue leverage, accounting gimmickry, etc.) and not the achievement of
     consistent gains in the earnings per share.”





                                                                                                                                                   108 / 124

--- Session 8, page 117 (combined page 976) ---
Example - Tesco



 ■The trajectory of ROCE for Tesco between 1998 and 2011 has not followed the upward
     trend in EPS...





 ■Tesco grew EPS by investing capital at falling returns. EPS growth is only good news if
    the return on the capital invested is high enough.


                                                                                                                                                   109 / 124

--- Session 8, page 118 (combined page 977) ---
Cash flow from operations per share (CFO per share)





 ■Cash flow from operations per share (CFO per share) measures the cash-generating
      ability of a company on a per-share basis.

                                  CFO
            CFO per Share =
                                  Outstanding shares (weighted average)

 ■A higher CFO per share indicates that the company is generating more cash for each
    share held by shareholders.

 ■This ratio is important because it provides insight into the company’s ability to generate
    cash, which is crucial for sustaining operations, paying dividends, and funding growth.





                                                                                                                                                   110 / 124

--- Session 8, page 119 (combined page 978) ---
Price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio





  ■Price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio measures the market’s valuation of a company’s earnings.

                                             Market price per share
                    Price-to-Earnings (P/E) ratio =
                                         EPS

 ■A higher P/E ratio indicates that investors are willing to pay more for each unit of
     earnings, which may suggest that they expect higher future growth or perceive lower
      risk.

  ■Conversely, a lower P/E ratio may indicate that the market has lower expectations for
    the company’s future performance or perceives higher risk.





                                                                                                                                                   111 / 124

--- Session 8, page 120 (combined page 979) ---
Case -----   : ?Amazon vs Walmart?

--- Session 8, page 121 (combined page 980) ---
Reference





 ■These slides are based on the Ivey Publishing case “Amazon vs. Walmart: Using
     financial ratios to compare companies” by Vineet Chetan Yagnik.





                                                                                                                                                   112 / 124

--- Session 8, page 122 (combined page 981) ---
Amazon vs. Walmart





 ■What is the context of this case?
         • An analyst is trying to compare Amazon and Walmart using financial ratios for the period
         2019-2020.

         • In particular, the analyst is trying to make the case that Amazon is a better investment than
         Walmart.

         • Is this “true”? Is Amazon a “better” Walmart?





                                                                                                                                                   113 / 124

--- Session 8, page 123 (combined page 982) ---
Amazon





 ■Amazon was founded in 1994 by Jeff Bezos, as a marketplace for books.

 ■Since then, Amazon grew a lot, becoming an online marketplace for almost all
    consumer goods.

  ■It also expanded to other areas, such as AWS, which is a cloud computing service.
         • AWS offers companies the opportunity to rent cloud space and pay only for what is used.

         •  It is a segment with very high margins but very asset heavy, since Amazon has to actually
         have the data centers and infrastructure necessary for this.





                                                                                                                                                   114 / 124

--- Session 8, page 124 (combined page 983) ---
Walmart





  ■Walmart was founded in 1962 with the idea of having “the lowest prices anywhere,
     anytime”.

  ■It started with one store in Arkansas and grew to 10,616 stores in 2024 around the
     world.

 ■As Amazon, Walmart also expanded to having an online marketplace which both sells
     directly to consumers as well as sells third party goods.





                                                                                                                                                   115 / 124

--- Session 8, page 125 (combined page 984) ---
DuPont analysis of ROE


 ■We can break ROE down into its components. Compared with ROCE (earlier in -----  ),
     there is a third one: financial gearing.

                      Net income     Revenue      Avg. total assets
           ROE =       ×          ×
                      Revenue    Avg. total assets     Avg. equity
                         |                              {z    }  |                                               {z                                                        }  |                                                                   {z      }                       Net margin                                     Asset                                               turnover                                                          Equity multiplier

 ■Net margin: how much of each $1 of sales is left for shareholders, after all expenses,
     interest and taxes.

 ■Asset turnover: how many dollars of sales each $1 of assets generates.

 ■Equity multiplier: how many dollars of assets each $1 of equity supports (financial
     gearing).

 ■So a company can increase its ROE by being more profitable, more efficient, or by
    borrowing more. The last one also increases risk.


                                                                                                                                                   116 / 124

--- Session 8, page 126 (combined page 985) ---
ROE comparison



 ■In 2019 and 2020, Amazon earned a higher ROE than Walmart. But the DuPont
    breakdown shows that they got there in very different ways:


     Company   Year    Net margin   Asset turnover   Equity multiplier   ROE

     Amazon     2019        4.1%              1.45                3.67   21.9%
                 2020        5.5%              1.41                3.51   27.4%

      Walmart     2019        2.9%              2.28                3.10   20.2%
                 2020        2.4%              2.27                3.14   17.4%

 ■Amazon earns a higher margin and uses more financial gearing. Walmart turns over its
    assets much faster.



 Averages of opening and closing total assets and equity. Net income and equity attributable to each company’s shareholders.
 Walmart’s fiscal year ends on January 31 of the following year (e.g., 2020 is the fiscal year ended January 31, 2021), and its revenue
 is net sales. Figures may not multiply exactly due to rounding. Sources: Form 10-K filings of Amazon.com, Inc. and Walmart Inc.


                                                                                                                                                   117 / 124

--- Session 8, page 127 (combined page 986) ---
Different business models



 ■The problem is that Amazon and Walmart have very different business models.

 ■Amazon also owns AWS, a cloud computing business: about an eighth of Amazon’s
     sales, but about 60% of its operating income.


                 AWS share of                Operating margin

       Amazon   Net sales   Op. income   AWS   N. America   International

        2019        12.5%       63.3%   26.3%        4.1%       −2.3%
        2020        11.8%       59.1%   29.8%        3.7%         0.7%

 ■So Amazon’s higher margin in the previous slide mostly reflects AWS. If we want to
    compare Amazon with Walmart, we should take ratios by segment.



 North America and International are Amazon’s retail segments. Operating margin is segment operating income divided by segment
 net sales. Source: Amazon.com, Inc., Form 10-K filings for 2019 and 2020.


                                                                                                                                                   118 / 124

--- Session 8, page 128 (combined page 987) ---
Ratios by retail segment





 ■Segments do not report net income or equity (financing and taxes are decided for the
    company as a whole).

 ■So at the segment level we use the operating part of the DuPont analysis:

                                 Operating income       Revenue
                Operating ROA =           ×
                                Revenue       Avg. segment assets
                                   |                                           {z                                                    }  |                                                                 {z                                                                            }                                 Operating                                        margin                                                     Asset                                                                turnover

 ■We will see that the ratios by retail segment tell a different story!





                                                                                                                                                   119 / 124

--- Session 8, page 129 (combined page 988) ---
Ratios by retail segment





                              Operating margin  Asset turnover   Operating ROA

       Segment               2019     2020  2019     2020    2019   2020

      Amazon North America    4.1%     3.7%   2.86      2.62   11.8%   9.6%
      Amazon International    −2.3%     0.7%   2.95      2.86  −6.7%   2.0%

       Walmart U.S.            5.1%     5.2%   3.17      3.31   16.1%  17.1%
       Walmart International     2.8%     3.0%   1.18      1.13    3.3%   3.4%

 ■Walmart U.S. earned a higher margin than Amazon North America.
 ■Walmart U.S. also turned over its assets faster, so its operating ROA was much higher.
   Why do you think that happens?


 Operating ROA = segment operating income / average segment assets = operating margin × asset turnover. Segment assets are
 as reported by each company (Amazon’s exclude corporate assets such as cash and goodwill). Operating leases came onto the
 balance sheet in 2019 (ASC 842), so the 2019 average includes them only at year-end. Walmart’s fiscal year ends on January 31 of
 the following year, and its revenue is net sales. Sources: Form 10-K filings of Amazon.com, Inc. and Walmart Inc.


                                                                                                                                                   120 / 124

--- Session 8, page 130 (combined page 989) ---
Asset intensity



Capex-to-Sales         2019   2020      ■That happens mainly because Amazon runs a
                                             dedicated fulfillment and delivery network for most ofAmazon              10.7%  15.0%
  North America        6.9%  12.6%                 its online orders (including many of those of third-party
  International          4.4%   7.7%              sellers).
 AWS               37.3%  36.4%
                                 ■Walmart fulfills many of its online orders from its
Walmart               2.1%   1.8%             existing stores, so its online business needs fewer
  Walmart U.S.         1.9%   1.7%
                                                    “additional assets”.
  Walmart International   2.3%   2.0%
                         ■One way to see that Walmart is relatively more asset
                                                           light than Amazon is to look at Capex-to-Sales, which
                                                            is much larger for Amazon.
                                ■This measures the amount of additional investment in
                                             assets that is needed to sustain operations.
 Capex-to-Sales = capital expenditure / net sales. For Amazon, capital expenditure is net additions to property and equipment, as
 reported by segment (including assets acquired under finance leases and similar arrangements, about $5–8bn a year in its retail
 segments). For Walmart, it is cash capital expenditure. Walmart’s fiscal year ends on January 31 of the following year. Sources:
 Form 10-K filings of Amazon.com, Inc. and Walmart Inc.

Today’s roadmap

--- Session 9, page 3 (combined page 997) ---
Pension Plans (not on the ----    )

--- Session 9, page 4 (combined page 998) ---
Why do we care about pension plans?



    1  They are really big.
         • Companies with a long history (airlines, telecoms, manufacturers...) have promised pensions
           to generations of employees.

         • These promises can be as large as the company itself! (next slide)

    2  They are debt in disguise.
         • A defined benefit plan is a promise to pay retirees for decades: economically, it is very
           similar to a bond.

         • Rating agencies add pension deficits to debt, and so do many companies (e.g. Lufthansa
          includes its net pension obligations in its “Adjusted net debt”).

    3  They use the company’s cash.
         • When the plan is in deficit, the company must top it up: this is cash that cannot be used for
          dividends, investment or debt repayment.

         • Example: in fiscal 2025, BT paid £840m into its pension scheme (£790m of it to reduce the
            deficit) which was close to 80% of its profit for the year (£1,077m).


                                                                                                                                                     3 / 121

--- Session 9, page 5 (combined page 999) ---
How big are pension plans?





                                 DB   Net deficit   Obligation /   Contributions /
                FY2025                obligation     (surplus)    market cap       net income

                    Lufthansa (ebn)              20.7           1.9       206%          40%
               BT Group (£bn)               36.3           4.2       173%          78%
                 IAG (ebn)                    17.8           (2.0)        82%          1%
                   General Motors ($bn)         51.3           3.6        70%          7%
                  Boeing ($bn)                 51.4           4.3        30%          0%
                AT&T ($bn)                  30.6           2.0        18%          5%
                IBM ($bn)                    44.3           (0.5)        16%          0%
                 Siemens (ebn)               26.9           0.1        15%          2%
                     Unilever (ebn)                13.9           (3.8)        11%          2%
                    Ryanair (ebn)                  0.0           (0.0)        0%          0%

 ■For Lufthansa and BT, the pension obligation is larger than the market value of the whole company!
      Ryanair, a young company, has almost none.
  ■Plan assets cover most of the obligation, so net deficits look small... but the company still bears the risk on the
     gross amounts.


 Fiscal years ending in 2025 (BT and Ryanair: March 2026; Siemens: September 2025). Defined benefit pensions only (AT&T: US plans only). Net deficit after the asset ceiling; brackets
 indicate a surplus. Contributions are employer contributions to the plans (excluding pensions paid directly on unfunded plans, e.g. IBM $0.6bn). Market capitalisation at fiscal year-end.
 Net income attributable to shareholders includes one-off disposal gains at Siemens, Unilever and AT&T. Sources: annual reports, 10-Ks and 20-Fs.


                                                                                                                                                     4 / 121

--- Session 9, page 6 (combined page 1000) ---
What are the key issues with pension plans?




    1  They make equity volatile.
         • Pensions are paid over decades, so the present value of the obligation is very sensitive to
          the discount rate.

         • Rule of thumb: with a duration of 15–20 years, a 1 p.p. change in the discount rate moves
          the obligation by roughly 15–20%.

         • These remeasurements go to OCI, so they affect equity (and not profit).

    2  They rest on assumptions.
         • Discount rate, life expectancy, future salary and pension increases... and, under US GAAP,
          the expected return on plan assets.

         • All of these are estimated by management (with the help of actuaries), and small changes
         can move the obligation by billions.

We will come back to these issues later, but first let’s see some examples of just how
 important they are...




                                                                                                                                                     5 / 121

--- Session 9, page 7 (combined page 1001) ---
Example - Lucent, equity volatility





                            $bn, at 30 September     1999    2000    2001    2002

                             Plan assets                 41.1     45.3     35.5     28.6
                            Pension obligation           27.4     26.1     29.9     30.3

                           Surplus (deficit)            13.7     19.1       5.7      (1.7)

                              Discount rate           7.25%    7.5%    7.0%    6.5%

  ■Remember Lucent (Session 5)? In 2000, its pension plan held $45.3bn of assets... almost as much as
      Lucent’s own total assets ($47.5bn)!
 ■In just two years, the surplus went from $19.1bn to a deficit of $1.7bn: a swing of almost $21bn!
 ■Why? The stock market fell (the plan lost $6.8bn in FY2001 and $2.5bn in FY2002), discount rates fell, and
      the layoffs added $2.0bn of termination benefits to the obligation.





 Pension plans only. Sources: Lucent’s FY2001 10-K (Note 12) and FY2002 Annual Report (Note 11). The FY2002 report restates FY2001 slightly (surplus $5.5bn).


                                                                                                                                                     6 / 121

--- Session 9, page 8 (combined page 1002) ---
Example - Lucent, equity volatility (continued)





                         $bn, at 30 September                 2000    2001    Change

                          Surplus                                  19.1       5.7        (13.5)
                           Less: gains not yet recognised             (14.5)      (1.8)
                             Plus: other unrecognised items              1.8       1.1

                       Pension asset on the balance sheet        6.4       5.0          (1.4)

  ■Under the rules of the time (US GAAP before 2006), gains and losses on the plan were unrecognised: kept
       off the balance sheet, and only amortised slowly into profit.
 ■So, in FY2001, the surplus fell by $13.5bn... but the pension asset on the balance sheet fell by only $1.4bn.
    The off-balance sheet “unrecognised gains” absorbed the rest!





 Other unrecognised items: prior service cost and transition asset. Source: Lucent’s FY2001 10-K (Note 12).

--- Session 9, page 9 (combined page 1003) ---
Example - Lucent, equity volatility (continued)



 ■Today (US GAAP since 2006, and IFRS), the balance sheet shows the full surplus or
     deficit, and gains and losses go through OCI (straight into equity).


                            Lucent’s pension asset, $bn    As reported    Today’s rules

                              At 30 September 2000                     6.4              19.1
                              At 30 September 2001                     5.0                5.7

                       Change in FY2001                          (1.4)              (13.5)

 ■In FY2001 alone, about $12bn more would have hit Lucent’s equity, which is almost half of its equity at the
       start of the year ($26.2bn)!
 ■And in FY2002, the full $7.2bn fall into deficit would have been recognised (under the old rules, only $2.9bn
    was charged to equity, through a special “minimum pension liability” rule).





 Before deferred taxes. Sources: Lucent’s FY2001 10-K (Note 12) and FY2002 Annual Report (Note 11).

--- Session 9, page 10 (combined page 1004) ---
Example - Lucent, assumptions



 ■In FY2001, Lucent’s pension plan lost $6.8bn... and yet pensions and retiree health
    added about $1bn to its pre-tax income. How?


               $m                                  FY2001    FY2002    FY2003

                     Expected return on plan assets (assumed)      9.0%      9.0%     8.75%
                    Net pension and retiree health credit          1,083       972       669

 ■Under US GAAP, income includes the return Lucent expected to earn on the plan that
    year (9%) (the gap with the actual return is a remeasurement). In FY2001, it booked an
    expected return of $3.4bn, while the plan lost $6.8bn.
 ■Small changes in assumptions matter: according to Lucent, 0.5 p.p. on the expected
     return moves pre-tax income by $200m a year.





 Pensions and retiree health combined, excluding restructuring. Sources: Lucent’s FY2001 10-K (Note 12), and FY2002 and FY2003 Annual Reports (MD&A).


                                                                                                                                                     9 / 121

--- Session 9, page 11 (combined page 1005) ---
Types of pension plans




 ■A pension plan is a promise: the company pays its employees after they retire, in return
      for their work today.

 ■There are two main types of pension plans...
           1  Defined contribution (DC): the company promises to pay a fixed contribution into a fund
        each year (e.g., 5% of salary). The pension the employee gets depends on how the
          investments perform.

           2  Defined benefit (DB): the company promises a fixed pension at retirement, usually based
        on salary and years of service (e.g., 1/60 of the final salary for each year of service). The
        company must make sure there is enough money to pay it.

 ■So, the key difference is who bears the risk: in a DC plan, the employee... in a DB
     plan, the company (investment returns, interest rates, life expectancy...).

                                                                                                                                                    11 / 121

--- Session 9, page 13 (combined page 1007) ---
Pension plans around the world (continued)





 ■Over the past decades, there has been a clear shift from DB to DC plans.

 ■Why? DB plans are riskier for employers and a much larger burden than DC plans: the
    company bears the investment, interest rate and longevity risks, and the deficit sits on
      its balance sheet.

  ■Still, there is a large variation across countries. This reflects:
         • Path dependence: how pensions were first adopted in each country. Countries that created
         funded systems recently (e.g., Chile, Australia) chose DC... while countries with a long
           tradition of DB plans (e.g., Canada, Finland) still carry them.

         • Different legal mandates for workplace pensions.





                                                                                                                                                    12 / 121

--- Session 9, page 14 (combined page 1008) ---
Accounting for defined contribution plans




 ■The accounting for DC plans is simple!

 ■The company’s obligation is just the contribution for the year... so the contribution is
    recognised as an expense in P&L (employee costs), in the year in which employees
    work.

  ■If the contribution has not been paid by the end of the year, the company recognises a
      liability (and if it has paid too much, an asset).

 ■Once the contribution is paid, the company has no further obligation:
         • No actuaries, no assumptions, no remeasurements.

         •  If the fund performs badly, it is the employees’ problem... not the company’s.





                                                                                                                                                    13 / 121

--- Session 9, page 15 (combined page 1009) ---
Example




 ■Company X has a DC plan: each year, it pays 5% of salaries into its employees’
    pension accounts.

 ■In 2025, salaries are £2,000,000.

 ■By 31 December 2025, X has paid £80,000 into the plan. It will pay the rest in January
    2026.

 ■What are the journal entries?
         • Dr Pension expense 100,000 (employee costs, P&L) ; Cr Cash 80,000 ; Cr Pension
           contribution payable 20,000

 ■And what if the pension fund loses 10% of its value in 2026?
         • Nothing! X has no further obligation... the employees bear the loss.





                                                                                                                                                    14 / 121

--- Session 9, page 16 (combined page 1010) ---
Accounting for defined benefit plans (IFRS)



 ■The accounting for DB plans is much harder... the company must estimate today what it
      will pay over the next decades!

 ■On the balance sheet, the company recognises the net defined benefit liability:

                         Net DB liability = DBO −Plan assets


         • DBO (defined benefit obligation): the present value of the pensions earned by employees so
             far. It is estimated by actuaries, using assumptions: discount rate (the yield on high-quality
          corporate bonds), salary growth, life expectancy...

         • Only past service is counted: no new hires, and no future years of work...

         • Plan assets: the investments (shares, bonds, property...) held in a separate fund to pay the
          pensions, at fair value.

  ■If the plan assets are larger than the DBO, the company shows a net asset... but only
    up to the benefit it can actually get from it (the asset ceiling).


                                                                                                                                                    15 / 121

--- Session 9, page 17 (combined page 1011) ---
Accounting for defined benefit plans (IFRS)



 ■Each year, the cost of the plan is split into three pieces:


             Component     What is it?                               Where?

                Service cost       Pensions earned by employees this year (plus the   P&L (employee
                                          effect of any changes to the plan)                     costs)

               Net interest        Discount rate × net DB liability at the start of the    P&L (usually finance
                                   year                                                 costs)

              Remeasurements  Changes in assumptions and experience (actuarial  OCI
                                   gains and losses), and the return on plan assets
                               above or below the discount rate

  ■Remeasurements go to OCI and are never recycled to P&L.
  ■Contributions paid by the company reduce cash and the net DB liability... but they are not an expense.
 ■And the pensions paid by the fund to retirees? They reduce both the DBO and the plan assets... no effect on
      the net DB liability.





                                                                                                                                                    16 / 121

--- Session 9, page 18 (combined page 1012) ---
Example




 ■Company X has a DB plan. At 1 January 2025, the DBO is £1,000m and the plan
    assets are £800m.

 ■During 2025:
         • The current service cost is £60m, and the discount rate is 5%.

         • X pays contributions of £50m into the fund, and the fund pays pensions of £40m to retirees
          (both at the end of the year).

         • The actual return on plan assets is only £30m.

         • At 31 December 2025, the actuaries value the DBO at £1,100m.

 ■What goes to P&L? What goes to OCI? And what is the net DB liability at the end of the
    year?





                                                                                                                                                    17 / 121

--- Session 9, page 19 (combined page 1013) ---
Example





             £m                 DBO    Plan assets    Net DB liability    Where?

                 1 January 2025              1,000           800              200
                   Service cost                 60                             60    P&L
                      Interest (5%)                 50            40               10    P&L
                    Contributions paid by X                       50                 (50)    Cash
                  Pensions paid by the fund       (40)              (40)                –
                Remeasurements             30              (10)               40    OCI

                 31 December 2025          1,100           840              260

  ■Interest is computed at the discount rate on the opening balances: 5% × 1,000 on the DBO and 5% × 800 on
      the assets... so the net interest is 5% × 200 = 10.
 ■The DBO should have been 1,070 (= 1,000 + 60 + 50 −40)... but the actuaries say 1,100: an actuarial loss
      of 30.
 ■The assets actually earned 30, but P&L was credited with interest of 40: the difference is a remeasurement
     loss of 10.

--- Session 9, page 20 (combined page 1014) ---
Example - Lufthansa





 ■In 2022, interest rates rose sharply in Europe and so did the discount rates used for
    pensions.

  ■Lufthansa’s pension plans are a great example to learn three things:
           1  What happens when the discount rate assumption changes?

           2  What happens when the asset ceiling binds?

           3  What should we include in net debt?

                                                                                                                                                    20 / 121

--- Session 9, page 22 (combined page 1016) ---
Example - Lufthansa, where did the gain go?





                em, gain (loss)                                         2021      2022

                   P&L: service cost                                               (419)       (516)
                   P&L: net interest                                                    (82)        (83)
                      Total P&L                                                      (501)       (599)

                   OCI: actuarial gains on the DBO                              1,919      7,858
                   OCI: return on plan assets above (below) the interest credited     1,132     (2,784)
                   OCI: effect of the asset ceiling                                   (121)       (426)
                      Total OCI                                                  2,930      4,648

 ■P&L: a pension expense of e599m, about the same as in 2021 (e501m). The discount rate barely touched
       profit because these P&L entries are defined at the beginning of the year.
  ■OCI: a gain of e4.6bn (e3.0bn after tax) straight into equity mainly driven by the discount rate.
  ■Lufthansa’s equity almost doubled, from e4.5bn to e8.5bn: pensions added e3.0bn... almost four times the
       profit for the year (e804m)!





 Service cost includes past service cost (2021: gain of 89; 2022: cost of 19). Before tax. Equity includes minority interests. Source: Lufthansa Annual Report 2022, T113 (p.213) and
 p.48.


                                                                                                                                                    21 / 121

--- Session 9, page 23 (combined page 1017) ---
Example - Lufthansa, the asset ceiling





                   Swiss plans, em, at 31 December                       2021      2022

                      Discount rate (Switzerland)                             0.3%      2.4%

                     Plan assets (Switzerland)                                  4,119      3,865
                      Less: pension obligation, DBO (Switzerland)                  (3,968)     (3,307)
                    Surplus (Switzerland)                                  151       558
                      Less: effect of the asset ceiling (Switzerland)                   (121)       (560)

                    Net asset (liability) on the balance sheet (Switzerland)        30          (2)

  ■Higher rates increased the surplus of Lufthansa’s Swiss plans to e558m.
 ■But Lufthansa cannot get this money back: the surplus “cannot be withdrawn from the plan through payouts or
      future contributions [...] that are less than the service cost”. So the asset ceiling binds... and the surplus is not
     recognised at all.





 Source: Lufthansa Annual Report 2022, T111 (p.212), T113 (p.213) and T115 (p.215).


                                                                                                                                                    22 / 121

--- Session 9, page 24 (combined page 1018) ---
Example - Lufthansa, net debt





                              em, at 31 December       2021     2022

                                    Net indebtedness           8,776     6,624
                                    Net pension obligations      6,540     1,993

                                   Adjusted net debt        15,316     8,617

  ■Lufthansa adds its net pension obligations to its net debt... and sets its gearing target on this “Adjusted net
      debt” (Adjusted net debt / Adjusted EBITDA below 3.5x).
 ■Why? Pensions are a fixed promise to pay cash in the future, on which interest accrues.
  ■Rating agencies make similar adjustments.





 Net indebtedness includes lease liabilities, and treats 50% of the 2015 hybrid bond as equity. Source: Lufthansa Annual Report 2022, T011 (p.25).

--- Session 9, page 25 (combined page 1019) ---
Example - IAG/BA





 ■In 2018, British Airways (part of IAG) closed its main defined benefit plan (NAPS) to
     future accrual and moved its employees to a new defined contribution plan.

 ■With IAG/BA example, we can see two things:
           1  What happens when a company changes its pension plan?

           2 How should we treat one-off pension gains when we analyse profit?





                                                                                                                                                    24 / 121

--- Session 9, page 26 (combined page 1020) ---
Example - IAG/BA, changing the plan





                         NAPS closure, em, 2018

                                   Reduction in the DBO (past service gain)     872
                                     Less: transition costs paid to members       (192)

                                 Net pension credit in P&L                678

 ■Why did the DBO fall? Before the closure, the DBO assumed that NAPS pensions would grow with future pay
      rises and promotions. After the closure, benefits are frozen at current levels, so they only grow with inflation
      (CPI, up to 5% a year).
 ■A change to the plan is a past service cost (here, a gain) and it goes straight to P&L, in employee costs.
  ■Differently from remeasurements that we saw in the Lufthansa example, changes to the plan are recognised
     immediately in the income statement.





 Remeasurement loss before tax. Source: IAG Full Year Results 2018, notes 4 and 30.


                                                                                                                                                    25 / 121

--- Session 9, page 27 (combined page 1021) ---
Example - IAG/BA, one-off gains





                         IAG, em, 2018

                         Operating profit before exceptional items                         3,230
                  NAPS closure: net pension credit                              678
                         Other exceptional items (BA restructuring, pension equalisation)     (230)

                       Operating profit after exceptional items                        3,678

 ■The e678m gain is a one-off gain. So IAG reported it as an exceptional item, and focused on its profit “before
      exceptional items”.
 ■As analysts, we should exclude such gains when we assess recurring profit and check that companies do the
     same!
 ■In this case, IAG marked the gain as exceptional.





 Source: IAG Full Year Results 2018, notes 4 and 30.


                                                                                                                                                    26 / 121

--- Session 9, page 28 (combined page 1022) ---
Accounting for pension plans under US GAAP





  ■Defined contribution plans: the accounting is the same as under IFRS. The
     contribution is an expense in the year in which employees work.

  ■Defined benefit plans: the balance sheet is (almost) the same, but the income
    statement is quite different.
         • Balance sheet: similar. Since 2006, US GAAP also shows the full deficit (or surplus) of the
           plan, called the funded status. The obligation is called the PBO (projected benefit
           obligation), and is measured like the DBO.

         • Income statement: different, mainly because of the expected return on plan assets.





                                                                                                                                                    27 / 121

--- Session 9, page 29 (combined page 1023) ---
Accounting for pension plans under US GAAP





 ■The key difference between US GAAP and IFRS is related to the calculation of the
    pension expense in P&L.

 ■Under IFRS, as we saw before, the return on plan assets is credited to P&L at the
     discount rate.

 ■Under US GAAP, the return on plan assets is credited to P&L at the expected return,
    which is an assumption chosen by management (usually based on the long-term
    expected return of the plan’s investments).

 ■Under both IFRS and US GAAP, the interest cost on the obligation is calculated at the
     discount rate.





                                                                                                                                                    28 / 121

--- Session 9, page 30 (combined page 1024) ---
US GAAP vs IFRS: defined benefit plans





                  IFRS (IAS 19)                    US GAAP (ASC 715)

  Balance sheet      Net DB liability (DBO −plan assets), limited  Funded status (PBO −plan assets), no
                    by the asset ceiling                        asset ceiling

  Return on plan       Interest income at the discount rate       Expected return on plan assets (an
  assets in P&L                                              assumption chosen by management)

  Actuarial gains and  OCI, never recycled to P&L               OCI, then gradually amortised into P&L (if
  losses                                                          outside a “corridor”)

  Plan amendments  P&L immediately                         OCI, then amortised into P&L over the
  (past service cost)                                           employees’ remaining service





 Corridor: accumulated gains and losses above 10% of the larger of the PBO and the plan assets are amortised over the employees’ average remaining service. Companies can also
 choose to recognise gains and losses immediately in P&L (e.g., AT&T).


                                                                                                                                                    29 / 121

--- Session 9, page 31 (combined page 1025) ---
Example




  ■Let’s go back to company X and its DB plan.

 ■Same facts as before:
         • At 1 January 2025, the obligation (PBO or DBO) is £1,000m and the plan assets are £800m.

         • Service cost £60m, discount rate 5%, contributions £50m, pensions paid £40m, actual return
        on plan assets £30m.

         • At 31 December 2025, the actuaries value the PBO at £1,100m.

 ■In addition, under US GAAP, X assumes an expected return on plan assets of 7%.

 ■For simplicity, there are no gains or losses accumulated in OCI from previous years.

 ■What changes relative to IFRS?





                                                                                                                                                    30 / 121

--- Session 9, page 32 (combined page 1026) ---
Example





                       £m, 2025                               IFRS   US GAAP

                            Service cost                               60          60
                                Interest cost on the obligation (5% × 1,000)      50          50
                           Return on plan assets credited to P&L            (40)           (56)

                        Pension expense in P&L                    70          54
                        Remeasurements in OCI                     40          56

                             Total (P&L + OCI)                         110         110

                          Net DB liability at 31 December               260         260

 ■Same balance sheet and same total cost (110), but a different split between P&L and OCI.
 ■IFRS credits P&L with interest on the plan assets at the discount rate (5% × 800 = 40). US GAAP uses the
     expected return (7% × 800 = 56).





                                                                                                                                                    31 / 121

--- Session 9, page 33 (combined page 1027) ---
Introduction to financial institutions
          (on the ----    )

--- Session 9, page 34 (combined page 1028) ---
Introduction




  ■Financial institutions are unique in both the services they perform and the level of
     regulatory oversight they face.

 ■As we will see, the financial statements of these institutions also have some unique
     features.

  ■Understanding these features is crucial for accurate financial analysis and comparison
     within the sector.

 ■As motivation, note that the many regulations regarding financial institutions rely on
    measures from their financial statements...
         • In the US, the CAMELS framework is used by banking regulators to assess the overall
          condition of financial institutions.

         • In Europe, the SREP framework is used for a similar purpose.





                                                                                                                                                    32 / 121

--- Session 9, page 35 (combined page 1029) ---
CAMELS Framework





 ■The CAMELS framework is a rating system used by banking regulators to assess the
     overall condition of financial institutions.

  ■It evaluates six key areas:
           1  Capital adequacy

           2  Asset quality

           3  Management quality

           4  Earnings

           5  Liquidity

           6  Sensitivity to market risk





                                                                                                                                                    33 / 121

--- Session 9, page 36 (combined page 1030) ---
SREP Framework





 ■The Supervisory Review and Evaluation Process (SREP) is used by European banking
     regulators to assess banks’ risks and capital adequacy.

  ■It is similar to CAMELS but tailored for the European regulatory environment.

 ■SREP evaluates:
         • Business model analysis

         • Internal governance and risk management

         • Risks to capital

         • Risks to liquidity and funding





                                                                                                                                                    34 / 121

--- Session 9, page 37 (combined page 1031) ---
The financial statements of banks

--- Session 9, page 38 (combined page 1032) ---
Financial intermediaries





  ■Differently from non-financial corporations, banks are financial intermediaries.

 ■So our analysis in the income statement has to shift from “sales and COGS” to net
     interest income and non-interest income (fees, trading, commissions).

 ■On the balance sheet, we focus on:
         • Loans and investment securities on the asset side.

         • Deposits and other funding on the liability side.

 ■Because timing of cash flows and liquidity matter more than an operating cycle, banks
     usually present assets by order of liquidity rather than current/non-current.





                                                                                                                                                    35 / 121

--- Session 9, page 39 (combined page 1033) ---
The balance sheet of a bank





 ■Let us look at the balance sheet of a bank.

 ■We will look at the balance sheets of...
           1  Bank of America, a large US bank.

           2  Heartland Financial USA, a regional US bank.

           3 BNP Paribas, a large European bank.

                                                                                                                                                    37 / 121

--- Session 9, page 41 (combined page 1035) ---
BNP Paribas - Leases under IFRS





 ■Where do finance leases go for BNP Paribas under IFRS (i.e. BNP is a lessor in a
     finance lease)?
         • They go within loans and advances to customers (i.e. within assets).

 ■Where do operating leases go for BNP Paribas under IFRS (i.e. BNP is a lessor in an
    operating lease)?
         • The underlying asset stays within PP&E.

 ■Where do ROU assets go for BNP Paribas under IFRS (i.e. BNP is a lessee in an
    operating lease)?
         • They are placed within BNP’s PP&E.

--- Session 9, page 43 (combined page 1037) ---
Bank of America - Assets





 ■Note that for BofA, “Customer receivables” and “Other assets” are placed below
    “Goodwill” and “Premises and equipment”.

 ■This is because BofA presents its main assets in terms of liquidity, but leaves
    heterogeneous items like customer receivables at the bottom of the list.

 ■For listed US banks (like BofA and Heartland), there is an SEC regulation that sets the
    balance sheet captions and their order.

 ■In contrast, BNP Paribas presents all of its assets in terms of liquidity ordering.

--- Session 9, page 45 (combined page 1039) ---
What you usually find in the assets of a bank





 ■Cash and cash equivalents (including reserves at central banks).

  ■This is the most liquid asset a bank can have.

  ■It includes:
         • Vault cash that is the currency and coin needed to meet customer withdrawals.

         • Deposits held at the central bank that can be used to meet reserve requirements and settle
          interbank transactions.

         • Deposits at other financial institutions, which are used to purchase services from those
            institutions.





                                                                                                                                                    42 / 121

--- Session 9, page 46 (combined page 1040) ---
What you usually find in the assets of a bank





  ■Investment securities.

 ■These are debt and equity securities that the bank holds for investment purposes.

 ■They can be classified as held-to-maturity, available-for-sale, or trading securities,
    depending on the bank’s intent and ability to hold them.

  ■Investment securities are highly liquid, have low default risk, and can usually be sold in
     active markets.





                                                                                                                                                    43 / 121

--- Session 9, page 47 (combined page 1041) ---
What you usually find in the assets of a bank





  ■Investment securities are mostly held to manage liquidity and interest rate risk.

 ■Because their return is usually lower than that of loans and leases, banks usually hold a
     limited amount of these securities.

 ■These securities can include government bonds, corporate bonds, mortgage-backed
     securities, and equity securities.

 ■These can either be short-term or long-term, depending on the bank’s investment
     strategy.





                                                                                                                                                    44 / 121

--- Session 9, page 48 (combined page 1042) ---
What you usually find in the assets of a bank





 ■Loans and advances to customers (including finance leases).

  ■This is the largest asset category for most banks.

  ■It includes various types of loans, such as commercial loans, consumer loans,
    mortgage loans, and credit card loans.

 ■Banks earn interest income from these loans, which is a major source of revenue.

 ■These are also the largest source of credit risk for banks, as borrowers may default on
     their loan payments.





                                                                                                                                                    45 / 121

--- Session 9, page 49 (combined page 1043) ---
What you usually find in the assets of a bank





 ■We can broadly categorize loans into:
         • Commercial and industrial loans: loans to businesses for working capital, equipment
         purchases, or expansion.
         • Consumer loans: loans to individuals for personal expenses, such as auto loans, -------
          loans, and personal lines of credit.

         • Mortgage loans: loans secured by real estate properties, including residential and
         commercial mortgages.





                                                                                                                                                    46 / 121

--- Session 9, page 50 (combined page 1044) ---
Allowances for credit losses





 ■Banks usually set aside allowances for expected credit losses on these loans, which
    reduce their carrying value on the balance sheet.

 ■These allowances for credit losses are presented as a contra-asset account, reducing
     the gross amount of loans and advances to reflect the estimated losses from defaults.
         • Under US GAAP, you usually see this line separately as “Allowance for loan and lease
           losses”.

         • Under IFRS, you usually see the carrying amounts of loans net of “Expected credit losses”.

  ■This allowance is based on historical loss experience, current economic conditions, and
    management’s judgment about future trends.





                                                                                                                                                    47 / 121

--- Session 9, page 51 (combined page 1045) ---
What you usually find in the assets of a bank





  ■Derivative assets: financial instruments that derive their value from an underlying
     asset, such as interest rates, foreign exchange rates, or commodities.

  ■Trading assets: financial instruments held for short-term trading purposes, such as
     stocks, bonds, and derivatives.

 ■Other assets, like PP&E, intangibles, deferred taxes...





                                                                                                                                                    48 / 121

--- Session 9, page 52 (combined page 1046) ---
The liabilities side




  ■Broadly, the liabilities of a bank consist of:
           1  Deposits from customers: often a stable and low-cost source of funding for banks, as they
         pay lower interest rates than other types of borrowings.

           2  Short-term debt (excluding deposits, but including borrowings from other banks/central
         banks, repos, commercial paper...).

           3  Long-term debt issued to investors.

           4  Trading liabilities and derivative liabilities.

           5  Other liabilities, like deferred taxes and accrued expenses.

 ■These accounts are usually ordered by liquidity, where liquidity captures how quickly the
    bank might have to pay out to settle them.
