--- Session 9, page 56 (combined page 1050) ---
What you usually find in the liabilities of a bank





  ■Deposits from customers.

  ■This is the largest liability category for most banks.

  ■It includes various types of deposits:
         • Demand deposits: transaction accounts held by individuals, corporations, and governments
           that can be withdrawn on demand and pay no explicit interest.

         • Savings deposits: non-transaction accounts that earn interest and have limited withdrawal
           capabilities.

         • Time deposits: fixed-term deposits that earn higher interest rates but cannot be withdrawn
          before maturity without penalty.





                                                                                                                                                    53 / 121

--- Session 9, page 57 (combined page 1051) ---
What you usually find in the liabilities of a bank





  ■Short- and long-term debt.

 ■These are short-term and long-term borrowings that banks obtain from other banks, the
     central bank, or the capital markets.

 ■They can include central banks’ funds purchased, repurchase agreements, commercial
     paper, and bonds.

 ■These borrowings are usually more expensive than deposits, as they often carry higher
     interest rates and fees.





                                                                                                                                                    54 / 121

--- Session 9, page 58 (combined page 1052) ---
Off-balance sheet items



  ■Off-balance sheet items are commitments and guarantees (e.g. undrawn credit lines,
     letters of credit) that may become assets or liabilities in the future, depending on
     certain events (e.g. the client draws on the line, or defaults).

 ■These items are not recorded on the balance sheet, but they are disclosed in the notes
     to the financial statements.
         • Note that banks still book a provision for the expected credit losses on these items (e.g.
          BofA’s $1.1bn reserve for unfunded lending commitments at the end of 2024).

 ■Examples of off-balance sheet items include loan commitments and letters of credit.

 ■Note that these are not the contingent liabilities we saw in Session 4. This is because
     they are financial instruments and so follow the expected credit loss model, which books
    a provision for expected losses even when a loss is not probable.
         • Remember that for contingent liabilities such as the ones in Session 4 (like a lawsuit) we
          only book a provision when the outflow is probable.




                                                                                                                                                    55 / 121

--- Session 9, page 59 (combined page 1053) ---
Off-balance sheet items




  ■Off-balance sheet items can have significant implications for a bank’s risk profile and
     capital adequacy.
         • This is because they may expose the bank to credit risk, market risk, and liquidity risk.

         • Note that capital adequacy is about the size and quality of a bank’s loss-absorbing cushion
           relative to its risks.

         • Off-balance sheet items can create additional risks that may require the bank to hold more
           capital.

 ■Both under IFRS and US GAAP, banks are required to disclose their off-balance sheet
    items in the notes to the financial statements.
         • This includes both the nature and extent of these items,

         • as well as any associated risks and uncertainties.





                                                                                                                                                    56 / 121

--- Session 9, page 60 (combined page 1054) ---

                                                                                                                                                    58 / 121

--- Session 9, page 62 (combined page 1056) ---
Within off-balance sheet items...





 ■Loan commitments (or financing commitments): these are agreements by the bank to
     lend a certain amount of money to a borrower in the future, subject to certain conditions.

 ■Loan commitments can be either revocable or irrevocable, and they can expose the
    bank to credit risk if the borrower defaults on the loan.

 ■The loan commitment only goes to the balance sheet when the loan is drawn down by
     the borrower.





                                                                                                                                                    59 / 121

--- Session 9, page 63 (combined page 1057) ---
Within off-balance sheet items...





  ■Letters of credit (or guarantee commitments): these are guarantees by the bank to pay
    a third party on behalf of a customer, subject to certain conditions.

  ■Letters of credit can be either commercial or standby, and they can expose the bank to
     credit risk if the customer fails to fulfill their obligations.

 ■The letter of credit only goes to the balance sheet when the bank makes a payment
    under the guarantee.





                                                                                                                                                    60 / 121

--- Session 9, page 64 (combined page 1058) ---
Example - Lehman Brothers


 ■What happens when liabilities are kept off the balance sheet and not disclosed at all?

 ■Lehman Brothers was a large US investment bank that went bankrupt in September
    2008.

 ■In 2007–2008, investors were watching its leverage very closely...

 ■So at the end of each quarter, Lehman used a trick called “Repo 105” to make its
    balance sheet look smaller.
         • In a repo, a bank gives securities to a counterparty, receives cash, and promises to buy the
           securities back a few days later.

         • So the securities stay on the balance sheet and the bank has a liability because of its
         promise to repurchase them.

         • Lehman gave $105 of securities for every $100 of cash, but used this to book these repos as
          sales: so the securities left the balance sheet and there was no liability recorded.

         •  It used the cash to pay down other debt and bought the securities back 7 to 10 days after the
          quarter-end.


                                                                                                                                                    61 / 121

--- Session 9, page 65 (combined page 1059) ---
Example - Lehman Brothers, ordinary repo vs Repo 105




                                              Ordinary repo           Repo 105

                                         Assets     Liabilities    Assets      Liabilities

           Before the repo                     1,000       950        1,000        950
             Securities handed over              –          –          (105)         –
          Cash received                   +100        –        +100         –
            Obligation to repay the 100           –        +100        –      not booked
            Right to buy the securities back        –          –         +5          –
          Pay 100 of other liabilities             (100)        (100)        (100)         (100)

            After the payment                 1,000       950        900        850
          Leverage (assets / equity of 50)           20x                   18x

 ■In both cases, Lehman receives 100 of cash and uses it to pay 100 of other liabilities.
  ■With an ordinary repo, the obligation to repay the 100 is a liability: one liability replaces another, the original
      securities stay on the balance sheet, and leverage does not change.
  ■With Repo 105, Lehman does not book this obligation and the securities leave the balance sheet. So assets
     and liabilities both fall by 100 and leverage looks lower.
  Illustrative numbers. Lehman handed over $105 of securities for $100 of cash, and booked the difference as a $5 derivative. Source: Examiner’s Report (A. Valukas), 2010, Volume 3,
 pp. 759–781.


                                                                                                                                                    62 / 121

--- Session 9, page 66 (combined page 1060) ---
Example - Lehman Brothers (continued)





      Quarter-end      Repo 105     Reported net leverage     Net leverage without Repo 105

      November 2007      $38.6bn              16.1x                          17.8x
      February 2008       $49.1bn              15.4x                          17.3x
     May 2008           $50.4bn              12.1x                          13.9x


 ■Lehman never told investors about Repo 105. Internally, staff called it “window-dressing”
    and “another drug we r on”.

  ■After the bankruptcy, the court-appointed examiner concluded that this made Lehman’s
     reports materially misleading.

 ■The lesson is that accounting leverage is a snapshot at the quarter-end and it can be
    managed!





 Net leverage is Lehman’s own measure (net assets / tangible equity). Source: Report of the Examiner (A. Valukas) in the Lehman Brothers bankruptcy, 2010, Volume 3, pp. 732–748.


                                                                                                                                                    63 / 121

--- Session 9, page 67 (combined page 1061) ---
What else do we find in the notes?





  ■Derivatives’ notionals: Derivative assets and liabilities are recorded on the balance
    sheet at fair value.

 ■The issue is that fair values can be a tiny amount relative to the notional value of a
     derivative.

  ■Relative to the fair value, the notional amount is more representative of the size of a
     derivative. Hence notionals are usually disclosed in the notes.

 ■Note that derivatives are on the balance sheet; it is just their notional amounts that are
     off-balance sheet.





                                                                                                                                                    64 / 121

--- Session 9, page 68 (combined page 1062) ---
Fee-generating activities





 ■Even by looking at the on- and off-balance sheet items, we still do not have the full
     picture of a bank’s activities.

 ■This is because banks engage in some other income-generating activities that do not
    generate interest income or expense.

 ■These activities include: trust & investment management services, processing services,
    and correspondent banking services for which banks receive payment in fees.

 ■You’d look for them in the income statement.





                                                                                                                                                    65 / 121

--- Session 9, page 69 (combined page 1063) ---
Fee-generating activities





 ■Trust & investment management services: banks provide fiduciary services to clients,
    such as managing their assets, estates, and retirement plans.

 ■Processing services: banks provide financial data processing services for their
    business customers.
         • These include managing a customer’s account receivables and account payables, and
           payroll processing.

 ■Correspondent banking services: banks provide services to other banks, such as
     clearing and settlement of payments, foreign exchange transactions, and trade finance.





                                                                                                                                                    66 / 121

--- Session 9, page 70 (combined page 1064) ---
The income statement of banks





 ■So let us look now at the income statement of banks.

 ■The income statement of banks is different from that of non-financial corporations, as it
    focuses on net interest income and non-interest income rather than sales and cost of
    goods sold.





                                                                                                                                                    67 / 121

--- Session 9, page 71 (combined page 1065) ---
The income statement of banks




  ■Interest income represents the revenue earned from loans and investment securities.
         •  It includes interest on loans to customers, interest on investment securities, and interest on
          deposits with other financial institutions.

  ■Interest expense represents the cost of funding for the bank.
         •  It includes interest on deposits from customers, interest on short-term debt, and interest on
          long-term debt.

 ■Net interest income is the difference between interest income and interest expense.
         •  It represents the core profitability of the bank’s lending and investing activities.

  ■Provision for credit losses: this is the amount set aside to cover expected credit
    losses on loans and advances to customers. It is a noncash expense.





                                                                                                                                                    68 / 121

--- Session 9, page 72 (combined page 1066) ---
Provision for credit losses





 ■Note that not all provisions for credit losses are tax deductible — this depends on the
     jurisdiction.

 ■Across many jurisdictions, provisions are not deductible for tax purposes until the
     related loan actually defaults.

 ■In this case, what does this create? A DTA or a DTL?

 ■A DTA, because the bank will be able to deduct the loss in the future when the loan
     defaults, reducing its taxable income at that time.





                                                                                                                                                    69 / 121

--- Session 9, page 73 (combined page 1067) ---
Example: Provision for credit losses in 2009



 ■During the financial crisis of 2008-2009, many banks increased their provisions for
     credit losses significantly, as they anticipated higher default rates on mortgages and
     other loans.

 ■This sharply reduced the profits of banks during that period.

 ■In the second quarter of 2009, BofA recorded a provision for credit losses of $13.4
      billion:
         • $8.7 billion covered the loans it wrote off during the quarter (net charge-offs),

         • and $4.7 billion increased its loan loss reserve.

 ■This provision was recorded in recognition of expected losses on mortgages and loans
     tied to the financial crisis, and was more than double the provision made 1 year before
    ($5.8 billion).





 Source: Bank of America, second quarter 2009 earnings release (Form 8-K of 17 July 2009).

                 Citigroup                  $17.4bn     39%       $(3.9)bn      (9%)
             JPMorgan Chase           $17.5bn     32%       $(9.3)bn     (18%)

                                                                                                                                                    72 / 121

--- Session 9, page 76 (combined page 1070) ---
Provisions and earnings quality




 ■Remember the “cookie jar reserves” from Session 4: provisions can be built up in good
    years and released in bad years to smooth earnings.

 ■With expected credit losses, banks do not wait for signs of trouble (e.g. a missed
    payment) to book a provision: from the day a loan is granted, they provision for the
    losses they expect, based on forecasts of the economy and on management’s
    judgement.
         • In practice, there is room for discretion.
         • The provisions are based on models, but management can adjust the model outputs to
            reflect their own judgement.
         • For example, at Barclays, management adjustments to its models (“overlays”) were 24% of
              its allowance at the end of 2021.
         • Usually these overlays are discussed in the annual report, but the depth of the disclosures
           varies.





                                                                                                                                                    73 / 121

--- Session 9, page 77 (combined page 1071) ---
Example - SunTrust, a cookie jar at a bank



 ■In 1998, SunTrust, a large US bank, was buying another bank (Crestar).

 ■While reviewing the documents of this deal, the SEC questioned SunTrust’s allowance
     for loan losses... it seemed larger than needed.

  ■SunTrust had to restate its results: it lowered its provisions for 1994–1996, and its
    allowance by $100 million (from $726 million to $626 million at the end of 1996).



                                  Provision for loan losses       Net income

                   $ million     Before         After        Before      After

                   1994         137.8         112.8         522.7      537.9
                   1995         112.1          77.1          565.5      586.8
                   1996         115.9          75.9          616.6      641.0





 Before and after the restatement. Sources: SunTrust Banks, Form 10-K for 1997 (original) and Form 10-K/A for 1997 (filed on 13 November 1998), Selected Financial Data; Form
 10-Q/A for the quarter ended 31 March 1998, Note 7.

--- Session 9, page 78 (combined page 1072) ---
The income statement of banks (what else?)





  ■Non-interest income includes all other income received by the bank as a result of its
      activities.

 ■This is the revenue earned from fee-based services (e.g. trust & investment
    management) and trading activities (e.g. derivatives and sales of loans).





                                                                                                                                                    75 / 121

--- Session 9, page 79 (combined page 1073) ---
The income statement of banks (what else?)





  ■Non-interest expense includes all operating expenses incurred by the bank in its
     day-to-day operations.

  ■It includes salaries and employee benefits, occupancy expenses, technology expenses,
    marketing expenses, professional fees, and other general and administrative expenses.

  ■It tends to be a large portion of a bank’s total expenses, as banks rely heavily on human
     capital and technology to deliver their services.

  ■Usually, it is larger than non-interest income.





                                                                                                                                                    76 / 121

--- Session 9, page 80 (combined page 1074) ---
The income statement of banks (what else?)





 ■Income before taxes represents the difference between total operating income and
      total expenses (including the provision for credit losses and non-interest expense).

  ■It represents the bank’s profitability before accounting for income taxes.

                                                                                                                                                    81 / 121

--- Session 9, page 85 (combined page 1079) ---
Some general observations...



  ■Financial institutions face two main types of risks: credit risk and interest rate risk.

  ■Credit risk is the risk of loss due to the failure of a borrower to repay their loan.
         • To account for this risk, banks set aside provisions for credit losses on their income
         statements and maintain allowances for credit losses on their balance sheets.

  ■Interest rate risk is the risk of loss due to changes in interest rates that affect the bank’s
    net interest income and the value of its assets and liabilities.
         • The key to managing this type of risk is to match the maturities and repricing characteristics
           of assets and liabilities.

         • From an outsider’s perspective, we can analyze the bank’s interest rate risk exposure by
          looking at the composition of its assets and liabilities on the balance sheet.

         • And also by looking at the sensitivity analysis disclosed in the notes to the financial
          statements.

         • Remember Session 6, when we discussed SVB and how it had about $15bn of unrealized
          losses on its held-to-maturity portfolio that did not appear on the balance sheet.


                                                                                                                                                    82 / 121

--- Session 9, page 86 (combined page 1080) ---
Some general observations...





 ■Look for information on the following items in the notes to the financial statements:
           1  Breakup of assets/liabilities by maturity or repricing buckets.

           2  Loan loss reserves and actual loan losses over time.

           3  “Non-performing” loans and troubled debt restructurings.

           4  Related party transactions.

           5  Off-balance sheet items and their associated risks.

           6  Contingent liabilities and commitments.





                                                                                                                                                    83 / 121

--- Session 9, page 87 (combined page 1081) ---
Analysing financial statements of
           banks

--- Session 9, page 88 (combined page 1082) ---
Different ratios for banks




 ■As we have seen, banks have different financial statements compared to non-financial
     corporations.

  ■Therefore, the analysis of banks also requires different ratios and metrics.

 ■For instance, it does not make sense to use ratios that depend on sales or COGS, as
    banks do not have these items.

  ■It also does not make sense to use EBIT/EBITDA, as banks main source of
    income/expenses comes from interest.

 ■To explore the financial statements of banks, we will focus on decomposing the ROE
    based on other ratios that are relevant for banks.





                                                                                                                                                    84 / 121

--- Session 9, page 89 (combined page 1083) ---
Step 1: ROE decomposition





 ■Decompose ROE into ROA and an Equity Multiplier:

                  Net Income (ordinary shareholders, OS)
        ROE =
                                  Equity (OS)
                  Net Income (OS)   Net Income     Assets
           =           ×       ×
                     Net Income       Assets     Equity (OS)
           = NCI & Preferred Dividends Burden × ROA × Equity Multiplier





                                                                                                                                                    85 / 121

--- Session 9, page 90 (combined page 1084) ---
Step 2: ROA decomposition





 ■Decompose ROA into Profit Margin and Asset Turnover:

                       Net Income
            ROA =
                          Assets
                            Net Income          Total Operating Income
              =              ×
                            Total Operating Income          Assets
              = Profit Margin × Asset Turnover

 ■In this case, Total Operating Income = Net Interest Income + Non-Interest Income.





                                                                                                                                                    86 / 121

--- Session 9, page 91 (combined page 1085) ---
Step 3: Profit Margin decomposition




 ■Decompose Profit Margin into Tax Burden, Credit-cost Burden, and Operating Margin:

                            Net Income
             Profit Margin =
                             Total Operating Income
                           Net Income           Income Before Taxes
              =             ×
                      Income Before Taxes   Pre-Provision Operating Income
                            Pre-Provision Operating Income
               ×
                                    Total Operating Income
              = Tax Burden × Credit-cost Burden × Operating Margin

    where Pre-Provision Operating Income = Total Operating Income - Non-Interest
    Expense.





                                                                                                                                                    87 / 121

--- Session 9, page 92 (combined page 1086) ---
Step 3: Profit Margin decomposition





 ■The tax burden captures how much of the income before taxes is kept after paying
    income taxes.

 ■The credit-cost burden captures how much of the bank’s everyday earnings gets
    “eaten” by expected credit losses.

 ■The operating margin captures how much of the bank’s operating income is kept after
    paying non-interest expenses. It captures both operating efficiency and pricing power.





                                                                                                                                                    88 / 121

--- Session 9, page 93 (combined page 1087) ---
Step 4: Asset Turnover decomposition





 ■The asset turnover can be further decomposed into the sum of the net interest income
     ratio and the non-interest income ratio:

                               Total Operating Income
          Asset Turnover =
                                 Assets
                         Net Interest Income   Non-Interest Income
               =            +
                                Assets               Assets
               = Net Interest Income Ratio + Non-Interest Income Ratio





                                                                                                                                                    89 / 121

--- Session 9, page 94 (combined page 1088) ---
Putting it all together





 ■We can put it all together in the following schedule...

        ROE = NCI & Preferred Dividends Burden × ROA × Equity Multiplier
           = NCI & Preferred Dividends Burden
           × Tax Burden × Credit-cost Burden × Operating Margin

           × Net Interest Income Ratio + Non-Interest Income Ratio
           × Equity Multiplier





                                                                                                                                                    90 / 121

--- Session 9, page 95 (combined page 1089) ---
Example





 ■We can now use this decomposition to analyse the performance of banks.

 ■We will use Bank of America, BNP Paribas and Heartland Financial USA as examples...





                                                                                                                                                    91 / 121

--- Session 9, page 96 (combined page 1090) ---
Example - ROE = NCI & Pref. Div. Burden x ROA x Equity Multiplier





          2024        ROE    NCI & Pref. Div. Burden    ROA      Equity Multiplier

          BofA           9.5%            94.0%            0.84%           12.0
           Heartland       8.6%            95.3%            0.94%            9.7
        BNP Paribas    9.6%            89.0%            0.46%           23.4


 ■In the analysis that follows, I will “ignore” the NCI & Preferred Dividends Burden for
     simplicity, as it is similar for all three banks (between 89% and 95%).

 ■What does the high equity multiplier of BNP Paribas tell us?

  ■It tells us that BNP Paribas is apparently more leveraged than the other two banks...





 For BNP Paribas, AT1 notes (perpetual bonds classified as equity) are treated like preferred stock. Source: 2024 annual reports (BofA 10-K; Heartland audited statements, filed by UMB
 Financial; BNP Paribas Universal Registration Document); own calculations.


                                                                                                                                                    92 / 121

--- Session 9, page 97 (combined page 1091) ---
Example - ROE = ROA x Equity Multiplier





 ■Is BNP Paribas indeed more leveraged than the other two banks?

  ■First, note that this accounting leverage is not the same as regulatory leverage...
         • Regulatory leverage considers risk-weighted assets rather than total assets, and adjusts for
          off-balance sheet exposures.

 ■Second, it is very common for European banks to appear more leveraged than US
    banks because of accounting presentation differences (IFRS vs US GAAP).





                                                                                                                                                    93 / 121

--- Session 9, page 98 (combined page 1092) ---
Example - ROE = ROA x Equity Multiplier





 ■Which accounting differences?

  ■Generally the way IFRS and US GAAP present netting effects of derivatives and
    repurchase agreements (repos) on the balance sheet.

 ■IFRS shows derivatives and repo-style trades more gross, while US GAAP more often
    nets them by counterparty.

 ■Gross assets and liabilities are therefore larger under IFRS, which pushes up the equity
     multiplier even if the underlying risk is similar.





                                                                                                                                                    94 / 121

--- Session 9, page 99 (combined page 1093) ---
Example - ROE = ROA x Equity Multiplier




  ■Consider this simple example...

 ■A bank has entered into a derivative contract with the same counterparty including a
     derivative asset, whose fair value is $50,000, and a derivative liability with a fair value of
    $45,000.

 ■Under IFRS (in most cases)1, the bank would report both the asset and the liability on
    the balance sheet, resulting in total assets of $50,000 and total liabilities of $45,000.

 ■Under US GAAP (in most cases), the bank would report a net derivative asset of $5,000
    on the balance sheet, resulting in total assets of $5,000 and total liabilities of $0.

 ■This netting effect deflates assets under US GAAP as compared to IFRS.


   1There are exceptions! Check out Appendix 17A of Chapter 17 in: “Intermediate Accounting IFRS Edition” by
 Donald E. Kieso, Jerry J. Weygandt, & Terry D. Warfield, 4e.

                                                                                                                                                    95 / 121

--- Session 9, page 100 (combined page 1094) ---
Example - ROE = ROA x Equity Multiplier





 ■Note that this also affects the ROA!

 ■In this case, ROA is lower under IFRS because assets are higher.

  ■Therefore, when comparing banks across jurisdictions, it is important to consider these
    accounting differences and their impact on financial ratios...

 ■Under IFRS, we can go to the note about “offsetting financial assets and financial
      liabilities” to find more information about netting effects.

 ■We can then use those numbers to adjust the balance sheet and recalculate the ratios
    on a more comparable basis.





                                                                                                                                                    96 / 121

--- Session 9, page 101 (combined page 1095) ---
Example - ROE = ROA x Equity Multiplier





                                                                                                                                                    97 / 121

--- Session 9, page 102 (combined page 1096) ---
Example - ROE = ROA x Equity Multiplier



 ■The adjusted ROA is 0.53% versus 0.46%; the adjusted equity multiplier is 20.3 versus
    23.4 (see row “BNP Paribas*”).

 ■Note that total equity does not change with this adjustment, as we are only changing
    the presentation of assets and liabilities (so ROE does not change).



         2024         ROE    NCI & Pref. Div. Burden    ROA      Equity Multiplier

         BofA           9.5%            94.0%            0.84%           12.0
           Heartland        8.6%            95.3%            0.94%            9.7
        BNP Paribas     9.6%            89.0%            0.46%           23.4
        BNP Paribas*     9.6%            89.0%            0.53%           20.3





 BNP Paribas*: total assets of e2,334bn (2024) and e2,257bn (2023) instead of e2,705bn and e2,591bn, after netting e371bn and e335bn. Source: BNP Paribas Universal
 Registration Document 2024, note 4.o.


                                                                                                                                                    98 / 121

--- Session 9, page 103 (combined page 1097) ---
Example - ROA = Profit Margin x Asset Turnover



 ■Now we can decompose ROA...

  ■Profit margins are quite similar across the three banks (between 24% and 27%): the
    main driver of ROA differences is the asset turnover.

 ■The profit margin measures how many dollars you keep after accounting for provisions
    and operating expenses, while the asset turnover measures how many dollars you
    generate for each dollar of assets.



                   2024         ROA       Profit Margin     Asset Turnover

                   BofA           0.84%       26.6%            3.2%
                     Heartland        0.94%       24.3%            3.8%
               BNP Paribas     0.46%       25.0%            1.8%
               BNP Paribas*     0.53%       25.0%            2.1%





                                                                                                                                                    99 / 121

--- Session 9, page 104 (combined page 1098) ---
Example - Profit Margin = Tax Burden x Credit-cost Burden x Operating
Margin





     2024               Profit Margin     Tax Burden     Credit-cost Burden     Operating Margin

     BofA              26.6%          92.7%            83.4%               34.4%
      Heartland          24.3%          78.0%            80.5%               38.7%
    BNP Paribas        25.0%          75.3%            86.9%               38.2%
    BNP Paribas*       25.0%          75.3%            86.9%               38.2%


 ■When we compare BofA with Heartland Financial USA and BNP Paribas, we see that
    the key difference between them is the effective tax rate.

 ■BofA pays a much lower effective tax rate than the other two banks — the tax burden is
     lighter (higher ratio) for BofA.

 ■In contrast, both other banks have a higher operating margin.




                                                                                                                                                   100 / 121

--- Session 9, page 105 (combined page 1099) ---
Example - Detour





 ■Why are there such big differences in effective tax rates across these banks (BofA 7%,
    Heartland Financial USA 22%, BNP Paribas 25%)?

 ■Part of the reason is that BofA operates mainly in the US, where the corporate tax rate
      is 21% (federal) plus some state taxes.

 ■BNP Paribas operates mainly in France, where the corporate tax rate is 25%, plus a
     social contribution of 3.3% of the tax itself (about 25.8% in total) as of 2024.

 ■The other part, is that these banks can have different tax planning strategies, tax credits,
    deductions, and provisions that affect their effective tax rates.





                                                                                                                                                   101 / 121

--- Session 9, page 106 (combined page 1100) ---
Example - Detour





 ■One key difference between large banks such as BofA and small banks such as
    Heartland Financial USA is the investment in “tax-credit portfolios”.

 ■Banks like BofA invest heavily in renewable energy and affordable-housing structures
     that generate tax credits.

 ■In one of their disclosures for 4Q24, BofA mentioned that its effective tax rate was
    approximately 7% in 2024, and that it would have been about 25% without its tax credits
    (and some one-off tax items).2





   2https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/70858/000007085825000011/bac12312024ex992.htm

                                                                                                                                                   102 / 121

--- Session 9, page 107 (combined page 1101) ---
Example - Asset Turnover = Net Interest Income Ratio + Non-Interest
Income Ratio




     2024            Asset Turnover     Net Interest Income Ratio     Non-Interest Income Ratio

     BofA                3.2%                 1.7%                      1.4%
     Heartland            3.8%                 3.3%                      0.5%
    BNP Paribas          1.8%                 0.7%                      1.1%
    BNP Paribas*         2.1%                 0.9%                      1.3%

  ■Heartland Financial USA has the highest turnover, and relies heavily on net interest
     income...

 ■This is common for smaller/regional banks as they tend to focus more on traditional
    banking activities (gather deposits and make loans).

 ■BofA has a more balanced mix of net interest income and non-interest income.

 ■BNP Paribas has the lowest turnover, and earns more from non-interest income (fees,
     trading, insurance and leasing) than from net interest income.


                                                                                                                                                   103 / 121

--- Session 9, page 108 (combined page 1102) ---
What about other dimensions of analysis?





 ■Up until now we have focused on profitability analysis: decomposing ROE.

  ■However, there are other dimensions of analysis that are important for banks...

 ■Let us look at other 6 ratios that are relevant for some dimensions of CAMELS.





                                                                                                                                                   104 / 121

--- Session 9, page 109 (combined page 1103) ---
Earning Assets Ratio




 ■This ratio measures the proportion of a bank’s assets that are generating interest
    income. Usually:
         • Loans, deposits at central banks and other banks, reverse repos & securities that earn
           interest (e.g. government securities).
         • Derivatives do not earn interest, so we leave them out.
                                               Earning Assets
                         Earning Assets Ratio =
                                                        Total Assets
 ■We usually want this ratio to be as high as possible, as it indicates that the bank is
     effectively utilizing its assets to generate income.

 ■This is about asset quality (A).





                                                                                                                                                   105 / 121

--- Session 9, page 110 (combined page 1104) ---
Average Return and Cost



 ■These ratios measure the average return on earning assets and the average cost of
     interest-bearing liabilities.

                                                    Interest Income
                          Average Return =
                                            Earning Assets

                                                  Interest Expense
                      Average Cost =
                                           Interest-Bearing Liabilities

 ■The difference between these two ratios is the spread, which is a key driver of a bank’s
     net interest margin and profitability.
         •  If interest rates rise, what happens to these ratios?

         •  It depends on the bank’s asset-liability management strategy.

         •  If the cost of borrowing is more sensitive/increases sooner than the return on assets, the
         spread will decrease.

  ■This is about earnings (E).


                                                                                                                                                   106 / 121

--- Session 9, page 111 (combined page 1105) ---
Average Return and Cost





 ■Note that in interest-bearing liabilities we usually include:

 ■Customer deposits that pay interest (e.g. savings accounts, time deposits).

  ■Borrowings that pay interest (e.g. bonds, commercial paper, repurchase agreements).

 ■As with earning assets, we leave out derivative liabilities, which do not pay interest.

 ■In the notes, banks usually provide a breakdown of interest-bearing liabilities and of
     interest-earning assets.





                                                                                                                                                   107 / 121

--- Session 9, page 112 (combined page 1106) ---
Average Return and Cost - Bank of America





 Earning assets. FTE = fully taxable-equivalent basis (tax-exempt interest grossed up). Interest includes the effect of hedges. Source: Bank of America, Form 10-K 2024, Table 8 (p.33).


                                                                                                                                                   108 / 121

--- Session 9, page 113 (combined page 1107) ---
Average Return and Cost - Bank of America





 Interest-bearing liabilities. Net interest spread = yield on earning assets −rate on interest-bearing liabilities (5.08% −4.07%). Source: Bank of America, Form 10-K 2024, Table 8
  (p.33).

                                                                                                                                                   109 / 121

--- Session 9, page 114 (combined page 1108) ---
Expense Ratio





 ■This ratio measures the efficiency of a bank in managing its operating expenses relative
     to its total operating income.

                                            Non-Interest Expense
                     Expense Ratio =
                                               Total Operating Income

 ■A lower expense ratio indicates that the bank is more efficient in controlling its costs and
    generating income.

 ■This is about management quality (M).





                                                                                                                                                   110 / 121

--- Session 9, page 115 (combined page 1109) ---
Loan Loss Coverage Ratio




 ■This ratio measures how many times the bank’s earnings before provisions cover its
     actual losses from loans.

                                       Pre-tax Income + Provision for Loan Losses
        Loan Loss Coverage Ratio =
                                               Net charge-offs

 ■We use net charge-offs (loan write-offs minus recoveries) to measure actual losses from
     loans.

 ■A higher loan loss coverage ratio indicates that the bank can absorb its loan losses
    more comfortably out of its earnings.

 ■This is about asset quality (A).





                                                                                                                                                   111 / 121

--- Session 9, page 116 (combined page 1110) ---
Loan Loss Coverage Ratio





                                                                                                                                                   112 / 121

--- Session 9, page 117 (combined page 1111) ---
Capital Adequacy Ratio



 ■This ratio measures the bank’s capital relative to its risk-weighted assets.

                                           “Measure of Capital”
                       Capital Adequacy Ratio =
                                              Risk Weighted Assets

 ■One measure of capital is “Common Equity Tier 1” (CET1): common equity, including
     retained earnings, minus regulatory deductions such as goodwill and other intangibles.

  ■Another one is “Total Capital”, which is defined by Tier 1 Capital (CET1 + AT1
     instruments, such as perpetual bonds) + Tier 2 Capital (includes subordinated debt).

 ■A higher capital adequacy ratio indicates that the bank has a stronger capital position to
    absorb losses and meet regulatory requirements.

 ■Banks report these ratios in their annual reports; below, we use the reported CET1 ratio.

 ■This is about capital adequacy (C).


                                                                                                                                                   113 / 121

--- Session 9, page 118 (combined page 1112) ---
Ratios for our example banks...





        2024                        BofA      Heartland    BNP Paribas    BNP Paribas*

        Earning Assets Ratio         87.8%      90.8%        68.1%          77.3%
        Average Return              5.2%       6.0%         6.2%           6.2%
        Average Cost                4.2%       3.2%         4.5%           4.5%
        Spread                     1.0%       2.8%         1.7%           1.7%
       Expense Ratio              65.6%      61.3%        61.8%          61.8%
       Loan Loss Coverage Ratio      5.82         3.18            4.98             4.98
         Capital Adequacy Ratio       11.9%      13.2%        12.9%          12.9%





 Capital adequacy ratio = reported CET1 ratio at year-end (BofA: Standardized approach). BNP Paribas does not split out its non-interest-bearing deposits, so its average cost is
 understated relative to the US banks. BNP Paribas’s average return and cost exclude its trading book, whose interest IFRS reports outside net interest income. BNP Paribas*: netting
 only changes the size of the balance sheet, so only the earning assets ratio changes. Source: 2024 annual reports; own calculations.


                                                                                                                                                   114 / 121

--- Session 9, page 119 (combined page 1113) ---
Ratios for our example banks...





 ■These ratios reveal some facts about these banks...
           1  Heartland Financial USA has the highest earning assets ratio, indicating that it is more
         focused on generating interest income from its assets.

           2  In a similar vein, the spread between average return and average cost is much higher for
         Heartland Financial USA...

           3  Despite being much smaller, Heartland Financial USA is not less efficient: its expense ratio
             is similar to BNP Paribas’s and lower than BofA’s.

           4  Heartland Financial USA has the lowest loan loss coverage ratio. This is mainly a one-off
           effect because ahead of its merger with UMB Financial in 2024, it wrote off weaker loans.

           5  The three banks have similar capital adequacy ratios (12–13%).

                                                                                                                                                     2 / 113

--- Session 10, page 3 (combined page 1124) ---
Introduction

--- Session 10, page 4 (combined page 1125) ---
Introduction




  ■Throughout the ------    , we talked a lot about “hard information”.
         • What can we see on the balance sheet/income statement/cash flow statement & notes to
           financial statements...

 ■But firms disclose more than just the numbers of “firm performance”. For instance, they
     disclose:
           1 A “narrative”: the “how” and “why” of firm performance.

           2  Information on governance & sustainability.

           3  The main risks (and opportunities) affecting their business.

           4  Guidance for the future (e.g. EPS in the next year).

           5  Benchmarks you can use for performance (e.g. profitability targets).





                                                                                                                                                     3 / 113

--- Session 10, page 5 (combined page 1126) ---
Introduction




 ■Firm communication (for public firms) happens not only through the annual financial
    statements and the discussion and information there...

 ■But also in additional disclosures:
           1  Quarterly/periodic updates on financials.

           2  Earnings conference calls.

           3  Investor days.

           4  Various press releases & reports (e.g. sustainability, ESG and climate reports).

  ■All of these communications contain material information that affects current or future
     firm performance.

 ■Our “job” as outsiders is judging just how material these pieces of information are...





                                                                                                                                                     4 / 113

--- Session 10, page 6 (combined page 1127) ---
Introduction





 ■A frequently scrutinized for of firm communication is earnings conference calls.

 ■This is because in those calls analysts & investors not only have the chance to hear
    management’s prepared statements...

 ■They also have the opportunity to ask questions and get more “natural” (less prepared)
    responses from management.

 ■For example, Matsumoto, Pronk and Roelofsen (2011) show that it is the Q&A part of
    the earnings call that generates most of the market reaction around the call.





                                                                                                                                                     5 / 113

--- Session 10, page 7 (combined page 1128) ---
Lazy Prices




 ■Annual reports (10-Ks) have become very long, and most of the text is repeated from
    one year to the next...

 ■Cohen, Malloy and Nguyen (2020) show that when firms change the language of their
    10-Ks and 10-Qs, this is an important signal about the future.

 ■Firms that change their reports the most (“changers”) have lower future returns. These
    changes also predict future earnings, profitability, and even bankruptcies.

 ■But there is no market reaction when the report is released: investors only incorporate
     this information gradually, over the following months.

  ■Prices are “lazy”: investors do not read the annual reports carefully!





                                                                                                                                                     6 / 113

--- Session 10, page 8 (combined page 1129) ---
Lazy Prices





 ■The average 10-K went from about 15,000 words in 1995 to more than 60,000 words in
    2017!



                                                                                                                                                     7 / 113

--- Session 10, page 9 (combined page 1130) ---
Lazy Prices





                                               Sim Cosine

                             Q1      Q2      Q3     Q4     Q5    Q5 – Q1

               Excess return         0.63*      0.72*     0.72**   0.85**   0.92***   0.31***
                                          (1.68)      (1.96)     (2.11)    (2.59)    (2.80)      (3.13)
                Three-factor alpha   −0.15**   −0.08    −0.05     0.09     0.18***   0.34***
                                     (−2.19)    (−1.10)   (−0.72)    (1.21)    (2.66)      (4.45)
                 Five-factor alpha    −0.12*    −0.05    −0.04     0.10     0.21***   0.32***
                                     (−1.75)    (−0.74)   (−0.53)    (1.29)    (3.28)      (4.21)

 ■Firms are sorted by how similar their report is to last year’s: Q1 = the biggest
    “changers”, Q5 = the “non-changers”.

 ■Buying the non-changers and selling the changers (Q5 – Q1) earns an abnormal return
     of about 0.3% per month!

--- Session 10, page 17 (combined page 1138) ---
Biases in interpreting firm
      performance

--- Session 10, page 18 (combined page 1139) ---
What do the numbers tell us?





 ■Our “job” as outsiders is to look at this vast supply of information and decide...
         • How “good” or “bad” firm performance has been.

         • How “good” or “bad” firm performance will be.

  ■Looking at information and taking these two steps is not easy, and our biases may come
     in the way.

 ■One simple to think about these biases is focusing on reaction to new pieces of
     information.





                                                                                                                                                    16 / 113

--- Session 10, page 19 (combined page 1140) ---
What do the numbers tell us?





 ■Suppose you had some forecast for earnings in the 3Q-2025 and in the 4Q-2925.

 ■Suppose you see a new piece of information: the earnings realization for the 3Q-2025.

 ■How do your beliefs about the 4Q-2025 change?
         •  If you react by “too much”, you will overshoot the “correct” value for 4Q-2025.

         •  If you react by “too little”, you will undershoot the “correct” value for 4Q-2025.

         •  If you react “optimally”, you will (on average) get the right value for 4Q-2025.

 ■To learn about biases, we can check if on average, people update too much, too little, or
     in just the right amount to new information.





                                                                                                                                                    17 / 113

--- Session 10, page 20 (combined page 1141) ---
What do the numbers tell us?





 ■We can also test for other biases:


    1  Does the way you react to news depend on the “valence” of the news?
         • Are you more or less prone to over-reacting/under-reaction to good versus bad news?

    2  Does the way you react to news depend on who the source of the news is?
         •  If you see the same news for firms that are identical except for one non-material
           characteristic, would you react differently?

Main findings





  ■Let’s start with setting 1 and look at analysts’ EPS forecasts.

 ■We want to learn if analysts are updating their forecasts in response to news...
         • By too little: under-reaction.

         • By too much: over-reaction.

         • By the “correct” amount.





                                                                                                                                                    22 / 113

--- Session 10, page 25 (combined page 1146) ---
Main variables and notation




 ■Let t be a given announcement date, a a given analyst, and i a given company.
         • Xi,t+1 is the realization of earnings per share for firm i announced at period t + 1.
         •  ˆEa,t(Xi,t+1) is analyst a’s forecast for Xi,t+1 at period t.


 ■So the forecast error for analyst a and firm i at period t + 1 is

                         Forecast Errora,i,t+1 = Xi,t+1 −ˆEa,t(Xi,t+1)


 ■and the respective forecast revision at t is

                               Revisiona,i,t = ˆEa,t(Xi,t+1) −ˆEa,t−1(Xi,t+1)

         associated with female CEOs.





                                                                                                                                                    25 / 113

--- Session 10, page 28 (combined page 1149) ---
The Coibion-Gorodnichenko (CG) regression: reaction to news



  ■Consider running the regression...

                    Forecast Errora,i,t+1 = α + β × Revisiona,i,t + ua,i,t+1,


 ■β > 0 implies that agents under-react to news.
         • Between t −1 and t, agents receive some piece of news and revise expectations
          accordingly, in the correct direction.

         • But systematically do so by less than they should.


 ■β < 0 implies that agents over-react to news.
         • Between t −1 and t, agents receive some piece of news and revise expectations
          accordingly, in the correct direction.

         • But systematically do so by more than they should.

Earnings conference calls




  ■Earnings conference calls are quarterly events held by public companies to go over the

           1  Presentation.

           1  Analysts and executives verbally interact with each other.

           2  These meetings are specifically aimed at discussing firm performance.

 ■In this setting, I observe analysts’ full name: so I can determine analysts’ gender.
         • On average, male analysts represent more than 85% of the analyst pool in these calls.

--- Session 10, page 31 (combined page 1152) ---
Measuring sentiment



  ■Sentiment is computed using the classification of positive (e.g. improve or upturn) and
    negative (e.g. deterioration or misconduct) words from the dictionary in Loughran and
    McDonald (2011).


 ■For a given text excerpt,

                                             #words,+ −#words,−
                           Sentiment =                                .
                                                  #words

  ■Executives’ sentiment is computed using the text from executives’ presentations in
    earnings conference calls.


  ■Analysts’ sentiment is computed using the text from analysts’ questions in earnings
    conference calls.


                                                                                                                                                    29 / 113

--- Session 10, page 32 (combined page 1153) ---
Measuring the sentiment gap





 ■I measure the sentiment gap between analysts and executives by the distance between
     their sentiment scores...

             Sentiment Gap = |Analysts′ Sentiment −Executive′s Sentiment|.


 ■The worse is analysts’ sentiment relative to that conveyed by firm executives, the larger
      is this measure.

--- Session 10, page 33 (combined page 1154) ---
Interpreting the sentiment gap: disagreement between analysts and the
firm’s narrative





   1  Analysts ask about “negative” topics more often than executives bring them up in their
    speeches.
         • “This week, unions for aircraft controllers, pilots and flight attendants sounded the alarm over
          the shutdown’s effects on the U.S. aviation system and raised questions about safety, that
          the entire system could, at some point break. How concerned are you about that right now?”
        (EC American Airlines Group, Q4-2018)

   2  Analysts use more “negative” language to describe a topic compared to firm executives.
         • “I am just trying to reconcile your [previous] statement (...). I just saw a report today (...)
         showing defaults down 10%, from their distress side we are seeing a lack of flow in terms of
           restructurings, a lot of stuff coming out of bankruptcy, how does that fit with your expectation
           that things wouldn’t slow down on that side? ” (EC FTI Consulting, Q2-2003)

--- Session 10, page 35 (combined page 1156) ---
What have we learned?





 ■We learned that:


    1 On average, analysts react optimally to bad news about firm performance. But they
    under-react to good news.

    2 On average, analysts over-react to bad news from female-led companies: they react
   more to bad news from female- relative to male-led companies.

  ■This suggests that the valence of the news and the source of the news matter for
     interpretation!





                                                                                                                                                    33 / 113

--- Session 10, page 36 (combined page 1157) ---
What other biases?




 ■We saw that analysts react differently to the same news depending on the gender of the
    CEO.

  ■Another important question is whether analysts’ “initial” beliefs are also biased.

 ■Or is it that only the reaction to news is biased by CEO gender?

 ■To answer this question, we need to look at average forecast errors!

  ■If analysts are rational, then on average they should not make systematic errors in their
     forecasts.

  ■This means that average forecast errors should be zero...

--- Session 10, page 40 (combined page 1161) ---
Why further research?





  ■Regardless of CEO gender, the literature in behavioral finance still does not understand
     exactly how individuals interpret financial statements.

 ■We have theories that predict what is the “optimal” final outcome of this interpretation —
     that is, the “optimal” forecast.

 ■But we don’t know how actual people think about statements, and how they map:

              Large pool of financial information ⇀Final forecast for earnings

  ■This is an opportunity to learn about this from talented individuals (you)!

--- Session 10, page 47 (combined page 1168) ---
The setting





 ■To understand reaction to news, each case had a treatment with expert forecasts.

 ■These forecasts were produced by taking the true realization and adding noise.
         • Either experts’ forecasts were pessimistic (negative noise).

         • Or experts’ forecasts were optimistic (positive noise).

 ■The forecasts were accompanied by a piece of text explaining the sentiment around the
    numbers.
         • An AI agent was trained with analyst reports.

         • The text excerpt was then generated by this agent based on the case material and the
           forecasts, using realistic language from analysts.

                                                                                                                                                    50 / 113

--- Session 10, page 54 (combined page 1175) ---
Sustainability-related disclosures

--- Session 10, page 55 (combined page 1176) ---
Introduction




  ■Recently there has been much discussion about sustainability-related disclosures,
    whether they should be made and how they should be made.

 ■When we say “sustainability-related” we mean any type of risk or opportunity that could
     affect a company’s cash flows, access to finance or cost of capital, but that might
    not be completely captured in usual financial disclosures.

 ■When these risks and opportunities are large enough, information about them is
     material: it affects investors’ assessment of the value of a company.

 ■These risks/opportunities encompass not only environment-related ones, but also social
    ones (e.g. human capital) and governance ones. →This is “ESG accounting”.





                                                                                                                                                    51 / 113

--- Session 10, page 56 (combined page 1177) ---
Introduction



 ■In 2023, the ISSB issued the first global baseline framework for sustainability
     disclosures (IFRS S1 and IFRS S2).

 ■These standards are effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1
    January 2024.

 ■They are obligatory only in jurisdictions that adopt them: more than 45 jurisdictions
    have adopted them or are taking steps to do so.
         • Australia, Hong Kong and Singapore require climate-related disclosures (IFRS S2) starting
            in 2025 — in Singapore, only for the largest listed companies for now.

         • The UK (listed companies, comply-or-explain, from 2027) and Japan (largest listed
         companies, from the fiscal year ending March 2027) come next.

         • Some are slowing down: Brazil replaced its 2026 mandate with comply-or-explain from 2027,
        and Chile delayed its start by one year.





                                                                                                                                                    52 / 113

--- Session 10, page 57 (combined page 1178) ---
Introduction





 ■We still have not seen many full disclosures following this new standard. . .

 ■What I will try to do today:
           1 Show you the most important organizing concepts for this IFRS framework.

           2 Show you illustrative examples from companies that have made sustainability disclosures
           that are in line with the new standards.





                                                                                                                                                    53 / 113

--- Session 10, page 58 (combined page 1179) ---
Introduction - What about the EU?





 ■The EU has its own sustainability-related framework for corporate disclosure, which is
    mandatory for large companies — phasing in started in FY2024.

 ■This framework is the ESRS (European Sustainability Reporting Standards).

  ■It is likely that companies in the EU with substantial outside investors will prepare
     disclosures that attend both ESRS and IFRS requirements.

 ■IFRS and ESRS guidance are fairly similar in their format...





                                                                                                                                                    54 / 113

--- Session 10, page 59 (combined page 1180) ---
Introduction - What about the EU?




 ■The key difference between them is about what is material information that should be
     disclosed...
         • IFRS takes the view of “financial materiality”.

         • ESRS takes a “double materiality” perspective: both financial materiality and impact
           materiality should be considered.

  ■Financial materiality:
         • Could a sustainability matter create or change risks or opportunities that materially affect
          enterprise value (performance, position, cash flows)?

 ■Impact materiality:
         • Are the company’s activities causing, contributing to, or directly linked (via value chain) to
           significant impacts on people or the environment?





                                                                                                                                                    55 / 113

--- Session 10, page 60 (combined page 1181) ---
Introduction





 ■Up until now, two organizing standards have been issued: IFRS S1 and IFRS S2.

 ■IFRS S1 defines the organizing framework for how sustainability-related disclosures
    should be made.

 ■IFRS S2 focuses on climate-related disclosures, giving more guidance and structure to
     this specific dimension of sustainability

  ■Recently (April 2024), IFRS has launched a specific human capital related project to
    issue more guidance on this dimension — a decision is expected by 2027.





                                                                                                                                                    56 / 113

--- Session 10, page 61 (combined page 1182) ---
Human-capital related risks and opportunities




 ■What do we mean by “human capital" related risks/opportunities?

 ■We mean anything about a company’s workforce that might significantly affect its
     current/future cash flows.

 ■Some examples:
         • High employee turnover and low employee satisfaction.

         • High injury or illness rates.

         • A safety automation program, requiring wearables that could lead to reduced incidents and
         insurance premiums.

         • Pay-equity gaps.

         • Strikes.

         • Forced labor or child labor.





                                                                                                                                                    57 / 113

--- Session 10, page 62 (combined page 1183) ---
Governance-related risks and opportunities




 ■What do we mean by “governance" related risks/opportunities?

 ■We mean anything about a company’s governance that might significantly affect its
     current/future cash flows.

 ■For example...
         • Executive remuneration tied to sustainability-related performance.

         • Existence/non-existence of board-level oversight of cybersecurity/data
          privacy/environmental/labor risks.

 ■A note: cybersecurity & data privacy risks & opportunities are included in sustainability
     related topics.





                                                                                                                                                    58 / 113

--- Session 10, page 63 (combined page 1184) ---
What we will do. . .





 ■So now let’s understand what IFRS S1 prescribes as a general framework for
     sustainability-related risks and opportunities.

 ■Then we will discuss the climate-related specific guidance contained in IFRS S2.





                                                                                                                                                    59 / 113

--- Session 10, page 64 (combined page 1185) ---
A detour about financial disclosures




 ■The regular IFRS standard aim to provide material information regarding...
         • The current economic situation of the company.

         • The future risks and opportunities that might affect the economic conditions of the company.

 ■The objective is to ensure that a company discloses the necessary information to
    assess the future cash flows, financial performance and value of that company.

 ■Under IFRS, companies are allowed to not disclose commercially sensitive
     opportunities.
         • That is, information that could materially hurt the company’s performance and prospects.

 ■But all of the risks must be depicted since, otherwise, investors could be materially hurt.





                                                                                                                                                    60 / 113

--- Session 10, page 65 (combined page 1186) ---
Investor’s perspective is the key in IFRS S1



 ■The sustainability-disclosure framework proposed by IFRS has a similar objective:
         • Take the “investor’s perspective” and ensure that all financially material sustainability-related
           risks and opportunities are disclosed.

         • The focus is on information that can indeed affect the business’ cash flows, financial
         performance and value in the short, medium, and long terms.

 ■This is because the primary users of financial/sustainability-related disclosures are
     investors assessing their investment decisions.

 ■From this perspective, sustainability-related disclosures are not aimed at being
     “normative”...
         • The idea is not penalizing/benefiting some companies with respect to their
           sustainability-related impacts.

         • Rather, the idea is: if sustainability-related issues might create financially material risks and
           opportunities, this information should be disclosed.




                                                                                                                                                    61 / 113

--- Session 10, page 66 (combined page 1187) ---
(financial) Materiality




 ■I will now refer to “material” as in “financially material”...

 ■What is material information?
         • Material information is that information that could be reasonably expected to affect the
          decisions of the primary users of financial reports.

         • This involves determining who are the primary users and which decisions they make based
        on financial reports.

         • Generally, primary users will be investors assessing their investment decisions.

 ■In this sense, the “size” of the anticipated impact on cash flows/ financial performance is
     the key determinant of materiality.

 ■Of course, that is open to judgement. . .





                                                                                                                                                    62 / 113

--- Session 10, page 67 (combined page 1188) ---
On the scope of materiality





 ■The idea is that the same entity that prepares financial statements will also prepare a
     sustainability-related disclosure following the new issued standards.

  ■If the entity prepares consolidated financial statements, the respective
     sustainability-related disclosure should consider all risks and opportunities that affect
    parent and subsidiaries.

  ■Moreover, companies have to consider all of the sustainability-related risks and
     opportunities that...
         • Affect themselves directly.
         • Affect them indirectly, through their value chain.





                                                                                                                                                    63 / 113

--- Session 10, page 68 (combined page 1189) ---
On the scope of materiality - Example




 ■Suppose Entity Smartphone produces and sells smartphones.

 ■Suppose Entity Cobalt is a major supplier in its value-chain, refining cobalt for Entity
    Smartphone’s smartphone battery supplier, Entity Battery.

 ■The media and NGOs have reported unsafe working conditions and improper waste
     disposal in Entity Cobalt’s plants.

 ■The local regulator at Entity Cobalt’s location opens an investigation.

 ■The local regulator at Entity Battery’s location signals import restrictions on cobalt from
     Entity Cobalt...

 ■This is a risk that affects Entity Smartphone and must be disclosed!





                                                                                                                                                    64 / 113

--- Session 10, page 69 (combined page 1190) ---
On the timing of materiality





 ■Under IFRS guidance, companies need to disclose sustainability-related information
     that is material for the short, medium and long term’s prospects.

 ■But what is short, medium and long term?
         • Because this timing might significantly depend on business model and industry, IFRS
         decided to leave this decision to companies.

         • But IFRS requires that the company specifies its definition of short, medium and long term.

--- Session 10, page 72 (combined page 1193) ---
Judgment?





 ■There might be room for management judgment...
         • Who is the primary user and which decisions this user makes?

         • What is material information to this user?

         • What is short, medium, and long term?

         • What is the extent of one’s value chain?

         • What is material information beyond the company itself, i.e. with respect to its value chain?

 ■Note that the standard does prescribe methods to determining these steps...

--- Session 10, page 73 (combined page 1194) ---
The “core content”




 ■Beyond of only identifying these sustainability-related risks and opportunities, IFRS
     requires information about the associated...
           1  Governance.

           2  Strategy.

           3  Risk management.

           4  Metrics and targets.

 ■The idea is:
         • The company must identify the risks and opportunities.

         • And also provide information on how it will manage them!

         • Knowing whether a company has structure in place to manage the risks & opportunities is
           material.
