--- Session 10, page 78 (combined page 1199) ---
The “core content” - strategy (Commonwealth Bank)




              Outcome of our most severe acute event scenario
                  A key outcome of our acute event scenario analysis is an estimate of the
                           potential impact on our credit losses from two severe Category 4 cyclones
                          affecting the South‑East Queensland region over consecutive years.
                     The scenario incorporates a severe regional economic shock, including a
                        sharp increase in local unemployment, reflecting the potential combined
                          effects of physical damage and economic disruption on our retail and
                         corporate portfolios. Under this scenario, total credit losses are estimated
                           at $1.5 billion over five years, with most losses occurring in the first two
                         years following the events. The scenario is intentionally designed as a
                         severe stress to assess the Bank’s resilience under extreme conditions.

                       Losses under this scenario are primarily driven by retail exposures,
                            reflecting borrower repayment stress and declines in property values
                              in impacted areas. Customer insurance coverage may mitigate some
                         property losses but does not fully offset borrower repayment risk.
                       Estimated impacts to corporate exposures reflect business disruption,
                        supply chain impacts and broader economic decline following the events,
                             particularly in the commercial property and wholesale trade sectors.

                          This is a severe scenario and is currently considered to have a very low
                            probability of occurring. The overall impact is not currently assessed as
                             financially material to the Bank, reflecting the appropriateness of current
                           provisioning levels and the strength of our balance sheet. The estimated
                          losses are also consistent with, or below, those experienced in more severe
                              historical stress periods. They are also not expected to impact the Bank’s     Impact of a Category 4 cyclone over Brisbane
                            capital or liquidity risk appetite settings.

                                For information on the key inputs and assumptions used, see page 375 in the Sustainability Report Appendix.

                                                                                                                                                    76 / 113

--- Session 10, page 81 (combined page 1202) ---
Metrics and targets





 ■For each risk/opportunities, the company must disclose the specific metrics and targets
     that should be used to evaluate its sustainability-related performance.

  ■Specifically, companies should disclose metrics and targets that are “industry-based”.
         • “Industry” should capture dimensions such as particular business models, activities or other
       common features among companies.

 ■The specific requirements for climate-related risks and opportunities are set in IFRS S2.

 ■For other topics, ISSB still has not issued specific guidance. In this case, companies
    must provide guidance on how they determined these metrics and targets.





                                                                                                                                                    77 / 113

--- Session 10, page 82 (combined page 1203) ---
Metrics and targets - SASB





 ■IFRS has not issued guidance on specific topics (beyond climate).

 ■The SASB standard remains the main source of industry-based guidance for materiality
     of information, and definition of metrics and targets.

 ■SASB was created in 2011 and consists of industry-specific standards covering 77
     industries (grouped into 11 sectors).

 ■Since 2022, SASB has been under command of IFRS, and the expectation is that its
    guidance will be incorporated within the topic-specific standards that IFRS creates.





                                                                                                                                                    78 / 113

--- Session 10, page 83 (combined page 1204) ---
On the importance of SASB guidance





 ■SASB specific guidance on materiality is effective in guiding investors towards the
     relevant sustainability-related information in each industry.

 ■By “relevant information”, I mean information that moves markets (i.e., information that
    matters for determining cash flows, financial performance, and value).

 ■This is what a paper by George Serafeim and co-authors has shown!





                                                                                                                                                    79 / 113

--- Session 10, page 84 (combined page 1205) ---
Khan, Serafeim and Yoon (2016)




 ■The authors use SASB’s industry materiality framework and hand-map it to MSCI KLD
    data to classify U.S. firms’ sustainability performance into material vs immaterial
     buckets.

 ■With that, they create a firm-level rating of material sustainability and of non-material
     sustainability.

 ■They then test which of these ratings actually generates higher alphas (risk-adjusted
    expected return).

 ■To make sure these effects are due to material vs non-material information, the authors
     control for a series of important firm-level characteristics:
         • Size, leverage, profitability, market-to-book-value, and sector.

--- Session 10, page 88 (combined page 1209) ---
What about in the US?





 ■In March 2024, the SEC issued rules regarding the disclosure of climate-change related
     disclosures.
         • The implementation was pending judicial review.

 ■In March 2025, the SEC announced that it will stop defending these rules in court... and
     in May 2026 it proposed to rescind them altogether.

 ■Even so, the expectation is that many companies continue issuing climate-related
     disclosures.
         • This is due to state-specific regulations, like California’s emissions law.

         • and also because of stakeholder pressure.





                                                                                                                                                    84 / 113

--- Session 10, page 89 (combined page 1210) ---
Climate-related disclosures - IFRS S2





 ■This is a topic-specific disclosure associated with the risks and opportunities that an
     entity expects to arise from climate change.

 ■This includes not only the direct risks and opportunities that affect the specific entity, but
     also the indirect ones that affect the entity through its value chain.

 ■There are two main dimensions for the identification of these risks and opportunities:
         • Those arising from the physical exposure of the company to climate change.

         • Those arising from the transition to a low-carbon economy.





                                                                                                                                                    85 / 113

--- Session 10, page 90 (combined page 1211) ---
The “core content” of climate-related disclosure





  ■Similar to what we saw for IFRS S1, this core content relates to the 4 pillars
    (governance, strategy, risk management, and metrics & targets).

 ■These pillars determine how the company identifies and manages the risks and
     opportunities that arise due to climate change.

 ■The key difference between IFRS S2 and the general approach of IFRS S1 is in the
     prescription of specific metrics and targets.





                                                                                                                                                    86 / 113

--- Session 10, page 91 (combined page 1212) ---
Metrics and targets



 ■IFRS S2 requires metrics and targets in three dimensions...


   1  Information related to seven cross-industry metric categories (i.e., metrics that are
     industry-agnostic).

   2  Industry-based metrics according to the entity’s business model and economic
      activities.

   3  Climate-related targets which may be set either by the entity or by some regulations
    and laws that apply to that entity.
         • These are the targets that are used to assess the firm’s climate-related performance over
           time.

  ■Let’s take a more detailed look at the first...





                                                                                                                                                    87 / 113

--- Session 10, page 92 (combined page 1213) ---
Cross-industry metrics





   1 GHG emissions: absolute gross GHG emissions generated during the reported period
      (in metric tonnes of CO2 equivalent), classified as: Scopes 1, 2, and 3.

   2  Climate-related transition risks: the amount and percentage of assets or business
     activities vulnerable to climate-related transition risks.

   3  Climate-related physical risks: the amount and percentage of assets or business
     activities vulnerable to climate-related physical risks.

   4  Climate-related opportunities: the amount and percentage of assets or business
     activities aligned with climate-related opportunities.





                                                                                                                                                    88 / 113

--- Session 10, page 93 (combined page 1214) ---
Cross-industry metrics





   5  Capital deployment: the amount of capital expenditure, financing or investment
    deployed towards climate-related risks and opportunities.

   6  Internal carbon pricing: the price of GHG emissions (per metric tonne) that the entity
    uses to assess the cost of its GHG emissions.

   7  Remuneration: whether and how climate-related considerations factor into executive
    compensation, and the percentage of executive remuneration in the period that is
     related to climate considerations.





                                                                                                                                                    89 / 113

--- Session 10, page 94 (combined page 1215) ---
A bit of background




 ■What are the different scopes of GHG emissions?

 ■These are based on the “GHG Protocol” (Greenhouse Gas Protocol: A Corporate
    Accounting and Reporting Standard, 2004).

 ■The GHG Protocol provides guidance and standards to account for and measure seven
    types of GHG, one of them being CO2.

 ■For reporting purposes, companies have to standardize all emissions in equivalent
    tonnes of CO2.

 ■The three scopes relate to the the “directness” of those emissions in relation to the
     reporting entity.





                                                                                                                                                    90 / 113

--- Session 10, page 95 (combined page 1216) ---
A bit of background





 ■Scope 1 emissions are those directly made by the reporting entity (and should be
    broken down between the consolidated group and other investees, such as associates
    and joint ventures).

 ■Scope 2 and Scope 3 emissions are those indirectly made by the reporting entity.
         • Scope 2 emissions are those that arise from the generation of purchased or acquired
             electricity, steam, heating, or cooling that an entity consumes.

         • Scope 2 emissions also have to be broken down between the consolidated group and other
          investees.

         • Scope 3 emissions are those that arise through the entity’s value chain.





                                                                                                                                                    91 / 113

--- Session 10, page 96 (combined page 1217) ---
A bit of background





 ■A reporting entity may have many different entities in its legal and organizational
     structure:
         • Wholly owned subsidiaries.

         • Partially owned subsidiaries.

         • Equity-method investments.





                                                                                                                                                    92 / 113

--- Session 10, page 97 (combined page 1218) ---
A bit of background





 ■The reporting entity must choose one of two methods to determine its “boundary” and
    what emissions it should consider in Scopes 1 and 2...


   1  Equity-share method: the company adjusts total emissions of the entities in its
     organization’s structure based on its share ownership.

   2  Control method: the company includes 100% of the emissions of all the operations it
    has control over, independently of share ownership or legal ownership.
         • As “control”, the company chooses either financial control (of its economic risks and
           benefits) or operational control (literally, of the operational decisions).





                                                                                                                                                    93 / 113

--- Session 10, page 98 (combined page 1219) ---
Example



  ■Consider that ParentCo has four business interests:
           1  S-Co, its subsidiary: 60% owned, and controlled by ParentCo. Emissions of 100.

           2  J-Co, a joint venture: 30% owned, and operated by ParentCo. Emissions of 80.

           3  A-Co, an associate investment: 45% owned, but controlled by another company. Emissions
           of 60.

           4  M-Co, a minority holding: 10% owned, but controlled by another company. Emissions of 40.

  ■Emissions listed are Scope 1 and 2.

 ■Under the equity-share method, what emissions (Scope 1 & 2) from business interests
    should be considered by ParentCo?

              60% × 100 + 30% × 80 + 45% × 60 + 10% × 40 = 115

 ■The “remaining” emissions, 40% from S-Co, 70% of J-Co, 55% of A-Co, and 90% of
   M-Co belong to their other owners — not to ParentCo!


                                                                                                                                                    94 / 113

--- Session 10, page 99 (combined page 1220) ---
Example



  ■Consider that ParentCo has four business interests:
           1  S-Co, its subsidiary: 60% owned, and controlled by ParentCo. Emissions of 100.

           2  J-Co, a joint venture: 30% owned, and operated by ParentCo. Emissions of 80.

           3  A-Co, an associate investment: 45% owned, but controlled by another company. Emissions
           of 60.

           4  M-Co, a minority holding: 10% owned, but controlled by another company. Emissions of 40.

  ■Emissions listed are Scope 1 and 2.

 ■Under the control-method (operational control), what emissions (Scope 1 & 2) from
    business interests should be considered by ParentCo?

                                100 + 80 = 180

 ■The “remaining” emissions, 45% × 60 from A-Co and 10% × 40 from M-Co, are included
    as Scope 3 emissions by ParentCo.


                                                                                                                                                    95 / 113

--- Session 10, page 100 (combined page 1221) ---
Example




 ■What about Scope 3 emissions of ParentCo?

 ■Scope 3 are the emissions are those outside of the organizational boundary.

 ■Your first determine this boundary using the equity-share method or control methods for
    Scopes 1 and 2.

 ■Then you look at the value chain beyond this boundary...
         • Under the equity-share method, all of the four business interests are within your boundary,
          adjusted for equity-share.

         • Under the control method, only S-Co and J-Co are within your boundary!

         • That is why, in that case, you include emissions from A-Co and M-Co in your Scope 3.





                                                                                                                                                    96 / 113

--- Session 10, page 101 (combined page 1222) ---
Example (Standard Chartered PLC)





                                                                                                                                                    97 / 113

--- Session 10, page 102 (combined page 1223) ---
Example (car maker)



  ■Consider that CarCo makes and sells 1 million petrol cars per year. The fuel and
     electricity used in its factories generate 0.5m tonnes of CO2 (Scopes 1 and 2).

 ■Each car uses about 1 tonne of steel bought from suppliers. CarCo does not know the
     actual emissions of its suppliers, so it uses an industry average: 2 tonnes of CO2 per
    tonne of steel.

 ■Each car is driven 150,000 km over its life, and emits 120 g of CO2 per km.

 ■What are the Scope 3 emissions of CarCo from the steel it buys and from its customers
     driving its cars?
         • Upstream (purchased steel): 1m cars × 1 tonne × 2 = 2m tonnes.
         • Downstream (use of sold products): 1m cars × 150,000 km × 0.12 kg = 18m tonnes.

 ■These two categories alone are 40 times Scopes 1 and 2! And they are estimates:
     industry averages and assumptions about how customers will drive the cars over many
     future years — all reported in the year the cars are sold.


                                                                                                                                                    98 / 113

--- Session 10, page 103 (combined page 1224) ---
The issues with sustainability-related disclosures



 ■The key issue with sustainability-related disclosures is that they are not audited like
     financial statements, and companies have incentives to “greenwash” their disclosures.
         • For example, Bingler et al. (2022) find that firms’ climate-risk disclosures are mostly cheap
            talk: firms cherry-pick what to disclose, and end up disclosing mostly non-material climate
            risks.

 ■To signal truthfulness, many companies voluntarily hire third-party reviewers to provide
    assurance on their sustainability-related disclosures.
         • This assurance is “limited”, meaning that the reviewer does not provide a full audit (e.g.
          there are no site visits to check meters, and no large-sample tests tracing emissions back to
           fuel invoices).

         • The reviewer basically checks whether the company followed the relevant reporting
          standards, and whether the information disclosed is consistent with the company’s internal
          records.

 ■In places like the EU, this type of limited assurance is required for large companies.



                                                                                                                                                    99 / 113

--- Session 10, page 104 (combined page 1225) ---
Accounting in the age of AI

--- Session 10, page 105 (combined page 1226) ---
Accounting in the age of AI





 ■In the next few years, the expectation is that AI will impact many dimensions associated
     with “accounting”.

  ■It is bound to change the internal procedures for financial reporting and auditing.

 ■And it is bound to change the accessibility of financial disclosures to the general public...


         •  It facilitates “reading” the notes and grasping the amount of information disclosed.

         •  It facilitates “comprehending” what the numbers mean and might help better
          decision-making.





                                                                                                                                                   100 / 113

--- Session 10, page 106 (combined page 1227) ---
Accounting in the age of AI





 ■A recent paper by Ashraf (2024) asks: Does automation improve financial reporting?

  ■Evidence suggests that yes, it does!

 ■US firms who adopted automation in their reporting process show significantly fewer
     internal control material weaknesses (as classified by Audit Analytics).





                                                                                                                                                   101 / 113

--- Session 10, page 107 (combined page 1228) ---
Accounting in the age of AI





                                                                                                                                                   102 / 113

--- Session 10, page 108 (combined page 1229) ---
Accounting in the age of AI





 ■An FT article was released a couple of days ago and reported that AI “asked better
     questions” in the audit process than KPMG auditors.

  ■Basically, KPMG ran parallel audits of a FTSE 250 company, one with AI and one with
   human auditors.

 ■The AI audit, based on more than 100 agents, asked questions that in some cases were
    more relevant than those that the human auditors asked.

 ■But human supervision was still critical, as the AI agents sometimes failed to perform
     tasks correctly — like matching a set of regulations to the company’s documents.





                                                                                                                                                   103 / 113

--- Session 10, page 109 (combined page 1230) ---
Accounting in the age of AI




 ■A recent paper by Cao, Jiang, Wang and Yang (2024) asks: Can AI do the job of a stock
     analyst?

 ■They build an “AI analyst” that predicts 12-month stock returns using firms’ disclosures,
     industry trends and macroeconomic data... and compare it to the forecasts of human
     analysts.

 ■The AI analyst beats human analysts in 54.5% of the forecasts!

 ■But humans still win when institutional knowledge matters: e.g. firms with more
     intangible assets, illiquid firms, or firms close to financial distress.

 ■And the combination is even better: “Man + Machine” beats the AI alone, and avoids
    about 90% of the extreme errors made by analysts (and 40% of those made by the AI).





                                                                                                                                                   104 / 113

--- Session 10, page 110 (combined page 1231) ---
Accounting in the age of AI





 ■Man vs. Machine: in most years, human analysts beat the AI analyst in less than 50%
     of the forecasts.


                                                                                                                                                   105 / 113

--- Session 10, page 111 (combined page 1232) ---
Accounting in the age of AI





 ■Man + Machine: adding analysts’ forecasts to the AI (blue) beats the AI alone in most
     years. Humans alone (green) do not!


                                                                                                                                                   106 / 113

--- Session 10, page 112 (combined page 1233) ---
Accounting in the age of AI




  ■Another recent paper by Shaffer and Wang (2025) asks: Can LLMs do the work of an
     analyst and measure a firm’s “core earnings” from the 10-K?
         • Core earnings are the persistent profitability from a company’s central business activities.

 ■The paper gives the 10-Ks of US firms (2000–2023) to GPT-4o in two ways:
         • “Lazy analyst”: one prompt with the definition of core earnings and the full 10-K.
         • “Sequential prompt”: first list the unusual expenses, then the unusual income, then adjust net
         income for them.

 ■The lazy analyst often gets the concept wrong (e.g. it adds back interest and
     depreciation, as in EBITDA). The sequential prompt does better than net income and
     the Compustat measures (benchmark) →how you ask matters!





                                                                                                                                                   107 / 113

--- Session 10, page 113 (combined page 1234) ---
Accounting in the age of AI





 ■Each row regresses stock prices two years later on the earnings measure, with a
     different P/E multiple for each industry and year.
 ■The LLM measures line up better with stock prices than net income and the Compustat
    measures! (One year later, all measures are very close.)
